, requires some more technical complications that are addressed in Supplementary Section
S3
.

4.
Local laws: set-up and results

In this section we state our second main technical result: the
optimal averaged two-resolvent local law
for matrices with a Gaussian component, formulated in Proposition
4.2
. This result provides a bound for fluctuations of quantities of the form
\langle G^{z_{1}}(w_{1})B_{1}G^{z_{2}}(w_{2})B_{2}\rangle
around their deterministic counterparts. Here
B_{1},B_{2}\in\mathbf{C}^{(2N)\times(2N)}
are deterministic matrices, and
|\Im w_{1}|,|\Im w_{2}|\gg N^{-1}
. Proposition
4.2
is one of the key ingredients in the proof of Proposition
3.4
, allowing for the precise bound on the error term in (
3.35
), see the discussion around
8
8

8

While (
3.40
) concerns the fluctuation of
\langle G_{1}AE_{\sigma}G_{2}^{2}E_{\sigma}\rangle
with
G_{j}:=G^{z_{j}}(w_{j})
for
j=1,2
, which is not directly controlled to the two-resolvent local law, the number of resolvents in this quantity can be easily reduced to two using the identity
G_{2}^{2}=\partial_{w_{2}}G_{2}
.
(
3.40
).

Throughout the paper we refer to concentration bounds of products of resolvents (or, more briefly,
resolvent chains
) as
local laws
. The proof of Proposition
3.4
is not the only place where the local laws are used, they appear repeatedly in the proof of Theorem
2.4
, in fact, in each of the technical ingredients listed in Sections
3.2.1
–
3.2.3
. Apart from Proposition
4.2
below, two additional new local laws are required. The first one is an averaged two-resolvent local law similar to Proposition
4.2
, which provides a weaker bound compared to Proposition
4.2
, but for general i.i.d. matrices
X
, i.e. without imposing the Gauss-divisibility assumption. We state this result in Proposition
4.5
and prove it by extending
[
20
, Theorem 3.4]
from the imaginary axis to the bulk regime. The second local law is in fact a full hierarchy of concentration bounds for products of several resolvents and deterministic matrices sandwiched in between. We state these new multi-resolvent local laws in Proposition
4.8
for general i.i.d. matrices
X
. Though multi-resolvent local laws may involve arbitrarily large (but independent of
N
) number of resolvents, they operate with bounds purely in terms of imaginary parts of spectral parameters and do not take into account the decorrelation decay in
|z_{1}-z_{2}|
. This makes the proof of Proposition
4.8
substantially easier and fairly standard.

We start this section with recalling the well-established single-resolvent local laws, which are frequently used throughout the proofs of Propositions
3.1
and
3.2
. Next, in Section
4.2
we state the new two-resolvent local laws in Propositions
4.2
and
4.5
. Finally, in Section
4.3
we generalize the concepts introduced in Section
4.2
to longer products of resolvents and state the new multi-resolvent local laws in Proposition
4.8
.

4.1.
Single-resolvent local law

Recall the definition of
M^{z}
from (
3.30
). In (
3.34
) we have already indicated that
G^{z}(w)
concentrates around
M^{z}(w)
in the averaged sense for
w
on the imaginary axis. The same concentration result holds also away from the imaginary axis:

Proposition 4.1

(Single-resolvent local law,
[
29
, Theorem 3.1]
)
.

Fix a (small)
\delta>0
and a (large)
L>0
. Let
X
be an
N\times N
either complex or real i.i.d. matrix satisfying Assumption
2.1
. Uniformly in
|z|\leq 1-\delta
and
w\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}
with
|w|\leq N^{L}
and
\eta:=|\Im w|\geq N^{-L}
, it holds that

(4.1)

\displaystyle\left|\langle(G^{z}(w)-M^{z}(w))A\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\prec\frac{\|A\|}{N\eta},

(4.2)

\displaystyle\left|\langle\bm{x},(G^{z}(w)-M^{z}(w))\bm{y}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\prec\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\eta}}+\frac{1}{N\eta}\right)\|\bm{x}\|\|\bm{y}\|,

for any deterministic matrix
A\in\mathbf{C}^{(2N)\times(2N)}
and vectors
\bm{x},\bm{y}\in\mathbf{C}^{2N}
.

The estimates (
4.1
) and (
4.2
) are known as the
averaged
and
isotropic
single-resolvent local laws, respectively. An important consequence of (
4.1
) is the bulk
eigenvalue rigidity
from
[
33
, Eq.(7.4)]
:

(4.3)

|\lambda_{i}^{z}-\gamma_{i}^{z}|\prec\frac{1}{N},\quad|i|\leq(1-\tau)N,

uniformly in
|z|\leq 1-\delta
, for any fixed
\tau>0
. Here the quantiles
\{\gamma_{i}^{z}\}_{|i|\leq N}
of
\rho^{z}
, from (
3.32
), are implicitly defined by

(4.4)

\frac{i}{N}=\int_{0}^{\gamma_{i}^{z}}\rho^{z}(E)\mathrm{d}E,\quad\gamma_{-i}^{z}:=-\gamma_{i}^{z},\quad 1\leq i\leq N.

In
[
33
, Eq.(7.4)]
, (
4.3
) is stated only for
|i|\leq cN
for some small constant
c>0
. However, (
4.3
) holds throughout the bulk regime
|i|\leq(1-\tau)N
as currently formulated. For the detailed derivation of (
4.3
) from Proposition
4.1
see e.g.
[
42
, Section 5]
.

Concluding the discussion of eigenvalue rigidity, we record the following estimate on quantiles which we will use occasionally. We have

(4.5)

|\gamma_{i}^{z}|\sim|i|/N,

uniformly in
|i|\leq N
and
|z|\leq 1-\delta
for any fixed
\delta>0
. This follows directly from (
4.4
) together with the fact that
\rho^{z}(0)>0
for
|z|<1
.

4.2.
Novel averaged two-resolvent local law

Now we introduce the set-up for our new averaged two-resolvent local law. For
z_{j}\in\mathbf{C}
and
w_{j}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}
,
j=1,2
, denote
M_{j}:=M^{z_{j}}(w_{j})
. Consider a resolvent chain
G_{1}B_{1}G_{2}
for a
(2N)\times(2N)
deterministic matrix
B_{1}
, which we customarily call an
observable
. Unlike one may naively think,
G_{1}B_{1}G_{2}
does not concentrate around
M_{1}B_{1}M_{2}
as
N
goes to infinity. Instead, the
deterministic approximation
of
G_{1}B_{1}G_{2}
is given by (see e.g.
[
33
, Eq.(5.7),(5.8)]
)

(4.6)

M_{12}^{B_{1}}=M_{12}^{B_{1}}(w_{1},w_{2})=\mathcal{M}[G_{1}B_{1}G_{2}]:=\mathcal{B}_{12}^{-1}[M_{1}B_{1}M_{2}],

where
\mathcal{B}_{12}
is the
two-body stability operator
defined by its action on
(2N)\times(2N)
deterministic matrices:

(4.7)

\left(\mathcal{B}_{12}(w_{1},w_{2})\right)[R]=\mathcal{B}_{12}[R]:=R-M_{1}\mathcal{S}[R]M_{2},\quad\forall R\in\mathbf{C}^{(2N)\times(2N)},

and
\mathcal{S}
is defined in (
3.28
). The dependence of
\mathcal{B}_{12}
and
M_{12}^{B_{1}}
on
z_{1},z_{2}
is indicated by the subscript. When
z_{1}=z_{2}
, we simply denote these objects by
\mathcal{B}_{11}
and
M_{11}^{B_{1}}
, respectively. If additionally
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
coincide, we call
\mathcal{B}_{11}(w_{1},w_{1})
the
one-body stability operator
. The invertibility of
\mathcal{B}_{12}
follows from
[
33
, Eq.(6.2)]
, so the lhs. of (
4.6
) is well-defined. We interchangeably use the three notations introduced in (
4.6
) for the deterministic approximation of
G_{1}B_{1}G_{2}
, leaning towards
\mathcal{M}[G_{1}B_{1}G_{2}]
in the calculations where longer resolvent chains are involved.

For simplicity, we restrict our attention to observables of size
(2N)\times(2N)
which are linear combinations of
E_{+},E_{-},F
and
F^{*}
, as this setting is sufficient for the proof Proposition
3.4
. The general case can be managed without any additional technical difficulties by projecting observables on
\mathrm{span}\{E_{\pm},F^{(*)}\}
. Our restriction is equivalent to saying that observables posses
2\times 2
block-constant structure. Since the matrices
M_{j}
,
j=1,2
, share this structure, and the operator
\mathcal{B}_{12}
preserves it, we can identify observables, deterministic approximations to resolvents and to the 2-resolvent chains (
4.6
) with
2\times 2
matrices. As it will become apparent later, this identification extends to deterministic approximations to longer resolvent chains as well. From now on, we will use this identification interchangeably, viewing the objects mentioned in this paragraph as
(2N)\times(2N)
matrices with
2\times 2
block-constant structure and as
2\times 2
matrices.

We use the following control parameter to quantify the concentration of
G_{1}B_{1}G_{2}
around
M_{12}^{B_{1}}
:

(4.8)

\widehat{\beta}_{12}=\widehat{\beta}_{12}(w_{1},w_{2}):=\min\left\{\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{B}_{12}\big(w_{1}^{(*)},w_{2}^{(*)}\big)\right)[R]\right\rVert\,:\,R\in\mathrm{span}\{E_{\pm},F^{(*)}\},\|R\|=1\right\},

where
w_{j}^{(*)}
indicates both choices
w_{j}
and
\overline{w}_{j}
. The dependence of
\widehat{\beta}_{12}
on
z_{1},z_{2}
is recorded in the subscript similarly to
\mathcal{B}_{12}
. An important property of
\widehat{\beta}_{12}
which immediately follows from its definition and (
4.6
) is that

(4.9)

\|M_{12}^{B_{1}}\|\leq\big(\widehat{\beta}_{12}\big)^{-1},\quad\forall B_{1}\in\mathrm{span}\{E_{\pm},F^{(*)}\}.

Moreover, this bound is optimal among the ones which are uniform in
B_{1}
. We further have that
\widehat{\beta}_{12}(w_{1},w_{2})
is insensitive to the complex conjugations of
w_{1},w_{2}
and that

(4.10)

0<\widehat{\beta}_{12}(w_{1},w_{2})\leq 1,\quad\widehat{\beta}_{12}(w_{1},w_{2})=\widehat{\beta}_{21}(w_{2},w_{1}),\qquad\forall\,z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbf{C},\,w_{1},w_{2}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}.

Here the positivity of
\widehat{\beta}_{12}
follows from invertibility of
\mathcal{B}_{12}
, and to verify the bound
\widehat{\beta}_{12}\leq 1
it suffices to take
R:=F
in the rhs. of (
4.8
) and use that
\mathcal{B}_{12}[F]=F
. The second part of (
4.10
) directly follows from the identity

(4.11)

\left(\left(\mathcal{B}_{12}(w_{1},w_{2})\right)[R]\right)^{*}=\left(\mathcal{B}_{21}(\overline{w}_{2},\overline{w}_{1})\right)[R^{*}],\quad\forall R\in\mathrm{span}\{E_{\pm},F^{(*)}\}.

Now we are ready to state our second main technical result, the optimal two-resolvent local law for matrices with a Gaussian component, postponing the proof to Section
S2
.

Proposition 4.2

(Optimal averaged two-resolvent local law in the bulk, Gauss-divisible case)
.

Let
X_{0}
be a complex
N\times N
i.i.d. matrix satisfying Assumption
2.1
and let
\widetilde{X}
be a complex Ginibre matrix independent of
X_{0}
. For a possibly
N
-dependent
\mathfrak{s}=\mathfrak{s}_{N}\in(0,1)
set
X:=\sqrt{1-\mathfrak{s}^{2}}X_{0}+\mathfrak{s}\widetilde{X}
. Fix
b\in[0,1]
and a (large)
L>0
, and assume that
\mathfrak{s}\geq L^{-1}N^{-b}
.

For spectral parameters
w_{1},w_{2}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}
, denote
\eta_{l}:=|\Im w_{l}|
and
\eta_{*}:=\eta_{1}\wedge\eta_{2}\wedge 1
. Then for any fixed
\delta,\epsilon,\kappa>0
we have

(4.12)

\left|\left\langle\left(G^{z_{1}}(w_{1})B_{1}G^{z_{2}}(w_{2})-M_{12}^{B_{1}}(w_{1},w_{2})\right)B_{2}\right\rangle\right|\prec\frac{1}{N\eta_{*}\left(\widehat{\beta}_{12}(w_{1},w_{2})\wedge N^{-b}+\eta_{*}\right)},

uniformly in
B_{1},B_{2}\in\mathrm{span}\{E_{\pm},F^{(*)}\}
,
|z_{l}|\leq 1-\delta
,
\Re w_{l}\in\mathbf{B}_{\kappa}^{z_{l}}
and
N^{-1+\epsilon}\leq\eta_{l}\leq N^{100}
for
l=1,2
.

We now discuss the result in (
4.12
), relate it to previous results, and give a sketch of its proof.

Gauss divisibility assumption.
We start the discussion of Proposition
4.2
by commenting on the Gauss divisibility assumption. First, we note that the word
optimal
in the title of Proposition
4.2
refers only to the case
b=0
, i.e. when
X
contains a Gaussian component of order one. In this case (
4.12
) simplifies to

(4.13)

\left|\left\langle\left(G^{z_{1}}(w_{1})B_{1}G^{z_{2}}(w_{2})-M_{12}^{B_{1}}(w_{1},w_{2})\right)B_{2}\right\rangle\right|\prec\frac{1}{N\eta_{*}\widehat{\beta}_{12}(w_{1},w_{2})}.

Here we omitted
\eta_{*}
in the second factor in the rhs. of (
4.12
); this does not change the bound, since
\widehat{\beta}_{12}\gtrsim\eta_{*}
by
[
29
, Lemma 6.1]
. The bound (
4.13
) is optimal in the sense that, for certain observables
B_{1},B_{2}
, the variance of the lhs. of (
4.13
) is of the same order as the square of the rhs. of (
4.13
). At the opposite extreme
b=1
, (
4.12
) yields an upper bound of order
(N\eta_{*}^{2})^{-1}
, which is already known from
[
20
, Theorem 3.4]
for general i.i.d. matrices
X
in the regime when
w_{1},w_{2}
are on the imaginary axis. For intermediate values
b\in(0,1)
, (
4.12
) interpolates between (
4.13
) and this
(N\eta_{*}^{2})^{-1}
bound. In fact, the magnitude of fluctuation of the lhs. of (
4.12
) is not sensitive to the size of Gaussian component, and the apparent deterioration of (
4.12
) as
b
increases from 0 to 1 is purely technical. In principle, one could remove the Gaussian component from
X
without weakening (
4.13
), and prove that (
4.13
) holds for general i.i.d. matrices
X
. This could be achieved by a routine, though technically involved, GFT argument. Since (
4.12
) is already sufficient for the proof of Proposition
3.4
, we do not pursue these additional refinements.

Relation to the upper bound on
\|M_{12}^{B}\|
.
We further focus on the case
b=0
in Proposition
4.2
, which is our main contribution. An important feature of (
4.13
) is that this bound on the fluctuation of
\langle G_{1}B_{1}G_{2}B_{2}\rangle
is stronger than the bound on its deterministic approximation
\langle M_{12}^{B_{1}}B_{2}\rangle
(
4.9
) by a factor of
(N\eta_{*})^{-1}\ll 1
. Thus, (
4.12
) should be viewed as a concentration bound. The emergence of the
(N\eta_{*})^{-1}
improvement in multi-resolvent averaged local laws compared to the size estimate is well-expected and was first observed in the Hermitian setting for Wigner matrices in
[
32
]
.

Relation to the previous results.
Several weaker versions of (
4.13
) appeared earlier in the literature, see in chronological order
[
33
, Theorem 5.2]
,
[
35
, Theorem 3.3]
and
[
20
, Theorem 3.4]
. We remark that none of these works assumes Gauss divisibility of
X
. In
[
33
]
the bound in the averaged two-resolvent local law is worse than (
4.13
) by a typically large factor
\eta_{*}^{-11/12}
, while in
[
35
]

\widehat{\beta}_{12}
is replaced by a weaker control parameter and is not gained in the optimal power. Finally, for
w_{1},w_{2}
on the imaginary axis,
[
20
, Theorem 3.4]
asserts that

(4.14)

\left|\left\langle\left(G^{z_{1}}(w_{1})B_{1}G^{z_{2}}(w_{2})-M_{12}^{B_{1}}(w_{1},w_{2})\right)B_{2}\right\rangle\right|\prec\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\eta_{*}}\left(|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}+(\eta_{1}+\eta_{2})\wedge 1\right)}\wedge\frac{1}{N\eta_{*}^{2}}.

Besides the fact that the local law is needed for the proof of Proposition
3.4
for
w_{1},w_{2}
not only on the imaginary axis, but also in a neighborhood of it (as already mentoned above Proposition
4.2
), there is an additional loss in (
4.14
) compared to (
4.13
). Although the
\widehat{\beta}_{12}
factor appears in the rhs. of (
4.14
), as by
[
20
, Lemma 3.3]
it is of the same order as
|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}+(\eta_{1}+\eta_{2})\wedge 1
for spectral parameters on the imaginary axis, there is still a substantial loss of a large factor
\sqrt{N\eta_{*}}
in the denominator. In particular, even if (
4.14
) was extended to the bulk regime, the replacement of Proposition
4.2
by (
4.14
) in the proof of Proposition
3.4
would considerably weaken the bound on the error term in (
3.35
).

Proof strategy.
The proof of Proposition
4.2
relies on the
method of characteristics
, which consists in studying the evolution of the resolvent along a stochastic flow which enables us to transfer information from spectral parameters with large imaginary part to those with much smaller imaginary part. In
[
83
]
, it was observed that the resolvent of a Wigner matrix evolving along the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck flow (see (
4.15
) below) solves complex Burgers-like equations, and it can thus be analyzed along the characteristics (which have the convenient property described above). More recently this idea was used to prove universality at the edge of certain deformed Hermitian matrices
[
67
]
, and, closer to our setting, to prove local laws for single resolvents
[
2
,
59
,
96
]
. Only more recently it was shown that this
method of characteristics
is powerful to also consider products of resolvents
[
15
,
35
]
. We refer the interested reader to
[
37
, Section 1.4]
for a detailed description of the history and related results.

Specifically, consider the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck flow

(4.15)

\mathrm{d}X_{t}=-\frac{1}{2}X_{t}\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\mathrm{d}B_{t}}{\sqrt{N}},

where
B_{t}
is an
N\times N
matrix composed of
N^{2}
independent standard complex-valued Brownian motions, and choose the initial condition in such a way that
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}X_{T}
for some
T\sim N^{-b}
. We complement (
4.15
) by the
characteristic flow

(4.16)

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}z_{j,t}=-\frac{1}{2}z_{j,t},\qquad\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}w_{j,t}=-\frac{1}{2}w_{j,t}-\langle M^{z_{j,t}}(w_{j,t})\rangle,\quad t\in[0,T],\,j=1,2,

with the final condition
z_{j,T}:=z_{j}
,
w_{j,T}:=w_{j}
(instead of the customary initial condition). This flow is well-defined by
[
35
, Lemma 5.2]
. Define
G_{j,t}:=G^{z_{j,t}}(w_{j,t})
and set

(4.17)

\mathcal{Y}_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},t}:=\left\langle\left(G_{1,t}E_{\sigma_{1}}G_{2,t}-M_{12,t}^{E_{\sigma_{1}}}\right)E_{\sigma_{2}}\right\rangle,\quad\mathcal{Y}_{t}:=\left(\mathcal{Y}_{+,+,t},\mathcal{Y}_{+,-,t},\mathcal{Y}_{-,+,t},\mathcal{Y}_{-,-,t}\right)^{\mathfrak{t}}\in\mathbf{C}^{4},

where
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\{\pm\}
and
M_{12,t}^{B}
is the time-dependent deterministic approximation. An elementary application of Itô calculus gives

(4.18)

\mathrm{d}\mathcal{Y}_{[2],t}=\left(I+\mathcal{A}_{[2],t}\right)\mathcal{Y}_{[2],t}\mathrm{d}t+\mathcal{F}_{[2],t}\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}\mathcal{E}_{[2],t},

for a similar calculation see e.g.
[
37
, Eq.(5.42)–(5.47)]
and
[
20
, Eq.(4.67)–(4.69)]
. In (
4.18
) the
generator

\mathcal{A}_{[2],t}\in\mathbf{C}^{4\times 4}
is an explicit
4\times 4
matrix constructed from the deterministic approximations to two-resolvent chains,
\mathcal{F}_{[2],t}
is a
forcing term
and
\mathcal{E}_{[2],t}
is a
martingale term
. The exact form of
\mathcal{A}_{[2],t}
is given in Appendix
A.2
later (see also Supplementary Eq. (
S2.62
)).

We will solve (
4.18
) by the Duhamel formula, so need to understand the propagator of this ODE. To this end, we prove the following bound on the propagator in Appendix
A.2
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Denote
f_{[2],r}:=\left[\max\mathrm{spec}(\Re\mathcal{A}_{[2],r})\right]_{+}
for
r\in[0,T]
, where
[\cdot]_{+}
stands for the positive part of a real number. Then for any
t\in[0,T]
we have

(4.19)

\exp\left\{\int_{s}^{t}f_{[2],r}\mathrm{d}r\right\}\lesssim\left(\frac{\widehat{\beta}_{12,s}}{\widehat{\beta}_{12,t}}\right)^{2},

with
\widehat{\beta}_{12,t}
equal to
\widehat{\beta}_{12}
(see (
4.8
) for its definition) evaluated at
z_{j,t}
and
w_{j,t}
,
j=1,2
.

This lemma is the analogue of Lemma 5.7 and Eq. (5.58) from
[
37
]
, where similar estimates on the propagator were derived in the regime when
z_{1},z_{2}
are close to the non-Hermitian spectral edge and
w_{1},w_{2}
are purely imaginary. A major simplification in
[
37
]
is that the off-diagonal entries of
\mathcal{A}_{[2],t}
are purely imaginary when
w_{1},w_{2}
are on the imaginary axis, so the real part of the generator is diagonal. In our setting this simplification is no longer available, so we separately treat the off-diagonal entries of
\mathcal{A}_{[2],t}
.

The remaining details in the proof of Proposition
4.2
are standard, and thus we present them in Supplementary Section
S2
.

Discussion of the control parameter
\widehat{\beta}_{12}
.
Although (
4.8
) explicitly defines
\widehat{\beta}_{12}
, it does not make transparent the dependence of
\widehat{\beta}_{12}
on
z_{1},z_{2}
and
w_{1},w_{2}
. This more detailed information is in fact not needed for the proof of Proposition
4.2
, where we mostly rely on the bound (
4.9
). However, effective estimates on
\widehat{\beta}_{12}
in terms of
z_{1},z_{2}
and
w_{1},w_{2}
become essential when applying the two-resolvent local law (
4.12
) in the proof of Proposition
3.4
, as well as in the potential future applications. The following statement fully covers this question by providing precise asymptotics for
\widehat{\beta}_{12}
expressed directly in these simpler terms.

Proposition 4.4
.

Fix (small)
\delta,\kappa>0
. Uniformly in Hermitization parameters
z_{1},z_{2}\in(1-\delta)\mathbf{D}
and spectral parameters
w_{1},w_{2}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}
such that
E_{j}:=\Re w_{j}\in\mathbf{B}_{\kappa}^{z_{j}}
and
\eta_{j}:=|\Im w_{j}|\in(0,1]
for
j=1,2
, it holds that

(4.20)

\widehat{\beta}_{12}(w_{1},w_{2})\sim\gamma(z_{1},z_{2},w_{1},w_{2}),

where

\gamma
is defined in (
3.36
). Moreover, uniformly in
|z_{j}|\lesssim 1
and
w_{j}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}
,
j=1,2
, we have

(4.21)

\widehat{\beta}_{12}\gtrsim\eta_{*},\quad\text{where}\quad\eta_{*}:=\eta_{1}\wedge\eta_{2}\wedge 1.

Our main contribution in Proposition
4.4
is the identification of the term linear in
z_{1}-z_{2}
and
w_{1}-w_{2}
in
\gamma
, which is captured by
\mathrm{LT}
defined in (
3.36
). The remaining terms in
\gamma
have already appeared as a lower bound on a quantity closely related to
\widehat{\beta}_{12}
in
[
29
, Lemma 6.1]
, together with the estimate (
4.21
). We remark that the linear term is analogous to the quantity introduced under the same name in
[
23
, Eq.(2.17)]
, where it played a crucial role in
[
23
, Proposition 3.1]
in improving the lower bound on the two-body stability operator from quadratic to linear dependence on the distance between spectral parameters in the Hermitian setting. For further details and the proof of Proposition
4.4
see Appendix
A.1
.

Since Proposition
3.4
concerns general i.i.d. matrices, while Proposition
4.2
is stated for i.i.d. matrices with a Gaussian component, a GFT argument is still required in the proof of Proposition
3.4
. However, a Gaussian component is removed from
X
directly at the level of covariance (
direct
GFT), rather than in the local law estimate, as it is technically simpler. There is one more instance in the proof of Proposition
3.2
, where an averaged two-resolvent local law is needed (though with less precision than in (
4.13
)), and where we prefer to perform GFT in the local law instead of working out a direct GFT, since the former one is more standard. Further details will be given later in Proposition
4.6
. In such a way, we establish a (suboptimal) bound in the two-resolvent averaged local law for general i.i.d. random matrices. We accomplish this in the following proposition by extending (
4.14
) to the entire bulk regime.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
X
be a complex
N\times N
i.i.d. matrix satisfying Assumption
2.1
. For any fixed
\delta,\epsilon,\kappa>0
it holds that

(4.22)

\left|\left\langle\left(G^{z_{1}}(w_{1})B_{1}G^{z_{2}}(w_{2})-M_{12}^{B_{1}}(w_{1},w_{2})\right)B_{2}\right\rangle\right|\prec\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\eta_{*}}\widehat{\beta}_{12}}

uniformly in
B_{1},B_{2}\in\mathrm{span}\{E_{\pm},F^{(*)}\}
,
|z_{l}|\leq 1-\delta
,
\Re w_{l}\in\mathbf{B}_{\kappa}^{z_{l}}
and
N^{-1+\epsilon}\leq\eta_{l}\leq N^{100}
for
l=1,2
.

The proof of Proposition
4.5
is presented in Supplementary Section
S2.5
. It is obtained by a minor modification of the proof of
[
20
, Theorem 3.4]
using the new propagator bound from Lemma
4.3
developed for the proof of Proposition
4.2
.

As a standard corollary of Proposition
4.5
, we get the following overlap bound for the left and right singular vectors of
X-z_{1}
and
X-z_{2}
associated to the bulk eigenvalues
9
9

9

Here we recall the relation between singular vectors of
X-z
and eigenvectors of
H^{z}
discussed in (
3.23
)–(
3.24
)
of
H^{z_{1}}
,
H^{z_{2}}
. We cover the entire bulk regime, which is possible since we prove Proposition
4.5
throughout the bulk. Meanwhile (
4.22
) restricted to the spectral parameters on the imaginary axis would imply an effective bound only on the singular vector overlaps associated to the eigenvalues of
H^{z_{1}},H^{z_{2}}
in the interval
[-N^{-1+\omega},N^{-1+\omega}]
for a small
\omega>0
.

Proposition 4.6

(Singular vector overlap)
.

Let
X
be a complex
N\times N
i.i.d. matrix satisfying Assumption
2.1
. Fix (small)
\delta,\tau>0
. Recall the notations introduced in (
3.30
), (
3.24
) and (
4.4
). It holds that

(4.23)

\left|\langle\bm{u}_{i}^{z_{1}},\bm{u}_{j}^{z_{2}}\rangle\right|^{2}+\left|\langle\bm{v}_{i}^{z_{1}},\bm{v}_{j}^{z_{2}}\rangle\right|^{2}\leq\frac{N^{\xi}}{N}\frac{1}{|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}+N^{-1}|i-j|}\wedge 1,

with very high probability for any fixed
\xi>0
, uniformly in
i,j\in[(1-\tau)N]
and
|z_{1}|,|z_{2}|\leq 1-\delta
.

The proof of Proposition
4.6
is presented in Appendix
A.3
. It relies on Proposition
4.5
along with the spectral decomposition, and on a precise analysis of the linear term in the second line of (
3.36
).

4.3.
Multi-resolvent local laws for longer chains

In this section we state local laws (both in the averaged and isotropic sense) for resolvent chains of the form

(4.24)

G_{1}B_{1}G_{2}\cdots B_{k-1}G_{k},\quad\text{with}\quad G_{j}:=G^{z_{j}}(w_{j}),\,z_{j}\in\mathbf{C},\,w_{j}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R},\,j\in[k],

for any
k\in\mathbf{N}
and observables
B_{1},\ldots,B_{k-1}
with the
2\times 2
block-constant structure. There are two instances where these resolvent chains appear in the proof of Theorem
2.4
. First, they arise as building blocks of covariances from the hierarchy obtained from the iterative underline expansions similar to (
3.39
). Those ones, however, contain only resolvents of the same type
10
10

10

Since Proposition
3.4
concerns two resolvents
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
, one may think that both of them may appear in the same product, which has to be further expanded similarly to (
3.39
). However, this does not happen and only the error terms contain the products of
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
under the same trace. These terms are reduced to two-resolvent quantities (see Footnote
8
) and then estimated by Proposition
4.2
without involving local laws for products of more than two resolvents.
, i.e.
G_{j}=G_{1}
for all
j\in[k]
. Second, local laws for (
4.24
) with
k\leq 4
and at most two types of resolvents are used as an input for the proof of Proposition
4.2
for
b>0
. In particular, we could restrict attention to the case when there are at most two distinct Hermitization parameters in (
4.24
), but this would not yield any simplifications. However, importantly, in both applications of the concentration bounds for (
4.24
) it is unnecessary to include the refined control parameter
\widehat{\beta}_{12}
in the local law estimates. Instead, we carry out all estimates purely in terms of

(4.25)

\eta_{*}:=\eta_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\eta_{k}\wedge 1,\quad\text{where}\quad\eta_{j}:=|\Im w_{j}|,\,j\in[k].

This is a major simplification compared to the set-up of Proposition
4.2
, even though the number of resolvents increased.

To state local laws for resolvent chains of the form (
4.24
), we first introduce the notion of the deterministic approximation of (
4.24
). As in (
4.6
), we use interchangeably the notations

M_{[k]}^{B_{1},\ldots,B_{k}},\quad M_{[k]}^{B_{1},\ldots,B_{k}}(w_{1},\ldots,w_{k})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{M}\left[G_{1}B_{1}\cdots B_{k-1}G_{k}\right]

for the deterministic approximation of
G_{1}B_{1}\cdots B_{k-1}G_{k}
, and by induction on
k
define

(4.26)

M_{[k]}^{B_{1},\ldots,B_{k-1}}:=\mathcal{B}_{1k}^{-1}\bigg[M_{1}B_{1}M_{[2,k]}^{B_{2},\ldots,B_{k-1}}+\sum_{j=2}^{k-1}M_{1}\mathcal{S}\left[M_{[1,j]}^{B_{1},\ldots,B_{j-1}}\right]M_{[j,k]}^{B_{j},\ldots,B_{k-1}}\bigg],

using (
4.6
) as the starting point. The size of the lhs. of (
4.26
) is governed by the following bound.

Proposition 4.7
.

Fix
\delta,\kappa>0
and
k\in\mathbf{N}
. Recall the definition of
\eta_{*}
from (
4.25
). It holds that

(4.27)

\left\lVert M_{[k]}^{B_{1},\ldots,B_{k-1}}\right\rVert\lesssim\frac{1}{\eta_{*}^{k-1}},

uniformly in Hermitization parameters
z_{l}\in(1-\delta)\mathbf{D}
, spectral parameters
w_{j}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}
with
\Re w_{l}\in\mathbf{B}_{\kappa}^{z}
,
l\in[k]
, and observables
B_{l}\in\mathrm{span}\{E_{\pm},F^{(*)}\}
,
l\in[k-1]
.

Now we present the multi-resolvent local laws for general i.i.d. matrices
X
in the bulk regime.

Proposition 4.8

(Multi-resolvent local laws in the bulk regime)
.

Let
X
be a complex
N\times N
i.i.d. matrix satisfying Assumption
2.1
. Fix
\delta,\epsilon,\kappa>0
. Then we have the
averaged
local law

(4.28)

\left|\left\langle\left(G^{z_{1}}(w_{1})B_{1}\cdots G^{z_{k}}(w_{k})-M_{[k]}^{B_{1},\ldots,B_{k-1}}(w_{1},\ldots,w_{k})\right)B_{k}\right\rangle\right|\prec\frac{1}{N\eta_{*}^{k}},

and the
isotropic
local law

(4.29)

\left|\left\langle\bm{x},\left(G^{z_{1}}(w_{1})B_{1}\cdots G^{z_{k}}(w_{k})-M_{[k]}^{B_{1},\ldots,B_{k-1}}(w_{1},\ldots,w_{k})\right)\bm{y}\right\rangle\right|\prec\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\eta_{*}}\eta_{*}^{k-1}},

uniformly in the Hermitization parameters
z_{l}\in(1-\delta)\mathbf{D}
, spectral parameters
w_{l}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}
with
\eta_{l}:=|\Im w_{l}|\in[N^{-1+\epsilon},N^{100}]
and
\Re w_{l}\in\mathbf{B}_{\kappa}^{z}
,
l\in[k]
, observables
B_{l}\in\mathrm{span}\{E_{\pm},F^{(*)}\}
for
l\in[k]
, and deterministic vectors
\bm{x},\bm{y}\in\mathbf{C}^{2N}
with
\|\bm{x}\|=\|\bm{y}\|=1
.

The proofs of Propositions
4.7
and
4.8
are presented in Section
S2.3
. We remark that similarly to (
4.12
), the bound (
4.28
) improves upon the deterministic size bound (
4.27
) by the small factor
(N\eta_{*})^{-1}
. Meanwhile, the bound in the isotropic local law (
4.29
) yields an improvement upon (
4.27
) by a factor of
(N\eta_{*})^{-1/2}
. These gains are optimal and were first observed in the context of Wigner matrices in
[
32
, Theorem 2.5]
.

The special case of Proposition
4.8
when all Hermitization parameters coincide and the spectral parameters lie in a narrow cone with vertex at origin is proven in
[
81
, Theorem 1.3]
, however our proof does not rely on this work.

5.
Calculations with the Girko’s formula

In this section we prove Propositions
3.1
and
3.2
. First, in Section
5.1
we establish the bounds on the regularization terms
J_{T},I_{0}^{\eta_{L}}
and on the term
I_{\eta_{L}}^{\eta_{0}}
in (
3.22
). This is done for a general smooth test function to fit the set-up both of Propositions
3.1
and
3.2
, which are subsequently proven in Sections
5.2
and
5.3
, respectively.

Throughout the entire Section
5
we use
\xi>0
to denote an exponent which can be taken arbitrarily small, the exact value of
\xi
may change from line to line.

5.1.
Preliminary reductions in the Girko’s formula

We present a standard bound on
J_{T}
and
I_{0}^{\eta_{L}}
, implying that these terms are negligible in the rhs. of (
3.22
). Additionally we prove an upper bound on the moments of
I_{\eta_{L}}^{\eta_{c}}
relying on the tail bound estimate for
\lambda_{1}^{z}
from Proposition
3.5
. Though for the proof of Propositions
3.1
and
3.2
one needs only the bound on the first two moments of
J_{T}
,
I_{0}^{\eta_{L}}
and
I_{\eta_{L}}^{\eta_{0}}
, we consider the general
p
-th moment of these random variables, since this does not cause any additional difficulties.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
X
be a complex
N\times N
i.i.d. matrix satisfying Assumption
2.1
. Fix
\delta>0
and let
g_{N}\in C^{2}(\mathbf{C})
be a complex-valued function with
{\rm{supp}}(g_{N})\subset(1-\delta)\mathbf{D}
. Suppose that there exists
D>0
such that
\|g_{N}\|_{\infty}\!+\|\Delta g_{N}\|_{1}\lesssim N^{D}
for all
N\in\mathbf{N}
. Then for any fixed
p\in\mathbf{N}
and
K>0
there exist
L,D^{\prime}>0
such that

(5.1)

\mathbf{E}|J_{T}(g_{N})|^{p}+\mathbf{E}|I_{0}^{\eta_{L}}(g_{N})|^{p}\lesssim N^{-K},

where
\eta_{L}=N^{-L}
and
T=N^{D^{\prime}}
. We further have

(5.2)

\mathbf{E}\left|I_{\eta_{L}}^{\eta_{0}}(g_{N})\right|^{p}\lesssim\left((N\eta_{0})^{2}+N^{-2\nu_{1}}\right)\|\Delta g_{N}\|_{1}^{p}N^{\xi}

for any
\eta_{0}\in(\eta_{L},N^{-1})
, where
\nu_{1}:=1/10
is given by Proposition
3.5
. The implicit constants in (
5.1
) and (
5.2
) depend only on
\delta
,
p
,
K
and the model parameters in Assumption
2.1
.

Proof of Lemma
5.1
.

We start with the proof of (
5.1
) and closely follow the proof of similar estimates in
[
27
, Lemma 3]
. From
[
6
, Eq.(5.27)–(5.28)]
we have that

(5.3)

|J_{T}(g_{N})|=\left|\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbf{C}}\Delta g_{N}(z)\log|\det(H^{z}-\mathrm{i}T)|\mathrm{d}^{2}z\right|\prec\frac{N\lVert\Delta g_{N}\rVert_{L^{1}}}{T^{2}}

with very high probability. By choosing
T:=N^{D^{\prime}}
for a sufficiently large fixed
D^{\prime}>0
, we make the rhs. of (
5.3
) smaller than
N^{-K}
. On the small probability event where (
5.3
) does not hold, we use the trivial bound

(5.4)

|J_{T}(g_{N})|\lesssim N\lVert\Delta g_{N}\rVert_{L^{1}}\log T,

Together with (
5.3
) this gives
\mathbf{E}|J_{T}(g_{N})|^{p}\lesssim N^{-K}
.

To prove the upper bound on
\mathbf{E}\left|I_{0}^{\eta_{L}}(g_{N})\right|^{p}
from (
5.1
), we first estimate
\langle\Im G^{z}(\mathrm{i}\eta)\rangle\leq 1/\eta
, use (
5.4
) and rearrange (
3.20
) to express
I_{0}^{\eta_{L}}(g_{N})
. We get the following bound on
I_{0}^{\eta_{L}}(g_{N})
which holds on the entire probability space:

|I_{0}^{\eta_{L}}(g_{N})|\!\lesssim\!\left|\mathcal{L}_{N}(g_{N})\right|+N\|\Delta g_{N}\|_{1}\log N+N\|\Delta g_{N}\|_{1}\!\!\int_{N^{-L}}^{T}\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{\eta}\!\lesssim\!N\|g_{N}\|_{\infty}+N\|\Delta g_{N}\|_{1}\log N\lesssim N^{D+2}.

Therefore, it is sufficient to prove that
\mathbf{E}\left|I_{0}^{\eta_{L}}(g_{N})\right|\lesssim N^{-K^{\prime}}
for some fixed
K^{\prime}>K+(p-1)(D+2)
. We have

(5.5)

\mathbf{E}\left|I_{0}^{\eta_{L}}(g_{N})\right|=\mathbf{E}\int_{\mathbf{C}}|\Delta g_{N}(z)|\int_{0}^{\eta_{L}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\eta}{(\lambda_{i}^{z})^{2}+\eta^{2}}\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}^{2}z\lesssim N\|g_{N}\|_{1}\sup_{|z|\leq 1-\delta}\mathbf{E}\log\left(1+(N^{L}\lambda^{z}_{1})^{-2}\right).

By
[
12
, Lemma 4.12]
(see also
[
6
, Proposition 5.7]
), under the regularity assumption (
2.2
) it holds that

(5.6)

\mathbf{P}[\lambda_{1}^{z}<N^{-1}u]\leq C_{\mathfrak{a}}u^{2\mathfrak{a}/(1+\mathfrak{a})}N^{\mathfrak{b}+1},

uniformly in
|z|\leq 1
and
u>0
, for some constant
C_{\mathfrak{a}}>0
. We proceed by a standard dyadic argument and decompose the probability space into the events
2^{k}N^{-L}<\lambda_{1}^{z}\leq 2^{k+1}N^{-L}
,
k\in\mathbf{Z}
, whose probabilities are then estimated by (
5.6
). We thus conclude that one can choose
L>0
sufficiently large in the definition of
\eta_{L}=N^{-L}
, so that the rhs. of (
5.5
) is smaller than
N^{-K^{\prime}}
. This finishes the proof of (
5.1
).

Now we prove (
5.2
). Denote
I:=I_{\eta_{L}}^{\eta_{0}}(g_{N})
and fix a (small)
\xi_{0}>0
. We use (
3.25
) to go from
\langle G^{z}(\mathrm{i}\eta)\rangle
to the sum over eigenvalues in
I
and split this summation into two regimes:

(5.7)

I=I_{1}+I_{2}:=-\frac{N}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbf{C}}\Delta g_{N}(z)\mathrm{d}^{2}z\int_{\eta_{L}}^{\eta_{0}}\frac{1}{N}\bigg(\sum_{i\leq N^{\xi_{0}}}+\sum_{i>N^{\xi_{0}}}\bigg)\frac{\eta}{(\lambda_{i}^{z})^{2}+\eta^{2}}\mathrm{d}\eta.

In (
5.7
), as well as everywhere further in this proof, the summation index
i
takes values only from the set
[1,N]
even if this is not mentioned explicitly. To estimate
\mathbf{E}|I_{1}|^{p}
, we observe that for any random variable
h^{z}
and any
p\in\mathbf{N}
it holds that

(5.8)

\begin{split}\mathbf{E}\left|\int_{\mathbf{C}}\Delta g_{N}(z)h^{z}\mathrm{d}^{2}z\right|^{p}&\leq\int_{\mathbf{C}^{p}}\prod_{j=1}^{p}|\Delta g_{N}(z_{j})|\mathbf{E}\Bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{p}|h^{z_{j}}|\Bigg]\prod_{j=1}^{p}\mathrm{d}^{2}z_{j}\\
&\leq\sum_{l=1}^{p}\int_{\mathbf{C}^{p}}\prod_{j=1}^{p}|\Delta g_{N}(z_{j})|\mathbf{E}\left[|h^{z_{l}}|^{p}\right]\prod_{j=1}^{p}\mathrm{d}^{2}z_{j}\leq p\|\Delta g_{N}\|_{1}^{p}\sup_{|z|\leq 1-\delta}\mathbf{E}|h^{z}|^{p},\end{split}

where in the last bound we used that
\mathrm{supp}(\Delta g_{N})\subset(1-\delta)\mathbf{D}
. We use (
5.8
) for

(5.9)

h^{z}_{1}:=\int_{\eta_{L}}^{\eta_{0}}\sum_{i\leq N^{\xi_{0}}}\frac{\eta}{(\lambda_{i}^{z})^{2}+\eta^{2}}\mathrm{d}\eta.

Performing integration in
\eta
in the rhs. of (
5.9
) we get

(5.10)

h^{z}_{1}\leq\sum_{i\leq N^{\xi_{0}}}\log\left(1+\frac{(N\eta_{0})^{2}}{(N\lambda_{i}^{z})^{2}+(N\eta_{L})^{2}}\right)\leq N^{\xi_{0}}\log\left(1+\frac{(N\eta_{0})^{2}}{(N\lambda_{1}^{z})^{2}+(N\eta_{L})^{2}}\right).

To estimate the
p
-th moment of the logarithm in the rhs. of (
5.10
) we decompose the probability space into the events
P_{0}:=\{\lambda_{1}^{z}\leq N\eta_{L}\}
and
P_{k}:=\{2^{k-1}N\eta_{L}<\lambda_{1}^{z}\leq 2^{k}N\eta_{L}\}
,
k\in\mathbf{N}
. Since
\eta_{L}=N^{-L}
, only
k\lesssim\log N
are involved in this decomposition. We have from Proposition
3.5
that

(5.11)

\mathbf{P}\left[P_{k}\right]\lesssim N^{\xi}\left((2^{k}N\eta_{L})^{2}+N^{-2\nu_{1}}\right),\quad\forall k\geq 0,

uniformly in
|z|\leq 1-\delta
. An elementary calculation based on (
5.11
) shows that

(5.12)

\mathbf{E}\left|\log\left(1+\frac{(N\eta_{0})^{2}}{(N\lambda_{1}^{z})^{2}+(N\eta_{L})^{2}}\right)\right|^{p}\!\lesssim\!\!\sum_{0\leq k\lesssim\log N}\!\!\mathbf{P}[P_{k}]\left|\log\left(1+\frac{(N\eta_{0})^{2}}{(2^{k}N\eta_{L})^{2}}\right)\right|^{p}\!\!\lesssim N^{\xi}\left((N\eta_{0})^{2}+N^{-2\nu_{1}}\right)\!.

Together with (
5.8
) applied to
h^{z}_{1}
and (
5.10
) this implies

(5.13)

\mathbf{E}|I_{1}|^{p}\lesssim N^{p\xi_{0}+\xi}((N\eta_{0})^{2}+N^{-2\nu_{1}})\lVert\Delta g_{N}\rVert_{1}^{p}.

Next we estimate
\mathbf{E}|I_{2}|^{p}
. Similarly to (
5.9
) we take

(5.14)

h^{z}_{2}:=\int_{\eta_{L}}^{\eta_{0}}\sum_{i>N^{\xi_{0}}}\frac{\eta}{(\lambda_{i}^{z})^{2}+\eta^{2}}\mathrm{d}\eta.

However, instead of immediately performing integration in the rhs. of (
5.14
), we first bound the sum over
i
for any
\eta>0
:

(5.15)

\sum_{|i|\geq N^{\xi_{0}}}\frac{\eta}{(\lambda_{i}^{z})^{2}+\eta^{2}}\lesssim\sum_{|i|\geq N^{\xi_{0}}}\frac{\eta N^{\xi}}{(\lambda_{i}^{z})^{2}+(N^{-1+\xi_{0}})^{2}}\leq\eta N^{2+\xi-\xi_{0}}\langle\Im G^{z}(\mathrm{i}N^{-1+\xi_{0}})\rangle\lesssim\eta N^{2+\xi-\xi_{0}},

with very high probability uniformly in
|z|\leq 1-\delta
. Here we used the eigenvalue rigidity
[
33
, Eq.(7.4)]
in the first step and the averaged single-resolvent local law
[
6
, Eq.(5.4)]
in the last step to bound
|\langle\Im G^{z}\rangle|\lesssim 1
. Together with the trivial bound
h^{z}_{2}\lesssim N\log N
which holds with probability 1, (
5.15
) implies

(5.16)

\mathbf{E}|h_{2}^{z}|^{p}\lesssim N^{p(\xi-\xi_{0})}\left|N^{2}\int_{\eta_{L}}^{\eta_{0}}\eta\mathrm{d}\eta\right|^{p}\lesssim N^{p(\xi-\xi_{0})}(N\eta_{0})^{2p}.

Using (
5.16
) along with (
5.8
) applied to
h^{z}_{2}
we get

(5.17)

\mathbf{E}|I_{2}|^{p}\lesssim N^{p(\xi-\xi_{0})}(N\eta_{0})^{2p}\|\Delta g_{N}\|_{1}^{p}.

Finally, we combine (
5.13
) with (
5.17
) and choose
\xi_{0}>0
to be sufficiently small. This finishes the proof of Lemma
5.1
.
∎

5.2.
Calculation of the expectation: proof of Proposition
3.1

In this section we denote
\omega_{N}:=\omega_{a,N}^{(z_{0})}
and omit the arguments from the notations introduced in (
3.21
), which are meant to be equal to
\omega_{N}
throughout the proof. Since
|z_{0}|<1-\delta
, it holds that
{\rm supp}(\omega_{N})\subset(1-\delta/2)\mathbf{D}
for sufficiently large
N
. From the definition of
\omega_{N}
given in (
3.3
) we have that

(5.18)

\|\omega_{N}\|_{\infty}\lesssim N^{2a}\quad\text{and}\quad\|\Delta\omega_{N}\|_{1}\lesssim N^{2a}.

Let
\delta_{0}\in(0,1/2+\nu_{0}-a)
and
\delta_{c}>0
be positive exponents which will be taken sufficiently small at the end. We take

(5.19)

\eta_{L}:=N^{-L},\quad\eta_{0}:=N^{-1/2-a-\delta_{0}},\quad\eta_{c}:=N^{-1+\delta_{c}}\quad\text{and}\quad T:=N^{D^{\prime}}

for sufficiently large
L,D^{\prime}>0
. Actually, the choice of
\eta_{L}
,
\eta_{c}
and
T
was already made in Section
3.2
and is simply recalled here. However, the choice of
\eta_{0}
is specific to the proof of Proposition
3.1
. We have from Lemma
5.1
for
p=1
that

(5.20)

|\mathbf{E}J_{T}|+|\mathbf{E}I_{0}^{\eta_{L}}|+|\mathbf{E}I_{\eta_{L}}^{\eta_{0}}|\lesssim((N\eta_{0})^{2}+N^{-2\nu_{1}})N^{2a+\xi}\lesssim N^{1-\delta_{0}+\xi}.

Therefore, the contribution from the terms in the lhs. of (
5.20
) to
\mathbf{E}\mathcal{L}(\omega_{N})
can be incorporated into the error term in (
3.5
). We further analyze separately
I_{\eta_{c}}^{T}
and
I_{\eta_{0}}^{\eta_{c}}
. First we compute
I_{\eta_{c}}^{T}
up to the leading order. As we will see, this is the main contributing regime. Afterwards we show that
I_{\eta_{0}}^{\eta_{c}}
is negligible by comparison with GinUE.

Analysis of
I_{\eta_{c}}^{T}
.
Performing integration by parts in
I_{\eta_{c}}^{T}
, first with respect to
z
and then to
\overline{z}
, we get

(5.21)

\begin{split}I_{\eta_{c}}^{T}&=-\frac{N}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\mathbf{C}}\Delta\omega_{N}(z)\int_{\eta_{c}}^{T}\langle G^{z}(\mathrm{i}\eta)\rangle\mathrm{d}\eta=\frac{2N}{\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\mathbf{C}}\partial_{\bar{z}}\omega_{N}(z)\mathrm{d}^{2}z\int_{\eta_{c}}^{T}\partial_{z}\langle G^{z}(\mathrm{i}\eta)\rangle\mathrm{d}\eta\\
&=\frac{2N}{\pi}\int_{\mathbf{C}}\partial_{\bar{z}}\omega_{N}(z)\langle G^{z}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{c})F\rangle\mathrm{d}^{2}z+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{N^{1+a}}{T}\right)\\
&=-\frac{2N}{\pi}\int_{\mathbf{C}}\omega_{N}(z)\langle G^{z}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{c})F^{*}G^{z}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{c})F\rangle\mathrm{d}^{2}z+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{N}{T}\right).\end{split}

Here to go from the first to the second line we used that
\partial_{z}\langle G^{z}(\mathrm{i}\eta)\rangle=-\mathrm{i}\partial_{\eta}\langle G^{z}(\mathrm{i}\eta)F\rangle
,
\lVert\partial_{\bar{z}}\omega_{N}\rVert_{1}\lesssim N^{a}
and
\|G^{z}(\mathrm{i}T)\|\leq T^{-1}
. Since
F
and
F^{*}
are off-diagonal matrices, they are regular in the sense of
[
24
, Definition 3.1]
, so the two-resolvent local law with regular observables
[
24
, Theorem 4.4]
implies that

(5.22)

\left\langle\big(G^{z}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{c})F^{*}G^{z}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{c})-M_{11}^{F^{*}}\big)F\right\rangle\prec(N\eta_{c})^{-1/2},

where
M_{11}^{F^{*}}
is the deterministic approximation to
G^{z}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{c})F^{*}G^{z}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{c})
(for the definition see (
4.6
) with
M_{1}=M_{2}=M^{z}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{c})
). By a straightforward explicit calculation using (
3.30
), (
3.31
), (
4.6
) and (
4.7
) we have

(5.23)

\langle M_{11}^{F^{*}}F\rangle=\frac{m^{2}}{2}\cdot\frac{1-m^{2}+|z|^{2}u^{2}}{1-m^{2}-|z|^{2}u^{2}}=-\frac{1}{2}+\mathcal{O}(\eta_{c}),

with
m=m^{z}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{c})
and
u=u^{z}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{c})
. Therefore, combining (
5.21
), (
5.22
), and (
5.23
) we conclude that

(5.24)

I_{\eta_{c}}^{T}=\frac{N}{\pi}\int_{\mathbf{C}}\omega_{N}(z)\mathrm{d}^{2}z\left(1+\mathcal{O}(N^{-\delta_{c}/2})\right)=\frac{N}{\pi}\left(1+\mathcal{O}(N^{-\delta_{c}/2})\right),

where we also used that the integral of
\omega_{N}(z)
over
\mathbf{C}
equals to 1 due to the scaling (
3.3
).

Analysis of
I_{\eta_{0}}^{\eta_{c}}
.
Let
\widetilde{X}
be an
N\times N
GinUE matrix. Denote the
\widetilde{X}
-counterparts of
\mathcal{L}_{N}
,
I_{\eta_{l}}^{\eta_{u}}
, and
G^{z}(w)
by
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{N}
,
\widetilde{I}_{\eta_{l}}^{\eta_{u}}
, and
\widetilde{G}^{z}(w)
, respectively, where
0\leq\eta_{l}\leq\eta_{u}
. Since
\mathrm{supp}(\omega_{N})\subset(1-\delta/2)\mathbf{D}
and the one-particle density of the eigenvalue process of GinUE converges to
(2\pi)^{-1}
with exponential speed in the bulk
[
51
, Eq.(1.44)]
, i.e. for
|z|\leq(1-\delta/2)
, (
3.5
) holds for
\mathbf{E}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{N}(\omega_{N})
with an error term bounded by
\mathrm{e}^{-cN}
for some
c>0
independent of
N
. Together with (
5.20
) and (
5.24
) applied to
\widetilde{X}
, this gives

(5.25)

\left|\mathbf{E}\widetilde{I}_{\eta_{0}}^{\eta_{c}}\right|\leq\left|\mathbf{E}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{N}(\omega_{N})-\mathbf{E}\widetilde{I}_{\eta_{c}}^{T}\right|+\left|\mathbf{E}\widetilde{J}_{T}\right|+\left|\mathbf{E}\widetilde{I}_{0}^{\eta_{L}}\right|+\left|\mathbf{E}\widetilde{I}_{\eta_{L}}^{\eta_{0}}\right|\lesssim N^{1-\delta_{0}+\xi}+N^{1-\delta_{c}/2}.

Finally, we compare
\mathbf{E}I_{\eta_{0}}^{\eta_{c}}
with
\mathbf{E}\widetilde{I}_{\eta_{0}}^{\eta_{c}}
by the means of Proposition
3.6
, which is applicable provided that
\eta_{0}\geq N^{-3/2+\epsilon}
for some fixed
\epsilon>0
:

(5.26)

\left|\mathbf{E}\left[I_{\eta_{0}}^{\eta_{c}}-\widetilde{I}_{\eta_{0}}^{\eta_{c}}\right]\right|\!\lesssim\!N\!\!\int_{\mathbf{C}}|\Delta\omega_{N}(z)|\mathrm{d}^{2}z\!\int_{\eta_{0}}^{\eta_{c}}\left|\mathbf{E}\left[\langle G^{z}(\mathrm{i}\eta)\rangle-\langle\widetilde{G}^{z}(\mathrm{i}\eta)\rangle\right]\right|\mathrm{d}\eta\lesssim N^{1+\xi}\|\Delta\omega_{N}\|_{1}\int_{\eta_{0}}^{\eta_{c}}\Phi_{1}(\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta.

In the regime
\eta\in[N^{-1},\eta_{c}]
we simply estimate
\Phi_{1}(\eta)
from above by taking
t:=N^{-2/3}
in (
3.43
):

(5.27)

\Phi_{1}(\eta)\lesssim N^{-1/6}N\eta\leq N^{-1/6+\delta_{c}}.

For
\eta\in[N^{-1-\nu_{0}},N^{-1})
an elementary optimization of the rhs. of the first line of (
3.43
) in
t
shows that this quantity attains its minimum for
t\sim N^{-2/3}(N\eta)^{8/3}
, which gives

(5.28)

\Phi_{1}(\eta)\sim N^{-1/6}(N\eta)^{-4/3}.

In fact,
\Phi_{1}(\eta)
remains much smaller than the local law estimate of order
(N\eta)^{-1}
on the error term in (
3.42
) for a certain range of
\eta<N^{-1-\nu_{0}}
. However, we do not explore this regime, since the rhs. of (
5.26
) is of order
N
when
\eta_{0}\sim N^{-1-\nu_{0}}
, as we will now see. Thus, for
\eta_{0}\ll N^{-1-\nu_{0}}
the rhs. of (
5.26
) is even much larger than
N
, which would contribute an error term to the rhs. of (
3.5
) exceeding the leading order. Together with the choice of
\eta_{0}
in (
5.19
) this explains the constraint
a<1+\nu_{0}
imposed in Proposition
3.1
.

Using (
5.27
) for
\eta\in[N^{-1},\eta_{c}]
, (
5.28
) for
\eta\in[N^{-1-\nu_{0}},N^{-1}]
, and recalling the definition of
\eta_{0}
from (
5.19
), we get

(5.29)

\int_{\eta_{0}}^{\eta_{c}}\Phi_{1}(\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta\lesssim N^{-7/6+2\delta_{c}}+N^{-1/6-4/3}\eta_{0}^{-1/3}\leq N^{-1+2\delta_{c}+\delta_{0}/3}\left(N^{-1/6}+N^{-1/3(1-a)}\right)

Finally, we combine (
5.26
), (
5.29
), (
5.18
) ,and use that
a\geq 1/2
:

(5.30)

\left|\mathbf{E}\left[I_{\eta_{0}}^{\eta_{c}}-\widetilde{I}_{\eta_{0}}^{\eta_{c}}\right]\right|\lesssim N^{1+(a-1/2-\nu_{0})7/3+2\delta_{c}+\delta_{0}/3+\xi}.

Importantly,
a<1/2+\nu_{0}
, so the rhs. of (
5.30
) is much smaller than
N
for sufficiently small
\delta_{c},\delta_{0},\xi>0
.

Using (
3.22
), collecting the error terms from (
5.20
), (
5.24
), (
5.25
), (
5.30
), and taking
\delta_{0}
and
\delta_{c}
sufficiently small, we finish the proof of Proposition
3.1
.

5.3.
Upper bound on the variance: proof of Proposition
3.2

Throughout this section we abbreviate
f_{N}:=f_{a,N}
. We start with introducing some additional notation. For a set
S\subset\mathbf{C}
and
h>0
denote the
h
-neighborhood of
S
by

(5.31)

{\mathfrak{N}}_{h}(S):=\{z\in\mathbf{C}\,:\,\mathrm{d}(z,S)<h\}

where
\mathrm{d}
stands for the Euclidean distance. In particular, for
z\in\mathbf{C}
we denote an open ball with the center at
z
and radius
r>0
by
B_{r}(z):={\mathfrak{N}}_{r}(\{z\})
. Since
\omega
is compactly supported, there exists
\mathcal{R}>0
such that
{\rm{supp}}(\omega)\subset B_{\mathcal{R}}(0)
. Denote the
tubular neighborhood
of
\partial\Omega_{N}
of radius
\mathcal{R}N^{-a}
by
11
11

11

We point out that here
\Delta
denotes the tubular neighborhood of a curve, and it should not be confused with the Laplacian.

(5.32)

\Delta\Omega_{N}:={\mathfrak{N}}_{\mathcal{R}N^{-a}}(\partial\Omega_{N}).

By construction of
f_{N}
in (
3.2
),
f_{N}(z)=1
for
z\in\Omega_{N}\setminus\Delta\Omega_{N}
and
f_{N}(z)=0
for
z\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\left(\Omega_{N}\cup\Delta\Omega_{N}\right)
. Therefore, only
z\in\Delta\Omega_{N}
contribute to the rhs. of the Girko’s formula (
3.20
). For the further reference we note that

(5.33)

|\Delta\Omega_{N}|\lesssim|\partial\Omega_{N}|\mathcal{R}N^{-a}\sim N^{-\alpha-a},

since
\Omega_{N}
satisfies Assumption
2.3
. We additionally observe that
\lVert f_{N}\rVert_{\infty}\lesssim 1
and

(5.34)

\lVert\Delta f_{N}\rVert_{1}\leq|\Delta\Omega_{N}|\|\Delta f_{N}\|_{\infty}\lesssim N^{-a-\alpha}N^{2a}=N^{a-\alpha}.

As the last preparation for the proof of Proposition
3.2
, we present a bound on the integrals of regularized singularities of the form
|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{-q}
,
q>0
, over
(\Delta\Omega_{N})^{2}
. Later these estimates will be used for
q\penalty\ \!=\penalty\ \!2q_{0},2,4
, where
q_{0}=1/20
is defined in Proposition
3.2
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Suppose that
\Omega_{N}
satisfies Assumption
2.3
. Then for any fixed exponent
q>0
and a sequence
\{r_{N}\}\subset(0,+\infty)
it holds that

(5.35)

\int_{\Delta\Omega_{N}}\int_{\Delta\Omega_{N}}\frac{1}{|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{q}+r_{N}}\mathrm{d}^{2}z_{1}\mathrm{d}^{2}z_{2}\lesssim N^{-2a}\begin{cases}N^{-\alpha}r_{N}^{-\frac{q-1}{q}}\left(|\log r_{N}|+\log N\right),&\text{if}\,\,q\geq 1,\\
N^{-\alpha(2-q)},&\text{if}\,\,0<q<1,\end{cases}

where the implicit constant depends only on the model parameters from Assumption
2.3
.

The proof of Lemma
5.2
is an elementary calculus exercise using that
\Delta\Omega_{N}
is a tubular neighborhood of a
C^{2}
curve
\partial\Omega_{N}
, we omit further details.

Now we are ready to prove Proposition
3.2
. The structure of the proof is similar to the one presented in Section
5.2
. First we note that
{\rm supp}(f_{N})\subset(1-\delta/2)\mathbf{D}
for sufficiently large
N
, so applying Lemma
5.1
for
p=2
along with the bound (
5.34
) we get

(5.36)

\mathbf{E}|J_{T}|^{2}+\mathbf{E}|I_{0}^{\eta_{L}}|^{2}+\mathbf{E}|I_{\eta_{L}}^{\eta_{0}}|^{2}\lesssim\left((N\eta_{0})^{2}+N^{-2\nu_{1}}\right)\|\Delta f_{N}\|_{1}^{2}N^{\xi}\lesssim\left((N\eta_{0})^{2}+N^{-2\nu_{1}}\right)N^{2(a-\alpha)+\xi}

for sufficiently large
L,D^{\prime}>0
and
\eta_{L}=:N^{-L},T:=N^{D^{\prime}}
. We further take
\eta_{c}:=N^{-1+\delta_{c}}
for a small
\delta_{c}>0
, and
\eta_{0}\in[N^{-1-\nu_{1}},N^{-1})
. The exact choice of
\eta_{0}
will be specified at the end. The Girko’s formula (
3.22
) implies that

(5.37)

\mathrm{Var}\left[\mathcal{L}_{N}(f_{N})\right]\lesssim\mathbf{E}|J_{T}|^{2}+\mathbf{E}|I_{0}^{\eta_{L}}|^{2}+\mathbf{E}|I_{\eta_{L}}^{\eta_{0}}|^{2}+\mathrm{Var}\left[I_{\eta_{0}}^{\eta_{c}}\right]+\mathrm{Var}\left[I_{\eta_{c}}^{T}\right].

The bound (
5.36
) takes care of the first three terms in (
5.37
), now we proceed to the analysis of the last two terms in the rhs. of (
5.37
).

Analysis of
\mathrm{Var}\left[I_{\eta_{c}}^{T}\right]
.
We write the variance of
I_{\eta_{c}}^{T}
as in (
3.26
) and use the formula for the covariance of resolvent traces from (
3.35
). To bound the contribution from the error term in (
3.35
) to
\mathrm{Var}\left[I_{\eta_{c}}^{T}\right]
, we estimate
\gamma\geq|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}+\eta_{c}
and perform the integration over
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
:

(5.38)

\begin{split}&\left(\frac{N}{2\pi}\right)^{2}\int_{\mathbf{C}}\int_{\mathbf{C}}|\Delta f_{N}(z_{1})||\Delta f_{N}(z_{2})|\left(\int_{\eta_{c}}^{T}\int_{\eta_{c}}^{T}\left(\frac{1}{N\gamma}+N^{-1/4}\right)\frac{N^{\xi}}{N^{2}\eta_{1}\eta_{2}}\mathrm{d}\eta_{1}\mathrm{d}\eta_{2}\right)\mathrm{d}^{2}z_{1}\mathrm{d}^{2}z_{2}\\
&\quad\lesssim\|\Delta f_{N}\|_{\infty}^{2}(\log N)^{2}N^{\xi}\int_{\Delta\Omega_{N}}\int_{\Delta\Omega_{N}}\left(\frac{1}{N}\frac{1}{|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}+\eta_{c}}+N^{-1/4}\right)\mathrm{d}^{2}z_{1}\mathrm{d}^{2}z_{2}\\
&\quad\lesssim N^{4a+\xi}\left(N^{-\alpha-2a-1/2-\delta_{c}/2}+N^{-2\alpha-2a-1/4}\right).\end{split}

To go from the second to the third line we used that
\|\Delta f_{N}\|_{\infty}\lesssim N^{2a}
, estimated the area of
\Delta\Omega_{N}
by (
5.33
), and applied Lemma
5.2
to
q=2
and
r_{N}=\eta_{c}
. Next, compute the contribution from the leading term in the rhs. of (
3.35
) using the following explicit formula from
[
33
, Lemma 4.8, Lemma 4.10]
:

(5.39)

\begin{split}&\left(\frac{N}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\right)^{2}\int_{\mathbf{C}}\int_{\mathbf{C}}\Delta f_{N}(z_{1})\Delta f_{N}(z_{2})\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{N^{2}}\cdot\frac{V_{12}+\kappa_{4}U_{1}U_{2}}{2}\mathrm{d}\eta_{1}\mathrm{d}\eta_{2}\right)\mathrm{d}^{2}z_{1}\mathrm{d}^{2}z_{2}\\
&\quad=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{C}|\nabla f_{N}(z)|^{2}\mathrm{d}^{2}z+\frac{\kappa_{4}}{\pi^{2}}\left|\int_{\mathbf{C}}f_{N}(z)\mathrm{d}^{2}z\right|^{2}\lesssim N^{a-\alpha}+N^{-4\alpha}.\end{split}

In the first line of (
5.39
) the trivial regimes
\eta_{l}\in[0,\eta_{c}]\cup[T,+\infty)
are included, since the identity stated in (
5.39
) is available only for the
\eta
-integrals over the entire regime
[0,+\infty)
. However, these trivial regimes are not present in the rhs. of (
3.26
), so we need to eliminate them from (
5.39
). An elementary calculation using
[
33
, Eq. (4.24)]
shows that

(5.40)

\left|\int_{\mathbf{C}}\int_{\mathbf{C}}\Delta f_{N}(z_{1})\Delta f_{N}(z_{2})\left(\left(\int_{0}^{\eta_{c}}+\int_{T}^{\infty}\right)^{2}\frac{V_{12}+\kappa_{4}U_{1}U_{2}}{2}\mathrm{d}\eta_{1}\mathrm{d}\eta_{2}\right)\mathrm{d}^{2}z_{1}\mathrm{d}^{2}z_{2}\right|\lesssim N^{2a-\alpha-1/2-\delta_{c}/2+\xi},

for the proof see Supplementary Section
S4.2
. Combining (
3.26
), (
5.38
), (
5.39
), (
5.40
), we conclude that

(5.41)

\mathrm{Var}\left[I_{\eta_{c}}^{T}\right]\lesssim N^{2a-\alpha-1/2+\xi}+N^{2a-2\alpha-1/4+\xi},

where we also used that
a>1/2
to show that the error terms coming from the rhs. of (
5.39
) are suppressed by the ones in the rhs. of (
5.41
).

Analysis of
\mathrm{Var}\left[I_{\eta_{0}}^{\eta_{c}}\right]
.
First we write the variance of
I_{\eta_{0}}^{\eta_{c}}
similarly to (
3.26
). In the regime
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in[\eta_{0},\eta_{c}]
the covariance of
\langle G^{z_{1}}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{1})\rangle
and
\langle G^{z_{2}}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{2})\rangle
is controlled by the means of Proposition
3.7
. However, for
|z_{1}-z_{2}|\leq N^{-1/2}
this control is not effective. Indeed, in this case
R
varies from
0
to
N|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}\leq 1
in the definition of
\Phi_{2}
in (
3.46
), so
N\mathcal{E}_{1}(t,R)\geq\sqrt{Nt}
, which gives that
\Phi_{2}\gg(N^{2}\eta_{1}\eta_{2})^{-1}
. Moreover,
\Phi_{2}
exceeds
(N^{2}\eta_{1}\eta_{2})^{-1}
by a non-negligible power of
N
as
|z_{1}-z_{2}|
decreases further. So, instead of applying Proposition
3.7
in the regime
|z_{1}-z_{2}|\leq N^{-1/2}
, we simply use the averaged single-resolvent local law (
3.34
) and get

(5.42)

\int_{\eta_{0}}^{\eta_{c}}\int_{\eta_{0}}^{\eta_{c}}\left|\mathrm{Cov}\left(\langle G^{z_{1}}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{1})\rangle,\langle G^{z_{2}}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{2})\rangle\right)\right|\mathrm{d}\eta_{1}\mathrm{d}\eta_{2}\lesssim N^{\xi}\int_{\eta_{0}}^{\eta_{c}}\int_{\eta_{0}}^{\eta_{c}}\frac{1}{N^{2}\eta_{1}\eta_{2}}\mathrm{d}\eta_{1}\mathrm{d}\eta_{2}\lesssim N^{-2+\xi},

uniformly in
z_{1},z_{2}\in\Delta\Omega_{N}
. We note that the contribution of the regime
|z_{1}-z_{2}|\leq N^{-1/2}
to the analogue of (
3.26
) for
I_{\eta_{0}}^{\eta_{c}}
gains smallness from the volume of the integration domain, rather than from (
5.42
). In particular, (
5.42
) is not affordable for general
z_{1},z_{2}
.

In the complementary regime
|z_{1}-z_{2}|>N^{-1/2}
, Proposition
3.7
already improves beyond the trivial local law bound, and gives that

(5.43)

\left|\mathrm{Cov}\left(\langle G^{z_{1}}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{1})\rangle,\langle G^{z_{2}}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{2})\rangle\right)\right|\lesssim N^{\xi}\Phi_{2}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},|z_{1}-z_{2}|)\lesssim\left(N(\mathcal{E}_{1}(t,R)+\mathcal{E}_{0}(t))+\frac{\sqrt{N}t}{(N\eta_{*})^{3}}\right)\frac{N^{6\delta_{c}+\xi}}{N^{2}\eta_{1}\eta_{2}},

with
\eta_{*}:=\eta_{1}\wedge\eta_{2}
, uniformly in
z_{1},z_{2}\in\Delta\Omega_{N}
,
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in[\eta_{0},\eta_{c}]
and
t\in[N^{-1+\omega_{*}},N^{-\omega_{*}}]
,
R\in[0,N|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}]
, for any fixed
\omega_{*}>0
. We integrate (
5.43
) over
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in[\eta_{0},\eta_{c}]
and obtain

(5.44)

\int_{\eta_{0}}^{\eta_{c}}\int_{\eta_{0}}^{\eta_{c}}\left|\mathrm{Cov}\left(\langle G^{z_{1}}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{1})\rangle,\langle G^{z_{2}}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{2})\rangle\right)\right|\mathrm{d}\eta_{1}\mathrm{d}\eta_{2}\lesssim N^{-2+8\delta_{c}+\xi}\left(N(\mathcal{E}_{1}(t,R)+\mathcal{E}_{0}(t))+\frac{\sqrt{N}t}{(N\eta_{0})^{3}}\right).

Next, we optimize the rhs. of (
5.44
) over
R
and
t
, which leads to the choice

(5.45)

R:=\left(N|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}\right)^{4/5},\quad t:=N^{-1}\left(N|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}\right)^{1/10}.

This gives

(5.46)

\int_{\eta_{0}}^{\eta_{c}}\int_{\eta_{0}}^{\eta_{c}}\left|\mathrm{Cov}\left(\langle G^{z_{1}}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{1})\rangle,\langle G^{z_{2}}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{2})\rangle\right)\right|\mathrm{d}\eta_{1}\mathrm{d}\eta_{2}\lesssim N^{-2+8\delta_{c}+\xi}\left(N|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}\right)^{-1/20},

where we additionally used that
N\eta_{0}\gtrsim(N|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2})^{1/20}N^{-1/6}
, since
|z_{1}-z_{2}|\lesssim 1
and
\eta_{0}\geq N^{-1-\nu_{1}}
, with
\nu_{1}=1/10
.

Using the analogue of (
3.26
) for
I_{\eta_{0}}^{\eta_{c}}
, and employing (
5.42
) for
|z_{1}-z_{2}|\leq N^{-1/2}
and (
5.46
) for
|z_{1}-z_{2}|>N^{-1/2}
, we get

(5.47)

\mathrm{Var}\left[I_{\eta_{0}}^{\eta_{c}}\right]\lesssim N^{8\delta_{c}+\xi}\int_{\Delta\Omega_{N}}\int_{\Delta\Omega_{N}}|\Delta f_{N}(z_{1})||\Delta f_{N}(z_{2})|(N|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}+1)^{-1/20}\mathrm{d}^{2}z_{1}\mathrm{d}^{2}z_{2}.

This integral is further estimated by Lemma
5.2
with
q:=2q_{0}=1/10
and
r_{N}:=N^{-1/2}
:

(5.48)

\mathrm{Var}\left[I_{\eta_{0}}^{\eta_{c}}\right]\lesssim N^{8\delta_{c}+\xi}\|\Delta f_{N}\|_{\infty}^{2}N^{-2a-\alpha(2-2q_{0})-q_{0}}\lesssim N^{2a-2\alpha-2q_{0}(1/2-\alpha)+8\delta_{c}+\xi},

where we also used that
\|\Delta f_{N}\|_{\infty}\lesssim N^{2a}
.

Note that the bound (
5.47
) is insensitive to the exact choice of
\eta_{0}\in[N^{-1-\nu_{1}},N^{-1})
, so we choose
\eta_{0}:=N^{-1-\nu_{1}}
to minimize the rhs. of (
5.36
). Taking
\delta_{c}
sufficiently small, using (
5.37
) and collecting the error terms from (
5.36
), (
5.41
), (
5.47
), we get

(5.49)

\mathrm{Var}\left[\mathcal{L}_{N}(f_{N})\right]\lesssim N^{2a-2\alpha+\xi}\left(N^{-2\nu_{1}}+N^{-1/4}+N^{-2q_{0}(1/2-\alpha)}\right)+N^{2a-\alpha-1/2+\xi}.

Recalling that
\nu_{1}=1/10
,
q_{0}=1/20
and
\alpha\in[0,1/2)
, we obtain that
N^{2a-2\alpha-2q_{0}(1/2-\alpha)+\xi}
dominates the rest of the terms in the rhs. of (
5.49
) and complete the proof of Proposition
3.2
.

6.
Proofs of the ingredients required for the analysis of the regime
\eta\lesssim N^{-1}

In this section we prove various technical results needed to estimate the contribution of the regime
\eta\lesssim N^{-1}
in Girko’s formula. Our arguments rely on the Dyson Brownian motion techniques: in the proofs of Propositions
3.5
and
3.6
we use the relaxation of the DBM from
[
14
, Proposition 4.6]
, while in the proof of Proposition
3.7
we employ the quantitative decorrelation of two DBMs from Theorem
6.1
stated below. DBM plays a central role in this section, since it regularizes the eigenvalue distribution of
H^{z}
on small scales at the cost of adding a small Gaussian component to
X
.
This component is subsequently removed via a separate Green function comparison argument (GFT), whose technical implementation in the proof of Proposition
3.5
differs from the one in the proofs of Propositions
3.6
and
3.7
.

First, we introduce the set-up for the quantitative decorrelation of two DBMs. Consider the flow

(6.1)

\mathrm{d}X_{t}=\frac{\mathrm{d}B_{t}}{\sqrt{N}},\qquad\quad X_{0}=X,

where the entries
(B_{t})_{ab}
are independent standard complex Brownian motions. Let
H_{t}^{z}
be the Hermitization of
X_{t}-z
defined as in (
3.18
) and denote its eigenvalues by
\lambda_{i}^{z}(t)
,
|i|\leq N
. Here and also further in this section we implicitly exclude zero from the set of indices
|i|\leq N
. It is known that the eigenvalues of
H_{t}^{z}
satisfy the following SDE called the Dyson Brownian motion

(6.2)

\mathrm{d}\lambda_{i}^{z}(t)=\frac{\mathrm{d}b_{i}^{z}(t)}{\sqrt{2N}}+\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}^{z}(t)-\lambda_{j}^{z}(t)}\mathrm{d}t,

though the exact form of this SDE is not important in this section. In (
6.2
),
\{b_{i}^{z}(t)\}_{i=1}^{N}
are independent standard real Brownian motions, and
b_{-i}^{z}(t)=-b_{i}^{z}(t)
. We consider the evolution of eigenvalues of
H_{t}^{z_{1}}
and
H_{t}^{z_{2}}
for some
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbf{C}
and prove the following result.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
X
be a complex i.i.d. matrix satisfying Assumption
2.1
(i), and let
X_{t}
be the solution of (
6.1
) with initial condition
X_{0}=X
. Fix any small
\omega_{*},\delta>0
,
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbf{C}
with
|z_{1}|,|z_{2}|<1-\delta
,
T>N^{-1+\omega_{*}}
, and a possibly
N
-dependent
0<R<N|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}
. Then there exist two diffusion processes
\mu^{(l)}_{i}(t)
,
|i|\leq N
,
t\in[0,T]
for
l=1,2
, adapted to the filtration induced by the Brownian motion in (
6.1
), which satisfy the following properties.

(i)

For any
t\in[0,T]
and
l=1,2
, the
\{\mu^{(l)}_{i}(t)\}_{|i|\leq N}
are distributed as the eigenvalues of the Hermitization of
\widetilde{X}^{(l)}_{t}
, where
\widetilde{X}^{(l)}_{t}
is defined by the flow (
6.1
) with the initial condition given by a complex Ginibre matrix
\widetilde{X}^{(l)}_{0}:=\widetilde{X}^{(l)}
.

(ii)

The processes
\{(\mu^{(1)}_{i}(t))_{t\in[0,T]}\}_{|i|\leq N}
and
\{(\mu^{(2)}_{i}(t))_{t\in[0,T]}\}_{|i|\leq N}
are independent.

(iii)

For
l=1,2
and any
\xi>0
it holds that

(6.3)

\left|\rho_{t}^{z_{l}}(0)\lambda_{i}^{z_{l}}-\rho_{sc,t}(0)\mu_{i}^{(l)}\right|\leq N^{\xi}\left(\mathcal{E}_{1}(t,R)+|i|\mathcal{E}_{0}(t)+\frac{|i|^{2}}{N^{2}}\right),

with very high probability simultaneously for all
t\in[N^{-1+\omega_{*}},T]
and indices
|i|\leq R
. Here
\mathcal{E}_{0}
and
\mathcal{E}_{1}
are defined in (
3.43
) and (
3.46
), respectively, and
\rho_{t}^{z}
,
\rho_{\mathrm{sc},t}
are the evolutions of
\rho^{z}
and
\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}
along the semicircular flow
12
12

12

Here we denoted
\mu_{\mathrm{sc},t}(\mathrm{d}x)=\rho_{\mathrm{sc},t}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x:=t^{-1/2}\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}(t^{-1/2}x)\mathrm{d}x
, where
\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}(x):=(2\pi)^{-1}\sqrt{[4-x^{2}]_{+}}
. The density
\rho_{t}^{z_{l}}
is defined as follows. Let
\mu^{z_{l}}(\mathrm{d}x):=\rho_{0}^{z_{l}}(x)\mathrm{d}x
, then

\rho_{t}^{z_{l}}(x):=\lim_{\Im z\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{\pi}\Im\int_{\mathbf{R}}\frac{\mu_{t}^{z_{l}}(\mathrm{d}x)}{x-z},

where
\mu_{t}^{z_{l}}:=\mu^{z_{l}}\boxplus\mu_{\mathrm{sc},t}
is given by the free additive convolution between
\mu^{z_{l}}
and the time-rescaled semicircular measure
\mu_{\mathrm{sc},t}(\mathrm{d}x)
. We refer to
[
14
, Section 4.4]
for a detailed description of the semicircular flow evolution.
, respectively.

Theorem
6.1
establishes decorrelation of the eigenvalues of
H^{z_{1}}_{t}
and
H^{z_{2}}_{t}
in the regime
|z_{1}-z_{2}|\gg N^{-1/2}
by coupling them to two independent processes
\{\mu^{(1)}_{i}(t)\}_{|i|\leq N}
and
\{\mu^{(2)}_{i}(t)\}_{|i|\leq N}
. This decorrelation has been obtained in various forms
[
14
,
33
,
38
]
(see also
[
34
]
) relying on the analysis of weakly correlated Dyson Brownian motions (DBM). Our proof of Theorem
6.1
relies on the recent proof of
[
14
, Theorem 4.1]
to give a quantitative estimate of the decorrelation exponent, and is presented in Supplementary Section
S1
. We remark that one of the main inputs required for the proof of Theorem
6.1
is the overlap bound from Proposition
4.6
.

For ease of reference, we now state
[
14
, Proposition 4.6]
, whose set-up is similar but much simpler than the one of Theorem
6.1
. Namely, it concerns a single Hermitization parameter
z\in\mathbf{C}
instead of two
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbf{C}
. Fix (small)
\omega_{*},\delta,\xi>0
. By
[
14
, Proposition 4.6]
there exists a diffusion process
\{\mu_{i}(t)\}_{|i|\leq N}
satisfying Theorem
6.1
(i), such that

(6.4)

\left|\rho_{t}^{z}(0)\lambda^{z}_{i}(t)-\rho_{sc,t}(0)\mu_{i}(t)\right|\leq N^{\xi}\left(|i|\mathcal{E}_{0}(t)+\frac{|i|^{2}}{N^{2}}\right),\quad\text{with}\quad\mathcal{E}_{0}(t):=\frac{1}{N}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{Nt}}+t\right),

with very high probability, uniformly in
|z|\leq 1-\delta
,
t\in[N^{-1+\omega_{*}},1]
and
|i|\leq N
. Here
\rho_{t}^{z}
and
\rho_{sc,t}
are defined as in Theorem
6.1
, and
\mathcal{E}_{0}
is defined in (
3.43
).

We further assume in this section that
\rho_{t}^{z}(0)=\rho_{sc,t}(0)
to simplify the application of both (
6.4
) and Theorem
6.1
. This assumption can be easily removed by a simple time-rescaling.

The proofs of Propositions
3.5
,
3.6
, and
3.7
share the same strategy consisting of two steps. First, we fix a small
\omega_{*}>0
and for any
N^{-1+\omega_{*}}\leq t\leq 1
establish the desired bounds for the i.i.d. matrix distributed as

(6.5)

\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+t}}X_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+t}}X+\frac{\sqrt{t}}{\sqrt{1+t}}\widetilde{X},

where
X_{t}
follows (
6.1
) and
\widetilde{X}
is a complex Ginibre matrix independent from
X
. Here we normalized the lhs. of (
6.5
) by
(1+t)^{-1/2}
to ensure that the second-order moment structure of
X
coincides with the one of the lhs. of (
6.5
). In the second step we embed
X
into the matrix-valued Ornstein-Uhlenbeck flow

(6.6)

\mathrm{d}X^{t}=-\frac{1}{2}X^{t}+\frac{\mathrm{d}B_{t}}{\sqrt{N}},\quad X^{0}=X,

with
B_{t}
defined in the same way as in (
6.1
). Since

(6.7)

X^{s}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathrm{e}^{-s/2}X+\sqrt{1-\mathrm{e}^{-s}}\widetilde{X}

for any
s>0
, it holds that

(6.8)

X^{s_{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+t}}X_{t}\quad\text{for}\quad s_{0}=\log(1+t).

This enables us to perform a GFT using the flow (
6.6
) and remove the Gaussian component from
X_{t}
. These two steps (adding and removing a Gaussian component) are afterwards complemented by the optimization in
t
of the sum of two error terms obtained one at each step.

We denote the analogue of
H^{z}
defined in (
3.18
) for
X_{t},X^{t}
and
\widetilde{X}
by
H^{z}_{t}
,
H^{t,z}
and
\widetilde{H}^{z}
, respectively. The same notational convention is used for the analogues of
W
and
G^{z}(w)
defined in (
3.18
) and (
3.19
), respectively. We also use
\xi
to denote a positive
N
-independent exponent which can be taken arbitrarily small and whose exact value may change from line to line. Now we separately prove each of the Propositions
3.5
,
3.6
, and
3.7
using the strategy explained above.

6.1.
Proof of Proposition
3.5

Fix a small
\omega_{*}>0
. First we prove that

(6.9)

\mathbf{P}\left[\lambda_{1}^{z}(t)<N^{-1}x\right]\lesssim\log N\cdot x^{2}+N^{\xi}(N\mathcal{E}_{0}(t))^{2}

for any fixed
\xi>0
uniformly in
t\in[N^{-1+\omega_{*}},1]
and
x\in(0,1]
. Let
\{\mu_{i}(t)\}_{|i|\leq N}
be given by
[
14
, Proposition 4.6]
. Applying (
6.4
) for
i=1
, we get that

(6.10)

\mathbf{P}\left[\lambda_{1}^{z}(t)\leq N^{-1}x\right]\leq\mathbf{P}\left[\mu_{1}(t)\leq N^{-1}x+N^{\xi}\mathcal{E}_{0}(t)\right]+\mathcal{O}(N^{-D})

for any (large) fixed
D>0
. Since
\mu_{1}(t)
is distributed as the least positive eigenvalue of the Hermitization of
\widetilde{X}_{t}
, where
(1+t)^{-1/2}\widetilde{X}_{t}
is a complex Ginibre matrix,
[
26
, Corollary 2.4]
for
z=0
implies that

(6.11)

\mathbf{P}\left[\mu_{1}(t)\leq N^{-1}y\right]\lesssim(|\log y|+1)y^{2}

uniformly in
y>0
. Here we additionally used that
1+t\sim 1
. Combining (
6.11
) for
y\sim x+N^{1+\xi}\mathcal{E}_{0}
with (
6.10
), we obtain (
6.9
).

Now we compare the probability in the lhs. of (
6.9
) at times
t
and
0
by closely following the GFT strategy introduced in
[
42
]
and later employed in
[
46
,
10
]
. Recall the definition of
X^{s}
from (
6.6
) and denote the least positive eigenvalue of
H^{s,z}
by
\lambda_{1}^{s,z}
for
s\geq 0
. By (
6.8
) we have that

(6.12)

\lambda_{1}^{s_{0},z}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}(1+t)^{-1/2}\lambda_{1}^{z}(t)\quad\text{for}\quad s_{0}=\log(1+t).

For
E>0
, let
\bm{1}_{E}
be the characteristic function of
[-E,E]
. Trivially, it holds that

(6.13)

\mathbf{P}[\lambda_{1}^{s,z}\leq E]=\mathbf{P}\left[\mathrm{Tr}\bm{1}_{E}(H^{s,z})\geq 1\right],\quad s\geq 0.

In order to represent the rhs. of (
6.13
) in terms of
G^{s,z}
, we consider a non-decreasing function
F\in C^{\infty}(\mathbf{R})
such that

F(y)=0\,\,\text{for}\,\,0\leq y\leq 1/9;\qquad F(y)=1\,\,\text{for}\,\,y\geq 2/9.

Since
\mathrm{Tr}\bm{1}_{E}(H^{s,z})
is integer-valued, (
6.13
) implies that

(6.14)

\mathbf{P}[\lambda_{1}^{s,z}\leq E]=\mathbf{E}\left[F(\mathrm{Tr}\bm{1}_{E}(H^{s,z}))\right],\quad s\geq 0.

Similarly to
[
46
, Lemma 2.3]
, for any
\eta,l>0
such that
\eta\leq l\leq E
, we have

(6.15)

\mathrm{Tr}\bm{1}_{E-l}*\theta_{\eta}(H^{s,z})-N^{\xi}\frac{\eta}{l}\leq\mathrm{Tr}\bm{1}_{E}(H^{s,z})\leq\mathrm{Tr}\bm{1}_{E+l}*\theta_{\eta}(H^{s,z})+N^{\xi}\frac{\eta}{l}\quad\text{with}\quad\theta_{\eta}(y):=\frac{\eta}{\pi(y^{2}+\eta^{2})}

for any fixed
\xi>0
, with probability at least
1-N^{-D}
.

Given
x\in(0,1]
, we fix a (small)
\epsilon>0
and choose

(6.16)

E:=N^{-1}x,\quad l:=N^{-\epsilon}E,\quad\eta:=N^{-2\epsilon}E.

In particular
\eta\ll l
, which in combination with (
6.15
) implies that

(6.17)

\mathbf{E}\left[F\left(\mathrm{Tr}\bm{1}_{E-l}*\theta_{\eta}(H^{s,z})\right)\right]-N^{-D}\leq\mathbf{E}\left[F\left(\mathrm{Tr}\bm{1}_{E}(H^{s,z})\right)\right]\leq\mathbf{E}\left[F\left(\mathrm{Tr}\bm{1}_{E+l}*\theta_{\eta}(H^{s,z})\right)\right]+N^{-D}

for any fixed
D>0
uniformly in
0\leq s\lesssim 1
. Observe that

(6.18)

\mathrm{Tr}\bm{1}_{E+l}*\theta_{\eta}(H^{s,z})=\frac{2N}{\pi}\int_{-(E+l)}^{E+l}\langle\Im G^{s,z}(y+\mathrm{i}\eta)\rangle\mathrm{d}y.

From now on we restrict to the case
\eta\geq N^{-3/2+\epsilon}
, which is sufficient for the proof of Proposition
3.5
. We claim that

(6.19)

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\mathbf{E}\left[F\left(\mathrm{Tr}\bm{1}_{E+l}*\theta_{\eta}(H^{s,z})\right)\right]\lesssim N^{1/2+\xi}(N\eta)^{-3}\mathbf{E}\left[F\left(\mathrm{Tr}\bm{1}_{E+3l}*\theta_{\eta}(H^{s,z})\right)\right]+N^{-D},

uniformly in
s\in[0,1]
. This is a direct analogue of
[
46
, Eq.(2.62)]
. The proof of (
6.19
) follows the same strategy as that of
[
46
, Eq.(2.62)]
and thus is omitted. However, one aspect of the proof simplifies. While
[
46
]
exploits the concentration property of resolvent entries, the so-called isotropic single-resolvent local law, resolvents appearing in the proof of
[
46
, Eq.(2.62)]
are decomposed into their deterministic counterparts and the fluctuations around them, and then these two terms are analyzed separately. In our situation
\eta
is below the scale of the spectral resolution and the fluctuation given by the isotropic local law exceeds the size of the deterministic approximation. So, we simply have from
[
16
, Theorem 3.4]
(see also
[
20
, Theorem 3.1]
) that

(6.20)

\left|(G^{s,z}(y+\mathrm{i}\eta))_{ab}\right|\prec\frac{1}{N\eta}

uniformly in
s\in[0,1]
,
|z|\leq 1-\delta
,
|y|\leq\tau^{\prime}
,
\eta\in(0,N^{-1})
and
a,b\in[2N]
, for some (small) constant
\tau^{\prime}>0
. In particular, in the proof of (
6.19
) we never decompose
G^{s,z}_{ab}
into
M^{s,z}_{ab}
and
(G^{s,z}-M^{s,z})_{ab}
, where
M^{s,z}
is the deterministic approximation to
G^{s,z}
, but just use the bound (
6.20
).

To complete the GFT, we fix
x
and
t
such that

(6.21)

N^{-1/2+2\epsilon+\xi}\leq x\leq 1\quad\text{and}\quad N^{-1+\omega_{*}}\leq t\leq N^{-1/2-2\xi}(N\eta)^{3}=N^{-1/2-6\epsilon-2\xi}x^{3}.

Here the lower bound on
x
is a consequence of the constraint
\eta\geq N^{-3/2+\xi}
with
\eta
defined in (
6.16
). The lower bound on
t
in (
6.21
) comes from the first step of this proof (see below (
6.9
)), while the upper bound ensures that

(6.22)

N^{1/2+\xi}(N\eta)^{-3}t\leq N^{-\xi},

which is crucial for the application of a Gronwall-type argument to (
6.19
). To make the set of
t
’s defined by (
6.21
) non-empty, we further restrict the range of
x
in (
6.21
) to

(6.23)

N^{-1/6+\omega_{*}+2\epsilon+\xi}\leq x\leq 1.

Finally, we take
s_{0}:=\log(1+t)
and observe that
s_{0}\sim t
. Combining (
6.17
), (
6.14
), (
6.12
), and (
6.9
) we get that

(6.24)

\begin{split}&\mathbf{E}\left[F\left(\mathrm{Tr}\bm{1}_{E+kl}*\theta_{\eta}(H^{s_{0},z})\right)\right]\leq\mathbf{P}\left[\lambda_{1}^{s_{0},z}\leq E+(k+1)l\right]+N^{-D}\\
&\quad=\mathbf{P}\left[\lambda_{1}^{z}(t)\leq(1+t)^{1/2}\left(E+(k+1)l\right)\right]+N^{-D}\lesssim\log N\cdot x^{2}+N^{\xi}(N\mathcal{E}_{0}(t))^{2}\end{split}

for any fixed
k\in\mathbf{N}
.

We derive from (
6.19
) that

(6.25)

\begin{split}&\mathbf{E}\left[F\left(\mathrm{Tr}\bm{1}_{E+l}*\theta_{\eta}(H^{0,z})\right)\right]\lesssim\mathbf{E}\left[F\left(\mathrm{Tr}\bm{1}_{E}*\theta_{\eta}(H^{s_{0},z})\right)\right]\\
&\quad+\sum_{m=1}^{k-1}\left(N^{1/2+\xi}(N\eta)^{-3}t\right)^{m}\mathbf{E}\left[F\left(\mathrm{Tr}\bm{1}_{E+(2m+1)l}*\theta_{\eta}(H^{s_{0},z})\right)\right]+\left(N^{1/2+\xi}(N\eta)^{-3}t\right)^{k}\end{split}

for any fixed
k\in\mathbf{N}
. The proof of (
6.25
) is identical to
[
10
, Eq.(3.19)–(3.20)]
and is thus omitted. Taking sufficiently large
N
-independent
k\in\mathbf{N}
in (
6.25
) and using (
6.22
), (
6.24
) as an input, we get

(6.26)

\mathbf{E}\left[F\left(\mathrm{Tr}\bm{1}_{E+l}*\theta_{\eta}(H^{0,z})\right)\right]\lesssim\log N\cdot x^{2}+N^{\xi}(N\mathcal{E}_{0}(t))^{2}.

Together with (
6.17
), (
6.14
), and (
6.16
) this implies

(6.27)

\mathbf{P}\left[\lambda_{1}^{z}\leq N^{-1}x\right]\lesssim\log N\cdot x^{2}+N^{\xi}(N\mathcal{E}_{0}(t))^{2},

where
\lambda_{1}^{z}=\lambda_{1}^{z}(0)
.

It remains to optimize (
6.27
) over the range of
t
given in (
6.21
) using the explicit form of
\mathcal{E}_{0}(t)
from (
6.4
). An elementary calculation shows that
\mathcal{E}_{0}(t)
achieves its minimum on
[N^{-1+\omega_{*}},N^{-1/2-6\epsilon-2\xi}x^{3}]
at the rightmost point of this interval. Choosing
\omega_{*},\epsilon,\xi>0
sufficiently small we thus get

(6.28)

\mathbf{P}\left[\lambda_{1}^{z}\leq N^{-1}x\right]\lesssim\log N\cdot x^{2}+N^{-1/2+C\xi}x^{-3},\quad x\in[N^{-1/6+\xi},1],

for any fixed
\xi>0
and for some constant
C>0
. Since for
x\geq N^{-\nu_{1}+C\xi}
with
\nu_{1}:=1/10
the first term in the rhs. of (
6.28
) dominates the second one, (
6.28
) completes the proof of Proposition
3.5
.

6.2.
Proof of Proposition
3.6

Let
\widetilde{X}_{t}
be the solution to (
6.1
) with the initial condition given by a complex Ginibre matrix. Denote the resolvent of the Hermitization of
\widetilde{X}_{t}-z
by
\widetilde{G}^{z}_{t}
. First we show that

(6.29)

\mathbf{E}\langle G_{t}^{z}(\mathrm{i}\eta)\rangle=\mathbf{E}\langle\widetilde{G}^{z}_{t}(\mathrm{i}\eta)\rangle+\mathcal{O}\left(N^{1+\xi}\mathcal{E}_{0}(t)\left(1+N\eta+\frac{N\mathcal{E}_{0}(t)}{N\eta}\right)\right)

for any fixed
\xi>0
, uniformly in
\eta\in(0,1]
,
|z|\leq 1-\delta
and
t\in[N^{-1+\omega_{*}},1]
.

Throughout the proof of (
6.29
) the parameters
\eta,z
, and
t
remain fixed, so we often omit them from notations. In particular, we simply denote
\lambda_{i}:=\lambda_{i}^{z}(t)
and
\mu_{i}:=\mu_{i}(t)
, where
\{\mu_{i}(t)\}_{|i|\leq N}
is given by
[
14
, Proposition 4.6]
. Introduce the notation

(6.30)

\mathscr{L}_{i}=\mathscr{L}_{i}(t,\eta):=\frac{\eta}{(\lambda_{i}(t))^{2}+\eta^{2}},\quad\mathscr{M}_{i}=\mathscr{M}_{i}(t,\eta):=\frac{\eta}{(\mu_{i}(t))^{2}+\eta^{2}},\quad\text{for}\quad i\in[N].

We claim that

(6.31)

\mathbf{E}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{L}_{i}(t,\eta)=\mathbf{E}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{M}_{i}(t,\eta)+\mathcal{O}\left(N^{1+\xi}\mathcal{E}_{0}(t)\left(1+N\eta+\frac{N\mathcal{E}_{0}(t)}{N\eta}\right)\right).

Once (
6.31
) is obtained, the rest of the proof of (
6.29
) goes as follows. Denote the eigenvalues of
\widetilde{H}^{z}_{t}
by
\{\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}^{z}(t)\}_{|i|\leq N}
and let
\{\widetilde{\mu}_{i}(t)\}_{|i|\leq N}
be the comparison process given by
[
14
, Proposition 4.6]
. Define
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{i}
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}_{i}
as the
\widetilde{X}
-counterparts of the quantities defined in (
6.30
). We apply (
6.31
) to
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{i},\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}_{i}
and note that
\widetilde{\mathscr{M}}_{i}
equals in distribution to
\mathscr{M}_{i}
by Theorem
6.1
(i) (we mentioned above (
6.4
) that this part of Theorem
6.1
is applicable in the set-up of (
6.4
)). Together with (
6.31
) this gives

(6.32)

\mathbf{E}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{L}_{i}(t,\eta)=\mathbf{E}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{i}(t,\eta)+\mathcal{O}\left(N^{1+\xi}\mathcal{E}_{0}(t)\left(1+N\eta+\frac{N\mathcal{E}_{0}(t)}{N\eta}\right)\right).

By (
3.25
), the lhs. of (
6.32
) equals to
-\mathrm{i}\mathbf{E}\langle G^{z}_{t}(\mathrm{i}\eta)\rangle
, while the sum in the rhs. of (
6.32
) equals to
-\mathrm{i}\mathbf{E}\langle\widetilde{G}^{z}_{t}(\mathrm{i}\eta)\rangle
. This finishes the derivation of (
6.29
) from (
6.31
).

Now we prove (
6.31
). In the calculations below the index
i
stands for a positive integer smaller than
N
. Recall the definition of the densities
\rho_{t}^{z}
and
\rho_{sc,t}
from Theorem
6.1
. Similarly to (
4.4
), we denote by
\{\gamma_{i}\}
and
\{\widetilde{\gamma}_{i}\}
the quantiles of
\rho^{z}_{t}
and
\rho_{sc,t}
, respectively. Although
\widetilde{\gamma}_{i}
depends on
t
and
\gamma_{i}
depends on both
z
and
t
, we suppress this dependence in the notation for brevity. Fix a (small) exponent
\xi_{0}>0
. We distinguish between the regimes
i>N^{\xi_{0}}
and
i\leq N^{\xi_{0}}
in the analysis of
|\mathscr{L}_{i}-\mathscr{M}_{i}|
.

Consider at first the case
i>N^{\xi_{0}}
, where we have that
\lambda_{i}\sim\gamma_{i}
with very high probability. In the regime
i\leq(1-\tau)N
, for any small fixed
\tau>0
, this follows from the eigenvalue rigidity (
4.3
) and (
4.5
), while for
i>(1-\tau)N
we simply use that
\lambda_{i}\sim 1
and
\gamma_{i}\sim 1
. Similarly we have
\mu_{i}\sim\widetilde{\gamma}_{i}
, since
\{\mu_{i}\}_{|i|\leq N}
are distributed as the eigenvalues of
\widetilde{H}^{0}_{t}
. Using additionally that
\gamma_{i}\sim\widetilde{\gamma}_{i}
by (
4.5
), we obtain

(6.33)

\left|\mathscr{L}_{i}-\mathscr{M}_{i}\right|=\frac{\eta\left|\lambda_{i}-\mu_{i}\right|(\lambda_{i}+\mu_{i})}{\big(\lambda_{i}^{2}+\eta^{2}\big)\big(\mu_{i}^{2}+\eta^{2}\big)}\sim\frac{\eta\left|\lambda_{i}-\mu_{i}\right|\gamma_{i}}{\big(\gamma_{i}^{2}+\eta^{2}\big)^{2}}\lesssim\frac{\eta\left|\lambda_{i}-\mu_{i}\right|}{\gamma_{i}^{3}}

with very high probability. Combining (
6.33
) with (
6.4
) and (
4.5
), we get

(6.34)

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i>N^{\xi_{0}}}\left|\mathscr{L}_{i}-\mathscr{M}_{i}\right|\lesssim N^{2+\xi}\eta\sum_{i>N^{\xi_{0}}}\frac{1}{|i|^{3}}\left(|i|\mathcal{E}_{0}(t)+\frac{|i|^{2}}{N^{2}}\right)\lesssim(N\eta)(N\mathcal{E}_{0}(t))

with very high probability, where in the last step we used that
\mathcal{E}_{0}(t)\geq N^{-2+\xi^{\prime}}
for some
\xi^{\prime}>0
.

For
i\leq N^{\xi_{0}}
we perform a more subtle analysis of the lhs. of (
6.33
). On the event

\mathfrak{E}_{i}:=\bm{1}\left\{\lambda_{i}\leq N^{2\xi_{0}}\mathcal{E}_{0}(t)\right\}

we estimate
\lambda_{i},\mu_{i}
trivially from below by zero and bound the probability of
\mathfrak{E}_{i}
from above first by the probability of
\mathfrak{E}_{1}
and then by (
6.9
) applied to
x:=N^{1+2\xi_{0}}\mathcal{E}_{0}(t)
:

(6.35)

\mathbf{E}\big[|\mathscr{L}_{i}-\mathscr{M}_{i}|\bm{1}\{\mathfrak{E}_{i}\}\big]\leq\mathbf{E}\big[\left(|\mathscr{L}_{i}|+|\mathscr{M}_{i}|\right)\bm{1}\{\mathfrak{E}_{1}\}\big]\lesssim\eta^{-1}\mathbf{P}\left[\mathfrak{E}_{1}\right]\lesssim\eta^{-1}N^{4\xi_{0}+\xi}(N\mathcal{E}_{0}(t))^{2}.

On the complementary event, (
6.4
) implies that
\lambda_{i}\sim\mu_{i}
and we perform the same calculation as in the first step in (
6.33
):

(6.36)

\mathbf{E}\big[\left|\mathscr{L}_{i}-\mathscr{M}_{i}\right|\left(1-\bm{1}\{\mathfrak{E}_{i}\}\right)\big]\!\lesssim\!\mathbf{E}\left[\!\frac{\eta|\lambda_{i}-\mu_{i}|\lambda_{i}}{\left(\lambda_{i}^{2}+\eta^{2}\right)^{2}}(1-\bm{1}\{\mathfrak{E}_{i}\})\!\right]\!\lesssim\!N^{\xi_{0}+\xi}\eta\mathcal{E}_{0}(t)\mathbf{E}\left[\!\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\lambda_{i}^{4}+\eta^{4}}(1-\bm{1}\{\mathfrak{E}_{i}\})\!\right]\!,

where in the second bound we also used that
|\lambda_{i}-\mu_{i}|\lesssim N^{\xi_{0}+\xi}\mathcal{E}_{0}(t)
for
i\leq N^{\xi_{0}}
by (
6.4
). To estimate the expectation in the rhs. of (
6.36
), consider the following dyadic decomposition

(6.37)

1-\bm{1}\{\mathfrak{E}_{i}\}\leq\sum_{k}\bm{1}\left\{2^{k-1}\eta<\lambda_{i}\leq 2^{k}\eta\right\},
