where the summation runs from
k\geq\log_{2}(\eta^{-1}\mathcal{E}_{0}(t))
to
k\lesssim\log N
. We use (
6.9
) together with the trivial bound
\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{i}
to estimate the probabilities of events in the rhs. of (
6.37
). This yields

(6.38)

\mathbf{E}\left[\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\lambda_{i}^{4}+\eta^{4}}(1-\bm{1}\{\mathfrak{E}_{i}\})\right]\lesssim\frac{N^{2+\xi}}{\eta}.

Finally, combining (
6.35
)–(
6.38
) we obtain

(6.39)

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\leq N^{\xi_{0}}}\mathbf{E}|\mathscr{L}_{i}-\mathscr{M}_{i}|\lesssim\left(1+\frac{N\mathcal{E}_{0}(t)}{N\eta}\right)N^{1+4\xi_{0}+\xi}\mathcal{E}_{0}(t).

We take
\xi_{0}>0
sufficiently small and collect error terms from (
6.34
) and (
6.39
). This finishes the proof of (
6.31
).

Now we proceed to the second step of the proof of Proposition
3.6
and remove the Gaussian component from
X_{t}
by the Green function comparison argument. Specifically, we show that

(6.40)

\left|\mathbf{E}\left\langle G^{s_{0},z}(\mathrm{i}\eta)\right\rangle-\mathbf{E}\left\langle G^{0,z}(\mathrm{i}\eta)\right\rangle\right|\lesssim N^{1/2+\xi}s_{0}\left(1+\frac{1}{N\eta}\right)^{4}

uniformly in
s_{0}\in[0,1]
,
\eta\in[N^{-3/2+\epsilon},1]
and
|z|\leq 1-\delta
, for any fixed
\epsilon,\delta>0
. Denote
G^{s}:=G^{s,z}(\mathrm{i}\eta)
for
s\geq 0
. The proof of (
6.40
) is based on the explicit formula

(6.41)

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\mathbf{E}\left\langle G^{s}\right\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{E}\left\langle W_{s}\left(G^{s}\right)^{2}\right\rangle+\mathbf{E}\left\langle\mathcal{S}[G^{s}]G^{s}\right\rangle,\quad\forall\,s\geq 0,

where
\mathcal{S}
is defined in (
3.28
). We estimate the rhs. of (
6.41
) in absolute value from above. To avoid carrying the dependence on
s
in notations, we demonstrate this bound only for
s=0
, while for general
s\geq 0
the estimates are exactly the same.

For a set of index pairs

(6.42)

\bm{\alpha}=\left(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k})\in([1,N]\times[N+1,2N]\cup[N+1,2N]\times[1,N]\right)^{k}

denote the normalized cumulant of the corresponding elements of
W
by

(6.43)

\kappa(\bm{\alpha})=\kappa(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}):=\kappa(\sqrt{N}w_{\alpha_{1}},\ldots,\sqrt{N}w_{\alpha_{k}}).

Since
X
has i.i.d. entries,
\kappa(\bm{\alpha})
does not vanish only when
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\in\{ab,ba\}
for some
a\in[1,N]
,
b\in[N+1,2N]
. We further denote
\partial_{\bm{\alpha}}:=\partial_{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\partial_{\alpha_{k}}
, where
\partial_{\alpha}
denotes the directional derivative in the direction
w_{\alpha}
, for an index pair
\alpha
. Performing the cumulant expansion
[
56
, Lemma 3.2]
(see also
[
57
, Lemma 3.1]
and
[
62
, Section II]
) in the first term in the rhs. of (
6.41
) we obtain

(6.44)

\mathbf{E}\langle WG^{2}\rangle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{a,b}\mathbf{E}\left[w_{ab}(G^{2})_{ba}\right]=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{a,b}\sum_{\ell=2}^{L}\sum_{\bm{\alpha}\in\{ab,ba\}^{\ell-1}}\frac{\kappa(ab,\bm{\alpha})}{N^{\ell/2}(\ell-1)!}\mathbf{E}\partial_{\bm{\alpha}}\left[(G^{2})_{ba}\right]+\mathcal{O}(N^{-D}),

for any fixed
D>0
. In (
6.44
) the upper index of summation
L\in\mathbf{N}
depends only on
D
, the model parameters from Assumption
2.1
, and
\epsilon>0
from the constraint
\eta\geq N^{-3/2+\epsilon}
. The
(a,b)
-summation in (
6.44
) runs over all
a,b
such that either
a\in[N]
,
b\in[N+1,2N]
or
a\in[N+1,2N]
,
b\in[N]
.

First, consider the second order (
\ell=2
) terms in (
6.44
). Performing in these terms differentiation in
\partial_{\bm{\alpha}}(G^{2})_{ba}
and summing up the resulting quantities over
a,b
, we conclude by a straightforward calculation that this sum exactly cancels out with the second term in the rhs. of (
6.41
). Thus, we only need to estimate the contribution from the third and higher order terms (
\ell\geq 3
) in the rhs. of (
6.44
), which we do by the means of the entry-wise bound

(6.45)

\left|(G^{s})_{ab}\right|\prec 1+\frac{1}{N\eta},

that holds uniformly in
a,b\in[2N]
,
|z|\leq 1-\delta
and
\eta\in(N^{-L},1]
for any fixed
L>0
. This bound immediately follows from the single-resolvent isotropic local law (
4.2
). Using (
6.45
), we obtain that the contribution from the third order cumulant terms to the lhs. of (
6.40
) is bounded by the rhs. of (
6.40
), while the contribution from the rest of the terms is even smaller. We omit the rest of the details, since the proof of (
6.40
) is fairly standard.

To complete the proof of Proposition
3.6
, we fix
t\in[N^{-1+\omega_{*}},N^{-\omega_{*}}]
and apply (
6.29
) with
(1+t)^{1/2}z
and
(1+t)^{1/2}\eta
instead of
z
and
\eta
, respectively. Here
z
and
\eta
satisfy the conditions of Proposition
3.6
. Due to the upper bound
t\leq N^{-\omega_{*}}
, we have that
(1+t)^{1/2}|z|\leq 1-\delta/2
for sufficiently large
N
. Thus, after rescaling, (
6.29
) compares the resolvents of
(1+t)^{-1/2}X_{t}
and
(1+t)^{-1/2}\widetilde{X}_{t}
. Using additionally that
(1+t)^{-1/2}\widetilde{X}_{t}
and
\widetilde{X}
have the same distribution, we get

(6.46)

\mathbf{E}\left\langle\left((1+t)^{-1/2}X_{t}-Z-\mathrm{i}\eta\right)^{-1}\right\rangle=\mathbf{E}\left\langle\left(\widetilde{X}-Z-\mathrm{i}\eta\right)^{-1}\right\rangle+\mathcal{O}\left(N^{1+\xi}\mathcal{E}_{0}(t)\left(1+N\eta+\frac{N\mathcal{E}_{0}(t)}{N\eta}\right)\right)

Combining (
6.46
) with (
6.40
) applied to
s_{0}:=\log(1+t)
and recalling (
6.8
), we obtain

(6.47)

\mathbf{E}\langle G^{z}(\mathrm{i}\eta)\rangle=\mathbf{E}\langle\widetilde{G}^{z}(\mathrm{i}\eta)\rangle+N^{\xi}\mathcal{O}\left(N\mathcal{E}_{0}(t)\left(1+N\eta+\frac{N\mathcal{E}_{0}(t)}{N\eta}\right)+\sqrt{N}t\left(1+\frac{1}{N\eta}\right)^{4}\right)

uniformly in
t\in[N^{-1+\omega_{*}},N^{-\omega_{*}}]
,
|z|\leq 1-\delta
and
\eta\in[N^{-3/2+\epsilon},1]
. Optimizing the error term in the rhs. of (
6.47
) over
t
, we finish the proof of Proposition
3.6
.

6.3.
Proof of Proposition
3.7

In this section we work in the set-up of Theorem
6.1
. Fix a small
\omega_{*}>0
, take
T:=1
and a possibly
N
-dependent
0<R<N|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}
. First, we show that

(6.48)

\left|\mathrm{Cov}\left(\langle G^{z_{1}}_{t}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{1})\rangle,\langle G^{z_{2}}_{t}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{2})\rangle\right)\right|\lesssim N^{1+\xi}\left(\mathcal{E}_{1}(t,R)+\mathcal{E}_{0}(t)\right)\left(N\eta_{1}+\frac{1}{N\eta_{1}}\right)\left(N\eta_{2}+\frac{1}{N\eta_{2}}\right)

uniformly in
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in(0,1]
,
|z_{1}|,|z_{2}|\leq 1-\delta
,
t\in[N^{-1+\omega_{*}},1]
and
0<R<N|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}
. To prove (
6.48
), we introduce the following notation similar to (
6.30
):

(6.49)

\mathscr{L}_{i}^{z_{l}}=\mathscr{L}_{i}^{z_{l}}(t,\eta):=\frac{\eta}{(\lambda_{i}^{z_{l}}(t))^{2}+\eta^{2}},\quad\mathscr{M}_{i}^{(l)}=\mathscr{M}_{i}^{(l)}(t,\eta):=\frac{\eta}{(\mu_{i}^{(l)}(t))^{2}+\eta^{2}},\quad\text{for}\quad i\in[N],\,l=1,2,

Since
\mu_{i}^{(1)}(t)
is independent of
\mu_{j}^{(2)}(t)
for any
|i|,|j|\leq N
by Theorem
6.1
(ii), we have

(6.50)

\begin{split}\left|\mathrm{Cov}\left(\langle G^{z_{1}}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{1})\rangle,\langle G^{z_{2}}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{2})\rangle\right)\right|\leq&\bigg|\mathrm{Cov}\bigg(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\mathscr{L}^{z_{1}}_{i}-\mathscr{M}^{(1)}_{i}\right),\langle G^{z_{2}}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{2})\rangle\bigg)\bigg|\\
+&\bigg|\mathrm{Cov}\bigg(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{M}^{(1)}_{i},\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\mathscr{L}^{z_{2}}_{i}-\mathscr{M}^{(2)}_{i}\right)\bigg)\bigg|,\end{split}

where we additionally used (
3.25
) to express
\langle G^{z_{l}}\rangle
in terms of
\lambda_{i}^{(l)}
’s, for
l=1,2
. We further focus on the second term in the rhs. of (
6.50
), as the analysis of the first term is identical. From Theorem
6.1
(i) and the averaged single-resolvent local law (
3.34
) we have

(6.51)

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{M}^{(1)}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\langle\Im\widetilde{G}^{0}_{t}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{1})\rangle\lesssim N^{\xi}\left(1+\frac{1}{N\eta_{1}}\right),

for any
\xi>0
with very high probability. Therefore, the second term in the rhs. of (
6.50
) has an upper bound of order

(6.52)

\mathbf{E}\left[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left|\mathscr{L}^{z_{2}}_{i}-\mathscr{M}^{(2)}_{i}\right|\right]\left(1+\frac{1}{N\eta_{1}}\right)N^{\xi}.

Fix a (small)
\xi_{0}>0
. Arguing similarly to (
6.33
)–(
6.34
) and (
6.36
)–(
6.39
), we get

(6.53)

\begin{split}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i>N^{\xi_{0}}}\left|\mathscr{L}_{i}^{z_{2}}-\mathscr{M}_{i}^{(2)}\right|&\lesssim(N\eta_{2})\left(N(\mathcal{E}_{1}(t,R)+\mathcal{E}_{0}(t))+\frac{1}{R}\right),\\
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\leq N^{\xi_{0}}}\mathbf{E}\left|\mathscr{L}_{i}^{z_{2}}-\mathscr{M}_{i}^{(2)}\right|&\lesssim\left(1+\frac{N\left(\mathcal{E}_{1}(t,R)+\mathcal{E}_{0}(t)\right)}{N\eta_{2}}\right)N^{1+4\xi_{0}+\xi}\left(\mathcal{E}_{1}(t,R)+\mathcal{E}_{0}(t)\right),\end{split}

where the first line of (
6.53
) holds with very high probability. The first and the second line of (
6.53
) coincide with (
6.34
) and (
6.39
) modulo the replacement of
\mathcal{E}_{0}
with
\mathcal{E}_{1}+\mathcal{E}_{0}
, and the first line of (
6.53
) features a new term
R^{-1}
compared to (
6.34
), which comes from
i>R
. For these indices (
6.3
) does not hold, so we simply bound

(6.54)

|\lambda_{i}^{z_{2}}(t)-\mu_{i}^{(2)}(t)|\leq\lambda_{i}^{z_{2}}(t)+\mu_{i}^{(2)}(t)\lesssim\frac{|i|}{N},

where in the second bound we argued similarly to the discussion above (
6.33
). However, this additional
R^{-1}
term does not play any role since it is smaller than
N\mathcal{E}_{1}(t,R)
. Combining (
6.50
), (
6.52
), and (
6.53
), we finish the proof of (
6.48
).

Next, similarly to (
6.40
) we show that

(6.55)

\begin{split}&\left|\mathrm{Cov}\left(\langle G^{s_{0},z_{1}}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{1})\rangle,\langle G^{s_{0},z_{2}}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{2})\rangle\right)-\mathrm{Cov}\left(\langle G^{0,z_{1}}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{1})\rangle,\langle G^{0,z_{2}}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{2})\rangle\right)\right|\\
&\quad\lesssim N^{1/2+\xi}s_{0}\left(1+\frac{1}{N(\eta_{1}\wedge\eta_{2})}\right)^{3}\frac{1}{N^{2}\eta_{1}\eta_{2}},\end{split}

for any fixed
\xi>0
, uniformly in
s_{0}\in[0,1]
,
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in[N^{-3/2+\epsilon},1]
and
z_{1},z_{2}\in(1-\delta)\mathbf{D}
. The proof of (
6.55
) is standard and thus is omitted. Finally, we combine (
6.48
) and (
6.55
) in the way similar to (
6.46
)–(
6.47
) and complete the proof of Proposition
3.7
.

7.
Chaos expansion for covariance: Proof of Proposition
3.4

Unlike in the proof of Proposition
4.2
, no dynamics is used in the proof of Proposition
3.4
. By this we mean that throughout the argument the random matrix
X
and the parameters
z_{1},z_{2},w_{1},w_{2}
satisfying assumptions of Proposition
3.4
remain unchanged. We denote
G_{l}:=G^{z_{l}}(w_{l})
for
l=1,2
, and set

(7.1)

\eta_{*}:=\eta_{1}\wedge\eta_{2}\wedge 1.

Since in Proposition
3.4
we assume that
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\leq 1
, here
\eta_{*}
simply equals to
\eta_{1}\wedge\eta_{2}
. Nevertheless, we present the definition of
\eta_{*}
in the form (
7.1
) to be consistent with (
4.10
). In the proof of Proposition
3.4
we frequently use that
\widehat{\beta}_{12}\sim\gamma
by Proposition
4.4
, where
\widehat{\beta}_{12}
and
\gamma
are defined in (
4.8
) and (
3.36
), respectively. For brevity, the reference to Proposition
4.4
is often omitted. Moreover, whenever
\widehat{\beta}_{12}
and
\gamma
appear in the proof with suppressed arguments, they are meant to be evaluated at
z_{1},z_{2},w_{1},w_{2}
.

First, we formulate the following version of Proposition
3.4
for matrices with a Gaussian component.

Proposition 7.1
.

Assume that
X
satisfies conditions of Proposition
4.2
for some fixed
b\in[0,1]
. Fix (small)
\delta,\epsilon,\kappa,\xi>0
and recall the notations introduced in Proposition
3.4
. Then, we have

(7.2)

\mathrm{Cov}\left(\langle G^{z_{1}}(w_{1})\rangle,\langle G^{z_{2}}(w_{2})\rangle\right)=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\cdot\frac{V_{12}+\kappa_{4}U_{1}U_{2}}{2}+\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\frac{1}{N(\gamma\wedge N^{-b}+\eta_{*})}+N^{-1/2}\right)\frac{N^{\xi}}{N^{2}\eta_{1}\eta_{2}}\right),

uniformly in
|z_{l}|\leq 1-\delta
,
E_{l}\in\mathbf{B}_{\kappa}^{z_{l}}
and
\eta_{l}\in[N^{-1+\epsilon},1]
, for
l=1,2
. In the Gaussian case the error
N^{-1/2}
in (
7.2
) can be removed.

Now we prove Proposition
3.4
relying on Proposition
7.1
.

Proof of Proposition
3.4
.

Let
X
be a general i.i.d. matrix satisfying assumptions of Proposition
7.1
. We embed
X
into the matrix-valued Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process (
6.6
) with
X^{0}:=X
, and recall the notation
G^{t,z}(w)
for the resolvent of Hermitization of
X^{t}-z
, introduced below (
6.8
). Fix
b\in[0,1]
and take
t_{b}:=N^{-b}
. As it follows from (
6.7
),
X^{t_{b}}
contains a Gaussian component of order
N^{-b}
, so it satisfies assumptions of Proposition
7.1
. We apply (
7.2
) to
X^{t_{b}}
and note that the first term in the rhs. of (
7.2
) does not depend on
t_{b}
, so it coincides with the first term in the rhs. of (
3.35
). Therefore, (
3.35
) holds for
X
with the error term of order

(7.3)

\begin{split}&\left(\frac{1}{N(\gamma\wedge N^{-b}+\eta_{*})}+N^{-1/2}\right)\frac{N^{\xi}}{N^{2}\eta_{1}\eta_{2}}\\
&+\left|\mathrm{Cov}\left(\langle G^{t,z_{1}}(w_{1})\rangle,\langle G^{t,z_{2}}(w_{2})\rangle\right)-\mathrm{Cov}\left(\langle G^{0,z_{1}}(w_{1})\rangle,\langle G^{0,z_{2}}(w_{2})\rangle\right)\right|.\end{split}

Arguing similarly to (
6.55
) (see also the proof of (
6.40
)), we get that the term in the second line of (
7.3
) has an upper bound of order

(7.4)

N^{1/2-b}\frac{N^{\xi}}{N^{2}\eta_{1}\eta_{2}}.

Finally, we optimize over
b\in[0,1]
the sum of the terms in the first line of (
7.3
) and in (
7.4
), which leads to the choice
b:=3/4
. This finishes the proof of Proposition
3.4
.
∎

The remainder of this section is devoted to the proof of Proposition
7.1
. To simplify presentation, we focus on the case
b=0
, which simplifies the error term in the rhs. of (
7.2
) to

\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\frac{1}{N\gamma}+N^{-1/2}\right)\frac{N^{\xi}}{N^{2}\eta_{1}\eta_{2}}\right).

However, this does not lead to any loss of generality, since the only adjustment of the proof below needed for the general case
b\in[0,1]
is that the local law from Proposition
4.2
is applied not for
b=1
, but for the given
b
.

The proof of Proposition
7.1
is structured as follows. In Section
7.1
we prove the initial expansion (
3.39
) and derive Proposition
7.1
from a suboptimal bound on the first term in the rhs. of (
3.39
) formulated in Proposition
7.2
. Next, in Section
7.2
we state a hierarchy of so called
master inequalities
for covariances arising along the chaos expansion, which consists in iterative expansions of the first term in the rhs. of (
3.39
). We solve this hierarchy and deduce Proposition
7.2
from it. Finally, in Section
7.2
we prove master inequalities.

7.1.
Initial expansion

We derive (
7.2
) from the following bound on the first term in the rhs. of (
3.39
) and prove (
3.39
) along the way.

Proposition 7.2
.

Assume the set-up and conditions of Proposition
7.1
with
b=0
. For any fixed
\xi>0
it holds that

(7.5)

\left|\mathrm{Cov}(\langle G_{1}-M_{1}\rangle\langle(G_{1}-M_{1})M_{1}\rangle,\langle G_{2}\rangle)\right|\lesssim\left(\frac{1}{N\gamma}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\right)\frac{N^{\xi}}{N^{2}\eta_{*}\eta_{2}},

where
M_{1}=M^{z_{1}}(w_{1})
,
G_{l}=G^{z_{l}}(w_{l})
for
l=1,2
, and
\eta_{*}
is defined in (
7.1
).

The proof of Proposition
7.2
is postponed to Section
7.2
. As we have mentioned below (
3.39
), the averaged single-resolvent local law (
4.1
) implies that the lhs. of (
7.5
) has an upper bound of order
(N^{3}\eta_{1}^{2}\eta_{2})^{-1}
, which is sharper than (
7.5
) already for
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}>N^{-1/2}
. However, our main regime of interest is when
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\sim N^{-1+\epsilon}
for a small
\epsilon>0
, where (
7.5
) improves upon the local law bound by a factor
\eta_{*}/\gamma+\sqrt{N}\eta_{*}
, which is much smaller than 1 already for
|z_{1}-z_{2}|\gg N^{-1/2}
. The estimate (
7.5
) is sufficient for the proof of Proposition
7.1
, as we now demonstrate, so we do not pursue optimality in Proposition
7.2
.

Proof of Proposition
7.1
.

We start with the proof of (
3.39
). It holds that

(7.6)

\langle G_{1}-M_{1}\rangle=\langle G_{1}-M_{1}\rangle\langle(G_{1}-M_{1})A\rangle-\langle\underline{WG_{1}}A\rangle,\quad A=\left(\left(\mathcal{B}^{*}_{11}\right)^{-1}[E_{+}]\right)^{*}M_{1},

where
\mathcal{B}^{*}_{11}
is defined with respect to the scalar product
\langle R,S\rangle:=\langle R^{*}S\rangle
for
R,S\in\mathbf{C}^{(2N)\times(2N)}
. This identity can be easily derived from (
3.37
) and (
3.29
), for a detailed derivation see
[
33
, Eq.(6.9)]
. An explicit calculation based on (
4.7
) shows that

(7.7)

\left(\mathcal{B}^{*}_{11}\right)^{-1}[R]=R+\frac{\langle M^{*}_{1}RM^{*}_{1}\rangle}{1-\langle(M^{*}_{1})^{2}\rangle}-\frac{\langle M^{*}_{1}RM^{*}_{1}E_{-}\rangle}{1+\langle(M^{*}_{1}E_{-})^{2}\rangle}E_{-},\quad\forall R\in\mathbf{C}^{(2N)\times(2N)}.

Therefore,
A=(1-\langle M_{1}^{2}\rangle)^{-1}M_{1}
and, in particular,
\|A\|\lesssim 1
. Using (
7.6
) we express the lhs. of (
3.35
) as follows:

(7.8)

\mathrm{Cov}(\langle G_{1}\rangle,\langle G_{2}\rangle)=\mathrm{Cov}\left(\langle G_{1}-M_{1}\rangle\langle(G_{1}-M_{1})A\rangle,\langle G_{2}\rangle\right)-\mathbf{E}\left[\langle\underline{WG_{1}}A\rangle\left(\langle G_{2}\rangle-\mathbf{E}\langle G_{2}\rangle\right)\right].

In the second term in the rhs. of (
7.8
) we extend the underline on the second factor:

(7.9)

\begin{split}\mathbf{E}\left[\langle\underline{WG_{1}}A\rangle\left(\langle G_{2}\rangle-\mathbf{E}\langle G_{2}\rangle\right)\right]&=\mathbf{E}\left[\underline{\langle WG_{1}A\rangle\left(\langle G_{2}\rangle-\mathbf{E}\langle G_{2}\rangle\right)}\right]-\mathbf{E}\widetilde{\mathbf{E}}\left[\langle\widetilde{W}G_{1}A\rangle\langle\widetilde{W}G_{2}^{2}\rangle\right]\\
&=\mathbf{E}\left[\underline{\langle WG_{1}A\rangle\left(\langle G_{2}\rangle-\mathbf{E}\langle G_{2}\rangle\right)}\right]-\frac{1}{4N^{2}}\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm\}}\sigma\langle G_{1}AE_{\sigma}G_{2}^{2}E_{\sigma}\rangle,\end{split}

where
\widetilde{W}
is an independent copy of
W
. To go from the first to the second line in (
7.9
) we additionally used the following simple identity:

(7.10)

\mathbf{E}\langle WR\rangle\langle WS\rangle=\frac{1}{4N^{2}}\left(\langle RE_{+}SE_{+}\rangle-\langle RE_{-}SE_{-}\rangle\right),\quad\forall R,S\in\mathbf{C}^{(2N)\times(2N)}.

Combining (
7.8
) with (
7.9
) we complete the proof of (
3.39
).

We decompose the last term in the first line of (
3.39
) into the deterministic approximation and the fluctuation around it, and represent
G_{2}^{2}
as a contour integral:

(7.11)

\begin{split}&\left\langle G_{1}AE_{\sigma}G_{2}^{2}E_{\sigma}\right\rangle-\left\langle M_{122}^{AE_{\sigma},E_{+}}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{2})E_{\sigma}\right\rangle\\
&\quad=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\mathscr{C}}\frac{1}{(\zeta-w_{2})^{2}}\left(\left\langle G_{1}AE_{\sigma}G_{2}(\zeta)E_{\sigma}\right\rangle-\left\langle M_{12}^{AE_{\sigma}}(w_{1},\zeta)E_{\sigma}\right\rangle\right)\mathrm{d}\zeta,\end{split}

where
\mathscr{C}
is a circle with the center at
w_{2}
and radius
\eta_{2}/2
, and
G_{2}(\zeta)=G^{z_{2}}(\zeta)
. To go from the first to the second line of (
7.11
), we used the standard meta-argument to show that the deterministic approximation also admits the integral representation (for more details on the meta-argument see e.g.
[
78
, Section 2.6]
,
[
24
, Proof of Lemma D.1]
and also the proof of Supplementary Lemma
S2.6
). For
\eta_{2}\sim 1
, the contour
\mathscr{C}
may exit the bulk regime, however for
\zeta\in\mathscr{C}
with
\Re\zeta\notin\mathbf{B}_{\kappa/2}^{z_{2}}
we have
|\Im\zeta|\sim 1
, in which case the fluctuation of the two-resolvent chain in the rhs. of (
7.11
) has an upper bound of order
N^{-1+\xi}
by the global law (for more details see Supplementary Eq. (
S2.6
)). Thus, applying (
4.13
) for
\Re\zeta\in\mathbf{B}_{\kappa/2}^{z_{2}}
and the global law in the complementary regime, we get from (
7.11
) that

(7.12)

\left|\left\langle G_{1}AE_{\sigma}G_{2}^{2}E_{\sigma}\right\rangle-\left\langle M_{122}^{AE_{\sigma},E_{+}}(w_{1},w_{2},w_{2})E_{\sigma}\right\rangle\right|\lesssim\frac{N^{\xi}}{N\eta_{*}\eta_{2}\gamma},

with very high probability for any fixed
\xi>0
. Here we additionally used (
4.10
) and (
4.20
) to show that

\widehat{\beta}_{12}(w_{1},\zeta)\sim\gamma(z_{1},z_{2},w_{1},\zeta)\sim\gamma(z_{1},z_{2},w_{1},w_{2}).

Combining (
3.39
), (
7.5
), and (
7.12
) we conclude that

(7.13)

\begin{split}\mathrm{Cov}\left(\langle G_{1}\rangle,\langle G_{2}\rangle\right)&=\frac{1}{4N^{2}}\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm\}}\sigma\left\langle M_{122}^{AE_{\sigma},E_{+}}E_{\sigma}\right\rangle-\mathbf{E}\left[\underline{\langle WG_{1}A\rangle\left(\langle G_{2}\rangle-\mathbf{E}\langle G_{2}\rangle\right)}\right]\\
&+\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\frac{1}{N\gamma}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\right)\frac{N^{\xi}}{N^{2}\eta_{*}\eta_{2}}\right),\end{split}

where we suppressed the arguments of
M_{122}
for brevity.

We now estimate the contribution from the fully underlined term in (
7.13
). Recall the notations introduced around (
6.42
)–(
6.43
). Using the cumulant expansion (see e.g.
[
56
, Lemma 3.2]
) similarly to (
6.44
), we get

(7.14)

\begin{split}&\mathbf{E}\underline{\langle WG_{1}A\rangle\left(\langle G_{2}\rangle-\mathbf{E}\langle G_{2}\rangle\right)}\\
&\quad=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{a,b}\sum_{\ell=3}^{L}\sum_{\bm{\alpha}\in\{ab,ba\}^{\ell-1}}\frac{\kappa(ab,\bm{\alpha})}{N^{\ell/2}(\ell-1)!}\mathbf{E}\partial_{\bm{\alpha}}\left[\left(G_{1}A\right)_{ba}\left(\langle G_{2}\rangle-\mathbf{E}\langle G_{2}\rangle\right)\right]+\mathcal{O}(N^{-D})\end{split}

for any fixed
D>0
and for some
L\in\mathbf{N}
which depends on
D
, but does not depend on
N
. Here we used that due to the definition of the underline (
3.37
), the second order cumulants are absent in the expansion. In (
7.14
) and throughout the proof, if not specified, summation runs over indices
a\in[1,N],b\in[N+1,2N]
and
a\in[N+1,2N],b\in[1,N]
.

We call the parameter
\ell
in (
7.14
) the order of the corresponding group of terms, and treat separately the terms of order
\ell\geq 5
,
\ell=4
, and
\ell=3
. We start with the high order terms (
\ell\geq 5
). Observe that for any
r\in\mathbf{N}
and
\widehat{\bm{\alpha}}\in\{ab,ba\}^{r}
it holds that

(7.15)

\left|\partial_{\widehat{\bm{\alpha}}}\left(G_{1}A\right)_{ba}\right|\prec 1,\quad\left|\partial_{\widehat{\bm{\alpha}}}\left(\langle G_{2}\rangle-\mathbf{E}\langle G_{2}\rangle\right)\right|\prec\frac{1}{N\eta_{2}}.

Let us prove the first part of (
7.15
), while the second part follows analogously. Performing the differentiation in
\partial_{\widehat{\bm{\alpha}}}\left(G_{1}A\right)_{ba}
we get a sum of at most
r!
terms of the form

(7.16)

(G_{1})_{\beta_{1}}\cdots(G_{1})_{\beta_{r}}(GA)_{\beta_{r+1}}\quad\text{with}\quad\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{r+1}\in\{ab,ba,aa,bb\}.

We decompose each of the factors into the deterministic approximation and the fluctuation around it, estimate the fluctuations from above by
(N\eta_{1})^{-1/2}
by the single-resolvent isoropic local law (
4.2
), and bound
\|M_{1}\|\lesssim 1
. Multiplying the resulting bounds we prove the first part of (
7.15
). From (
7.15
) we immediately conclude that for any fixed
\ell\geq 5
the sum of all
\ell
-th order terms in (
7.14
) has an upper bound of order

\frac{1}{N}N^{2}\frac{1}{N^{\ell/2}}\cdot\frac{1}{N\eta_{2}}N^{\xi}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\cdot\frac{N^{\xi}}{N^{2}\eta_{*}\eta_{2}}.

Now we consider the terms of order
\ell=4
in (
7.14
). Each of these terms contains 3 derivatives, which we distribute according to the Leibniz rule over the factors
\left(G_{1}A\right)_{ba}
and
\langle G_{2}\rangle-\mathbf{E}\langle G_{2}\rangle
. Consider at first the case when all of them hit
\left(G_{1}A\right)_{ba}
. We proceed by a slight refinement of the argument presented around (
7.16
). Performing the differentiation, we arrive to the sum consisting of terms of the form (
7.16
), each multiplied by
\langle G_{2}\rangle-\mathbf{E}\langle G_{2}\rangle
. Decomposing each isotropic factor in the way discussed below (
7.16
), we see that the product of deterministic approximations does not contribute to the expectation of the entire term because of the centered
\langle G_{2}\rangle
factor. Therefore, we gain from at least one fluctuation and get

(7.17)

\left|\mathbf{E}\left[(G_{1})_{\beta_{1}}(G_{1})_{\beta_{2}}(G_{1})_{\beta_{3}}(GA)_{\beta_{4}}(\langle G_{2}\rangle-\mathbf{E}\langle G_{2}\rangle)\right]\right|\prec\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\eta_{1}}}\cdot\frac{1}{N\eta_{2}}\leq\frac{\sqrt{N}}{N^{2}\eta_{*}\eta_{2}},

where we additionally used the single-resolvent averaged local law (
4.1
) to bound the last factor in the lhs. of (
7.17
). Thus, the fourth order terms in (
7.14
) where all three derivatives hit
(G_{1}A)_{ba}
contribute at most
N^{-1/2}(N^{2}\eta_{*}\eta_{2})^{-1}
.

In the case when at least one out of three derivatives in the
\ell=4
term hits
\langle G_{2}\rangle-\mathbf{E}\langle G_{2}\rangle
, all terms produced by the differentiation are of the form

(7.18)

\frac{1}{N}(G_{2}^{2})_{\beta_{1}}(G_{1}A)_{\beta_{2}}G_{\beta_{3}}G_{\beta_{4}}\quad\text{with}\quad\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{4}\in\{ab,ba,aa,bb\},

where each of
G
’s without subscript equals either to
G_{1}
or to
G_{2}
. Again decomposing each of the factors into the deterministic approximation and the fluctuation and arguing as in (
7.17
), we conclude that if at least one factor in (
7.18
) is replaced by its fluctuation, then the entire term is bounded by
N^{1/2}(N^{2}\eta_{*}\eta_{2})^{-1}
, e.g.

(7.19)

\frac{1}{N}\left(G_{2}^{2}-M_{22}^{E_{+}}\right)_{\beta_{1}}(M_{1}A)_{\beta_{2}}M_{\beta_{3}}M_{\beta_{4}}\prec\frac{1}{N}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\eta_{2}}\eta_{2}}\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{N}}{N^{2}\eta_{*}\eta_{2}},

where we used the convention that
M
without a subscript coincides either with
M_{1}
or with
M_{2}
, and employed (
4.29
) for
k=2
in the first bound.

In order to complete the analysis of the 4th order terms in (
7.14
) it remains to compute the contribution from (
7.18
), where each of the factors is replaced by its deterministic counterpart, to the rhs. of (
7.13
). In contrast to (
7.19
), the contribution from the sum of these terms cannot be fully incorporated into the error term in the rhs. of (
7.13
), as it contributes to the leading order in the rhs. of (
3.35
). We start with the identity

(7.20)

N^{-1}(M_{22}^{E_{+}})_{\beta_{1}}(M_{1}A)_{\beta_{2}}M_{\beta_{3}}M_{\beta_{4}}=N^{-1}\partial_{w_{2}}(M_{2})_{\beta_{1}}\partial_{w_{1}}(M_{1})_{\beta_{2}}M_{\beta_{3}}M_{\beta_{4}},

where we computed

(7.21)

M_{22}^{E_{+}}=\frac{M_{2}^{2}}{1-\langle M_{2}^{2}\rangle}=\partial_{w_{2}}M_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad M_{1}A=\frac{M_{1}^{2}}{1-\langle M_{1}^{2}\rangle}=\partial_{w_{1}}M_{1}.

The first identity in (
7.21
) follows from (
4.6
), (
4.7
), and (
3.29
), while the second one is an immediate consequence of (
3.29
) and the explicit formula for
A
given below (
7.7
). If at least one of
\beta_{j}
,
j\in\!\penalty\ [4]
, in (
7.20
) is off-diagonal, then due to the block-constant structure of
M^{z}(w)
, the rhs. of (
7.20
) may be non-zero only for
a\in[N],b=N+a
and
b\in[N],a=N+b
. Therefore, terms of the form (
7.20
) with at least one off-diagonal factor contribute at most
N^{-3}
to the sum in (
7.14
) and thus can be incorporated into the error term in the rhs. of (
7.13
). Here we additionally used that
\|\partial_{w}M^{z}(w)\|\lesssim 1
for
\Re w
in the bulk of
\rho^{z}
.

Consider now the case when all factors in the rhs. of (
7.20
) are diagonal, i.e.
\beta_{j}\in\{aa,bb\}
for
j\penalty\ \!\in\penalty\ \![4]
. Such terms arise in (
7.14
) only when
(G_{1}A)_{ba}
is differentiated once in
w_{ab}
and
\langle G_{2}\rangle-\mathbf{E}\langle G_{2}\rangle
is differentiated both in
w_{ab}
and
w_{ba}
. In this case
\kappa(ab,\bm{\alpha})=\kappa_{4}
and exactly one
M
without a subscript in the rhs. of (
7.20
) equals to
M_{1}
and one to
M_{2}
. Together with the fact that all diagonal entries of
M^{z}(w)
are equal to
m^{z}(w)
by (
3.30
), this gives that the rhs. of (
7.20
) equals to

(7.22)

N^{-1}\left(\partial_{w_{2}}\langle M_{2}\rangle\right)\left(\partial_{w_{1}}\langle M_{1}\rangle\right)\langle M_{1}\rangle\langle M_{2}\rangle=(4N)^{-1}\partial_{w_{1}}\langle M_{1}\rangle^{2}\partial_{w_{2}}\langle M_{2}\rangle^{2}=(2N)^{-1}U_{1}U_{2},

where
U_{1},U_{2}
are defined in (
3.36
). As it is easy to see, this term arises
6N^{2}
times in the rhs. of (
7.14
), and each time it carries the factor
-\kappa_{4}/(3!N^{3})
. Therefore, the deterministic approximations to the 4th order terms in the rhs. of (
7.14
) with all diagonal factors contribute
\kappa_{4}U_{1}U_{2}/(2N^{2})
to the rhs. of (
7.13
).

Finally, we analyze the 3rd order terms (
\ell=3
) in (
7.14
). We will show that their contribution to
\mathrm{Cov}(\langle G_{1}\rangle,\langle G_{2}\rangle)
is negligible and can be incorporated into the error term. Each of the third order terms contains two derivatives. Let us start with the case when both of them hit
(G_{1}A)_{ba}
factor. Performing the differentiation, we arrive to a product of 3 isotropic terms. Counting the number of
a
’s in the indices we see that there is at least one off-diagonal factor in this product. Consider the exemplary case when there is exactly one off-diagonal factor:

(7.23)

\frac{1}{N^{5/2}}\mathbf{E}\left(\sum_{a,b}\frac{\kappa(ab,ab,ba)}{2}(G_{1})_{bb}(G_{1}A)_{ba}(G_{1})_{aa}\right)\left(\langle G_{2}\rangle-\mathbf{E}\langle G_{2}\rangle\right).

Let us restrict the summation to
a\in[1,N]
,
b\in[N+1,2N]
, since the estimates in the regime
a>b
are similar. Decompose resolvents in the diagonal factors as
G_{1}=M_{1}+(G_{1}-M_{1})
. In the term with both deterministic terms we perform two isotropic resummations

\sum_{a=1}^{N}\sum_{b=N+1}^{2N}(M_{1})_{bb}(G_{1}A)_{ba}(M_{1})_{aa}=(G_{1}A)_{\bm{x}\bm{y}},\quad\bm{x},\bm{y}\in\mathbf{C}^{2N},

where
\bm{x}_{b}=(M_{1})_{bb}
for
a\in[N+1,2N]
and
\bm{x}_{b}=0
for
a\leq N
(
\bm{y}
is defined similarly with last
N
being equal to zero). Using the bound
(G_{1}A)_{\bm{x}\bm{y}}\prec N/\eta_{1}
we get

\frac{1}{N^{5/2}}\mathbf{E}\left(\sum_{a=1}^{N}\sum_{b=N+1}^{2N}\frac{\kappa(ab,ab,ba)}{2}(M_{1})_{bb}(G_{1}A)_{ba}(M_{1})_{aa}\right)\left(\langle G_{2}\rangle-\mathbf{E}\langle G_{2}\rangle\right)\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\cdot\frac{N^{\xi}}{N^{2}\eta_{1}\eta_{2}}.

In the case when only one
G_{1}
is replaced by the deterministic counterpart in (
7.23
), we perform one isotropic resummation and estimate the fluctuation factor by the isotropic local law (
4.2
)

\begin{split}&\sum_{a=1}^{N}\sum_{b=N+1}^{2N}(M_{1})_{bb}(G_{1}A)_{ba}(G_{1}-M_{1})_{aa}=\sum_{a=1}^{N}(G_{1}A)_{\bm{x}a}(G_{1}-M_{1})_{aa}\\
&\quad\prec\sum_{a=1}^{N}\left(\left|(M_{1}A)_{\bm{x}a}\right|+\frac{\sqrt{N}}{\sqrt{N\eta_{1}}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\eta_{1}}}\wedge\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}\eta_{1}}\right)\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{N}}{\eta_{1}}.\end{split}

In the case when both
G_{1}
are replaced by the fluctuation (i.e. by
G_{1}-M_{1}
), we use isotropic local law (
4.2
) for these factors and do not perform isotropic resummation. In such a way we conclude that the sum in (
7.23
) has an upper bound of order
N^{-5/2}(\eta_{1}\eta_{2})^{-1}
. The rest of the terms coming from the third order cumulant are estimated in the same way. For more details see
[
33
, Eq. (6.35)-(6.36)]
.

Combining (
7.13
) with the analysis of the cumulant expansion (
7.14
) performed above, we get that

(7.24)

\mathrm{Cov}\left(\langle G_{1}\rangle,\langle G_{2}\rangle\right)=\frac{1}{2N^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm\}}\sigma\left\langle M_{122}^{AE_{\sigma},E_{+}}E_{\sigma}\right\rangle+\kappa_{4}U_{1}U_{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\frac{1}{N\gamma}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\right)\frac{N^{\xi}}{N^{2}\eta_{*}\eta_{2}}\right).

Let us explicitly compute the sum over
\sigma\in\{\pm\}
in the rhs. of (
7.24
). First, for
\sigma\in\{\pm\}
we have

(7.25)

\left\langle M_{122}^{AE_{\sigma},E_{+}}E_{\sigma}\right\rangle=\partial_{w_{2}}\left\langle M_{12}^{AE_{\sigma}}E_{\sigma}\right\rangle=\partial_{w_{2}}\left\langle M_{21}^{E_{\sigma}}AE_{\sigma}\right\rangle=\partial_{w_{2}}\left\langle\mathcal{B}_{21}^{-1}[M_{2}E_{\sigma}M_{1}]AE_{\sigma}\right\rangle.

To get the first two identities in (
7.25
) we used the meta-argument twice, while the last identity follows from (
4.6
). Next, inverting
\mathcal{B}_{21}
on
M_{2}E_{\pm}M_{1}
using (
4.7
), we get

(7.26)

\begin{split}\mathcal{B}_{21}^{-1}[M_{2}E_{+}M_{1}]&=\frac{(1+\langle M_{1}E_{-}M_{2}E_{-}\rangle)M_{2}E_{+}M_{1}-\langle M_{1}E_{-}M_{2}\rangle M_{2}E_{-}M_{1}}{(1+\langle M_{1}E_{-}M_{2}E_{-}\rangle)(1-\langle M_{1}M_{2}\rangle)+\langle M_{1}E_{-}M_{2}\rangle\langle M_{2}E_{-}M_{1}\rangle},\\
\mathcal{B}_{21}^{-1}[M_{2}E_{-}M_{1}]&=\frac{\langle M_{2}E_{-}M_{1}\rangle M_{2}E_{+}M_{1}+(1-\langle M_{1}M_{2}\rangle)M_{2}E_{-}M_{1}}{(1+\langle M_{1}E_{-}M_{2}E_{-}\rangle)(1-\langle M_{1}M_{2}\rangle)+\langle M_{1}E_{-}M_{2}\rangle\langle M_{2}E_{-}M_{1}\rangle}.\end{split}

Combining (
7.25
), (
7.26
), the explicit formula for
A
given below (
7.7
), and the last identity in (
7.21
), we obtain

(7.27)

\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm\}}\!\!\sigma\left\langle M_{122}^{AE_{\sigma},E_{+}}E_{\sigma}\right\rangle\!=\!-\partial_{w_{1}}\partial_{w_{2}}\log\Big((1+\langle M_{1}E_{-}M_{2}E_{-}\rangle)(1-\langle M_{1}M_{2}\rangle)\!+\!\langle M_{1}E_{-}M_{2}\rangle\langle M_{2}E_{-}M_{1}\rangle\Big).

We use (
3.30
) for
M_{1},M_{2}
, and conclude that the rhs. of (
7.27
) equals to
2V_{12}
, where
V_{12}
is defined in (
3.36
). Together with (
7.24
) this verifies (
7.2
) in a slightly weaker version, specifically with
\eta_{1}
being replaced by
\eta_{*}
. Interchanging
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
and performing the same argument as above, we see that (
7.24
) holds also with
\eta_{2}
being replaced by
\eta_{*}
. Since
\eta_{*}\eta_{2}+\eta_{1}\eta_{*}\sim\eta_{1}\eta_{2}
, this finishes the proof of Proposition
7.1
.
∎

7.2.
Hierarchy of covariances: Proof of Proposition
7.2

To prove Proposition
7.2
, we iteratively expand the lhs. of (
7.5
) in a manner analogous to (
3.39
). Since our goal in (
7.5
) is to obtain a size bound, rather than a leading order term, we do not need to compute the leading order terms in the rhs. of these expansions, as it was done e.g. in (
7.20
)–(
7.22
) and (
7.25
)–(
7.27
) above. This allows us to work with a hierarchy of inequalities deduced from the iterative underline expansions instead of more complicated identities obtained by these expansions.

Before formulating this hierarchy of inequalities in Proposition
7.3
, we introduce some notation. For observables
B_{i}\in\mathbf{C}^{(2N)\times(2N)}
,
i\in[k]
, we define

(7.28)

\Delta(\bm{B}):=\langle\left(G_{1}B_{1}G_{1}\cdots B_{k-1}G_{1}-\mathcal{M}[G_{1}B_{1}G_{1}\cdots B_{k-1}G_{1}]\right)B_{k}\rangle,\quad\bm{B}=(B_{1},\ldots,B_{k}),

For brevity, the dependence of
\Delta(\bm{B})
on
z_{1},w_{1}
and
N
is suppressed. Note that
\Delta(\bm{B})
is a fluctuation of a resolvent chain constructed solely from
G_{1}
, while
G_{2}
does not appear in this definition. For a collection of matrix tuples
\bm{B}^{(j)}=\big(B_{1}^{(j)},\ldots,B_{k_{j}}^{(j)}\big)
,
j\in[n]
, we further set

\Delta\left(\overline{\bm{B}}\right)=\Delta\big(\bm{B}^{(1)};\ldots;\bm{B}^{(n)}\big):=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\Delta\big(\bm{B}^{(j)}\big),\quad\overline{\bm{B}}=\big(\bm{B}^{(1)},\ldots,\bm{B}^{(n)}\big).

Here the bar in
\overline{\bm{B}}
is used to distinguish between a vector of matrix tuples and a matrix tuple, which we denote simply by
\bm{B}
. Next, denote

(7.29)

\mathcal{C}\left(\overline{\bm{B}}\right)=\mathcal{C}\big(\bm{B}^{(1)};\ldots;\bm{B}^{(n)}\big):=\mathrm{Cov}\left(\Delta\left(\overline{\bm{B}}\right),\langle G_{2}\rangle\right).

For example, this notation allows us to abbreviate

\mathrm{Cov}(\langle G_{1}\rangle,\langle G_{2}\rangle)=\mathcal{C}(E_{+})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{Cov}\left(\langle G_{1}-M_{1}\rangle\langle(G_{1}-M_{1})M_{1}\rangle,\langle G_{2}\rangle\right)=\mathcal{C}\left(E_{+};M_{1}\right).

In the last identity we denoted
\mathcal{C}\left(E_{+};M_{1}\right):=\mathcal{C}\left((E_{+});(M_{1})\right)
, that is, we omit parentheses in the notation for a matrix tuple whenever the tuple consists of a single matrix. We will further adopt this convention when referring to
\Delta(\overline{\bm{B}})
and
\mathcal{C}(\overline{\bm{B}})
.

To introduce our main control parameters, which are the maxima of
|\mathcal{C}(\overline{\bm{B}})|
over appropriate sets of
\overline{\bm{B}}
, we restrict attention to observables
B\in\{E_{+},E_{-},F,F^{*}\}
, and for

(7.30)

\overline{\bm{B}}=\big(\bm{B}^{(1)},\ldots,\bm{B}^{(n)}\big)\,\,\text{with}\,\,\bm{B}^{(i)}=\big(B^{(i)}_{1},\ldots,B^{(i)}_{k_{i}}\big),i\in[n],\quad\text{denote}\quad\mathfrak{n}(\overline{\bm{B}}):=k_{1}+\ldots+k_{n},

i.e.
\mathfrak{n}(\overline{\bm{B}})
counts the number of matrices in
\overline{\bm{B}}
. For
n,S\in\mathbf{N}
, let
\mathfrak{S}_{n,S}
be the set of all vectors
\overline{\bm{B}}=\big(\bm{B}^{(1)},\ldots,\bm{B}^{(n)}\big)
consisting of
n
matrix tuples with
\mathfrak{n}(\overline{\bm{B}})\leq S
. Our main control parameters are given by

(7.31)

\Psi_{n,S}:=\max\left\{N^{n}\eta_{*}^{\mathfrak{n}(\overline{\bm{B}})}N\eta_{2}\left|\mathcal{C}\left(\overline{\bm{B}}\right)\right|\,:\,\overline{\bm{B}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n,S}\right\}.

In the case when
S<n
or
n\leq 0
, the set
\mathfrak{S}_{n,S}
is empty, and
\Psi_{n,S}
is set to be equal to zero. Note that
S
is an upper bound on the number of
G_{1}
’s in
\mathcal{C}(\overline{\bm{B}})
, and that there is one more resolvent
G_{2}
in the definition of
\mathcal{C}(\overline{\bm{B}})
. The normalization in (
7.31
) is chosen in such a way that for any
\xi>0
we have

(7.32)

\Psi_{n,S}\lesssim N^{\xi}

by the multi-resolvent local laws from Proposition
4.8
.

Now we state the hierarchy of inequalities, which relates the control parameters
\Psi_{n,S}
for different values of
n
and
S
.

Proposition 7.3

(Master inequalities)
.

Assume the set-up and conditions of Proposition
7.1
with
b=0
. Fix a (small)
\xi>0
and denote

(7.33)

\Upsilon=\Upsilon(z_{1},z_{2};\eta_{1},\eta_{2}):=\left(\sqrt{N}+\frac{1}{\gamma}\right)\eta_{*}N^{\xi},

where
\gamma
is defined in (
3.36
). Then for any
n,S\in\mathbf{N}
it holds that

(7.34)

\Psi_{n,S}\lesssim\Psi_{n-2,S-2}+\Psi_{n,S-1}+\frac{1}{N\eta_{*}}\left(\Psi_{n-1,S+1}+\Psi_{n+1,S+1}\right)+\left(1+\bm{1}_{S>n}(N\eta_{*})^{1/2}\right)\Upsilon.

We refer to the inequalities in Proposition
7.3
as
master inequalities
, adopting the terminology from
[
32
]
, where a similar hierarchy
[
32
, Eq. (3.20a), (3.20b)]
was employed to prove the analogues of the multi-resolvent local laws (
4.28
), (
4.29
) for Wigner matrices. The key difference is that in
[
32
]
the hierarchy was used to control the magnitude of fluctuations of a product of resolvents, while now we control the expectation.

The proof of Proposition
7.3
is postponed to Section
7.3
. Having (
7.34
) in hand, we complete the proof of Proposition
7.2
by iterating (
7.34
).

Proof of Proposition
7.2
.

First note that (
7.5
) is equivalent to the bound

(7.35)

\Psi_{2,2}\lesssim\Upsilon.

To prove (
7.35
) we show that for any
n,S\in\mathbf{N}
it holds that

(7.36)

\Psi_{n,S}\lesssim(N\eta_{*})^{1/2}\Upsilon.

Once (
7.36
) is obtained, we apply (
7.34
) to
n=S=2
and then use (
7.36
) for
\Psi_{1,3}
and
\Psi_{3,3}
, which gives

(7.37)

\Psi_{2,2}\lesssim\frac{1}{N\eta_{*}}\left(\Psi_{1,3}+\Psi_{3,3}\right)+\Upsilon\lesssim\frac{1}{N\eta_{*}}(N\eta_{*})^{1/2}\Upsilon+\Upsilon\lesssim\Upsilon,

i.e. (
7.35
) holds.

Now we prove (
7.36
). For
n>S
this bound is a triviality since in this case
\Psi_{n,S}=0
. Hence, whenever convenient, we will assume that
n\leq S
without mentioning this explicitly. We claim that

(7.38)

\Psi_{n,S}\lesssim\frac{1}{N\eta_{*}}\sum_{r=1}^{n+1}\Psi_{r,S+1}+(N\eta_{*})^{1/2}\Upsilon,\quad\forall n,S\in\mathbf{N}.

We derive (
7.38
) from (
7.34
), without exploiting the improvement in the last term in the rhs. of (
7.34
) occurring for
n\leq S
, and simply estimating this term from above by
(N\eta_{*})^{1/2}\Upsilon
.
The proof of (
7.38
) proceeds by induction on
n
and
S
which we now describe. As the base case we consider
n=S=1
, where (
7.38
) directly follows from (
7.34
) applied to
\Psi_{1,1}
. Next we fix
n:=1
and proceed by induction on
S
. To perform the induction step from
S\leq S_{0}
to
S=S_{0}+1
for some
S_{0}\in\mathbf{N}
, we use (
7.34
) for
n=1
and
S=S_{0}+1
:

(7.39)

\begin{split}\Psi_{1,S_{0}+1}&\lesssim\Psi_{1,S_{0}}+\frac{1}{N\eta_{*}}\Psi_{2,S_{0}+2}+(N\eta_{*})^{1/2}\Upsilon\lesssim\frac{1}{N\eta_{*}}\left(\Psi_{1,S_{0}+1}+\Psi_{2,S_{0}+1}+\Psi_{2,S_{0}+2}\right)+(N\eta_{*})^{1/2}\Upsilon\\
&\lesssim\frac{1}{N\eta_{*}}\left(\Psi_{1,S_{0}+2}+\Psi_{2,S_{0}+2}\right)+(N\eta_{*})^{1/2}\Upsilon.\end{split}

Here in the second step we used (
7.38
) for
(n,S_{0})
and to go from the first to the second line we used that
\Psi_{n,S_{1}}\leq\Psi_{n,S_{2}}
, for any
n,S_{1},S_{2}\in\mathbf{N}
with
S_{1}\leq S_{2}
, which follows from (
7.31
). Once (
7.38
) has been established for
n=1
and all
S\in\mathbf{N}
, we fix
n:=2
and repeat the same argument, continuing this process inductively for all
n\in\mathbf{N}
. The induction step from
S\leq S_{0}
to
S=S_{0}+1
for general
n,S\in\mathbf{N}
follows similarly to (
7.39
) from (
7.34
) for
(n,S_{0}+1)
together with (
7.38
) for
\Psi_{n-2,S_{0}-1}
and
\Psi_{n,S_{0}}
, and the monotonicity of
\Psi_{n,S}
in
S
. This completes the verification of (
7.38
).

We use (
7.38
) for
\Psi_{r,S+1}
for all
r\in[n+1]
and combine these bounds with (
7.38
) for
\Psi_{r,S}
, arriving to

(7.40)

\Psi_{n,S}\lesssim\left(\frac{1}{N\eta_{*}}\right)^{k}\sum_{r=1}^{n+k}\Psi_{r,S+k}+(N\eta_{*})^{1/2}\Upsilon.

for
k=2
. Iterating this procedure several times, we obtain (
7.40
) for any fixed
k\in\mathbf{N}
. Finally, we choose
k>1/\epsilon
in (
7.40
) and use the a priori bound (
7.32
) for all
\Psi
terms in the rhs. of (
7.40
). This finishes the proof of (
7.36
) and thereby of Proposition
7.2
.
∎

7.3.
Proof of the master inequalities in Proposition
7.3

In this section we prove Proposition
7.3
, which is the last technical ingredient in the proof of Proposition
3.4
. To do so, we first derive the system of identities similar to (
3.39
), relating the quantities of the form
\mathcal{C}(\overline{\bm{B}})
for different values of
\overline{\bm{B}}
. This is the statement of Lemma
7.4
. Then we prove Lemma
7.4
, and finally turn it into the system of master inequalities, thereby proving Proposition
7.3
.

To streamline the presentation, we introduce some additional short-hand notations. For
n\in\mathbf{N}
and for a set of distinct indices
\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{s}\}\subset[n]
denote

\overline{\bm{B}}^{[i_{1},\ldots,i_{s}]}:=\overline{\bm{B}}\setminus\left\{\bm{B}^{(i_{1})},\ldots,\bm{B}^{(i_{s})}\right\},\quad\text{where}\quad\overline{\bm{B}}=(\bm{B}^{(1)},\ldots,\bm{B}^{(n)}).

We also set

m(\bm{B})=m(B_{1},\ldots,B_{k}):=\langle\mathcal{M}[G_{1}B_{1}G_{1}\cdots B_{k-1}G_{1}]B_{k}\rangle.

Throughout the calculations below, whenever the index
\sigma
appears, it is meant that the corresponding expression is summed over
\sigma\in\{\pm\}
. Additionally we adopt the convention that a summation with a lower limit exceeding the upper one is considered to be empty.

The following statement is the analogue of the initial expansion (
3.39
) for the general covariance
\mathcal{C}(\overline{\bm{B}})
. In its formulation, sequences of matrices of the form
B_{i},B_{i+1},\ldots,B_{j}
frequently appear as arguments of
\mathcal{C}
for some
i,j\in\mathbf{N}\cup\{0\}
. By this notation we mean the sequence of matrices indexed from
i
to
j
when
i<j
, the single matrix
B_{i}
when
i=j
, and the empty sequence when
i>j
.

Lemma 7.4
.

Assume the set-up and assumptions of Proposition
7.1
with
b=0
. Let
\bm{B}^{(j)}=\big(B_{1}^{(j)},\ldots,B_{k_{j}}^{(j)}\big)
for
j\in[n]
be a vector consisting of
(2N)\times(2N)
deterministic matrices. Denote

(7.41)

A=A_{k_{1}}^{(1)}:=\left(\left(\mathcal{B}_{11}^{*}\right)^{-1}\left[\left(B_{k_{1}}^{(1)}\right)^{*}\right]\right)^{*}M_{1}.

Let
{\rm cycl}(k)
be the set of all cyclic permutations of length
k
. Then we have

(7.42)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(\overline{\bm{B}})\!

\displaystyle=\!\frac{\sigma}{4N^{2}}\!\mathbf{E}\left[\langle G_{1}B_{1}^{(1)}G_{1}\cdots G_{1}B_{k_{1}-1}^{(1)}G_{1}AE_{\sigma}G_{2}^{2}E_{\sigma}\rangle\Delta\left(\overline{\bm{B}}^{[1]}\right)\right]\!+\!\mathcal{C}\!\left(\!\left(AB_{1}^{(1)},B_{2}^{(1)},\ldots,B_{k_{1}-1}^{(1)}\right)\!;\overline{\bm{B}}^{[1]}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{k_{1}-1}\sigma m\left(E_{\sigma},B_{1}^{(1)},\ldots,B_{j}^{(1)}\right)\mathcal{C}\left(\left(AE_{\sigma},B_{j+1}^{(1)},\ldots,B_{k_{1}-1}^{(1)}\right);\overline{\bm{B}}^{[1]}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=0}^{k_{1}-2}\sigma m\left(AE_{\sigma},B_{j+1}^{(1)},\ldots,B_{k_{1}-1}^{(1)}\right)\mathcal{C}\left(\left(E_{\sigma},B_{1}^{(1)},\ldots,B_{j}^{(1)}\right);\overline{\bm{B}}^{[1]}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=0}^{k_{1}-1}\sigma\mathcal{C}\left(\left(E_{\sigma},B_{1}^{(1)},\ldots,B_{j}^{(1)}\right);\left(AE_{\sigma},B_{j+1}^{(1)},\ldots,B_{k_{1}-1}^{(1)}\right);\overline{\bm{B}}^{[1]}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4N^{2}}\sum_{i=2}^{n}\sum_{c\in{\rm{cycl}}(k_{i})}\sigma m\left(AE_{\sigma},c(\bm{B}^{(i)}),E_{\sigma},B_{1}^{(1)},\ldots,B_{k_{1}-1}^{(1)}\right)\mathcal{C}\left(\overline{\bm{B}}^{[1,i]}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4N^{2}}\sum_{i=2}^{n}\sum_{c\in{\rm{cycl}}(k_{i})}\sigma\mathcal{C}\left(\left(AE_{\sigma},c(\bm{B}^{(i)}),E_{\sigma},B_{1}^{(1)},\ldots,B_{k_{1}-1}^{(1)}\right);\overline{\bm{B}}^{[1,i]}\right)

\displaystyle-\mathbf{E}\left[\underline{\langle AWG_{1}B_{1}^{(1)}G_{1}\cdots G_{1}B_{k_{1}-1}^{(1)}G_{1}\rangle\Delta\left(\overline{\bm{B}}^{[1]}\right)\left(\langle G_{2}\rangle-\mathbf{E}\langle G_{2}\rangle\right)}\right].

We choose to invert
\mathcal{B}_{11}
on
\big(B_{k_{1}}^{(1)}\big)^{*}
in Lemma
7.4
only for definiteness, while a similar expansion holds with
B_{k_{1}}^{(1)}
replaced in (
7.41
) by
B_{i}^{(l)}
for any
l\in[n]
and
i\in[k_{l}]
.

Before delving into the proof of Lemma
7.4
, let us demonstrate (
7.42
) with some simple examples. For
n=1
and
k_{1}=1
only a few terms in the rhs. of (
7.42
) are left: the first term in the rhs. of the first line,
j=0
term in the fourth line, and the fully underlined term in the last line. Thus, (
7.42
) implies that

\mathcal{C}(B)=\frac{\sigma}{4N^{2}}\mathbf{E}\langle G_{1}AE_{\sigma}G_{2}^{2}E_{\sigma}\rangle+\mathcal{C}(E_{+};A)-\mathbf{E}\underline{\langle WG_{1}A\rangle\left(\langle G_{2}\rangle-\mathbf{E}\langle G_{2}\rangle\right)},

which generalizes (
3.39
) formulated in the spacial case
B=E_{+}
. Here we additionally used that
\langle G_{1}E_{-}\rangle=0
, so the
\sigma=-
term in the fourth line of (
7.42
) vanishes. Meanwhile, for
n=2
and
\bm{B}^{(1)}
,
\bm{B}^{(2)}
consisting of single matrices
B^{(1)}
and
B^{(2)}
, (
7.42
) is of the form

(7.43)

\begin{split}\mathcal{C}(B^{(1)};B^{(2)})&=\frac{\sigma}{4N^{2}}\mathbf{E}\langle G_{1}AE_{\sigma}G_{2}^{2}E_{\sigma}\rangle\langle(G_{1}-M_{1})B^{(2)}\rangle+\mathcal{C}(E_{+};A;B^{(2)})\\
&+\frac{\sigma}{4N^{2}}\mathcal{C}(AE_{\sigma},B^{(2)},E_{\sigma})-\mathbf{E}\underline{\langle WG_{1}A\rangle\langle(G_{1}-M_{1})B^{(2)}\rangle\left(\langle G_{2}\rangle-\mathbf{E}\langle G_{2}\rangle\right)},\end{split}

where the first term in the rhs. of (
7.43
) comes from the first term in the rhs. of (
7.42
), while the second and third terms come from the
j=0
term in the fourth line of (
7.42
) and
i=2
term in the sixth line of (
7.42
), respectively.

Proof of Lemma
7.4
.

At first we prove (
7.42
) for
n=1
. Denote for brevity
\bm{B}^{(1)}=\bm{B}=(B_{1},\ldots,B_{k})
,
G:=G_{1}
and
M:=M_{1}
. The case
k=1
with
B_{1}=E_{+}
is already covered by (
7.6
)–(
7.9
), and for general
B_{1}
the argument is identical, so we further focus on the case
k\geq 2
. From
[
33
, Eq.(5.2)]
and the fact that
\mathcal{S}[G-M]=\langle G-M\rangle
(see also Lemma
7.5
later) we have

(7.44)

G=M-M\underline{WG}+M\langle G-M\rangle G.

We multiply this identity on the right by
B_{1}G\cdots B_{k-1}G
and in the term containing
\underline{WG}
extend the underline onto the entire product using (
3.37
):

(7.45)

\begin{split}GB_{1}G\cdots B_{k-1}G&=MB_{1}G\cdots B_{k-1}G+M\langle G-M\rangle GB_{1}G\cdots B_{k-1}G\\
&+M\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\mathcal{S}[GB_{1}G\cdots B_{j}G]GB_{j+1}G\cdots B_{k-1}G-M\underline{WGB_{1}G\cdots GB_{k-1}G}.\end{split}

Next, in the
j=k-1
term in (
7.45
) we decompose the last
G
into
G-M
and
M
, and move the term containing
M
to the lhs. of this identity, obtaining
\mathcal{B}_{11}[GB_{1}G\cdots B_{k-1}G]
. Multiplying the resulting identity from the left by
AM^{-1}
with
A
defined in (
7.41
), and taking the normalized trace we get

(7.46)

\begin{split}&\langle GB_{1}G\cdots B_{k-1}GB_{k}\rangle=\langle AB_{1}G\cdots B_{k-1}G\rangle+\langle G-M\rangle\langle GAGB_{1}\cdots GB_{k-1}\rangle\\
&\quad+\sum_{j=1}^{k-2}\sigma\langle GE_{\sigma}GB_{1}\cdots GB_{j}\rangle\langle GAE_{\sigma}GB_{j+1}\cdots GB_{k-1}\rangle\\
&\quad+\sigma\langle GE_{\sigma}GB_{1}\cdots GB_{k-1}\rangle\langle(G-M)AE_{\sigma}\rangle-\langle A\underline{WGB_{1}G\cdots GB_{k-1}G}\rangle.\end{split}

We subtract the deterministic approximations from both sides of (
7.46
) using (
4.26
) and arrive to the following underline expansion for
\Delta(\bm{B})
:

(7.47)

\displaystyle\Delta(\bm{B})

\displaystyle=\Delta(AB_{1},B_{2},\ldots,B_{k-1})-\langle A\underline{WGB_{1}G\cdots GB_{k-1}G}\rangle

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\sigma m(E_{\sigma},B_{1},\ldots,B_{j})\Delta(AE_{\sigma},B_{j+1},\ldots,B_{k-1})

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=0}^{k-2}\sigma m(AE_{\sigma},B_{j+1},\ldots,B_{k-1})\Delta(E_{\sigma},B_{1},\ldots,B_{j})

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\sigma\Delta(E_{\sigma},B_{1},\ldots,B_{j})\Delta(AE_{\sigma},B_{j+1},\ldots,B_{k-1}).

To derive (
7.42
) from (
7.47
), we multiply (
7.47
) by
\langle G_{2}\rangle-\mathbf{E}\langle G_{2}\rangle
and then take the expectation. In this way, the first term in the rhs. of (
7.47
) becomes the second term in the rhs. of (
7.42
), while the sums in the second, third and fourth lines of (
7.47
) become the sums in the corresponding lines of (
7.42
). The terms in the fifth and sixth lines of (
7.42
) are absent for
n=1
, and the remaining term in the first line of (
7.42
) arises from extending the second order renormalization in the second term in the rhs. of (
7.47
) to the additional factor
\langle G_{2}\rangle-\mathbf{E}\langle G_{2}\rangle
. This extension is performed similarly to (
7.9
) by the means of (
7.10
).

In the case
n\geq 2
we multiply at first (
7.47
) by
\Delta\big(\overline{\bm{B}}^{[1]}\big)
and then take covariance with
\langle G_{2}\rangle
. The rest follows by a straightforward calculation.
∎

Proof of Proposition
7.3
.

Throughout the proof we fix
n,S\in\mathbf{N}
with
n\leq S
, in the complementary regime
\Psi_{n,S}=0
and (
7.34
) is trivial. Recall the definition of
\mathfrak{S}_{n,S}
introduced above (
7.31
). To prove (
7.34
) one needs to show that properly rescaled
|\mathcal{C}^{\rm{av}}(\overline{\bm{B}})|
is bounded by the rhs. of (
7.34
) for any
\overline{\bm{B}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n,S}
. We further focus on the critical case when
\overline{\bm{B}}
contains exactly
S
matrices, for
\overline{\bm{B}}
with less than
S
matrices estimates are simpler and thus are omitted.

We begin with the following simple bound:

(7.48)

\|(\mathcal{B}_{11}^{*})^{-1}[R]\|\lesssim 1\,\,\text{for}\,\,\ R\in\{E_{+},F,F^{*}\},

which immediately follows from (
7.7
) and
[
23
, Eq.(3.1),(3.4)]
(see also the justification of applicability of
[
23
]
around Supplementary Eq. (
S2.12
)).
In contrast to (
7.48
),
\|(\mathcal{B}_{11}^{*})^{-1}[E_{-}]\|
may be large, e.g. (
7.7
) together with (
3.30
) imply that this norm scales as
\eta_{1}^{-1}
for
w_{1}=\mathrm{i}\eta_{1}
with
\eta_{1}\ll 1
. While the exact size of
\|(\mathcal{B}_{11}^{*})^{-1}[E_{-}]\|
does not matter for our proof, its possible blow-up for small
\eta_{1}
makes it necessary to distinguish between the cases when all matrices in
\overline{\bm{B}}
coincide with
E_{-}
and when at least one of them differs from
E_{-}
, since the inversion of
\mathcal{B}_{11}^{*}
acting on one of the matrices from
\overline{\bm{B}}
naturally appears in (
7.41
).

Consider at first the case when each matrix in
\overline{\bm{B}}
equals to
E_{-}
. We do not use Lemma
7.4
in this case, instead, our proof relies on the following identities.

Lemma 7.5
.

For any
n\in\mathbf{N}
and for
G=G^{z}(w)
with
z\in\mathbf{C}
and
w\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}
, it holds that

(7.49)

\displaystyle\langle(GE_{-})^{2n-1}\rangle

\displaystyle=0,

(7.50)

\displaystyle\langle(GE_{-})^{2n}\rangle

\displaystyle=2\sum_{s=0}^{n-1}(-1)^{s+1}\binom{2n-2-s}{n-1}\frac{\langle G^{s+1}\rangle}{(2w)^{2n-s-1}}.

Moreover, (
7.49
)–(
7.50
) remain valid after taking the deterministic approximations to all quantities appearing in these identities.

Proof of Lemma
7.5
.

We start with the proof of (
7.49
). Inverting the
2\times 2
-block matrix
W-Z-w
defined in (
3.18
) we get

(7.51)

G\!=\!\begin{pmatrix}w\widetilde{H}_{z}(w)&X_{z}H_{z}(w)\\
H_{z}(w)X_{z}^{*}&wH_{z}(w)\end{pmatrix}\!\!,\,\,\text{where}\,\,X_{z}\!:=X-z,\,H_{z}(w)\!:=\!(X_{z}^{*}X_{z}-w^{2})^{-1}\!\!,\,\widetilde{H}_{z}(w)\!:=\!(X_{z}X_{z}^{*}-w^{2})^{-1}\!\!.

Here the matrices
X_{z}^{*}X_{z}-w^{2}
and
X_{z}X_{z}^{*}-w^{2}
are invertible, since either
\Re w=0
, in which case
-w^{2}=(\Im w)^{2}>0
, or
\Re w\neq 0
, which implies
\Im(w^{2})\neq 0
. Denote additionally
\widetilde{G}:=G^{z}(-w)
. From (
7.51
) and the symmetry relations
\widetilde{H}_{z}(w)=\widetilde{H}_{z}(-w)
and
H_{z}(w)=H_{z}(-w)
we have that

(7.52)

GE_{-}=-E_{-}\widetilde{G}.

Thus, the lhs. of (
7.49
) can be written as

(7.53)

\begin{split}\langle(GE_{-})^{2n-1}\rangle&=\langle(GE_{-}GE_{-})^{n-1}GE_{-}\rangle=(-1)^{n-1}\langle(G\widetilde{G})^{n-1}GE_{-}\rangle\\
&=(-1)^{n-1}(2w)^{-n+1}\langle(G-\widetilde{G})^{n-1}GE_{-}\rangle=w\langle(\widetilde{H}_{z}(w))^{n}-(H_{z}(w))^{n}\rangle.\end{split}

To go from the first to the second line we employed the resolvent identity and in the last identity we used (
7.51
). Observing additionally that
\widetilde{H}_{z}(\eta)
and
H_{z}(\eta)
have identical spectra, we conclude that the rhs. of (
7.53
) equals to zero. This finishes the proof of (
7.49
).

To prove (
7.50
), we compute similarly to (
7.53
) that

(7.54)

\langle(GE_{-})^{2n}\rangle=(-1)^{n}\langle(G\widetilde{G})^{n}\rangle=(-1)^{n}\langle G^{n}\widetilde{G}^{n}\rangle=-\frac{\partial_{w_{1}}^{n-1}\partial_{w_{2}}^{n-1}\langle G^{z}(w_{1})G^{z}(-w_{2})\rangle\big|_{w_{1}=w_{2}=w}}{((n-1)!)^{2}}.

Combining the resolvent identity with (
7.52
) we get

(7.55)

\langle G^{z}(w_{1})G^{z}(-w_{2})\rangle=\frac{\langle G^{z}(w_{1})\rangle-\langle G^{z}(-w_{2})\rangle}{w_{1}+w_{2}}=\frac{\langle G^{z}(w_{1})\rangle}{w_{1}+w_{2}}+\frac{\langle G^{z}(w_{2})\rangle}{w_{1}+w_{2}}.

Finally, we differentiate the first and the second term in the rhs. of (
7.55
)
n-1
times in
w_{2}
and
w_{1}
, respectively, and combine the result with (
7.54
):

\langle(GE_{-})^{2n}\rangle=\frac{(-1)^{n}2}{(n-1)!}\partial_{y}^{n-1}\frac{\langle G^{z}(y)\rangle}{(y+w)^{n}}\big|_{y=w}=\frac{2}{(n-1)!}\sum_{s=0}^{n-1}(-1)^{s+1}\frac{(2n-2-s)!}{(n-1-s)!}\cdot\frac{\langle G^{s+1}\rangle}{(2w)^{2n-1-s}}.

This finishes the proof of (
7.50
).

The proof of the analogues of (
7.49
) and (
7.50
) for the deterministic approximations follows by a standard meta-argument and thus is omitted.
∎

If
\overline{\bm{B}}
has at least one block of odd size, then
\mathcal{C}^{\rm{av}}(\overline{\bm{B}})
equals to zero by (
7.49
). Assume now that each block has even size. Let
2k
be the size of the first block. Applying (
7.50
) to this block we get

\left|\mathcal{C}\left(\overline{\bm{B}}\right)\right|\lesssim\sum_{r=0}^{k-1}\eta_{1}^{-2k+r+1}\left|\mathcal{C}\left((E_{+}\times(r+1));\overline{\bm{B}}^{[1]}\right)\right|,

where
(E_{+}\times(r+1))
is a block consisting of
r+1
matrices
E_{+}
. Combined with (
7.31
), this bound leads to

N^{n+1}\eta^{S}_{*}\eta_{2}\left|\mathcal{C}\left(\overline{\bm{B}}\right)\right|\!\lesssim\!\sum_{r=0}^{k-1}N^{n+1}\eta_{*}^{S-2k+r+1}\eta_{2}\left|\mathcal{C}\left((E_{+}\times(r+1));\overline{\bm{B}}^{[1]}\right)\right|\!\leq\!\sum_{r=0}^{k-1}\Psi_{n,S-2k+r+1}\!\lesssim\!\Psi_{n,S-1},

which is the desired bound on the rescaled covariance
\mathcal{C}(\overline{\bm{B}})
.

Consider now the case when at least one matrix in
\overline{\bm{B}}
is different from
E_{-}
. Without loss of generality one may assume that
B^{(1)}_{k_{1}}\neq E_{-}
. We apply (
7.42
) to
\mathcal{C}\left(\overline{\bm{B}}\right)
and note that
\|A\|\lesssim 1
by (
7.48
). For the first term in the rhs. of (
7.42
) we have the following bound

(7.56)

\begin{split}&N^{n}\eta^{S}_{*}N\eta_{2}\frac{1}{4N^{2}}\left|\langle G_{1}B_{1}^{(1)}G_{1}\cdots G_{1}B_{k_{1}-1}^{(1)}G_{1}AE_{\sigma}G_{2}^{2}E_{\sigma}\rangle\Delta\left(\overline{\bm{B}}^{[1]}\right)\right|\prec\left(1+\bm{1}_{k_{1}>1}(N\eta_{*})^{1/2}\right)\frac{\eta_{*}}{\gamma}.\end{split}

The proof of (
7.56
) relies on the local laws from Propositions
4.2
and
4.8
, and is presented in Supplementary Section
S4.3
. Clearly, the rhs. of (
7.56
) is bounded by the last term in the rhs. of (
7.34
) for
n<S
. Moreover, this bound also holds in the case
n=S
, since then each block of
\overline{\bm{B}}
consists of a single matrix, so we have
k_{1}=1
.

The second term in the first line of (
7.42
) is present only for
k_{1}\geq 2
, in which case it contains
n
blocks and
S-1
observables. Therefore,

N^{n}\eta_{*}^{S}N\eta_{2}\left|\mathcal{C}\left(\left(AB_{1}^{(1)},B_{2}^{(1)},\ldots,B_{k_{1}-1}^{(1)}\right);\overline{\bm{B}}^{[1]}\right)\right|\leq\eta_{*}\Psi_{n,S-1}

by the definition of
\Psi_{n,S-1}
(
7.31
). In the second line of (
7.42
) we estimate by (
4.27
) that

\left|m\left(B_{1}^{(1)},\ldots,B_{j}^{(1)},E_{\sigma}\right)\right|\lesssim\eta^{-j}_{*},

and counting powers of
N
and
\eta_{*}
conclude that the second line contributes at most
\Psi_{n,S-1}
. Similarly, the contribution from the third line is bounded by
\Psi_{n,S-1}
. In the fourth line the numbers of blocks and observables equal to
n+1
and
S+1
, respectively, so the corresponding sum is bounded by
(N\eta_{*})^{-1}\Psi_{n+1,S+1}
. The sixth line is only present for
n\geq 3
, in which case it contributes at most
\Psi_{n-2,S-2}
. Finally, we see that the last but one line in (
7.42
) is bounded by
(N\eta_{*})^{-1}\Psi_{n-1,S+1}
.

In order to conclude the proof of Proposition
7.3
it is left to estimate the fully underlined term in the last line of (
7.42
). Performing the cumulant expansion as in (
7.14
), we get that this term equals to

(7.57)

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{a,b}\sum_{\ell=3}^{L}\sum_{\bm{\alpha}\in\{ab,ba\}^{\ell-1}}\frac{\kappa(ab,\bm{\alpha})}{(\ell-1)!N^{\ell/2}}\mathbf{E}\partial_{\bm{\alpha}}\left[\left(G_{1}B_{1}^{(1)}G_{1}\cdots G_{1}B_{k_{1}-1}^{(1)}G_{1}A\right)_{ba}\!\!\Delta\left(\overline{\bm{B}}^{[1]}\right)\left(\langle G_{2}\rangle-\mathbf{E}\langle G_{2}\rangle\right)\right]

up to an error term of order
N^{-D}
, for any (large) fixed
D>0
. In contrast to the analysis of (
7.14
) in the proof of Proposition
3.4
, we do not distinguish between the terms with
\ell\geq 5
,
\ell=4
and
\ell=3
, but treat all these cases in the same robust way. Similarly to (
7.15
), observe that for any
r\in\mathbf{N}
and
\widehat{\bm{\alpha}}\in\{ab,ba\}^{r}
it holds that

(7.58)

\left|\partial_{\widehat{\bm{\alpha}}}\left(G_{1}B_{1}^{(1)}G_{1}\cdots G_{1}B_{k_{1}-1}^{(1)}G_{1}A\right)_{ba}\right|\prec\frac{1}{\eta_{1}^{k_{1}-1}},

(7.59)

\left|\partial_{\widehat{\bm{\alpha}}}\Delta\left(\bm{B}^{(j)}\right)\right|\prec\frac{1}{N\eta_{1}^{k_{j}}},\qquad\left|\partial_{\widehat{\bm{\alpha}}}\left(\langle G_{2}\rangle-\mathbf{E}\langle G_{2}\rangle\right)\right|\prec\frac{1}{N\eta_{2}}.

The proof of (
7.58
)–(
7.59
) is analogous to the argument around (
7.16
) and relies of the local laws from Proposition
4.8
and on the deterministic estimate from Proposition
4.7
.
Applying (
7.58
)–(
7.59
) to (
7.57
) we obtain that for any
\ell\geq 3
the sum of all terms of order
\ell
in (
7.57
) has an upper bound of order

\frac{1}{N}N^{2}\frac{1}{N^{\ell/2}}\cdot\frac{1}{\eta_{1}^{k_{1}-1}}\left(\prod_{j=2}^{n}\frac{1}{N\eta_{1}^{k_{j}}}\right)\frac{1}{N\eta_{2}}N^{\xi}=\frac{1}{N^{n}\eta_{*}^{S}N\eta_{2}}\cdot\frac{\eta_{*}}{N^{\ell/2-2}}N^{\xi}\leq\frac{1}{N^{n}\eta_{*}^{S}N\eta_{2}}\Upsilon.

Therefore, the term in the last line of (
7.42
) multiplied by
N^{n}\eta_{*}^{S}N\eta_{2}
is bounded by the last term in the rhs. of (
7.34
). This finishes the proof of Proposition
7.3
.
∎

Appendix A
Additional analysis of the matrix Dyson equation

A.1.
2-body stability analysis: Proof of Proposition
4.4

We begin by introducing the notation used throughout this section. Unless stated otherwise, we work under the assumptions
z_{1},z_{2}\in(1-\delta)\mathbf{D}
and
w_{1},w_{2}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}
. We denote
E_{j}:=\Re w_{j}
,
\eta_{j}:=|\Im w_{j}|
and
\eta_{*}:=\eta_{1}\wedge\eta_{2}\wedge 1
,
j=1,2
. Whenever a statement involves index
j
, it is understood to hold for
j=1,2
. We further recall the notations introduced in (
3.30
), (
3.31
) and set
M_{j}:=M^{z_{j}}(w_{j})
,
m_{j}:=m^{z_{j}}(w_{j})
and
u_{j}:=u^{z_{j}}(w_{j})
. Finally, we denote

(A.1)

\begin{split}\beta_{12,\pm}=\beta_{12,\pm}(w_{1},w_{2}):&=1-\Re\left[z_{1}\overline{z}_{2}\right]u_{1}u_{2}\pm\sqrt{m_{1}^{2}m_{2}^{2}-\left(\Im\left[z_{1}\overline{z}_{2}\right]\right)^{2}u_{1}^{2}u_{2}^{2}},\\
\beta_{12,*}=\beta_{12,*}(w_{1},w_{2}):&=\min\{|\beta_{12,+}|,|\beta_{12,-}|\},\end{split}

It is known from
[
35
, Appendix B]
that
\beta_{12,\pm}
are the eigenvalues of the two-body stability operator
\mathcal{B}_{12}
defined in (
4.7
), while the remaining two eigenvalues equal to 1, see e.g.
[
35
, Appendix B]
.

The crucial step in the proof of Proposition
4.4
is to reduce the analysis of
\widehat{\beta}_{12}
to that of
\beta_{12,*}
defined in (
A.1
). The latter quantity is much more accessible due its definition by the explicit formula. An elementary calculation based on the explicit inversion of
\mathcal{B}_{12}
gives that

(A.2)

\widehat{\beta}_{12}(w_{1},w_{2})\sim\min\left\{\beta_{12,*}\big(w_{1}^{(*)},w_{2}^{(*)}\big)\wedge 1\right\},

uniformly in
z_{j}\in(1-\delta)\mathbf{D}
and
w_{j}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}
, for
j=1,2
. In (
A.2
) the minimum is taken over all four choices of stars. For more details on the proof of (
A.2
) see Supplementary Section
S4.4
.

First we derive (
4.21
), which in the view of (
A.2
) is equivalent to

(A.3)

\beta_{12,*}\gtrsim\eta_{*},\quad\forall z_{j}\in(1-\delta)\mathbf{D},\,w_{j}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}.

We have from
[
29
, Lemma 6.1]
that

(A.4)

\beta_{12,*}(w_{1},w_{2})\gtrsim\left||E_{1}|-|E_{2}|\right|^{2}+|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}+\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}

uniformly in
|z_{j}|,|w_{j}|\lesssim 1
. This immediately implies (
A.3
) in the regime
|w_{j}|\lesssim 1
. To cover the complementary regime where
\max\{|w_{1}|,|w_{2}|\}
is large, we observe that

(A.5)

\beta_{*,12}(w_{1},w_{2})\geq 1-\lVert M^{z_{1}}(w_{1})\rVert\lVert M^{z_{2}}(w_{2})\rVert,

for any
z_{j}\in\mathbf{C}
,
w_{j}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}
, as it trivially follows from the eigenvalue equation for
\mathcal{B}_{12}
and the bound
\lVert\mathcal{S}[R]\rVert\leq\lVert R\rVert
, for all
R\in\mathrm{span}\{E_{\pm},F^{(*)}\}
. The MDE (
3.29
) implies that
\lVert M^{z}(w)\rVert\lesssim|w|^{-1}
uniformly in
|z|\leq 1
and
|w|\geq C
for some (large) implicit constant
C>0
. Combined with the trivial bound
\|M^{z}(w)\|\lesssim 1
from
[
29
, Eq.(3.5)]
and (
A.5
) this yields the lower bound of order one on the lhs. of (
A.3
) in the regime
\max\{|w_{1}|,|w_{2}|\}\geq C
and thus finishes the proof of (
4.21
).

Now we proceed to the proof of (
4.20
), and start with a small simplification. Due to the symmetry relation

(A.6)

M^{z}(w)E_{-}=-E_{-}M^{z}(-w)

it holds that

\mathcal{B}_{12}(w_{1},-w_{2})[RE_{-}]=\mathcal{B}_{12}(w_{1},w_{2})[R]E_{-},

so
\widehat{\beta}_{12}(w_{1},w_{2})=\widehat{\beta}_{12}(w_{1},-w_{2})
for any
z_{j}\in\mathbf{C}
and
w_{j}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}
. Similarly we have
\widehat{\beta}_{12}(w_{1},w_{2})=\widehat{\beta}_{12}(-w_{1},w_{2})
. Therefore, it is sufficient to prove (
4.20
) in the case when
E_{1},E_{2}\geq 0
.

We deduce (
4.20
) from the following estimates on
\beta_{12,*}
.

Lemma A.1
.

Fix (small)
\delta,\kappa>0
. Uniformly in
z_{j}\in(1-\delta)\mathbf{D}
and
w_{j}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}
with
E_{j}:=\Re w_{j}\in\mathbf{B}_{\kappa}^{z_{j}}
,
E_{j}\geq 0
,
\eta_{j}:=|\Im w_{j}|\in(0,1]
for
j=1,2
, it holds that

(A.7)

\displaystyle\beta_{12,*}(w_{1},w_{2})\sim\left|E_{1}-E_{2}+\frac{\langle(Z_{1}-Z_{2})\Im M_{1}\rangle}{\langle\Im M_{1}\rangle}\right|+|E_{1}-E_{2}|^{2}+|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}+\eta_{1}+\eta_{2},

if
w_{1},w_{2}
are in the opposite complex half-planes, and

(A.8)

\beta_{12,*}(w_{1},w_{2})\sim E_{1}+E_{2}+|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}+\eta_{1}+\eta_{2},

if
w_{1},w_{2}
are in the same half-plane.

We postpone the proof of Lemma
A.1
to the end of this section, and proceed to the proof of (
4.20
) relying on (
A.7
)–(
A.8
).

Proof of (
4.20
).

Using the explicit form of
M_{1}
from (
3.30
) we get

(A.9)

E_{1}-E_{2}+\frac{\langle(Z_{1}-Z_{2})\Im M_{1}\rangle}{\langle\Im M_{1}\rangle}=E_{1}-E_{2}-\frac{\Im u_{1}\Re[\overline{z}_{1}(z_{1}-z_{2})]}{\Im m_{1}}=\mathcal{O}(E_{1}+E_{2}),

where we additionally used that
|\Im m_{1}|\sim 1
due to the conditions
E_{1}\in\mathbf{B}_{\kappa}^{z_{1}}
and
\eta_{1}\lesssim 1
, and computed

(A.10)

\Im u_{1}=\Im u^{z_{1}}(w_{1})=\Im[u^{z_{1}}(w_{1})-u^{z_{1}}(\mathrm{i}\Im w_{1})]=\mathcal{O}(E_{1}),

since
u^{z}(w)
is real for
w
on the imaginary axis. It follows from (
A.7
), (
A.8
) and (
A.9
) that

(A.11)

\beta_{12,*}(w_{1},w_{2})\gtrsim\beta_{12,*}(w_{1},\overline{w}_{2})+\beta_{12,*}(\overline{w}_{1},w_{2}),

for all
w_{1},w_{2}
in the same half-plane satisfying assumptions of Lemma
A.1
. Combined with (
A.2
) and (
A.7
), this immediately finishes the proof of (
4.20
).
∎

Proof of Lemma
A.1
.

Instead of working with
\beta_{12,*}
directly, we use that

(A.12)

\beta_{12,*}\sim\left|(1-\langle M_{1}M_{2}\rangle)(1+\langle M_{1}E_{-}M_{2}E_{-}\rangle)+\langle M_{1}E_{-}M_{2}\rangle\langle M_{2}E_{-}M_{1}\rangle\right|,

and further analyze the rhs. of (
A.12
). For the proof of (
A.12
) see Supplementary equations (
S4.16
) and (
S4.19
).

1st case (
\Im w_{1}\Im w_{2}<0
): proof of (
A.7
).
We expand
M_{2}
around
M_{1}^{*}
up to the first order:

(A.13)

M_{2}=M_{1}^{*}+M_{1}^{*}DM_{1}^{*}+\frac{\left\langle D(M_{1}^{*})^{2}\right\rangle}{1-\langle(M_{1}^{*})^{2}\rangle}(M_{1}^{*})^{2}+\mathcal{O}(|\overline{w}_{1}-w_{2}|^{2}+|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}),\quad D:=w_{2}-\overline{w}_{1}+Z_{2}-Z_{1},

where the error term is a
2\times 2
matrix bounded in operator norm by
|\overline{w}_{1}-w_{2}|^{2}+|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}
, up to a multiplicative constant. A simple calculation based on (
3.30
) shows that
\langle M_{1}E_{-}M_{1}^{*}\rangle=\langle M_{1}^{*}E_{-}M_{1}\rangle=0
, which in combination with (
A.13
) gives

(A.14)

\langle M_{1}E_{-}M_{2}\rangle\langle M_{2}E_{-}M_{1}\rangle=\mathcal{O}\left(|\overline{w}_{1}-w_{2}|^{2}+|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}\right).

Denote
m_{1}:=m^{z_{1}}(w_{1})
and
u_{1}:=u^{z_{1}}(w_{1})
. We further compute from (
3.30
) and (
A.13
) that

(A.15)

\begin{split}1+\langle M_{1}E_{-}M_{2}E_{-}\rangle&=1+|m_{1}|^{2}-|z_{1}|^{2}|u_{1}|^{2}+\mathcal{O}\left(|\overline{w}_{1}-w_{2}|+|z_{1}-z_{2}|\right)\\
1-\langle M_{1}M_{2}\rangle&=\frac{\eta_{1}}{\eta_{1}+|\Im m_{1}|}+\langle DX\rangle+\mathcal{O}\left(|\overline{w}_{1}-w_{2}|^{2}+|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}\right),\end{split}

where
X\in\mathbf{C}^{2\times 2}
is given by

(A.16)

X:=M_{1}^{*}M_{1}M_{1}^{*}+\frac{\langle M_{1}(M_{1}^{*})^{2}\rangle}{1-\langle(M_{1}^{*})^{2}\rangle}(M_{1}^{*})^{2}.

In the second line of (
A.15
) we additionally used that

(A.17)

M_{1}M_{1}^{*}=\frac{\Im M_{1}}{\Im w_{1}+\Im m_{1}}

to compute
1-\langle M_{1}M_{1}^{*}\rangle
. We further use (
A.17
) to compute
X
:

(A.18)

\begin{split}X&=\frac{1}{2\mathrm{i}(\Im w_{1}+\Im m_{1})}\left(M_{1}^{*}(M_{1}-M_{1}^{*})+\frac{\langle M_{1}^{*}(M_{1}-M_{1}^{*})\rangle}{1-\langle(M_{1}^{*})^{2}\rangle}(M_{1}^{*})^{2}\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{2\mathrm{i}(\Im w_{1}+\Im m_{1})}\left(M_{1}M_{1}^{*}-\frac{1-\langle M_{1}M_{1}^{*}\rangle}{1-\langle(M^{*})^{2}\rangle}(M_{1}^{*})^{2}\right)=\frac{\Im M_{1}}{2\mathrm{i}(\Im m_{1})^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(\eta_{1}).\end{split}

Combining (
A.12
), (
A.14
), (
A.15
), (
A.18
), and additionally using that

1+|m_{1}|^{2}-|z_{1}|^{2}|u_{1}|^{2}\sim 1,

as it follows from the bound
|u_{1}|<1
from
[
29
, Eq. (3.5)]
, we arrive to

(A.19)

\begin{split}\beta_{12,*}\sim&\left|\frac{\eta_{1}}{\eta_{1}+|\Im m_{1}|}+\frac{\langle D\Im M_{1}\rangle}{2\mathrm{i}(\Im m_{1})^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left(|\overline{w}_{1}-w_{2}|^{2}+|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}+\eta_{1}^{2}\right)\right|\\
=&\left|\left(E_{1}-E_{2}+\frac{\langle(Z_{1}-Z_{2})\Im M_{1}\rangle}{\langle\Im M_{1}\rangle}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(|E_{1}-E_{2}|^{2}+|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}+\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}\right)\right|.\end{split}

To go from the first to the second line in (
A.19
) we used that
|\Im m_{1}|\sim 1
since
E_{1}\in\mathbf{B}^{z_{1}}_{\kappa}
and
\eta_{1}\lesssim 1
, included
\eta_{1}/(\eta_{1}+|\Im m_{1}|)
into the error term and recalled the definition of
D
from (
A.13
). Finally, to conclude (
A.7
) from (
A.19
), we make the following trivial observation. Let
a,b,c\in\mathbf{R}
satisfy conditions
|c|\sim|a+b|
and
|c|\gtrsim|b|
. Then it holds that
|c|\sim|a|+|b|
. The proof of this fact proceeds by distinguishing between the cases
|a|\geq L|b|
and
|a|<L|b|
, for sufficiently large fixed
L>0
. Applying this observation to
c:=\beta_{12,*}
and
a,b
given by the first and second terms in the second line of (
A.19
), respectively, and recalling the lower bound (
A.4
), we finish the proof of (
A.7
).

2nd case (
\Im w_{1}\Im w_{2}>0
): proof of (
A.8
).
We argue similarly to the previous case, and analyze the rhs. of (
A.12
). Expanding
M_{2}
around
M_{1}
we get analogously to (
A.14
) that

(A.20)

\langle M_{1}E_{-}M_{2}\rangle\langle M_{2}E_{-}M_{1}\rangle=\mathcal{O}\left(|w_{1}-w_{2}|^{2}+|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}\right).

We further have from
[
23
, Eq.(3.4)]
that

(A.21)

|1-\langle M_{1}M_{2}\rangle|\sim 1,

for the applicability of
[
23
]
see Supplementary equation (
S2.12
) and the discussion around. Next we compute
1+\langle M_{1}E_{-}M_{2}E_{-}\rangle
using (
3.30
) both for
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
:

(A.22)

\begin{split}&1+\langle M_{1}E_{-}M_{2}E_{-}\rangle=1+m_{1}m_{2}-\Re[z_{1}\overline{z}_{2}]u_{1}u_{2}\\
&\quad=1+\frac{m_{1}^{2}+m_{2}^{2}-(m_{1}-m_{2})^{2}}{2}-\frac{|z_{1}|^{2}+|z_{2}|^{2}-|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}}{2}\frac{u_{1}^{2}+u_{2}^{2}-(u_{1}-u_{2})^{2}}{2}\\
&\quad=1+\frac{m_{1}^{2}+m_{2}^{2}}{2}-\frac{|z_{1}|^{2}+|z_{2}|^{2}}{2}\frac{u_{1}^{2}+u_{2}^{2}}{2}+\mathcal{O}(|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}+|w_{1}-w_{2}|^{2})\\
&\quad=\frac{1+m_{1}^{2}-|z_{1}|^{2}u_{1}^{2}}{2}+\frac{1+m_{2}^{2}-|z_{2}|^{2}u_{2}^{2}}{2}+\frac{(|z_{1}|^{2}-|z_{2}|^{2})(u_{1}^{2}-u_{2}^{2})}{4}+\mathcal{O}(|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}+|w_{1}-w_{2}|^{2})\\
&\quad=\frac{w_{1}}{2(w_{1}+m_{1})}+\frac{w_{2}}{2(w_{2}+m_{2})}+\mathcal{O}(|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}+|w_{1}-w_{2}|^{2}).\end{split}

Here to go from the second to the third and from the fourth to the fifth line we used that

|u_{1}-u_{2}|=\mathcal{O}(|z_{1}-z_{2}|+|w_{1}-w_{2}|).

Additionally, in the last step we used (
3.31
) twice: for
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
.

Combining (
A.12
), (
A.20
), (
A.21
) and (
A.22
) we get

(A.23)

\beta_{12,*}\sim\left|\frac{E_{1}}{2(w_{1}+m_{1})}+\frac{E_{2}}{2(w_{2}+m_{2})}+\mathcal{O}(|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}+|E_{1}-E_{2}|^{2}+\eta_{1}+\eta_{2})\right|.

We repeat the argument presented below (
A.19
) using (
A.4
) and (
A.23
) as an input, and obtain

(A.24)

\beta_{12,*}\sim\left|\frac{E_{1}}{2(w_{1}+m_{1})}+\frac{E_{2}}{2(w_{2}+m_{2})}\right|+|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}+|E_{1}-E_{2}|^{2}+\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}.

Finally, we notice that the first term in the rhs. of (
A.24
) is of order
E_{1}+E_{2}
, since the imaginary parts of
w_{1}+m_{1}
,
w_{2}+m_{2}
share the same sign and have an absolute value of order 1. This finishes the proof of (
A.8
).
∎

A.2.
Bound on the propagator: proof of Lemma
4.3

In this section we incorporate the notations and conventions introduced in the beginning of Appendix
A.1
. We also frequently use (
A.2
) without mentioning this further. The time-dependent version of
M_{j}
is denoted by
M_{j,r}:=M^{z_{j,r}}(w_{j,r})
, for
r\in[0,T]
. The time-dependent versions of the related quantities such as
m_{j}
and
u_{j}
are denoted similarly by replacing
z_{j},w_{j}
with
z_{j,r},w_{j,r}
. Denote for short
\beta_{\sigma,r}:=\beta_{12,\sigma,r}
and
\beta_{*,r}:=\beta_{12,*,r}
for
\sigma\in\{\pm\}
and
r\in[0,T]
. Finally, we set

(A.25)

a_{12}^{\sigma}:=\sigma\langle M_{12}^{E_{\sigma}}E_{\sigma}\rangle,\,\sigma\in\{\pm\},\quad d_{12}:=\langle M_{12}^{E_{+}}E_{-}\rangle=-\langle M_{12}^{E_{-}}E_{+}\rangle.

The last identity is established for
w_{i},w_{j}
on the imaginary axis in
[
37
, Eq.(5.26)]
. Since the functions
\langle M_{ij}^{E_{+}}E_{-}\rangle
and
-\langle M_{ij}^{E_{-}}E_{+}\rangle
are analytic in
w_{i},w_{j}
in the upper and lower complex half-planes, this identity holds for all
w_{i},w_{j}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}
.

Recall that
N^{-b}\lesssim T\lesssim 1
,
|z_{j,T}|\leq 1-\delta
,
\Re w_{j,T}\in\mathbf{B}_{\kappa}^{z_{j}}
and
|\Im w_{j,T}|\geq N^{-1+\epsilon}
by the set-up of Lemma
4.3
, for some fixed
\delta,\kappa,\epsilon>0
. We assume that
z_{j,r},w_{j,r}
satisfy the same conditions for all
t\in[0,T]
with
\delta
and
\kappa
decreased to
\delta/2
and
\kappa/2
, respectively. This can be achieved by choosing
T
sufficiently small, for more details see the set-up of Supplementary Lemma
S2.9
.

An explicit calculation discussed around (
4.18
) gives that

(A.26)

\mathcal{A}_{[2],t}=\begin{pmatrix}2a_{12}^{+}&d_{12}&-d_{12}&0\\
d_{12}&a_{12}^{+}+a_{12}^{-}&0&-d_{12}\\
-d_{12}&0&a_{12}^{-}+a_{12}^{+}&d_{12}\\
0&-d_{12}&d_{12}&2a_{12}^{-}\end{pmatrix}.

Estimating the off-diagonal part of
\Re\mathcal{A}_{[2],t}
simply by operator norm and recalling the definition of
f_{[2],r}
given in Lemma
4.3
, we get

(A.27)

f_{[2],r}\leq 2\max\{a_{12,r}^{+},a_{12,r}^{-},0\}+d_{12,r},\quad r\in[0,T].

Thus, to verify (
4.19
), it suffices to show that

(A.28)

\int_{s}^{t}\max\{\Re a_{12,r}^{+},\Re a_{12,r}^{-},0\}\mathrm{d}r=\int_{s}^{t}\max\{\Re a_{12,r}^{+},\Re a_{12,r}^{-}\}\mathrm{d}r+\mathcal{O}(1)=\log\frac{\beta_{12,*,s}}{\beta_{12,*,t}}+\mathcal{O}(1),

(A.29)

\int_{s}^{t}|\Re d_{12,r}|\mathrm{d}r=\mathcal{O}(1),

for any
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
. We note that both identities in (
A.28
) are non-trivial and require verification. Further in the proof of (
A.28
) and (
A.29
) the time parameters
s,r,t\in[0,T]
are ordered as
s\leq r\leq t
, unless stated otherwise.

We start with the preliminary analysis of
\beta_{\pm,r}
. First we observe that there exists
\sigma\in\{\pm\}
such that

(A.30)

|\beta_{\sigma,r}|\sim 1,\quad\forall r\in[0,T].

Indeed, from (
4.16
) we have that

(A.31)

z_{j,r}=\mathrm{e}^{-r/2}z_{j,0},\quad m_{j,r}=\mathrm{e}^{r/2}m_{j,0},\quad u_{j,r}=\mathrm{e}^{r}u_{j,0},\qquad\forall\,r\in[0,T].

Therefore, the evolution in time of the square root in the rhs. of (
A.1
) is given by

(A.32)

\Re\sqrt{m_{1,r}^{2}m_{2,r}^{2}-\Im\left[z_{1,r}\overline{z}_{2,r}\right]u_{1,r}^{2}u_{2,r}^{2}}=\mathrm{e}^{r}\Re\sqrt{m_{1,0}^{2}m_{2,0}^{2}-\Im\left[z_{1,0}\overline{z}_{2,0}\right]u_{1,0}^{2}u_{2,0}^{2}}.

In particular, this quantity preserves the sign for all
r\in[0,T]
. By
[
29
, Eq. (3.5)]
it holds that
|u_{j,r}|<1
, so

(A.33)

\Re\left[1-\Re[z_{1,r}\overline{z}_{2,r}]u_{1,r}u_{2,r}\right]\geq 1-|z_{1,r}z_{2,r}|\geq\delta,

where in the last step we estimated
|z_{j,r}|\leq 1-\delta/2
. Combining (
A.1
) with (
A.32
) and (
A.33
), we finish the verification of (
A.30
).

Without loss of generality we may assume that

(A.34)

|\beta_{+,r}|\sim 1,\quad|\beta_{-,r}|\sim\beta_{*,r},\qquad\forall r\in[0,T].

We further observe that (
A.31
) implies

(A.35)

\beta_{12,\sigma,r}=1-\mathrm{e}^{r}(1-\beta_{12,\sigma,0}),\quad\sigma\in\{\pm\},\,\,r\in[0,T],

so the trajectory of
\beta_{\sigma,r}
is a part of a straight line. In particular,
\beta_{\sigma,r}
does not wind around the origin, so we have

(A.36)

\log\frac{\beta_{\sigma,s}}{\beta_{\sigma,t}}=\log\left|\frac{\beta_{\sigma,s}}{\beta_{\sigma,t}}\right|+\mathcal{O}(1),\quad\forall\,s,t\in[0,T],

which we will use in the remaining part of the proof.

Next, we proceed to the proof of (
A.28
). We borrow the following identity for
a_{12}^{\sigma}
,
\sigma\in\{\pm\}
, from
[
37
, Eq.(A.13)-(A.14)]
:

(A.37)

a_{12}^{\sigma}=\frac{\sigma m_{1}m_{2}-|z_{1}z_{2}|^{2}u_{1}^{2}u_{2}^{2}+m_{1}^{2}m_{2}^{2}+\Re[z_{1}\overline{z}_{2}]u_{1}u_{2}}{1+|z_{1}z_{2}|^{2}u_{1}^{2}u_{2}^{2}-m_{1}^{2}m_{2}^{2}-2\Re[z_{1}\overline{z}_{2}]u_{1}u_{2}},

Using the definition of
\beta_{\pm}
given in (
A.1
) we compute

(A.38)

\beta_{+}\beta_{-}=1+|z_{1}z_{2}|^{2}u_{1}^{2}u_{2}^{2}-m_{1}^{2}m_{2}^{2}-2\Re[z_{1}\overline{z}_{2}]u_{1}u_{2}.

Combining (
A.31
), (
A.37
) and (
A.38
) we obtain that

(A.39)

a_{12,r}^{\sigma}=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{r}\log\beta_{+,r}\beta_{-,r}+\sigma\frac{m_{1,r}m_{2,r}}{\beta_{+,r}\beta_{-,r}}.

Therefore, (
A.34
) implies that a half of the rhs. of (
A.28
) comes from the first term in the rhs. of (
A.39
), while the second half should come from the second term, i.e. the second identity in (
A.28
) is equivalent to

(A.40)

\int_{s}^{t}\left|\Re\frac{m_{1,r}m_{2,r}}{\beta_{+,r}\beta_{-,r}}\right|\mathrm{d}r=\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\beta_{*,s}}{\beta_{*,t}}+\mathcal{O}(1).

Let us show that the real part of
m_{1,r}m_{2,r}/(\beta_{+,r}\beta_{-,r})
changes the sign at most 3 times
13
13

13

The exact number of sign changes does not matter, but it is important that this happens only finitely many times independently of the trajectory of the characteristic flow
on
[0,T]
. To see this, we use (
A.38
), (
A.31
) and obtain

(A.41)

\Re\frac{m_{1,r}m_{2,r}}{\beta_{+,r}\beta_{-,r}}=\frac{\Re\left[\mathrm{e}^{r}\overline{m}_{1,0}\overline{m}_{2,0}\left(1+\mathrm{e}^{2r}|z_{1,0}z_{2,0}|^{2}u_{1,0}^{2}u_{2,0}^{2}-\mathrm{e}^{2r}m_{1,0}^{2}m_{2,0}^{2}-\mathrm{e}^{r}2\Re[z_{1,0}\overline{z}_{2,0}]u_{1,0}u_{2,0}\right)\right]}{|\beta_{+,r}\beta_{-,r}|^{2}}.

The numerator in the rhs. of (
A.41
) is a polynomial with real coefficients in variable
\mathrm{e}^{r}
of degree at most 3. In particular, it either vanishes identically or has at most 3 zeros. Therefore, the lhs. of (
A.41
) changes the sign at most 3 times for
r\in[0,T]
. This allows us to split the integration in the lhs. of (
A.40
) into at most 4 intervals such that on each of them the sign of the lhs. of (
A.41
) is preserved. Since the first term in the rhs. of (
A.40
) is an additive function of the time interval, i.e.

\log\frac{\beta_{*,s}}{\beta_{*,t}}=\log\frac{\beta_{*,s}}{\beta_{*,r}}+\log\frac{\beta_{*,r}}{\beta_{*,t}}

for any
s,r,t\in[0,T]
,
s\leq r\leq t
, to verify the second identity in (
A.28
) it is sufficient to show that

(A.42)

\left|\Re\int_{s_{1}}^{t_{1}}\frac{m_{1,r}m_{2,r}}{\beta_{+,r}\beta_{-,r}}\mathrm{d}r\right|=\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\beta_{*,s_{1}}}{\beta_{*,t_{1}}}+\mathcal{O}(1)

holds for any
s_{1},t_{1}\in[0,T]
such that
s_{1}\leq t_{1}
and the integrand in the lhs. of (
A.42
) preserves the sign on
[s_{1},t_{1}]
