. Actually, the last condition on
s_{1},t_{1}
is not needed for the proof of (
A.42
), so we will only assume that
0\leq s_{1}\leq t_{1}\leq T
.

Now we prove (
A.42
) and start with a small simplification. Let
c_{0}>0
be a constant which will be taken sufficiently small later. If
z_{1,0}
,
z_{2,0}
satisfy the inequality
|z_{1,0}-z_{2,0}|\geq c_{0}
, then

\beta_{*,r}\gtrsim|z_{1,r}-z_{2,r}|^{2}=\mathrm{e}^{-r}|z_{1,0}-z_{2,0}|^{2}\geq\mathrm{e}^{-T}c_{0}^{2},

where we used (
4.20
) in the first step and (
A.31
) in the second. Thus,
|\beta_{+,r}|
and
|\beta_{-,r}|
are of order 1 for all
r\in[s_{1},t_{1}]
and the lhs. of (
A.42
) has an upper bound of order 1, i.e. (
A.42
) holds. Therefore, we may further assume that
|z_{1,0}-z_{2,0}|<c_{0}
. Using (
A.35
) along with (
A.31
), we explicitly compute the integral in the lhs. of (
A.42
):

(A.43)

\int_{s_{1}}^{t_{1}}\frac{m_{1,r}m_{2,r}}{\beta_{-,r}\beta_{+,r}}\mathrm{d}r=\frac{m_{1,0}m_{2,0}}{\beta_{+,0}-\beta_{-,0}}\left(\log\frac{\beta_{-,s_{1}}}{\beta_{-,t_{1}}}-\log\frac{\beta_{+,s_{1}}}{\beta_{+,t_{1}}}\right).

We further compute the first factor in the rhs. of (
A.43
) by expressing
\beta_{+,0}-\beta_{-,0}
from (
A.1
):

(A.44)

\frac{m_{1,0}m_{2,0}}{\beta_{+,0}-\beta_{-,0}}=\pm\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{\Im[z_{1,0}\overline{z}_{2,0}]u_{1,0}u_{2,0}}{m_{1,0}m_{2,0}}\right)^{-1/2}.

The sign in the rhs. of (
A.44
) appears due to the fact that
\sqrt{m_{1,0}^{2}m_{2,0}^{2}}=\pm m_{1,0}m_{2,0}
depending on the phase of
m_{1,0}m_{2,0}
. We further estimate

(A.45)

\begin{split}&\left|\frac{\Im[z_{1,0}\overline{z}_{2,0}]u_{1,0}u_{2,0}}{m_{1,0}m_{2,0}}\right|=\left|\frac{\Im[(z_{1,T}-z_{2,T})\overline{z}_{2,T}]}{(w_{1,T}+m_{1,T})(w_{1,T}+m_{1,T})}\right|\\
&\quad\leq\frac{|z_{1,T}-z_{2,T}|}{\left(|\Im w_{1,T}|+|\kappa+\mathcal{O}(|\Im w_{1,T}|)|\right)\left(|\Im w_{2,T}|+|\kappa+\mathcal{O}(|\Im w_{2,T}|)|\right)}\lesssim\frac{|z_{1,T}-z_{2,T}|}{\kappa^{2}}.\end{split}

In the first line of (
A.45
) we used (
A.31
), while to go from the first to the second line we estimated each factor in the denominator from below by the corresponding imaginary part and used that
\Re w_{j,T}\in\mathbf{B}_{\kappa}^{z_{j,T}}
. In particular, taking
c_{0}
sufficiently small and recalling that
|z_{1,T}-z_{2,T}|\leq|z_{1,0}-z_{2,0}|\leq c_{0}
, we make the lhs. of (
A.45
) smaller than
1/2
. Thus the rhs. of (
A.44
) can be linearized, and we get

(A.46)

\frac{m_{1,0}m_{2,0}}{\beta_{+,0}-\beta_{-,0}}=\pm\frac{1}{2}+\mathcal{O}(|z_{1,T}-z_{2,T}|),

where we used (
A.44
) and (
A.45
). Together with (
A.43
), (
A.36
) and (
A.34
) this implies

(A.47)

\left|\Re\int_{s_{1}}^{t_{1}}\frac{m_{1,r}m_{2,r}}{\beta_{-,r}\beta_{+,r}}\mathrm{d}r\right|=\left(\frac{1}{2}+\mathcal{O}(|z_{1,T}-z_{2,T}|)\right)\left|\log\frac{\beta_{*,s_{1}}}{\beta_{*,t_{1}}}+\mathcal{O}(1)\right|.

Finally, notice that from (
4.20
) we have

|z_{1,T}-z_{2,T}|\left|\log\frac{\beta_{*,s_{1}}}{\beta_{*,t_{1}}}\right|\lesssim\left|(z_{1,T}-z_{2,T})\log|z_{1,T}-z_{2,T}|\right|\lesssim 1.

Thus, (
A.42
) holds with the logarithm replaced by its absolute value in the right hand side. To deal with the case when this logarithm is negative, we observe that

(A.48)

\beta_{*,s}\sim\beta_{*,t}+|t-s|,\quad\forall\,0\leq s\leq t\leq T,

for the proof see Supplementary Lemma
S2.8
. This implies an upper bound of order one on the absolute value of the logarithm, so it can be absorbed into the error term. This finishes the proof of (
A.42
) and thereby of the second identity in (
A.28
).

To complete the proof of (
A.28
), it remains to show that

(A.49)

\int_{s}^{t}\max\{\Re a_{12,r}^{+},\Re a_{12,r}^{-},0\}\mathrm{d}r=\log\frac{\beta_{*,s}}{\beta_{*,t}}+\mathcal{O}(1).

Since we have already established the second identity in (
A.28
), the lhs. of (
A.49
) is automatically greater or equal to the rhs., so it suffices to prove the reverse inequality. Proceeding as in (
A.41
) and using (
A.37
), we get that
\max\{\Re a_{12,r}^{+},\Re a_{12,r}^{-}\}
changes the sign only finitely many times for
r\in[s,t]
. Let
\{[s_{i},t_{i}]\}_{i=1}^{k}
be the intervals of positivity of this quantity, ordered so that

s_{1}\leq t_{1}\leq s_{2}\leq\cdots\leq s_{k}\leq t_{k}.

We may assume that
s_{1}=s
and
t_{k}=t
, since
s_{1}
and
t_{1}
(
s_{k}
and
t_{k}
, respectively) may coincide. Applying the second identity in (
A.28
) to each of the intervals
[s_{i},t_{i}]
, we get

(A.50)

\int_{s}^{t}\max\{\Re a_{12,r}^{+},\Re a_{12,r}^{-},0\}\mathrm{d}r=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\log\frac{\beta_{*,s_{i}}}{\beta_{*,t_{i}}}+\mathcal{O}(1)=\log\frac{\beta_{*,s}}{\beta_{*,t}}-\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\log\frac{\beta_{*,t_{i}}}{\beta_{*,s_{i+1}}}+\mathcal{O}(1).

By (
A.48
), each of the subtracted logarithms in the rhs. of (
A.50
) is bounded from below by
-C
for some constant
C>0
. Therefore, the rhs. of (
A.50
) is bounded from above by
\log(\beta_{*,s}/\beta_{*,t})+\mathcal{O}(1)
. This finishes the proof of (
A.49
).

The proof of (
A.29
) is analogous to the proof of (
A.28
) up to several simplifications, which we now discuss. First, by a simple calculation analogous to
[
37
, Eq. (A.14)]
we have

(A.51)

d_{12,r}=\langle M_{12,r}^{E_{+}}E_{-}\rangle=\frac{\mathrm{i}\Im\left[z_{1,r}\overline{z}_{2,r}\right]u_{1,r}u_{2,r}}{\beta_{-,r}\beta_{+,r}}.

Similarly to (
A.41
) one can show that
\Re d_{12,r}
changes the sign at most 3 times for
r\in[0,T]
, so it is sufficient to show that

(A.52)

\left|\Re\int_{s_{1}}^{t_{1}}d_{12,r}\mathrm{d}r\right|\lesssim 1

for any
s_{1},t_{1}\in[0,T]
,
s_{1}\leq t_{1}
. Similarly to the argument above (
A.43
), we may assume that there exists
r_{0}\in[s_{1},t_{1}]
such that
|\beta_{*,r_{0}}|<\delta/2
. Computing explicitly the integral in the lhs. of (
A.52
) by the means of (
A.31
), (
A.35
) and (
A.51
), we get

(A.53)

\int_{s_{1}}^{t_{1}}d_{12,r}\mathrm{d}r=\frac{\mathrm{i}\Im\left[z_{1,0}\overline{z}_{2,0}\right]u_{1,0}u_{2,0}}{\beta_{+,0}-\beta_{-,0}}\left(\log\frac{\beta_{-,s_{1}}}{\beta_{-,t_{1}}}-\log\frac{\beta_{+,s_{1}}}{\beta_{+,t_{1}}}\right).

Note that by (
A.31
), the time in the first factor in the rhs. of (
A.53
) can be changed from 0 to
r_{0}
:

\frac{\mathrm{i}\Im\left[z_{1,0}\overline{z}_{2,0}\right]u_{1,0}u_{2,0}}{\beta_{+,0}-\beta_{-,0}}=\frac{\mathrm{i}\Im\left[z_{1,r_{0}}\overline{z}_{2,r_{0}}\right]u_{1,r_{0}}u_{2,r_{0}}}{\beta_{+,r_{0}}-\beta_{-,r_{0}}}=\mathcal{O}(|z_{1,r_{0}}-z_{2,r_{0}}|),

where in the last step we used that
|\beta_{+,r_{0}}-\beta_{-,r_{0}}|\geq\delta/2
and
|u_{1,r_{0}}u_{2,r_{0}}|<1
by
[
29
, Eq. (3.5)]
. The rest of the proof follows similarly to the argument below (
A.47
).

A.3.
Proof of Proposition
4.6

Recall the definition of the quantiles
\{\gamma_{i}^{z}\}_{i=-N}^{N}
of density
\rho^{z}
,
z\in\mathbf{C}
, from (
4.4
). Since
i,j\in[(1-\tau)N]
and
|z_{1}|,|z_{2}|\leq 1-\delta
in Proposition
4.6
, there exists a (small)
\kappa>0
which depends only on
\tau
and
\delta
, such that
\gamma_{i}^{z_{1}}\in\mathbf{B}^{z_{1}}_{\kappa}
and
\gamma_{j}^{z_{2}}\in\mathbf{B}^{z_{2}}_{\kappa}
. We frequently use this fact throughout the proof and do not refer to it for brevity.

Fix a (small)
\epsilon>0
and set
\eta:=N^{-1+\epsilon}
. By spectral decomposition and eigenvalue rigidity (
4.3
) we get

(A.54)

N\!\left(\left|\langle\bm{u}_{i}^{z_{1}},\bm{u}_{j}^{z_{2}}\rangle\right|^{2}+\left|\langle\bm{v}_{i}^{z_{1}},\bm{v}_{j}^{z_{2}}\rangle\right|^{2}\right)\!\!\prec\!(N\eta)^{2}\left\langle\Im G^{z_{1}}(\gamma_{i}^{z_{1}}+\mathrm{i}\eta)\Im G^{z_{2}}(\gamma_{j}^{z_{2}}+\mathrm{i}\eta)\right\rangle\!\!\prec\!\frac{(N\eta)^{2}}{\widehat{\beta}_{12}(\gamma_{i}^{z_{1}}+\mathrm{i}\eta,\gamma_{j}^{z_{2}}+\mathrm{i}\eta)}.

Here in the first step we additionally used the relation (
3.23
) between eigenvectors of
H^{z}
and singular vectors of
X-z
, and in the second step employed (
4.22
) together with the bound (
4.9
). Using (
4.20
) and the fact that
|\partial_{w}m^{z}(w)|\lesssim 1
uniformly in
\Re w\in\mathbf{B}_{\kappa}^{z}
and
0<\Im w\lesssim 1
, we obtain

(A.55)

\begin{split}\widehat{\beta}_{12}(\gamma_{i}^{z_{1}}+\mathrm{i}\eta,\gamma_{j}^{z_{2}}+\mathrm{i}\eta)&\gtrsim|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}+\mathrm{LT}+\eta,\,\,\,\text{where}\,\,\,\mathrm{LT}=\left|\gamma_{i}^{z_{1}}-\gamma_{j}^{z_{2}}-\frac{\Im u_{1}}{\Im m_{1}}\Re\left[\overline{z}_{1}(z_{1}-z_{2})\right]\right|,\end{split}

and
m_{1}=m^{z_{1}}(\gamma^{z_{1}}_{i}+\mathrm{i}0)
,
u_{1}:=m^{z_{1}}(\gamma^{z_{1}}_{i}+\mathrm{i}0)
.

It remains to show that

(A.56)

\mathrm{LT}\sim N^{-1}|i-j|+\mathcal{O}(|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}).

Indeed, (
4.23
) immediately follows from (
A.54
), (
A.55
) and (
A.56
) once
\epsilon>0
is taken sufficiently small. Denote for short
\Delta z:=z_{2}-z_{1}
,
\zeta:=\Delta z/|\Delta z|
and let
\partial_{\zeta}
be derivative in the direction of
\zeta
in the complex variable
z
, i.e. for a function
f(z)
we set
\partial_{\zeta}f(z):=\lim_{h\to 0}(f(z+h\zeta)-f(z))/h
. In the sequel, in case of a function of several variables,
\partial_{\zeta}
will always act on the
z
or
z_{1}
variables and not on
w
and
E
. Since
|\partial_{\zeta}^{2}m^{z}(w)|\lesssim 1
uniformly in
\Re w\in\mathbf{B}_{\kappa}^{z}
and
0<\Im w\lesssim 1
, (
4.4
) together with (
3.32
) imply that

(A.57)

\gamma_{i}^{z_{1}}-\gamma_{i}^{z_{2}}=-|\Delta z|\partial_{\zeta}\gamma_{i}^{z_{1}}+\mathcal{O}(|\Delta z|^{2}).

Differentiating (
4.4
) in the direction of
\zeta
we compute

(A.58)

\partial_{\zeta}\gamma_{i}^{z_{1}}=-\frac{1}{\Im m_{1}}\Im\int_{0}^{\gamma_{i}^{z_{1}}}\partial_{\zeta}m^{z_{1}}(E+\mathrm{i}0)\mathrm{d}E.

We observe that
\Im u^{z_{1}}(+\mathrm{i}0)=0
and write
\Im u_{1}
as an integral of
\partial_{E}\Im u^{z_{1}}(E+\mathrm{i}0)
over
E\in[0,\gamma_{i}^{z_{1}}]
. Together with the second part of (
A.55
), (
A.57
) and (
A.58
) this gives

(A.59)

\mathrm{LT}=\left|\gamma_{i}^{z_{2}}-\gamma_{j}^{z_{2}}+\frac{1}{\Im m_{1}}\Im\int_{0}^{\gamma_{i}^{z_{1}}}\Big(|\Delta z|\partial_{\zeta}m^{z_{1}}(E+\mathrm{i}0)+\Re\left[\overline{z}_{1}\Delta z\right]\partial_{E}u^{z_{1}}(E+\mathrm{i}0)\Big)\mathrm{d}E\right|+\mathcal{O}(|\Delta z|^{2}).

Now we show that the function integrated in (
A.59
) vanishes for every
E\in[0,\gamma^{z_{1}}_{i}]
. Since along this argument
E
and
z_{1}
remain fixed, we simply denote
m:=m^{z_{1}}(E+\mathrm{i}0)
,
u:=u^{z_{1}}(E+\mathrm{i}0)
and
M:=M^{z_{1}}(E+\mathrm{i}0)
(these notations should not be confused with
m_{1}
and
u_{1}
, which correspond to
E:=\gamma^{z_{1}}_{i}
). We differentiate (
3.29
) in the direction of
\zeta
and use (
3.30
), arriving to

(A.60)

\partial_{\zeta}m=\partial_{\zeta}\langle M\rangle=-2\Re[\overline{z}_{1}\zeta]mu(1-\langle M^{2}\rangle)^{-1}.

To compute
\partial_{E}u
, we first differentiate (
3.29
) in
E
and get

(A.61)

\partial_{E}m=\partial_{E}\langle M\rangle=\langle M^{2}\rangle(1-\langle M^{2}\rangle)^{-1}.

We further recall the definition of
u^{z}
from (
3.30
) and apply (
A.61
):

(A.62)

\partial_{E}u=\partial_{E}\frac{m}{E+m}=\frac{mu}{1-\langle M^{2}\rangle}\left(\frac{\langle M^{2}\rangle}{m^{2}}-\frac{1}{m(E+m)}\right)=\frac{2mu}{1-\langle M^{2}\rangle},

where in the last step we used (
3.31
). Combining (
A.60
) with (
A.62
), we obtain

(A.63)

|\Delta z|\partial_{\zeta}m+\Re\left[\overline{z}_{1}\Delta z\right]\partial_{E}u=0,

which together with (
A.59
) implies

(A.64)

\mathrm{LT}=|\gamma^{z_{2}}_{i}-\gamma^{z_{2}}_{j}|+\mathcal{O}(|\Delta z|^{2}).

It remains to notice that by (
4.4
),
|\gamma^{z_{2}}_{i}-\gamma^{z_{2}}_{j}|\sim N^{-1}|i-j|
. This finishes the proof of (
A.56
), and thereby of Proposition
4.6
.
∎

Supplementary Material

Appendix S1
Decorrelation for the Dyson Brownian motion

In this section we prove Theorem
6.1
by explicitly constructing the comparison processes
\{\mu^{(l)}_{i}(t)\}_{|i|\leq N}
for
l=1,2
, and relying on the recent proof of
[
14
, Theorem 4.1]
. For completeness, we remind the reader the set-up of Theorem
6.1
. The complex i.i.d. matrix
X
is embedded into the flow

(S1.1)

\mathrm{d}X_{t}=\frac{\mathrm{d}B_{t}}{\sqrt{N}},\qquad\quad X_{0}=X,

where the entries
(B_{t})_{ab}
are independent standard complex Brownian motions.
Let
H_{t}^{z}
be the Hermitization of
X_{t}-z
defined as in (
3.18
), denote its eigenvalues by
\lambda_{i}^{z}(t)
, and by
{\bf w}_{i}^{z}(t)\in\mathbf{C}^{2N}
the corresponding eigenvectors. We recall that due to the chiral symmetry of
H_{t}^{z}
its spectrum is symmetric around zero, i.e.
\lambda_{-i}(t)=-\lambda_{i}(t)
for any
i\in[N]
and
t\geq 0
. As a consequence the eigenvectors corresponding to
\lambda_{\pm i}(t)
are given by
14
14

14

Here
\mathfrak{t}
denotes the transpose.

{\bf w}_{\pm i}^{z}(t)=({\bf u}_{i}^{z}(t),\pm{\bf v}_{i}^{z}(t))^{\mathfrak{t}}\in\mathbf{C}^{2N}
, for
i\in[N]
, where
{\bf u}_{i}^{z}(t),{\bf v}_{i}^{z}(t)
are the left/ right singular vectors of
X_{t}-z
, respectively. It is well known
[
47
, Eq.(5.8)]
that
\lambda_{i}^{z}(t)
are the unique strong solution of the Dyson Brownian motion:

(S1.2)

\mathrm{d}\lambda_{i}^{z}(t)=\frac{\mathrm{d}b_{i}^{z}(t)}{\sqrt{2N}}+\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}^{z}(t)-\lambda_{j}^{z}(t)}\mathrm{d}t,

where, for
i\in[N]
, the
b_{i}^{z}(t)
are independent standard real Brownian motions, and
b_{-i}^{z}(t)=-b_{i}^{z}(t)
. We point out that here, and throughout this section, the summation
\sum_{j\neq i}
is performed over indices
|j|\leq N
. However, for different
z
’s the driving Brownian motions have a non-trivial correlation depending on the overlap of the singular vectors

(S1.3)

\mathrm{d}\big[b_{i}^{z_{1}},b_{j}^{z_{2}}\big]_{t}=4\Re\big[\langle{\bf u}_{i}^{z_{1}}(t),{\bf u}_{j}^{z_{2}}(t)\rangle\langle{\bf v}_{j}^{z_{2}}(t),{\bf v}_{i}^{z_{1}}(t)\rangle\big]\mathrm{d}t.

We construct the comparison processes
\{\mu_{i}^{(l)}(t)\}_{|i|\leq N}
for
l=1,2
as follows. Consider two independent sets
\{\mu_{i}^{(l)}\}_{|i|\leq N}
, for
l=1,2
, of eigenvalues of the Hermitization of two independent Ginibre matrices, and let
\mu_{i}^{(l)}(t)
be the solution of (
S1.2
) with initial condition
\mu_{i}^{(l)}
and with the
{\bm{\lambda}}
’s being replaced by the
{\bm{\mu}}
’s. More precisely, we define the
\mu_{i}^{(l)}(t)
as the unique strong solutions of

(S1.4)

\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}^{(l)}(t)=\frac{\mathrm{d}\beta_{i}^{(l)}(t)}{\sqrt{2N}}+\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{1}{\mu_{i}^{(l)}(t)-\mu_{j}^{(l)}(t)}\mathrm{d}t.

Here
\{(\beta_{i}^{(l)}(t))_{i=1}^{N},l=1,2\}
is a family of i.i.d. standard real Brownian motions, and it is extended to negative indices by symmetry similarly to the
b_{i}^{z}(t)
’s. In particular, we stress that the two processes
\{\mu_{i}^{(l)}(t),\,|i|\leq N\}
, for
l=1,2
, are fully independent. In order to show the independence of the solutions of (
S1.2
) for
z_{1},z_{2}
with
|z_{1}-z_{2}|\gg N^{-1/2}
, we will (almost completely) couple the evolution of the
\lambda_{i}^{z_{l}}(t)
with the two fully independent processes
\mu_{i}^{(l)}(t)
.

Fix
T,R>0
. We will now couple the driving martingales in (
S1.2
)–(
S1.4
) for times
t\in[0,T]
and indices
|i|\leq R
. To simplify the notation, here and throughout this section we assume that
R
is an integer. We point out that this coupling idea first originated in
[
33
]
and it was then used in several further works (see e.g.
[
9
,
8
,
14
,
34
,
35
,
38
]
). By the martingale representation theorem
[
61
, Theorem 18.12]
we can write

(S1.5)

\left(\begin{matrix}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{b}_{t}^{z_{1}}\\
\mathrm{d}\mathbf{b}_{t}^{z_{2}}\end{matrix}\right)=\sqrt{C(t)}\left(\begin{matrix}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{\bm{\beta}}_{t}^{(1)}\\
\mathrm{d}\mathbf{\bm{\beta}}_{t}^{(2)}\end{matrix}\right),

where
\mathbf{b}_{t}^{z_{l}}:=\big(b_{1}^{z_{l}}(t),b_{2}^{z_{l}}(t),\dots,b_{R}^{z_{l}}(t)\big)
, for
l=1,2
, and
{\bm{\beta}}_{t}^{(l)}
is defined similarly. In the following we may use the short-hand notations
\mathbf{b}_{t}:=(\mathbf{b}_{t}^{z_{1}},\mathbf{b}_{t}^{z_{2}})^{t}
and
{\bm{\beta}}_{t}:=({\bm{\beta}}_{t}^{(1)},{\bm{\beta}}_{t}^{(2)})^{t}
. Here
C(t)
is the
(2R)\times(2R)
covariance matrix of the vector
\mathbf{b}_{t}
(see (
S1.3
)). Additionally, we stress that the
\beta_{i}^{(l)}(t)
for larger indices are independent from
\{(b_{i}^{z_{l}}(t))_{i=1}^{N},l=1,2\}
, and that
\{(b_{i}^{z_{l}}(t))_{i=R+1}^{N},l=1,2\}
are independent of the
\{(\beta_{i}^{(l)}(t))_{i=1}^{N},l=1,2\}
.

Theorem S1.1
.

Let
X
be a complex i.i.d. matrix, and let
X_{t}
be the solution of (
S1.1
) with initial condition
X_{0}=X
. Fix any small
\omega_{*},\delta>0
,
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbf{C}
with
|z_{1}|,|z_{2}|<1-\delta
,
T>N^{-1+\omega_{*}}
, and a possibly
N
-dependent
0<R<N|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}
. For
l=1,2
, let
\lambda_{i}^{z_{l}}(t)
,
\mu_{i}^{(l)}(t)
be the solutions of (
S1.2
) and (
S1.4
), respectively. Assume that for any small
\xi>0
and any large
D>0
we have

(S1.6)

\mathbf{P}\left(\big|\langle{\bf u}_{i}^{z_{1}}(t),{\bf u}_{j}^{z_{2}}(t)\rangle\big|^{2}+\big|\langle{\bf v}_{j}^{z_{2}}(t),{\bf v}_{i}^{z_{1}}(t)\rangle\big|^{2}\leq\frac{N^{\xi}}{N|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}}\,\,\forall\,t\in[0,T]\,\mathrm{and}\,|i|,|j|\leq R\right)\geq 1-N^{-D}.

Then (
6.3
) holds for
l=1,2
with very high probability simultaneously for all
t\in[N^{-1+\omega_{*}},T]
and indices
|i|\leq R
, i.e. we have

(S1.7)

\big|\rho_{t}^{z_{l}}(0)\lambda_{i}^{z_{l}}(t)-\rho_{\mathrm{sc},t}(0)\mu_{i}^{(l)}(t)\big|\leq N^{\xi}\!\!\left[\sqrt{\frac{t}{N}}\left(\frac{1}{R^{1/8}}\!+\!\sqrt{\frac{R}{N|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}}}\right)\!+\!\frac{\sqrt{Nt^{3}}}{R}\!+\!\frac{|i|}{N}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{Nt}}\!+\!\frac{|i|}{N}+t\right)\!\right]\!.

We point out that (
S1.7
) improves on the previous results in
[
14
,
33
]
, giving an explicit error in terms of
t
,
|z_{1}-z_{2}|
, and the number of coupled Brownian motions
R
. However, at the technical level no new inputs are needed compared to
[
14
]
. We just put together several estimates presented in
[
14
]
. For the convenience of the reader we present the main steps of the proof. At the end of this section we comment on a possible improvement of the bound in the rhs. of (
S1.7
), which, however, we do not pursue for brevity.

Relying on Theorem
S1.1
, we now prove Theorem
6.1
.

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

It suffices to verify (
S1.6
), which is equivalent to proving that the overlap bound (
4.23
) holds simultaneously for all
t\in[0,T]
with very high probability. Since the eigenvectors of
H^{z_{1}},H^{z_{2}}
are highly unstable, we instead show that

(S1.8)

\left|\langle\Im G^{z_{1}}_{t}(w_{1})\Im G^{z_{2}}_{t}(w_{2})\rangle\right|\lesssim N^{\xi}\left(\widehat{\beta}_{12}(w_{1},w_{2})\right)^{-1}

simultaneously for all
t\in[0,T]
and
w_{l}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}
with
\Re w_{l}\in\mathbf{B}_{\kappa}^{z_{l}}
and
N^{-1+\epsilon}\leq|\Im w_{l}|\leq 1
, for any fixed
\xi,\kappa,\epsilon>0
. Then (
S1.6
) follows from (
S1.8
) as explained in the proof of Proposition
4.6
presented in Supplementary Section
A.3
. By Proposition
4.2
and (
4.9
), (
S1.8
) holds for any
t\in[0,T]
and
w_{1},w_{2}
satisfying conditions above with very high probability. The fact that (
S1.8
) holds simultaneously for all values of these parameters with probability at least
1-N^{-D}
, for any fixed
D>0
, follows by a standard grid argument which proceeds by choosing sufficiently dense meshes for
t
,
w_{1}
,
w_{2}
, applying Proposition
4.2
to each of the mesh points, and then extending these result to the rest of the parameter values by Hölder continuity of the lhs. of (
S1.8
). This finishes the verification of (
S1.6
).
∎

It remains to prove Theorem
S1.1
.

Proof of Theorem
S1.1
.

The proof of (
S1.7
) follows by combining
[
14
, Proposition 4.5,4.6]
, as done in the proof of
[
14
, Theorem 4.1]
. The only difference in this case is that we have a better control on the eigenvector overlaps in (
S1.6
), and thus obtaining an explicit dependence in
t
,
R
, and
|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}
in the estimates in Proposition 4.5. We now explain the main steps of this proof, but omit several details that can be found in
[
14
]
. To keep the presentation simpler we assume that the limiting eigenvalue density at
x=0
is the same for
\lambda_{i}^{z_{l}}
and
\mu_{i}^{(l)}
; if this is not the case, it can be achieved by a simple time re-scaling, we omit the details for brevity.

To study the closeness of
\lambda_{i}^{z_{l}}(t)
and
\mu_{i}^{(l)}(t)
in (
S1.4
) we introduce the interpolating process (see e.g.
[
64
]
)

(S1.9)

\mathrm{d}x_{i}^{z_{l}}(\alpha,t)=\frac{\mathrm{d}b_{i}^{z_{l}}(\alpha,t)}{\sqrt{2N}}+\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{1}{x_{i}^{z_{l}}(\alpha,t)-x_{j}^{z_{l}}(\alpha,t)}\mathrm{d}t,\quad l=1,2,\quad\alpha\in[0,1],

with initial data
x_{i}^{z_{l}}(\alpha,0)=\alpha\lambda_{i}^{z_{l}}+(1-\alpha)\mu_{i}^{(l)}
, and
b_{i}^{z_{l}}(\alpha,t):=\alpha b_{i}^{z_{l}}(t)+(1-\alpha)\beta_{i}^{(l)}(t)
.

Since
z_{1},z_{2}
are fixed and once the coupling in (
S1.5
) is performed,
the index
l=1,2
is also fixed, from now on we may drop the
z_{l}
dependence of
x_{i}^{z_{l}}(\alpha,t)
from the notation and use
x_{i}(\alpha,t)
throughout this section. To further simplify the notation we consider
l=1
, so that in the computations below the only
(\sqrt{C(t)})_{ij}
will appear are for
i,j\leq R
. Additionally, since the whole analysis is performed for a fixed
\alpha\in[0,1]
, we may often omit the
\alpha
-dependence from the notation.

The well-posedness of (
S1.9
) and the fact that its tangential flow
u_{i}(t)=u_{i}(\alpha,t):=\partial_{\alpha}x_{i}(\alpha,t)
are well defined follows from
[
29
, Appendix A]
and
[
14
, Appendix B]
. In the following we will show that
|u_{i}(t)|\leq rhs.\,\eqref{eq:desbnew1}
, which immediately gives the desired bound in (
S1.7
) by integrating in
\alpha
. By differentiating (
S1.9
) in
\alpha
, one sees that
u_{i}(t)
is the solution of
15
15

15

Here
{\bm{u}}
should not be confused with the singular vectors introduced below (
S1.1
). A similar comment applies to
{\bm{v}}
below.
(here
|i|,|j|\leq N
)

(S1.10)

\mathrm{d}{\bm{u}}(t)=\big(\mathcal{B}{\bm{u}}\big)(t)\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\mathrm{d}{\bm{\xi}}}{\sqrt{N}},\qquad\quad(\mathcal{B}{\bm{u}})_{i}:=\sum_{j}\mathcal{B}_{ij}(u_{j}-u_{i}),\quad\xi_{i}:=\partial_{\alpha}b_{i}(\alpha,t),

where
\partial_{\alpha}b_{i}(\alpha,t)=b_{i}^{z_{l}}(t)-\beta_{i}^{(l)}(t)
and

\mathcal{B}_{ij}=\mathcal{B}_{ij}(\alpha,t):=\frac{1}{N(x_{i}(\alpha,t)-x_{j}(\alpha,t))^{2}}.

Note that for the stochastic term in (
S1.10
) we have (cf. with
[
14
, Assumption 4.2]
)

(S1.11)

\mathrm{d}\big[\xi_{i},\xi_{j}\big]_{s}=\mathrm{d}\big[b_{i}-\beta_{i},b_{j}-\beta_{j}\big]_{s}\sim\begin{cases}\big[(\sqrt{C(s)}-I)^{2}\big]_{i,j}\mathrm{d}s&\mathrm{for}|i|,|j|\leq R,\\
\delta_{ij}\mathrm{d}s&\mathrm{otherwise},\end{cases}

with

(S1.12)

\big|\big[(\sqrt{C(s)}-I)^{2}\big]_{i,j}\big|\lesssim\frac{R}{(N|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2})^{2}}.

This last bound follows from

(S1.13)

\begin{split}\big[(\sqrt{C(s)}-I)^{2}\big]_{ii}&=\sum_{l}(\sqrt{\lambda_{l}}-1)^{2}|\psi_{l}(i)|^{2}\leq\sum_{l}(\lambda_{l}-1)^{2}|\psi_{l}(i)|^{2}\\
&=\big[(C(s)-I)^{2}\big]_{ii}\leq\sum_{j}\big|(C(s)-I)_{ij}\big|^{2}\lesssim\frac{R}{(N|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2})^{2}},\end{split}

and a simple Schwarz inequality for off-diagonal terms.

To show that
|u_{i}(t)|
is bounded by the rhs. of (
S1.7
) we will follow two main steps. In particular, in the first step, we will study an analog of (
S1.10
) without the stochastic term, as it was done in
[
14
, Proposition 4.6]
. Then, in the second step we will show that the stochastic term in (
S1.10
) can in fact be removed if we assume that the
\lambda
and
\mu
flows have the same initial condition, i.e. for
{\bm{u}}_{i}(0)=0
; this will be achieved by two further intermediate steps by first estimating the long-range contribution and then studying the short-range one.

Let
{\bm{v}}(t)
be the solution of the analog of (
S1.10
) without the stochastic term, i.e.

(S1.14)

\mathrm{d}{\bm{v}}(t)=\big(\mathcal{B}{\bm{v}}\big)(t)\mathrm{d}t,

with two different initial datas
{\bm{v}}_{i}(0)=\mathrm{sgn}(i)(i^{1/2}/N)
or
{\bm{v}}_{i}(0)=\mathrm{sgn}(i)(i/N)^{2}
(see the paragraph after the proof of
[
14
, Lemma 4.21]
for more details). Then, by
[
14
, Proposition 4.6]
, it follows that
16
16

16

We point out that in the current setting we could improve the rhs. of (
S1.15
) by replacing
1/\sqrt{Nt}
with
1/(Nt)
, as our initial conditions satisfy rigidity bounds (while in
[
14
]
only a weaker condition was assumed). This confirms what was expected in
[
14
, Remark 4.19]
.

(S1.15)

\big|{\bm{v}}_{i}(t)\big|\lesssim N^{\xi}\frac{|i|}{N}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{Nt}}+\frac{|i|}{N}+t\right),\qquad\quad i\in[N],

with very high probability. The combination of these two steps will give the desired result.

We are thus left with giving the details of the proof of the first step. Let
{\bm{u}}(t)
be the solution of (
S1.10
), with
{\bm{u}}_{i}(0)=0
, and define the short- and long-range operators:

(S1.16)

(\mathcal{S}{\bm{u}})_{i}:=\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{L}}^{c},\atop j\neq i}\mathcal{B}_{ij}(u_{j}-u_{i}),\qquad\quad(\mathcal{L}{\bm{u}})_{i}:=\sum_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{L}}}\mathcal{B}_{ij}(u_{j}-u_{i}).

Here
0<\ell\leq R/100
is an
N
-dependent scale which we will choose later in the proof, and

\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{L}}:=\{(i,j):|i-j|>\ell,\min(|i|,|j|)\leq cN\},

for a small fixed
c>0
. We define the vector
{\bm{w}}\in\mathbf{R}^{N}
as the solution of the following evolution with the short-range operator

(S1.17)

\mathrm{d}{\bm{w}}(t)=\big(\mathcal{S}{\bm{w}}\big)(t)\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\mathrm{d}{\bm{\xi}}}{\sqrt{N}},\qquad\quad{\bm{w}}(0)=0.

We now first show that the contribution of the long-range part is negligible and then give a bound on
{\bm{w}}(t)
using a weighted
\ell^{2}
estimate. All the estimate below hold with very high probability, even if not stated explicitly. First, by
[
14
, Lemma 4.12]
we have

(S1.18)

\lVert{\bm{w}}(t)\rVert_{\infty}\lesssim N^{\xi}\sqrt{\frac{t}{N}},

with very high probability. Then, using Duhamel’s formula we write (see
[
14
, Eq.(4.24)–(4.26)]
)

(S1.19)

{\bm{u}}(t)={\bm{w}}(t)+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{S}}(s,t)\mathcal{L}{\bm{w}}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s={\bm{w}}(t)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{(\log N)^{2}\sqrt{Nt^{3}}}{\ell}\right),

where
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{S}}
is the propagator of
\partial_{t}{\bm{f}}_{t}=(\mathcal{S}{\bm{f}})_{t}
, and the error term is meant entry-wise. This shows that the long-range part can be neglected and so that it is enough to study
{\bm{w}}(t)
to obtain the desired bound on
{\bm{u}}(t)
.

Next, to show that the effect of the stochastic term in (
S1.17
) is also negligible, we use a weighted
\ell^{2}
-bound. More precisely, one studies the evolution of (here
\zeta:=N/\ell
and
\chi
is a non-negative, symmetric, and smooth cut-off with
\int\chi=1
)

(S1.20)

X_{s}:=\sum_{i}\phi_{i}(s){\bm{w}}(s),\qquad\quad\phi_{i}(s):=e^{-\zeta\psi(x_{i}(s))},\qquad\quad\psi(x):=\zeta\int\min\{|x-y|,c\}\chi(\zeta y)\,\mathrm{d}y

as in
[
14
, Lemma 4.13]
. The main difference in this proof compared to
[
14
, Lemma 4.13]
is that the bound in
[
14
, Assumption 4.2]
can be improved to (
S1.11
)–(
S1.12
) for
K=R
, giving a more explicit bound (which was already obtained in
[
14
]
, but not exploited). Using the notation of
[
14
, Lemma 4.13]
, choosing
\ell=R/100
,
N^{\omega_{K}}=R
, and replacing
N^{-\tilde{\omega}}
with
R/(N|z_{1}-\penalty\ \!z_{2}|^{2})^{2}
from (
S1.12
), we obtain

(S1.21)

\begin{split}X_{t}&\lesssim\frac{t\log N}{NR}+\frac{\sqrt{t}}{N^{3/2}R}+\frac{\sqrt{tR^{2}}}{N^{3/2}(N|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2})}+\frac{t}{NR^{1/4}}+\frac{tR^{2}}{N(N|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2})^{2}}\\
&\lesssim\frac{t}{N}\left(\frac{1}{R^{1/4}}+\frac{R^{2}}{(N|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2})^{2}}\right)+\frac{\sqrt{t}}{N^{3/2}}\left(\frac{1}{R}+\frac{R}{N|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}}\right).\end{split}

Next, using
R<N|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}
and that
Nt\gg 1
, we obtain

(S1.22)

\frac{t}{N}\left(\frac{1}{R^{1/4}}+\frac{R^{2}}{(N|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2})^{2}}\right)+\frac{\sqrt{t}}{N^{3/2}}\left(\frac{1}{R}+\frac{R}{N|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}}\right)\lesssim\frac{t}{N}\left(\frac{1}{R^{1/4}}+\frac{R}{N|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}}\right).

We have thus concluded that for
|i|\leq R
we have

(S1.23)

|w_{i}(t)|\lesssim X_{t}^{1/2}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{t}{N}}\left(\frac{1}{R^{1/4}}+\frac{R}{N|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}}\right)^{1/2}.

Finally, using that for
|i|\leq R
we have (schematically)

(S1.24)

\big|\lambda_{i}^{z_{l}}(t)-\mu_{i}^{(l)}(t)\big|\lesssim\int_{0}^{1}\big(|v_{i}(t,\alpha)|+|w_{i}(t,\alpha)|\big)\,\mathrm{d}\alpha,

and combining (
S1.15
), (
S1.19
), and (
S1.23
), we obtain the bound (
S1.7
) and thus conclude the proof.
∎

Remark S1.2

(Possible improvement on (
S1.7
))
.

The main ingredient in the proof of Theorem
S1.1
is an
\ell^{2}
-estimate to show that the stochastic term in (
S1.10
) can be neglected, at the price of an explicit error depending on
t,R,|z_{1}-z_{2}|
. Inspired by
[
30
,
74
]
, we believe, that this error can be improved complementing the
\ell^{2}
-estimate with a Nash-argument which gives the
\ell^{2}\to\ell^{\infty}
contractivity of the kernel
\mathcal{B}
. Following
[
74
, Sections 6-7]
, it seems that the best possible error one could possibly obtain with this procedure is something of the form (for
T_{1}<T_{2}<T
)

(S1.25)

|\lambda_{i}(T_{2})-\mu_{i}(T_{2})|\prec\frac{1}{N}\left(\frac{NT_{1}}{R}+T_{2}-T_{1}\right)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N(T_{2}-T_{1})}}\times\mathrm{rhs.\,\,\eqref{eq:desbnew1}\,with\,t=T_{1}}.

In addition, by Footnote
16
, the term
1/\sqrt{Nt}
in (
S1.7
) can be replaced with
1/(Nt)
. This bound would clearly give a better error in Proposition
3.7
. Carefully inspecting the proof of Theorem
2.4
one can see that with this better error
q_{0}
. Preliminary calculations indicate that with this better error it is possible to choose
q_{0}=1/3
. This is a significant improvement compared to
q_{0}=1/20
, however, it is still very far from the optimal choice
q_{0}=1
. For this reason we do not pursue the details of this proof and present all the details just to obtain
q_{0}=1/20
.

Appendix S2
Proof of the multi-resolvent local laws from Section
4

In this section we prove local laws from Propositions
4.2
,
4.5
, and
4.8
. Our main focus is on Proposition
4.2
in the special case
b=0
, since this is our main novelty. Meanwhile, the general case
b\in[0,1]
follows by a minor adjustment of this special case, and the proof of Proposition
4.5
follows by a minor modification of the proof of
[
20
, Theorem 3.4]
using our new propagator bound from Lemma
4.3
(see also Lemma
S2.9
later), allowing to extend the result of
[
20
, Theorem 3.4]
from the imaginary axis to the entire bulk regime.

This section is structured in the following way. First, by the end of Section
S2.2
we prove Proposition
4.2
for
b=0
. Then, in Sections
S2.5
and
S2.4
we prove Proposition
4.5
and Proposition
4.2
for general
b\in[0,1]
.

Recall that
X_{0}
is a complex i.i.d. matrix whose single-entry distribution does not need to be Gauss-divisible, whereas
X=\sqrt{1-\mathfrak{s}^{2}}X_{0}+\mathfrak{s}\widetilde{X}
contains a Gaussian component. Consider the matrix-valued Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process starting from
X_{0}
:

(S2.1)

\mathrm{d}X_{t}=-\frac{1}{2}X_{t}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\mathrm{d}B_{t},

where the entries of
B_{t}
are independent standard complex-valued Brownian motions. Then

(S2.2)

X_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathrm{e}^{-t/2}X_{0}+\sqrt{1-\mathrm{e}^{-t}}\widetilde{X},\quad\forall t\geq 0.

In particular,
X_{t}
and
X
follow the same distribution for
t=|\log(1-\mathfrak{s}^{2})|
. Denote

(S2.3)

G_{t}^{z}(w):=\begin{pmatrix}-w&X_{t}-z\\
X_{t}^{*}-\overline{z}&-w\end{pmatrix}^{-1},\quad\forall\,z\in\mathbf{C},\,w\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}.

With this notations the statement of Proposition
4.2
is equivalent to the 2-resolvent averaged local law for
G_{t}^{z_{1}}(w_{1})
,
G^{z_{2}}_{t}(w_{2})
for a fixed
t>0
. From now on we adopt this formulation.

For convenience, we slightly modify the notations introduced in Proposition
4.2
and use
X
to denote a general
N\times N
complex i.i.d. matrix satisfying Assumption
2.1
(i) without requiring Gauss-divisibility. The original meaning of
X
will appear only in the conclusion of the proof of Proposition
4.2
around (
S2.27
)–(
S2.28
), so no ambiguity will arise. We also denote the resolvent associated to
X
by
G^{z}(w)
and set

(S2.4)

G_{j}:=G^{z_{j}}(w_{j}),\quad\eta_{j}:=|\Im w_{j}|,\,\,\,j=1,2\quad\text{and}\quad\eta_{*}:=\eta_{1}\wedge\eta_{2}\wedge 1.

for
z_{j}\in\mathbf{C}
and
w_{j}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}
.

The proof of Proposition
4.2
proceeds in two steps. First we establish the global law, i.e. we prove (
4.12
) for
X_{0}
and for spectral parameters
w_{1},w_{2}
with imaginary parts at least of order one. In the next step, we extend this result dynamically down to the real line in the bulk regime. This is achieved by complementing the process
\{X_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
by the deterministic evolution of
z_{j}
’s and
w_{j}
’s along the
characteristic flow
for
j=1,2
, and then tracking the resolvents under the combined time dependence arising both from
X_{t}
and
z_{j},w_{j}
. Our argument is a special case of the
zigzag
strategy (introduced in
[
22
]
, see also
[
35
,
21
]
). Besides the global law and the
zig
(characteristic flow) step described above, the full zigzag method includes also the
zag
step which removes the Gaussian component from
X_{t}
by the means of the Green function comparison. The zig and the zag steps are repeated alternatingly, gradually decreasing the imaginary part of the spectral parameter. This strategy allows to prove multi-resolvent local laws for matrices without a Gaussian component, and can be also applied in our set-up to prove (
4.12
) with
b=0
for a general i.i.d. matrix
X
. However, since Proposition
4.2
is formulated for Gauss-divisible ensembles, we omit the zag step and perform only one zig step.

The characteristic flow method does not allow to prove (
4.12
) by operating solely with the quantities of the form
\langle G_{1}B_{1}G_{2}B_{2}\rangle
for some observables
B_{1},B_{2}
, as it creates longer products of resolvents. To close the bounds on the products of two resolvents we also need to follow the evolution of products of three and four observables, albeit with less precision. Specifically, we consider resolvent chains of the following form:

(S2.5)

\left\langle G_{1}B_{1}G_{2}B_{2}G_{1}^{(*)}B_{3}\right\rangle\quad\text{and}\quad\left\langle G_{1}B_{1}G_{2}B_{2}G_{1}^{(*)}B_{3}G_{2}^{(*)}B_{4}\right\rangle.

Here
G_{j}^{(*)}
indicates both choices
G_{j}
and
G_{j}^{*}
. Throughout this section we will use the same convention for the spectral parameters, denoting by
w_{j}^{(*)}
both choices
w_{j}
and
\overline{w}_{j}
. Moreover, these choices for
G_{j}
and
w_{j}
each time are performed consistently due to the relation
(G^{z_{j}}(w_{j}))^{*}=G^{z_{j}}(\overline{w}_{j})
. Note that the resolvent chains in (
S2.5
) may contain simultaneously
G_{1}
and
G_{1}^{*}
(and similarly for
G_{2}
), since the first
G_{1}
always comes without the conjugation, while the second one may be taken equal to
G_{1}^{*}
.

Proposition S2.1

(Global law)
.

Fix (small)
N
-independent constants
\delta,\varepsilon>0
. Let
X
be a complex
N\times N
i.i.d. matrix satisfying Assumption
2.1
(i) and let
G^{z}(w)
be defined as in (
3.19
). For
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbf{C}
and
w_{1},w_{2}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}
denote
G_{j}:=G^{z_{j}}(w_{j})
,
j=1,2
. We have

(S2.6)

\displaystyle\left\langle\left(G_{1}B_{1}G_{2}-M_{12}^{B_{1}}(w_{1},w_{2})\right)B_{2}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\prec\frac{1}{N},

(S2.7)

\displaystyle\left\langle\left(G_{1}B_{1}G_{2}B_{2}G_{1}^{(*)}-M_{121}^{B_{1},B_{2}}\big(w_{1},w_{2},w_{1}^{(*)}\big)\right)B_{3}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\prec\frac{1}{N},

(S2.8)

\displaystyle\left\langle\left(G_{1}B_{1}G_{2}B_{2}G_{1}^{(*)}B_{3}G_{2}^{(*)}-M_{1212}^{B_{1},B_{2},B_{3}}\big(w_{1},w_{2},w_{1}^{(*)},w_{2}^{(*)}\big)\right)B_{4}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\prec\frac{1}{N},

uniformly in
z_{j}\in(1-\delta)\bf{D}
,
w_{j}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}
with
|\Im w_{j}|\geq\varepsilon
for
j=1,2
, and
B_{i}\in\mathrm{span}\{E_{\pm},F^{(*)}\}
for
i\in[4]
.

Proof of Proposition
S2.1
.

The two-resolvent global law (
S2.6
) is a well-established result, e.g. this is a special case of
[
33
, Theorem 5.2]
. The proof of (
S2.7
)–(
S2.8
) follows the same strategy as the global multi-resolvent laws for Wigner matrices
[
32
, Appendix B]
, with the simplification that we do not track the dependence of the bounds (
S2.6
)–(
S2.8
) on
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
. In fact this dependence gives additional smallness in the regime
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\gg 1
, but is not needed here.

We present a sketch of the proof of (
S2.7
), referring to
[
32
, Appendix B]
for further details. Although
[
32
]
concerns the case of Wigner matrices, where the self-energy operator
\mathcal{S}
is given by the normalized trace rather than the sum of two traces as in (
3.28
), this does not affect the proof. The argument is based on the Gaussian renormalization technique (denoted by underline) analogous to the one used in the proof of Proposition
3.4
. Consider for definiteness the case where there is no star in (
S2.7
). Writing each observable simply by
B
for brevity, we obtain from (
3.37
) that

(S2.9)

\begin{split}\langle(G_{1}BG_{2}BG_{1}-M_{121})B\rangle&=\langle(G_{2}BG_{1}-M_{21})AB\rangle+\sigma\langle(G_{1}-M_{1})E_{\sigma}\rangle\langle G_{1}BG_{2}BG_{1}AE_{\sigma}\rangle\\
&+\sigma\langle M_{12}E_{\sigma}\rangle\langle(G_{2}BG_{1}-M_{21})AE_{\sigma}\rangle+\sigma\langle M_{21}AE_{\sigma}\rangle\langle(G_{1}BG_{2}-M_{12})E_{\sigma}\rangle\\
&+\sigma\langle(G_{1}-M_{1})AE_{\sigma}\rangle\langle G_{1}BG_{2}BG_{1}E_{\sigma}\rangle-\langle A\underline{WG_{1}BG_{2}BG_{1}}\rangle,\end{split}

where
A:=(\mathcal{B}_{11}^{-1}[B^{*}])^{*}M_{1}
(for more details see a similar calculation in (
7.44
)–(
7.47
)). In (
S2.9
) we omitted superscripts of deterministic approximations as all of them are given by the appropriate number of
B
’s. Observe that
\|G_{j}\|\leq\varepsilon^{-1}
and that
\|\mathcal{B}_{11}^{-1}\|\lesssim\varepsilon^{-1}
by (
4.21
) in the global regime (here we need this bound only for the one-body stability operator
\mathcal{B}_{11}
, but it holds for
\mathcal{B}_{12}
as well), which implies that
\|A\|\lesssim 1
. Using the global law for one- and two-resolvent chains, and estimating longer chains by operator norm, we get from (
S2.9
) that

(S2.10)

\langle(G_{1}BG_{2}BG_{1}-M_{121})B\rangle=-\langle A\underline{WG_{1}BG_{2}BG_{1}}\rangle+\mathcal{O}_{\prec}(N^{-1}).

Finally, using a minimalistic cumulant expansion as in
[
32
, Eq.(B.4)-(B.8)]
, we show that the first term in the rhs. of (
S2.10
) is stochastically dominated by
N^{-1}
and conclude the proof of (
S2.7
). The proof of (
S2.8
) follows the same strategy and relies on the global laws for shorter chains.
∎

To propagate the global estimates (
S2.6
)–(
S2.8
) towards the real line, we consider the deterministic evolution of
z
and
w
along the
characteristic flow
:

(S2.11)

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}z_{t}=-\frac{1}{2}z_{t},\qquad\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}w_{t}=-\frac{1}{2}w_{t}-\langle M^{z_{t}}(w_{t})\rangle,

where
M^{z}(w)
is defined in (
3.30
). While the precise initial conditions of this flow will be specified later, it is important to keep in mind that
(z_{j},w_{j})
appearing in (
4.12
) is not the initial condition, but the
target
of the characteristic flow, i.e. the value at the
final time

T
, which we choose to be a small fixed constant independent of
N
. An important feature of (
S2.11
) is that
|\Im w_{t}|
decreases with time, which allows us to propagate the local law estimates from the global regime
|\Im w|\gtrsim 1
towards the real axis.

In fact, (
S2.11
) is a special case of the characteristic flow for the deformed Wigner matrix model studied in
[
23
]
. Let
W^{\#}=(w^{\#}_{ab})_{a,b=1}^{2N}
be a
2N\times 2N
matrix from the
Gaussian unitary ensemble
(GUE), i.e.
W^{\#}
is Hermitian, its entries are independent up to the symmetry constraint, and for
a,b\in[2N]
with
a\neq b
(respectively,
a=b
)
w^{\#}_{ab}
is a centered complex (respectively, real) Gaussian with variance
(2N)^{-1/2}
. One may relax the Gaussianity assumption on the entries of
W^{\#}
in the way similar to Assumption
2.1
(i), but since only the second order moment structure of
W^{\#}
plays a role here, this generalization is not needed. For
z\in\mathbf{C}
, let
Z\in\mathbf{C}^{(2N)\times(2N)}
be defined as in (
3.18
). Then the matrix Dyson equation for the deformed Wigner matrix
H:=W^{\#}-Z
is given by

(S2.12)

-(M^{\#}(w))^{-1}=w+Z+\langle M^{\#}(w)\rangle,\quad\Im M^{\#}(w)\Im w>0,\quad w\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R},

see e.g.
[
23
, Eq.(1.4)]
, where
M^{\#}(w)\in\mathbf{C}^{(2N)\times(2N)}
. We compare (
S2.12
) to the MDE for the Hermitization (
3.29
). Since all diagonal entries of
M^{z}(w)
coincide by (
3.30
), we obtain from (
3.28
) that
\mathcal{S}[M^{z}(w)]=\langle M^{z}(w)\rangle
. Therefore, (
S2.12
) and (
3.29
) are identical, and by uniqueness of solutions to both equations we conclude that
M^{\#}(w)=M^{z}(w)
. In particular, the characteristic flows for these two models coincide. This allows us to borrow the characteristic flow analysis from
[
23
]
. However, we remark that the comparison between the deformed Wigner matrices with the deformation
-Z
and the Hermitization model does not extend to the two-body analysis: the two-body stability operators for these models differ, since for deformed Wigner matrices the self-energy operator is given by the normalized trace, whereas in our Hermitization setting
\mathcal{S}
is a sum of two traces (see (
3.28
)).

To prove Proposition
4.2
it is not sufficient to keep track of one trajectory for each spectral parameter, but instead we need to consider a family of trajectories. This leads us to the definition of the
bulk-restricted spectral domains
, which is a special case of
[
23
, Definition 4.2]
introduced in the context of deformed Wigner matrices.

Definition S2.2

(Bulk-restricted spectral domains)
.

Fix (small)
\epsilon,\kappa>0
, the final time
T>0
and
z_{T}\in\mathbf{C}
. Recall the definition of the bulk
\mathbf{B}^{z_{T}}_{\kappa}
from (
3.33
). We define the
bulk-restricted spectral domain
at time
T
by
17
17

17

In fact, the domain defined in (
S2.13
) depends on
T
only through
z_{T}
. However, we retain the
T
-dependence in this notation (and in
z_{T}
itself) to keep it consistent with the notation for the time-dependent domain introduced in (
S2.15
).

(S2.13)

\Omega_{\kappa,\epsilon,T}^{z_{T}}:=\left\{w\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}\,:\,|\Im w|\geq\max\left\{N^{\epsilon}(N\rho^{z_{T}})^{-1},\mathrm{d}(\Re w,\mathbf{B}^{z_{T}}_{\kappa})\right\}\right\}.

Next, for
w_{T}\in\Omega_{\kappa,\epsilon,T}^{z_{T}}
define the backward evolution operator associated to (
S2.11
) by

(S2.14)

\mathfrak{F}_{t,T}^{z_{T}}(w_{T}):=w_{t},\quad\forall\,t\in[0,T].

Finally, the bulk-restricted spectral domain at time
t\in[0,T)
is given by

(S2.15)

\Omega_{\kappa,\epsilon,t}^{z_{T}}:=\mathfrak{F}_{t,T}^{z_{T}}\left(\Omega_{\kappa,\epsilon,T}^{z_{T}}\right).

Note that for any
t\in[0,T]
the upper index of
\Omega_{\kappa,\epsilon,t}^{z_{T}}
always denotes the target value of the Hermitization parameter, that is
z_{T}
, rather than its value at the intermediate time
t
. In the following lemma we collect the properties of the characteristic flow which will be later used in the proof of Proposition
4.2
. The proof of this lemma is elementary and thus omitted.

Lemma S2.3

(Elementary properties of the characteristic flow)
.

Fix
T>0
,
z_{T}\in\mathbf{C}
and
w_{T}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}
. Let
z_{t}
,
w_{t}
be the solution to (
S2.11
),
t\in[0,T]
, and denote
\eta_{t}:=|\Im w_{t}|
. Then the following holds.

(1)

The map
t\mapsto\eta_{t}
is monotone decreasing.

(2)

For any
t\in[0,T]
we have
M^{z_{t}}(w_{t})=\mathrm{e}^{t/2}M^{z_{0}}(w_{0})
.

(3)

The solution to (
S2.11
) is explicitly given by

(S2.16)

z_{t}=\mathrm{e}^{-t/2}z_{0},\quad w_{t}=\mathrm{e}^{-t/2}w_{0}-2m^{z_{0}}(w_{0})\sinh\frac{t}{2},\quad\forall\,t\in[0,T].

Assume additionally that
w_{T}\in\Omega_{\kappa,\epsilon,T}^{z_{T}}
for some (small)
\kappa,\epsilon>0
.

(4)

For any
s,t\in[0,T]
,
s\leq t
, we have
\eta_{s}\sim\eta_{t}+|s-t|
.

(5)

For any
a>1
and
t\in[0,T]
we have
18
18

18

A similar property holds also for general
w_{T}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}
with the only modification that the integrands in (
S2.17
) should be multiplied by
\rho_{s}:=\rho^{z_{s}}(w_{s})
. For
w_{T}
in the bulk-restricted spectral domain this additional factor is of order one whenever
\eta_{s}\lesssim 1
.

(S2.17)

\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{(\eta_{s}\wedge 1)^{a}}\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\frac{1}{(\eta_{t}\wedge 1)^{a-1}},\quad\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{\eta_{s}\wedge 1}\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\log\frac{\eta_{0}\wedge 1}{\eta_{t}\wedge 1}.

To present the core ingredient for the proof of Proposition
4.2
, which consists in propagation of the bounds (
S2.6
)–(
S2.8
) down to the real axis, we introduce some additional notation. We set

(S2.18)

G_{j,t}:=G^{z_{j,t}}_{t}(w_{j,t}),\quad j=1,2,

where
z_{j,t}
and
w_{j,t}
follow (
S2.11
). Here we use the convention that whenever the arguments of
G^{z}_{t}(w)
are omitted, they are meant to be time-dependent. Using the same guiding principle, we denote the deterministic approximation of
G^{z_{1,t}}_{t}(w_{1})B_{1}G^{z_{2,t}}_{t}(w_{2})
by
M_{12,t}^{B_{1}}(w_{1},w_{2})
, and abbreviate

(S2.19)

M_{12,t}^{B_{1}}:=M_{12,t}^{B_{1}}(w_{1,t},w_{2,t}).

The time-dependent deterministic approximations to the resolvent chains in (
S2.5
) are defined in the same way. Finally, we denote by
\mathcal{B}_{12,t}(w_{1},w_{2})
the 2-body stabilty operator (
4.7
) associated to
(z_{j,t},w_{j})
,
j=1,2
, define
\widehat{\beta}_{12,t}(w_{1},w_{2})
as in (
4.8
) but with
\mathcal{B}_{12}(w_{1}^{(*)},w_{2}^{(*)})
replaced by
\mathcal{B}_{12,t}(w_{1}^{(*)},w_{2}^{(*)})
, and set

(S2.20)

\mathcal{B}_{12,t}:=\mathcal{B}_{12,t}(w_{1,t},w_{2,t}),\quad\widehat{\beta}_{12,t}:=\widehat{\beta}_{12,t}(w_{1,t},w_{2,t}),\quad\eta_{j,t}:=|\Im w_{j,t}|,\,j=1,2,\quad\eta_{*,t}:=\eta_{1,t}\wedge\eta_{2,t}\wedge 1.

As usual, local laws come along with the bounds on the corresponding deterministic approximations. In the following statement we present bounds on the deterministic counterparts to the time-dependent resolvent chains.

Proposition S2.4

(Bounds on the deterministic counterparts)
.

Fix (small)
\delta,\epsilon,\kappa>0
and the final time
T>0
. Let
\{\Omega_{\kappa,\epsilon,t}^{z_{T}}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
be constructed as in Definition
S2.2
for
z_{T}\in\mathbf{C}
. It holds that

(S2.21)

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle M_{12,t}^{B_{1}}(w_{1,t},w_{2,t})\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\widehat{\beta}_{12,t}},

(S2.22)

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle M_{121,t}^{B_{1},B_{2}}\big(w_{1,t},w_{2,t},w_{1,t}^{(*)}\big)B_{3}\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\eta_{*,t}\widehat{\beta}_{12,t}},

(S2.23)

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle M_{1212,t}^{B_{1},B_{2},B_{3}}\big(w_{1,t},w_{2,t},w_{1,t}^{(*)},w_{2,t}^{(*)}\big)B_{4}\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\eta_{*,t}(\widehat{\beta}_{12,t})^{2}},

uniformly in
t\in[0,T]
,
z_{j,T}\in\mathrm{e}^{-T/2}(1-\delta)\mathbf{D}
,
w_{j,T}\in\Omega_{\kappa,\epsilon,T}^{z_{T}}
,
j=1,2
, and
B_{i}\in\mathrm{span}\{E_{\pm},F^{(*)}\}
,
i\in[4]
.

The proof of Proposition
S2.4
is presented in Section
S2.1
. Since the lhs. of (
S2.21
)–(
S2.23
) depend on
t
only through
z_{j,t}
and
w_{j,t}
, there is no time-dependent nature in Proposition
S2.4
. Moreover, these bounds remain valid all the way down to the real line in the bulk regime, so the cut-off at the scale
N^{-1+\epsilon}
in
\Omega_{\kappa,\epsilon,T}^{z_{T}}
is not needed. However, we presented Proposition
S2.4
in this form to simplify the comparison with the time-dependent local laws, which we now state.

Proposition S2.5

(Propagation of the local law bounds)
.

Fix (small)
\delta,\epsilon,\kappa>0
. Let the final time
T>0
be sufficiently small and let
\{\Omega_{\kappa,\epsilon,t}^{z_{T}}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
be constructed as in Definition
S2.2
for
z_{T}\in\mathbf{C}
. Assume that for
t=0
it holds that

(S2.24)

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\left(G_{1,t}B_{1}G_{2,t}-M_{12,t}^{B_{1}}(w_{1,t},w_{2,t})\right)B_{2}\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\prec\frac{1}{N\eta_{*,t}\widehat{\beta}_{12,t}},

(S2.25)

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\left(G_{1,t}B_{1}G_{2,t}B_{2}G_{1,t}^{(*)}-M_{121,t}^{B_{1},B_{2}}\big(w_{1,t},w_{2,t},w_{1,t}^{(*)}\big)\right)B_{3}\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\prec\frac{1}{N\eta_{*,t}^{3}}\wedge\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\eta_{*,t}}\eta_{*,t}\widehat{\beta}_{12,t}},

(S2.26)

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\left(G_{1,t}B_{1}G_{2,t}B_{2}G_{1,t}^{(*)}B_{3}G_{2,t}^{(*)}-M_{1212,t}^{B_{1},B_{2},B_{3}}\big(w_{1,t},w_{2,t},w_{1,t}^{(*)},w_{2,t}^{(*)}\big)\right)B_{4}\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\prec\frac{1}{N\eta_{*,t}^{4}}\wedge\frac{1}{\eta_{*,t}(\widehat{\beta}_{12,t})^{2}},

uniformly in
z_{j,T}\in\mathrm{e}^{-T/2}(1-\delta)\mathbf{D}
,
w_{j,T}\in\Omega^{z_{j,T}}_{\kappa,\epsilon,T}
,
j=1,2
, and
B_{i}\in\{E_{+},E_{-},F,F^{*}\}
,
i\in[4]
. Then (
S2.24
)–(
S2.26
) hold uniformly in
t\in[0,T]
,
z_{j,T}
,
w_{j,T}
and
B_{i}
specified above.

Observe that (
S2.24
) improves upon the corresponding deterministic estimate (
S2.21
) by the optimal factor
(N\eta_{*})^{-1}
. In contrast, the gain in (
S2.25
) compared to (
S2.22
) is suboptimal, amounting only to the factor
(N\eta_{*})^{-1/2}
, while (
S2.26
) exhibits no improvement at all in some regimes, e.g. when
\widehat{\beta}_{12,t}\sim 1
and
\eta_{*,t}\ll N^{-1/3}
. Nevertheless, this suboptimal estimates for three- and four-resolvent chains are sufficient to propagate (
S2.24
) in time, as we will show in Section
S2.2
. We also remark that the bounds depending only on
\eta_{*,t}
in the rhs. of (
S2.25
) and (
S2.26
) are needed for technical reasons and can be propagated without the more intricate estimates involving
\widehat{\beta}_{12,t}
, while the latter ones cannot be propagated on their own.

Given Propositions
S2.1
and
S2.5
, we now prove Proposition
4.2
.

Proof of Proposition
4.2
for
b=0
.

Let
T_{0}>0
be such a constant that Proposition
S2.5
holds for any
T\leq T_{0}
. Without loss of generality we may assume that
|\log(1-\mathfrak{s}^{2})|\leq T_{0}
, otherwise we take sufficiently small
\mathfrak{t}\in(0,\mathfrak{s})
and observe that

(S2.27)

X=\sqrt{1-\mathfrak{s}^{2}}X_{0}+\mathfrak{s}\widetilde{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\sqrt{1-\mathfrak{t}^{2}}X_{0}^{\prime}+\mathfrak{t}\widetilde{X}

for some i.i.d. matrix
X_{0}^{\prime}
satisfying Assumption
2.1
(i). Similarly we may also assume that
\mathrm{e}^{T_{0}/2}(1-\delta)<(1-\delta/2)
. Take
T:=|\log(1-\mathfrak{s}^{2})|
and let
\kappa,\epsilon>0
be as in Proposition
4.2
. From Lemma
S2.3
(4) we have that

(S2.28)

\Omega^{z_{T}}_{\kappa,\epsilon,0}\subset\{w\in\mathbf{C}\,:\,|\Im w|\geq\varepsilon\},\quad\forall\,z_{T}\in(1-\delta)\mathbf{D},

for some
\varepsilon>0
. Therefore, (
S2.24
)–(
S2.26
) hold for
t=0
by the global law from Proposition
S2.1
. Finally, by Proposition
S2.5
these estimates are valid for
t=T
, which in combination with (
S2.2
) completes the proof of Proposition
4.2
.
∎

S2.1.
Proof of Proposition
S2.4
: bounds on the deterministic counterparts

The bound (
S2.21
) on the two-resolvent deterministic approximation follows directly from (
4.9
), so we are left with (
S2.22
)–(
S2.23
). First we establish these bounds in a weaker form, replacing
\widehat{\beta}_{12}
by the smaller parameter
\eta_{*}
, and formulate them in the time-independent way. Then in the separate argument we improve these estimates by replacing back the appropriate number of
\eta_{*}
’s by
\widehat{\beta}_{12}
’s.

Lemma S2.6

(Weak bounds on the deterministic counterparts)
.

Fix
\delta>0
. Uniformly in
z_{j}\in(1-\delta)\mathbf{D}
,
w_{j}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}
,
j=1,2
, and
B_{i}\in\mathrm{span}\{E_{\pm},F^{(*)}\}
,
i\in[4]
it holds that

(S2.29)

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle M_{121}^{B_{1},B_{2}}\big(w_{1},w_{2},w_{1}^{(*)}\big)B_{3}\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\eta_{*}^{2}},

(S2.30)

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle M_{1212}^{B_{1},B_{2},B_{3}}\big(w_{1},w_{2},w_{1}^{(*)},w_{2}^{(*)}\big)B_{4}\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\eta_{*}^{3}},

where
\eta_{*}:=|\Im w_{1}|\wedge|\Im w_{2}|\wedge 1
.

Proof of Lemma
S2.6
.

The proof of (
S2.29
)–(
S2.30
) relies on the
tensorization argument
(also known as the
meta argument
), see e.g.
[
78
, Section 2.6]
and
[
24
, Proof of Lemma D.1]
. Although this argument is standard and well-known in the literature, we keep it here for the reader convenience, since it is frequently used in the proof of Proposition
4.2
and later in the proof of Proposition
3.4
. Fix
N\in\mathbf{N}
,
z_{j}\in\mathbf{C}
and
w_{j}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}
,
j=1,2
. For
d\in\mathbf{N}
let
X^{(d)}=(x^{(d)}_{ab})_{a,b=1}^{Nd}
be an
Nd\times Nd
i.i.d. matrix such that
x^{(d)}_{ab}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}(Nd)^{-1/2}\chi
, where
\chi
satisfies Assumption
2.1
(i). Recall the definition of
Z_{j}\in\mathbf{C}^{(2N)\times(2N)}
from (
3.18
) and denote

(S2.31)

G_{j}^{(d)}:=\left(W^{(d)}-Z_{j}^{(d)}-w_{j}\right)^{-1},\quad\text{where}\quad Z_{j}^{(d)}:=Z_{j}\otimes I_{d}\in\mathbf{C}^{(2Nd)\times(2Nd)},\,\,j=1,2.,

where
W^{(d)}
is defined as in (
3.18
), and
I_{d}
is an operator acting identically on
\mathbf{C}^{d}
. As it immediately follows from this construction, the deterministic approximations to the products of
G_{j}^{(d)}
’s and observables from the set
\mathrm{span}\{E_{\pm},F^{(*)}\}
, which are viewed as
(2Nd)\times(2Nd)
matrices, have
2\times 2
block-constant structure, and only the size of blocks grows with
d
, while the scalars associated to these blocks are fixed. Thus from the global law (
S2.7
) we have

(S2.32)

\big\langle G_{1}^{(d)}B_{1}^{(d)}G_{2}^{(d)}B_{2}^{(d)}G_{1}^{(d)}B_{3}^{(d)}\big\rangle=\langle M_{121}^{B_{1},B_{2}}B_{3}\rangle+\mathcal{O}\left(c(\eta_{*})(Nd)^{-1}\right),\quad B_{i}^{(d)}:=B_{i}\otimes I_{d},\,i\in[3],

where
c(\eta_{*})
is an implicit constant which depends on
\eta_{*}
, but not on
d
. For simplicity of presentation we further slightly abuse the notation and denote
B_{i}^{(d)}
again by
B_{i}
.

The Cauchy-Schwarz inequality along with the Ward identity imply

(S2.33)

\left|\big\langle G_{1}^{(d)}B_{1}G_{2}^{(d)}B_{2}G_{1}^{(d)}B_{3}\big\rangle\right|\leq\eta_{*}^{-1}\big\langle G_{1}^{(d)}B_{1}B_{1}^{*}(G_{1}^{(d)})^{*}B_{3}^{*}B_{3}\big\rangle^{1/2}\big\langle\Im G_{1}^{(d)}B_{2}^{*}\Im G_{2}^{(d)}B_{2}\big\rangle^{1/2}.

Applying (
S2.32
) to the lhs. of (
S2.33
), using the same argument for both chains in the rhs. of (
S2.33
) and taking the limit as
d
goes to infinity, we upper bound
|\langle M_{121}^{B_{1},B_{2}}B_{3}\rangle|
by the product of two deterministic approximations to two-resolvent chains. By (
S2.21
) each of them has an upper bound of order
\eta_{*}^{-1}
. This finishes the proof of (
S2.29
) with the choice, where there is no complex conjugation in the second
w_{2}
, while in the second case the proof is identical. The proof of (
S2.30
) follows the same strategy, but instead of (
S2.33
) makes use of

(S2.34)

\left|\langle G_{1}B_{1}G_{2}B_{2}G_{1}B_{3}G_{2}B_{4}\rangle\right|\leq\eta_{*}^{-1}\langle G_{2}B_{1}^{*}\Im G_{1}B_{1}G_{2}B_{2}B_{2}^{*}\rangle^{1/2}\langle G_{2}B_{3}^{*}\Im G_{1}B_{3}G_{2}B_{4}B_{4}^{*}\rangle^{1/2},

where we omitted index
(d)
in
G_{j}
’s, and relies on (
S2.29
) to bound the deterministic counterparts to the quantities in the rhs. of (
S2.34
).
∎

To prove (
S2.22
)–(
S2.23
), we use the bounds (
S2.29
)–(
S2.30
) as an input and improve them using the characteristic flow method. For this purpose we differentiate the time-dependent deterministic approximations to the three- and four-resolvent chains along the characteristic flow. This idea of proving bounds on deterministic approximations via the characteristic flow was first used in
[
45
, Section 11.2]
in the context of random band matrices.

We remind the reader of the convention introduced above Lemma
7.4
: whenever index
\sigma
appears, it is meant to be summed over
\sigma\in\{\pm\}
.

Lemma S2.7
.

Fix the final time
T>0
. For any
s\in[0,T]
and
z_{j,T}\in\mathbf{C}
,
w_{j,T}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}
,
B_{j}\in\{E_{+},E_{-},F,F^{*}\}
for
j\in[4]
, it holds that

(S2.35)

\displaystyle\partial_{s}\left\langle M_{123,s}^{B_{1},B_{2}}B_{3}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{2}\left\langle M_{123,s}^{B_{1},B_{2}}B_{3}\right\rangle+\sum_{i=1}^{3}\sigma\left\langle M_{i,i+1,s}^{B_{i}}E_{\sigma}\right\rangle\left\langle M_{i+1,i+2,i+3,s}^{B_{i+1},B_{i+2}}E_{\sigma}\right\rangle,

(S2.36)

\displaystyle\partial_{s}\left\langle M_{1234,s}^{B_{1},B_{2},B_{3}}B_{4}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{2}\left\langle M_{1234,s}^{B_{1},B_{2},B_{3}}B_{4}\right\rangle+\sum_{i=1}^{4}\sigma\left\langle M_{i,i+1,s}^{B_{i}}E_{\sigma}\right\rangle\left\langle M_{i+1,i+2,i+3,i+4,s}^{B_{i+1},B_{i+2},B_{i+3}}E_{\sigma}\right\rangle

\displaystyle+\sigma\left\langle M_{123,s}^{B_{1},B_{2}}E_{\sigma}\right\rangle\left\langle M_{341,s}^{B_{3},B_{4}}E_{\sigma}\right\rangle+\sigma\left\langle M_{234,s}^{B_{2},B_{3}}E_{\sigma}\right\rangle\left\langle M_{412,s}^{B_{4},B_{1}}E_{\sigma}\right\rangle.

In (
S2.35
) we consider indices modulo 3 and in (
S2.36
) modulo 4.

In Lemma
S2.7
we considered the evolution of four pairs
(z_{j},w_{j})
along the characteristic flow to make the formulation more transparent. In all applications below we will take
z_{1}=z_{3}
and
z_{2}=z_{4}
. The proof of Lemma
S2.7
follows by a standard application of the meta-argument and the corresponding differential equations for the products of resolvents, where the fluctuation terms analogous to the lhs. of (
S2.6
)–(
S2.8
) are negligible as the tensorization parameter
d
goes to infinity, see e.g. the proof of
[
21
, Lemma 4.8]
.

As a final preparation for the proof of Proposition
S2.4
, we formulate several properties of
\widehat{\beta}_{12}
analogous to the admissibility properties of the control parameter from
[
23
, Definition 4.4]
. Our description of
\widehat{\beta}_{12}
is slightly more extensive than what is required for the proof of Proposition
S2.4
, however the additional properties will be later used in the proof of Proposition
S2.5
, as it is explained in more detail below Lemma
S2.8
. We now remind the reader the definition of an auxiliary family of quantities introduced in (
A.1
). These quantities are used to analyze
\widehat{\beta}_{12}
, and will also naturally emerge on their own in the proof of Proposition
S2.5
:

(S2.37)

\begin{split}\beta_{12,\pm}=\beta_{12,\pm}(w_{1},w_{2}):&=1-\Re\left[z_{1}\overline{z}_{2}\right]u_{1}u_{2}\pm\sqrt{m_{1}^{2}m_{2}^{2}-\left(\Im\left[z_{1}\overline{z}_{2}\right]\right)^{2}u_{1}^{2}u_{2}^{2}},\\
\beta_{12,*}=\beta_{12,*}(w_{1},w_{2}):&=\min\{|\beta_{12,+}|,|\beta_{12,-}|\},\end{split}

for any
z_{j}\in\mathbf{C}
and
w_{j}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}
, where
m_{j}:=m^{z_{j}}(w_{j})
and
u_{j}:=u^{z_{j}}(w_{j})
are given by (
3.30
), (
3.31
). Note that

\beta_{12,\pm}(w_{1},w_{2})=\beta_{21,\pm}(w_{2},w_{1})\quad\text{and}\quad\beta_{12,*}(w_{1},w_{2})=\beta_{21,*}(w_{2},w_{1}).

We further define the time-dependent parameters
\beta_{12,\pm,t}
and
\beta_{12,*,t}
by replacing
m_{j}
and
u_{j}
in (
S2.37
) by
m_{j,t}:=m^{z_{j,t}}(w_{j,t})
and
u_{j,t}:=u^{z_{j,t}}(w_{j,t})
, respectively.

Lemma S2.8

(Properties of
\beta_{12,*}
and
\widehat{\beta}_{12}
)
.

Fix a (small)
\delta>0
.

(1)

[Relation between
\beta_{12,*}
and
\widehat{\beta}_{12}
]
Uniformly in
z_{j}\in(1-\delta)\mathbf{D}
and
w_{j}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}
,
j=1,2
, it holds that

(S2.38)

\widehat{\beta}_{12}(w_{1},w_{2})\sim\min\left\{\beta_{12,*}\big(w_{1}^{(*)},w_{2}^{(*)}\big)\wedge 1\right\}.

(2)

[Monotonicity in time]
Fix additionally (small)
\epsilon,\kappa>0
and the final time
T>0
. Uniformly in
z_{j,T}\in\mathrm{e}^{-T/2}(1-\delta)\mathbf{D}
and
w_{j,T}\in\Omega^{z_{j,T}}_{\kappa,\epsilon,T}
we have

(S2.39)

\beta_{12,*,s}\sim\beta_{12,*,t}+|t-s|,\quad\widehat{\beta}_{12,s}\sim\widehat{\beta}_{12,t}+|t-s|,\quad\forall\,0\leq s\leq t\leq T.

(3)

[Lipschitz continuity in space]
Fix additionally a (small)
\varepsilon>0
and for any
z\in\mathbf{C}
denote

(S2.40)

\mathcal{D}^{z}_{\kappa,\varepsilon}:=\{w\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}\,:\,\Re w\in\mathbf{B}^{z}_{\kappa}\}\cup\{w\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}\,:\,|\Im w|\geq\varepsilon\}.

Uniformly in
z_{j}\in(1-\delta)\mathbf{D}
,
w_{j}\in\mathcal{D}^{z_{j}}_{\kappa,\varepsilon}
for
j=1,2
, and
w_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}^{z_{2}}_{\kappa,\varepsilon}
it holds that

(S2.41)

\widehat{\beta}_{12}(w_{1},w_{2})\lesssim\widehat{\beta}_{12}(w_{1},w_{2}^{\prime})+|w_{2}-w_{2}^{\prime}|.

(4)

[Vague monotonicity in imaginary part]
Uniformly in
z_{j}\in(1-\delta)\mathbf{D}
,
w_{j}\in\mathcal{D}^{z_{j}}_{\kappa,\varepsilon}
,
j=1,2
, such that
\Im w_{2}>0
, and
x\geq 0
it holds that

(S2.42)

\widehat{\beta}_{12}(w_{1},w_{2})\lesssim\widehat{\beta}_{12}(w_{1},w_{2}+\mathrm{i}x).

The proof of Lemma
S2.8
is presented in Section
S4.4
. It is structured in such a way that first we establish (
S2.38
) and then use this relation between
\widehat{\beta}_{12}
and
\beta_{12,*}
to transfer the properties of
\beta_{12,*}
, which follow from the explicit formula (
S2.37
), to the less explicit quantity
\widehat{\beta}_{12}
. In particular, not only (
S2.39
) holds both for
\widehat{\beta}_{12}
and
\beta_{12,*}
, as currently stated, but also (
S2.41
) and (
S2.42
) hold in this generality, although the
\beta_{12,*}
counterparts of these two statements are not needed for the proof of Proposition
4.2
. Meanwhile, (
S2.38
), the second part of (
S2.39
), and (
S2.41
) are used in the proof of Proposition
S2.4
, whereas the remaining statements in Lemma
S2.8
are needed for the proof of Proposition
S2.5
.

Now we are ready to prove Proposition
S2.4
. For definiteness we consider only the case when there are no stars in (
S2.22
)–(
S2.23
), while the argument is identical in the rest of the cases.

Proof of (
S2.22
).
First we prove (
S2.22
) in the case when
B_{3}\in\{E_{+},E_{-}\}
. From (
S2.16
) and Definition
S2.2
we have that there exist
\kappa^{\prime},\varepsilon>0
such that

(S2.43)

\Omega^{z_{T}}_{\kappa,\epsilon,t}\subset\{w\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}\,:\,\Re w\in\mathbf{B}^{z_{t}}_{\kappa^{\prime}}\}\cup\{w\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}\,:\,|\Im w|\geq\varepsilon\},\quad\forall z_{T}\in(1-\delta)\mathbf{D},\,\forall\,t\in[0,T].

We use the time-independent formulation and consider any
z_{j}\in(1-\delta)\mathbf{D}
and
w_{j}
in the ambient domain in the rhs. of (
S2.43
). For
B_{3}=E_{-}
we use that
G^{z_{1}}(w_{1})E_{-}=-E_{-}G^{z_{1}}(-w_{1})
due to (
3.23
), which in combination with the resolvent identity gives that

(S2.44)

\begin{split}&\left\langle G^{z_{1}}(w_{1})B_{1}G^{z_{2}}(w_{2})B_{2}G^{z_{1}}(w_{1})E_{-}\right\rangle=\frac{1}{2w_{1}}\left\langle\left(G^{z_{1}}(-w_{1})-G^{z_{1}}(w_{1})\right)B_{1}G^{z_{2}}(w_{2})B_{2}E_{-}\right\rangle\\
&\quad=-\frac{1}{2w_{1}}\left(\left\langle G^{z_{1}}(w_{1})E_{-}B_{1}G^{z_{2}}(w_{2})B_{2}\right\rangle+\left\langle G^{z_{1}}(w_{1})B_{1}G^{z_{2}}(w_{2})B_{2}E_{-}\right\rangle\right).\end{split}

Together with the meta-argument and the bound on the deterministic approximation to the two-resolvent chain (
S2.21
) which is already established, this implies (
S2.22
) for
B_{3}=E_{-}
. For
B_{3}=E_{+}
the argument is similar, but instead of the resolvent identity we employ the integral representation of
G_{1}^{2}
:

(S2.45)

\left\langle(G^{z_{1}}(w_{1}))^{2}B_{1}G^{z_{2}}(w_{2})B_{2}\right\rangle=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\mathscr{C}}\frac{1}{(\zeta-w_{1})^{2}}\left\langle G^{z_{1}}(\zeta)B_{1}G^{z_{2}}(w_{2})B_{2}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\zeta,

where
\mathscr{C}
is a circle with the center at
w_{1}
and radius
\eta_{1}/2
, and use that

(S2.46)

\widehat{\beta}_{12}(\zeta,w_{2})\sim\widehat{\beta}_{12}(w_{1},w_{2}),\quad\forall\zeta\in\mathscr{C},

by (
4.21
) and (
S2.41
).

Now we establish (
S2.22
) in the general case
B_{3}\in\mathrm{span}\{E_{\pm},F^{(*)}\}
in its time-dependent form. Multiplying (
S2.35
) by
\mathrm{e}^{-3s/2}
and integrating it in
s
from 0 to
t
we get

(S2.47)

\begin{split}\mathrm{e}^{-3t/2}\left\langle M_{121,t}^{B_{1},B_{2}}B_{3}\right\rangle&=\left\langle M_{121,0}^{B_{1},B_{2}}B_{3}\right\rangle+\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{-3s/2}\sigma\left\langle M_{11,s}^{B_{3}}E_{\sigma}\right\rangle\left\langle M_{121,s}^{B_{1},B_{2}}E_{\sigma}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s\\
&+\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{-3s/2}\sigma\left(\left\langle M_{12,s}^{B_{1}}E_{\sigma}\right\rangle\left\langle M_{121,s}^{E_{\sigma},B_{2}}B_{3}\right\rangle+\left\langle M_{21,s}^{B_{2}}E_{\sigma}\right\rangle\left\langle M_{121,s}^{B_{1},E_{\sigma}}B_{3}\right\rangle\right)\mathrm{d}s,\end{split}

for any
t\in[0,T]
. To estimate the integral in the first line of (
S2.47
), we bound the
M_{11}
term by
\eta_{*,s}^{-1}
by means of (
4.9
) and (
4.21
), and the
M_{121}
term by
(\eta_{*,s}\widehat{\beta}_{12,s})^{-1}
, since it corresponds to the already treated choice
B_{3}=E_{\pm}
. Performing the integration with the help of (
S2.17
) and using that by (
S2.39
)
\widehat{\beta}_{12,t}\lesssim\widehat{\beta}_{12,s}
for any
s\in[0,t]
, we get

(S2.48)

\int_{0}^{t}\left|\left\langle M_{11,s}^{B_{3}}E_{\sigma}\right\rangle\left\langle M_{121,s}^{B_{1},B_{2}}E_{\sigma}\right\rangle\right|\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{\eta_{*,s}}\frac{1}{\eta_{*,s}\widehat{\beta}_{12,s}}\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\frac{1}{\eta_{*,t}\widehat{\beta}_{12,t}}.

For the integral in the second line of (
S2.47
) the resulting bound is the same. In this case, however, we estimate the
M_{12}
term by
\widehat{\beta}_{12,s}^{-1}
using (
S2.21
) and the
M_{121}
term by
\eta_{*,s}^{-2}
using (
S2.29
). Finally, observing that the first term in the rhs. of (
S2.47
) is bounded by order one by (
S2.29
) and that
\eta_{*,0}\sim 1
, we complete the proof of (
S2.22
).

Proof of (
S2.23
).
We prove (
S2.23
) in two steps. First we follow an argument similar to (
S2.47
)–(
S2.48
) using the weak bound (
S2.30
) on the four-resolvent deterministic approximation as an input, and improve (
S2.30
) by a single
\eta_{*,t}/\widehat{\beta}_{12,t}
factor. Next we use this intermediate estimate as a new input and partially rerun the previous part of the argument, gaining an additional factor
\eta_{*,t}/\widehat{\beta}_{12,t}
and thereby proving (
S2.23
).

As in (
S2.47
), we multiply (
S2.36
) by
\mathrm{e}^{-2s}
and integrate the resulting equation in
s
from 0 to
t
. We treat the integral of the sum of four terms in the first line of (
S2.36
) analogously to the integral in the second line of (
S2.47
) using (
S2.21
) and (
S2.30
). This gives an upper bound of order
(\eta_{*,t}^{2}\widehat{\beta}_{12,t})^{-1}
on this integral. The integral of the first term in the second line of (
S2.36
) is estimated by

(S2.49)

\int_{0}^{t}\left|\left\langle M_{121,s}^{B_{1},B_{2}}E_{\sigma}\right\rangle\left\langle M_{121,s}^{B_{3},B_{4}}E_{\sigma}\right\rangle\right|\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\left(\frac{1}{\eta_{*,s}\widehat{\beta}_{12,s}}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\frac{1}{\eta_{*,t}(\widehat{\beta}_{12,t})^{2}},

where we used (
S2.22
). For the second term in the second line of (
S2.36
) the bound is identical. Thus, we get

(S2.50)

\left|\left\langle M_{1212,t}^{B_{1},B_{2},B_{3}}\big(w_{1,t},w_{2,t},w_{1,t}^{(*)},w_{2,t}^{(*)}\big)B_{4}\right\rangle\right|\lesssim\frac{1}{\eta_{*,t}^{2}\widehat{\beta}_{12,t}}.

To gain an additional
\eta_{*,t}/\widehat{\beta}_{12,t}
improvement in the rhs. of (
S2.50
), we return to the analysis on the integral of (
S2.36
), and bound the sum of four terms in the first line of (
S2.36
) by (
S2.50
) instead of (
S2.30
). Keeping the rest of the analysis unchanged, we conclude the desired
(\eta_{*,t})^{-1}(\widehat{\beta}_{12,t})^{-2}
bound on the lhs. of (
S2.50
). This finishes the proof of Proposition
S2.4
. ∎

S2.2.
Proof of Proposition
S2.5

In this section we prove Proposition
S2.5
in several steps. We begin with the case when all observables in Proposition
S2.5
are equal to
E_{\pm}
and show that the bounds (
S2.24
)–(
S2.26
), specialized to this setting, propagate in time. To this end, we differentiate the lhs. of (
S2.24
)–(
S2.26
) in time and analyze the structure of the resulting
zig equations
in Section
S2.2.1
. In Section
S2.2.2
we derive the desired bounds (
S2.24
)–(
S2.26
) from these equations using a Grönwall-type argument, deferring the necessary estimates on the error terms to Section
S2.2.3
. Finally, in Section
S2.2.4
we show how to adapt the argument presented in Sections
S2.2.1
–
S2.2.3
to the case when some of the observables are equal to
F^{(*)}
.

S2.2.1.
Structure of the zig equations

In this section we derive (
4.18
) and analyze the structure of the resulting system of SDEs. To make the presentation more transparent, we consider the general case of a
k
-resolvent chain, where
k\geq 2
is an
N
-independent integer, and follow the evolution of
k
resolvents
G_{1,t},\ldots,G_{k,t}
defined as in (
S2.18
). Later in the proof of Proposition
S2.5
we will need only the cases
k\in\{2,3,4\}
, but in Section
S2.2.1
we follow the most general set-up.

For any observables
B_{i}\in\mathrm{span}\{E_{\pm},F^{(*)}\}
and
t\in[0,T]
we get from the Itô calculus that

(S2.51)

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}\left\langle G_{1,t}B_{1}G_{2,t}B_{2}\cdots G_{k,t}B_{k}\right\rangle&=\frac{k}{2}\left\langle G_{1,t}B_{1}G_{2,t}B_{2}\cdots G_{k,t}B_{k}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{E}_{[k],t}(B_{1},\ldots,B_{k})\\
&+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}\sigma\left\langle G_{i,t}B_{i}\cdots G_{j,t}E_{\sigma}\right\rangle\left\langle G_{j,t}B_{j}\cdots G_{i,t}E_{\sigma}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}t\\
&+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sigma\left\langle(G_{j,t}-M_{j,t})E_{\sigma}\right\rangle\left\langle G_{1,t}B_{1}\cdots B_{j-1}G_{j,t}E_{\sigma}G_{j,t}B_{j}\cdots G_{k}B_{k}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}t,\end{split}

where the
martingale term

\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{E}_{[k],t}
is given by

(S2.52)

\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{E}_{[k],t}(B_{1},\ldots,B_{k}):=\sum_{a,b=1}^{2N}\partial_{ab}\left\langle G_{1,t}B_{1}\cdots G_{k,t}B_{k}\right\rangle\frac{\mathrm{d}B_{ab,t}}{\sqrt{N}}.

In (
S2.61
) we adopted the same notational convention as in Lemma
S2.7
that the free index
\sigma
is meant to be summed over
\{\pm\}
. In the second line of (
S2.51
) the resolvents are indexed by
[i,j]
in the first trace and by
[j,i]:=\{j,j+1,\ldots,k,1,\ldots,i\}
in the second. Applying the meta-argument to (
S2.51
) in the same manner as in the proof of Lemma
S2.6
, we get the following generalization of Lemma
S2.7
for the deterministic approximation to the chain of arbitrary length:

(S2.53)

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\langle M_{[k],t}^{B_{1},\ldots,B_{k-1}}B_{k}\right\rangle=\frac{k}{2}\left\langle M_{[k],t}^{B_{1},\ldots,B_{k-1}}B_{k}\right\rangle+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}\sigma\left\langle M_{[i,j],t}^{B_{i},\ldots,B_{j-1}}E_{\sigma}\right\rangle\left\langle M_{[j,i],t}^{B_{j},\ldots,B_{i-1}}E_{\sigma}\right\rangle.

To follow the time evolution of a fluctuation of a resolvent chain around its deterministic approximation, we introduce the following notation. For any
n\in\mathbf{N}
, set of indices
\mathcal{J}:=\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\}\in[k]^{n}
, observables
B_{i}\in\mathrm{span}\{E_{\pm},F^{(*)}\}
,
i\in[n]
, and time
t\in[0,T]
, denote

(S2.54)

Y_{\mathcal{J},t}(B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}):=\left\langle\left(G_{j_{1},t}B_{1}G_{j_{2},t}\cdots G_{j_{n},t}-M_{\mathcal{J},t}^{B_{1},\ldots,B_{n-1}}\right)B_{n}\right\rangle.

Subtracting (
S2.53
) from (
S2.51
) we get

(S2.55)

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}Y_{[k],t}(B_{1},\ldots,B_{k})&=\frac{k}{2}Y_{[k],t}(B_{1},\ldots,B_{k})\mathrm{d}t\\
&+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sigma\left\langle M_{[j,j+1],t}^{B_{j}}E_{\sigma}\right\rangle Y_{[k],t}(B_{1},\ldots,B_{j-1},E_{\sigma},B_{j+1},\ldots,B_{k})\mathrm{d}t\\
&+\mathfrak{F}_{[k],t}(B_{1},\ldots,B_{k})\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{E}_{[k],t}(B_{1},\ldots,B_{k}),\end{split}

where in the second line of (
S2.55
) we denoted
[k,k+1]:=\{k,1\}
. Here the
forcing term

\mathfrak{F}_{[k],t}
is given by

(S2.56)

\begin{split}&\mathfrak{F}_{[k],t}(B_{1},\ldots,B_{k}):=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sigma Y_{j,t}(E_{\sigma})\left\langle G_{1,t}B_{1}\cdots B_{j-1}G_{j,t}E_{\sigma}G_{j,t}B_{j}\cdots G_{k}B_{k}\right\rangle\\
&+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}Y_{[i,j],t}(B_{i},\ldots,B_{j-1},E_{\sigma})Y_{[j,i],t}(B_{j},\ldots,B_{i-1},E_{\sigma})\\
&+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq k\\
j\neq i+1\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\sigma\left(\!\left\langle\!M_{[i,j],t}^{B_{i},\ldots,B_{j-1}}E_{\sigma}\!\right\rangle\!Y_{[j,i],t}(B_{j},\ldots,B_{i-1},E_{\sigma})\!+\!Y_{[i,j],t}(B_{i},\ldots,B_{j-1},E_{\sigma})\!\left\langle\!M_{[j,i],t}^{B_{j},\ldots,B_{i-1}}E_{\sigma}\!\right\rangle\!\right)\!.\end{split}

In the last line of (
S2.56
) the constraint
j\neq i+1
is interpreted modulo
k
, i.e the pair
(i,j)=(k,1)
is also removed from the summation. In particular, for
k=2
no terms appear in the third line of (
S2.56
). We remark that the terms in the first (respectively, second and third) line of (
S2.56
) originate from the third (respectively, second) line of (
S2.51
). The terms with
j=i+1
are not included into the third line of (
S2.56
), instead, they are separated in the second line of (
S2.55
), as they correspond to the fluctuations of the
k
-resolvent chains, while the terms in the third line of (
S2.56
) arise from shorter chains. Moreover, fluctuations of chains of length at least
k
(namely, of length
k
and
k+1
) appear in the forcing term only when multiplied by a fluctuation of another chain, which makes these terms small (for more details see the proof of Proposition
S2.10
later). For this reason we treat
\mathfrak{F}_{[k],t}
as an error term, and refer to the terms in the first two lines in the rhs. of (
S2.55
) as the
linear terms
.

Now we specialize (
S2.55
) to the case when
B_{i}=E_{\pm}
for all
i\in[k]
. We identify the space of diagonal
2\times 2
matrices with
\mathbf{C}^{2}
by mapping
E_{+}
and
E_{-}
to the standard basis vectors in
\mathbf{C}^{2}
, which we denote by
\bm{e}_{+}
and
\bm{e}_{-}
, respectively. We further identify the space of
k
-tuples
(B_{1},\ldots,B_{k})
of diagonal
2\times 2
matrices with the tensor product
(\mathbf{C}^{2})^{\otimes k}
, by mapping
(E_{\sigma_{1}},\ldots,E_{\sigma_{k}})
to

(S2.57)

\bm{e}_{\bm{\sigma}}=\bm{e}_{\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{k}}:=\bm{e}_{\sigma_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\bm{e}_{\sigma_{k}},\quad\text{where}\quad\bm{\sigma}:=(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{k})\in\{\pm\}^{k}.

Note that
\{\bm{e}_{\bm{\sigma}}\}_{\bm{\sigma}\in\{\pm\}^{k}}
is an orthonormal basis in
(\mathbf{C}^{2})^{\otimes k}
. Define

(S2.58)

\mathcal{Y}_{[k],t}:=\sum_{\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{k}\in\{\pm\}}Y_{[k],t}(E_{\sigma_{1}},\ldots,E_{\sigma_{k}})\bm{e}_{\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{k}}\in(\mathbf{C}^{2})^{\otimes k},\quad t\in[0,T],

the
martingale term

\mathrm{d}\mathcal{E}_{[k],t}
and the
forcing term

\mathcal{F}_{[k],t}
are defined similarly to (
S2.58
) from (
S2.52
) and (
S2.56
), respectively. For
\sigma\in\{\pm\}
,
i,j\in[k]
and
t\in[0,T]
denote further

(S2.59)

\begin{split}a_{ij}^{\sigma}=a_{ij}^{\sigma}(z_{i},z_{j};w_{i},w_{j})&:=\sigma\langle M_{ij}^{E_{\sigma}}E_{\sigma}\rangle,\quad\qquad\qquad\quad\,\,\,\,\,\,\,a_{ij,t}^{\sigma}:=a^{\sigma}_{ij}(z_{i,t},z_{j,t};w_{i,t},w_{j,t}),\\
d_{ij}=d_{ij}(z_{i},z_{j};w_{i},w_{j})&:=\langle M_{ij}^{E_{+}}E_{-}\rangle=-\langle M_{ij}^{E_{-}}E_{+}\rangle,\quad d_{ij,t}:=d_{ij}(z_{i,t},z_{j,t};w_{i,t},w_{j,t}).\end{split}

Here
\sigma\in\{\pm\}
is fixed and we do not perform summation over
\sigma
. The identity in the last line of (
S2.59
) is established for
w_{i},w_{j}
on the imaginary axes in
[
37
, Eq.(5.26)]
. Since the functions
\langle M_{ij}^{E_{+}}E_{-}\rangle
and
-\langle M_{ij}^{E_{-}}E_{+}\rangle
are analytic in
w_{i},w_{j}
in the upper and lower complex half-planes, this identity holds for all
w_{i},w_{j}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}
. Finally, we define two families of linear operators acting on
(\mathbf{C}^{2})^{\otimes k}
: the projections onto the basis vectors
\{P_{\bm{\sigma}}\}_{\bm{\sigma}\in\{\pm\}^{k}}
and the
spin-flip operators

\{S_{j}\}_{j=1}^{k}
, where
S_{j}
flips the sign of
j
-th spin, i.e.

(S2.60)

P_{\bm{\sigma}}\bm{e}_{\bm{\omega}}:=\delta_{\bm{\sigma},\bm{\omega}}\bm{e}_{\bm{\sigma}}\quad\text{and}\quad S_{j}\bm{e}_{\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{j},\ldots,\omega_{k}}:=\bm{e}_{\omega_{1},\ldots,-\omega_{i},\ldots,\omega_{k}},\quad\forall\bm{\sigma},\bm{\omega}\in\{\pm\}^{k},\,j\in[k].

Considering in (
S2.55
) all
2^{k}
possible choices of observables
B_{i}=E_{\pm}
,
i\in[k]
, we get

(S2.61)

\mathrm{d}\mathcal{Y}_{[k],t}=\left(\frac{k}{2}I+\mathcal{A}_{[k],t}\right)\mathcal{Y}_{[k],t}\mathrm{d}t+\mathcal{F}_{[k],t}\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}\mathcal{E}_{[k],t},

where the
generator

\mathcal{A}_{[k],t}
is given by

(S2.62)

\mathcal{A}_{[k],t}:=\sum_{\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{k}\in\{\pm\}}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{k}a_{j(j+1),t}^{\sigma_{j}}\bigg)P_{\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{k}}-\sum_{j=1}^{k}d_{j(j+1),t}S_{j},

with the standard convention that the subscripts of
a
’s and
d
’s are viewed modulo
k
. Similarly to the discussion below the scalar equation (
S2.51
), we view the forcing term
\mathcal{F}_{[k],t}\mathrm{d}t
and the martingale term
\mathrm{d}\mathcal{E}_{[k],t}
in (
S2.61
) as error terms and refer to the remaining first term in the rhs. of (
S2.61
) as to the
linear term
. The terminology "error terms" is introduced purely for convenience: these terms are not negligible, but for the analysis of (
S2.61
) only upper bounds on them are needed, while the linear term will be treated with higher precision. We turn (
S2.61
) into an upper bound on
\|\mathcal{Y}_{[k],t}\|
using the Grönwall inequality. To do so, one needs a control on the operator norm of the
propagator

\mathcal{P}_{[k],t,s}:(\mathbf{C}^{2})^{\otimes k}\to(\mathbf{C}^{2})^{\otimes k}
for all
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
, where for any
\bm{x}\in(\mathbf{C}^{2})^{\otimes k}
,
\mathcal{P}_{[k],t,s}\bm{x}
is defined by

(S2.63)

\mathcal{P}_{[k],t,s}\bm{x}:=\bm{x}_{t},\quad\text{with}\quad\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\bm{x}_{r}=\mathcal{A}_{[k],r}\bm{x}_{r},\,\,\forall r\in[s,t],\,\,\bm{x}_{s}:=\bm{x}.

Trivially, we have

(S2.64)

\left\lVert\mathcal{P}_{[k],t,s}\right\rVert\leq\exp\left\{\int_{s}^{t}f_{[k],r}\mathrm{d}r\right\},\quad\text{where}\quad f_{[k],r}:=\left[\max\mathrm{spec}(\Re\mathcal{A}_{[k],r})\right]_{+},\,\,\,\forall\,0\leq s\leq r\leq t\leq T.

Here
[\cdot]_{+}
stands for the positive part of a real number.

In the following statement which generalizes Lemma
4.3
we show that the off-diagonal entries of
\Re\mathcal{A}_{[k],t}
, namely
-\Re d_{ij,t}
, are negligible, and provide an exact calculation of the integral of the diagonal entries up to an error term of order 1. This allows us to get an optimal upper bound on integral of
f_{[k],r}
introduced in (
S2.64
), which we also present. The result is expressed in terms of quantities
\beta_{ij,*}
for
i,j\in[k]
, defined analogously to
\beta_{12,*}
in (
S2.37
), except that
z_{1},z_{2}
and
w_{1},w_{2}
are replaced by
z_{i},z_{j}
and
w_{i},w_{j}
, respectively.

Lemma S2.9

(Bound on the propagator)
.

Fix (small)
\delta,\epsilon,\kappa>0
and the final time
T>0
. Fix additionally an
N
-independent integer
k\geq 2
. The following statements hold uniformly in
z_{j,T}\in\mathrm{e}^{-T/2}(1-\delta)\mathbf{D}
,
w_{j,T}\in\Omega_{\kappa,\epsilon,T}^{z_{j,T}}
,
j\in[k]
, and
s,t\in[0,T]
with
s\leq t
. First, for any
i,j\in[k]
we have

(S2.65)
