\int_{s}^{t}\max\{\Re a_{ij,r}^{+},\Re a_{ij,r}^{-},0\}\mathrm{d}r=\int_{s}^{t}\max\{\Re a_{ij,r}^{+},\Re a_{ij,r}^{-}\}\mathrm{d}r+\mathcal{O}(1)=\log\frac{\beta_{ij,*,s}}{\beta_{ij,*,t}}+\mathcal{O}(1),

(S2.66)

\int_{s}^{t}|\Re d_{ij,r}|\mathrm{d}r=\mathcal{O}(1).

It further holds that

(S2.67)

\exp\left\{\int_{s}^{t}f_{[k],r}\mathrm{d}r\right\}\lesssim\prod_{j=1}^{k}\frac{\beta_{j(j+1),*,s}}{\beta_{j(j+1),*,t}},

where
\beta_{k(k+1),*,r}:=\beta_{k1,*,r}
for any
r\in[0,T]
, and
f_{[k],r}
is defined in (
S2.64
).

The proofs of (
S2.65
) and (
S2.66
) were already presented in Appendix
A.2
, while (
S2.67
) immediately follows from (
S2.65
)–(
S2.66
), similarly to the argument around (
A.27
). We omit further details.

S2.2.2.
Application of the Grönwall inequality

In this section we restrict the set-up of Section
S2.2.1
and consider only
k\in\{2,3,4\}
in (
S2.61
). Moreover, for
k=3
we consider only the case when
z_{3,t}=z_{1,t}
and
w_{3,t}=w_{1,t}^{(*)}
for all
t\in[0,T]
, and for
k=4
we additionally assume that
z_{4,t}=z_{2,t}
and
w_{4,t}=w_{2,t}^{(*)}
. These quantities form a self-consistent family under the zig flow in the sense that no other constellations of
z_{i}
’s and
w_{i}
’s are needed to analyze (
S2.61
).

To control the error terms in the rhs. of (
S2.61
) for
k\in\{2,3,4\}
, we follow a stopping time argument. Denote

(S2.68)

\alpha_{2,t}:=\frac{1}{N\eta_{*,t}\widehat{\beta}_{12,t}},\quad\alpha_{3,t}:=\frac{1}{N\eta^{3}_{*,t}}\wedge\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\eta_{*,t}}\eta_{*,t}\widehat{\beta}_{12,t}},\quad\alpha_{4,t}:=\frac{1}{N\eta^{4}_{*,t}}\wedge\frac{1}{\eta_{*,t}(\widehat{\beta}_{12,t})^{2}}

for
t\in[0,T]
. These control parameters exactly match the rhs. of (
S2.24
)–(
S2.26
), and depend on the trajectory of the characteristic flow, but as usual this dependence is omitted from notations. Fix small tolerance exponents
\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4}
satisfying

(S2.69)

\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4}\in(0,\epsilon/10),\quad\xi_{2}<\xi_{3}<\xi_{4}<2\xi_{2}.

Define the stopping times

(S2.70)

\tau_{k}:=\inf\left\{t\in[0,T]\,:\,\max_{s\in[0,t]}\max_{|z_{j,T}|\leq\mathrm{e}^{-T/2}(1-\delta)}\max_{w_{j,T}\in\Omega_{\kappa,\epsilon,T}^{z_{j,T}}}\alpha_{k,s}^{-1}\|\mathcal{Y}^{(k)}_{s}\|\geq N^{2\xi_{k}}\right\},\quad k=2,3,4,

where we denoted

(S2.71)

\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}_{s}:=\mathcal{Y}_{12,s},\quad\mathcal{Y}^{(3)}_{s}:=\mathcal{Y}_{121,s}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{Y}^{(4)}_{s}:=\mathcal{Y}_{1212,s}.

Here we slightly abuse the notation and abbreviate the quantities with stars in the lhs. of (
S2.25
), (
S2.26
) using the same notation
\mathcal{Y}_{121}
(
\mathcal{Y}_{1212}
, respectively). Finally, we set

(S2.72)

\tau:=\min\left\{\tau_{2},\tau_{3},\tau_{4}\right\}.

To prove Proposition
S2.5
we need to show that
\tau=T
almost surely.

We have the following bounds on the error terms in the rhs. of (
S2.61
). The proof of this proposition is postponed to Section
S2.2.3
.

Proposition S2.10

(Bounds on the error terms)
.

Assume the set-up and conditions of Proposition
S2.5
. Let
\mathcal{F}^{(k)}_{s}
and
\mathrm{d}\mathcal{E}^{(k)}_{s}
be defined using the same convention as in (
S2.71
) for
k=2,3,4
, and let
\mathcal{C}^{(k)}_{s}\mathrm{d}s\in\mathbf{C}^{2^{k}\times 2^{k}}
be the covariation process of
\mathrm{d}\mathcal{E}^{(k)}_{s}
. Then for any
t\in[0,T]
we have

(S2.73)

\left(\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\|\mathcal{F}_{s}^{(k)}\|\mathrm{d}s\right)^{2}+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\|\mathcal{C}^{(k)}_{s}\|\mathrm{d}s\lesssim N^{2\xi_{k}}\alpha_{k,t\wedge\tau}^{2},\quad\text{for}\,\,k=2,3,4.

Having Proposition
S2.10
in hand, we now prove Proposition
S2.5
.

Proof of Proposition
S2.5
.

Throughout the proof
k
is chosen from the range
\{2,3,4\}
and the time parameters
r,s,t
are in
[0,T]
. Similarly to (
S2.71
), we denote

\mathcal{A}^{(2)}_{s}:=\mathcal{A}_{12},\quad\mathcal{A}^{(3)}_{s}:=\mathcal{A}_{121,s},\quad\mathcal{A}^{(4)}_{s}:=\mathcal{A}_{1212,s}\quad\text{and}\quad f_{s}^{(k)}:=\left[\max\mathrm{spec}\left(\Re\mathcal{A}^{(k)}_{s}\right)\right]_{+}.

From (
S2.61
), Proposition
S2.10
and the stochastic matrix-valued Grönwall inequality from
[
37
, Lemma 5.6]
we have

(S2.74)

\begin{split}\sup_{0\leq s\leq t\wedge\tau}\|\mathcal{Y}^{(k)}_{s}\|^{2}&\lesssim\|\mathcal{Y}_{0}^{(k)}\|^{2}+N^{2\xi_{k}+3\zeta}\alpha_{k,t\wedge\tau}^{2}\\
&+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\left(\lVert\mathcal{Y}_{0}^{(k)}\rVert^{2}+N^{2\xi_{k}+3\zeta}\alpha_{k,s}^{2}\right)f_{s}^{(k)}\exp\left\{2(1+N^{-\zeta})\int_{s}^{t\wedge\tau}f_{r}^{(k)}\mathrm{d}r\right\}\mathrm{d}s,\end{split}

for any (small) fixed
\zeta>0
.

We further focus on the case
k=3
, while for
k=2,4
the argument is similar, and so omitted. It follows from (
S2.67
) that

(S2.75)

\exp\left\{\int_{s}^{t\wedge\tau}f_{r}^{(3)}\mathrm{d}r\right\}\lesssim\left(\frac{\beta_{12,*,s}}{\beta_{12,*,t\wedge\tau}}\right)^{2}\frac{\beta_{11,*,s}}{\beta_{11,*,t\wedge\tau}}.

We plug (
S2.75
) into (
S2.74
), estimate
\|\mathcal{Y}^{(3)}_{0}\|
from the initial condition (
S2.25
) at time
t=0
, and bound
f_{s}^{(3)}\lesssim\eta_{*,s}^{-1}
by (
4.9
), (
4.21
), arriving to

(S2.76)

\sup_{0\leq s\leq t\wedge\tau}\|\mathcal{Y}^{(3)}_{s}\|^{2}\lesssim N^{2\xi_{3}+3\zeta}\left(\alpha^{2}_{3,t\wedge\tau}+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\alpha_{3,s}^{2}\frac{1}{\eta_{*,s}}\left(\frac{\beta_{12,*,s}}{\beta_{12,*,t\wedge\tau}}\right)^{4}\left(\frac{\beta_{11,*,s}}{\beta_{11,*,t\wedge\tau}}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}s\right).

We now show that

(S2.77)

\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\alpha_{3,s}^{2}\frac{1}{\eta_{*,s}}\left(\frac{\beta_{12,*,s}}{\beta_{12,*,t\wedge\tau}}\right)^{4}\left(\frac{\beta_{11,*,s}}{\beta_{11,*,t\wedge\tau}}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\alpha_{3,t\wedge\tau}^{2}\log N.

Recall from (
S2.68
) that
\alpha_{3,s}
is a minimum of two control parameters:
(N\eta_{*,s}^{3})^{-1}
and
(\sqrt{N\eta_{*,s}}\eta_{*,s}\widehat{\beta}_{12,s})^{-1}
. To show that the lhs. of (
S2.77
) is smaller than the square of the first of them evaluated at the time
t\wedge\tau
, we observe that

(S2.78)

\frac{\beta_{12,*,s}}{\beta_{12,*,t\wedge\tau}}\sim\frac{\beta_{12,*,t\wedge\tau}+|s-t\wedge\tau|}{\beta_{12,*,t\wedge\tau}}\lesssim\frac{\eta_{*,t\wedge\tau}+|s-t\wedge\tau|}{\eta_{*,t\wedge\tau}}\sim\frac{\eta_{*,s}}{\eta_{*,t\wedge\tau}}

for any
s\in[0,t\wedge\tau]
, where we used the first part of (
S2.39
) in the first step, (
S2.38
) and (
4.21
) in the second, and Lemma
S2.3
(4) in the third. Estimating
\beta_{11,*,s}/\beta_{11,*,t\wedge\tau}
in the same way, we obtain that the lhs. of (
S2.77
) has an upper bound of order

(S2.79)

\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\left(\frac{1}{N\eta_{*,s}^{3}}\right)^{2}\frac{1}{\eta_{*,s}}\left(\frac{\eta_{*,s}}{\eta_{*,t\wedge\tau}}\right)^{6}\mathrm{d}s=\left(\frac{1}{N\eta_{*,t\wedge\tau}^{3}}\right)^{2}\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{\eta_{*,s}}\lesssim\left(\frac{1}{N\eta_{*,t\wedge\tau}^{3}}\right)^{2}\log N.

In the last bound we used the second part of (
S2.17
) and that
\eta_{*,t\wedge\tau}>N^{-1}
.

Now we prove that the lhs. of (
S2.77
) is smaller than the square of second component of
\alpha_{3,t\wedge\tau}
(up to a
\log N
factor). We again estimate the ratio of
\beta_{11,*}
’s as in (
S2.78
) and further split the interval of integration into two regimes:
[0,\widetilde{t}]
and
[\widetilde{t},T]
, where the random variable
\widetilde{t}
is defined by

\widetilde{t}:=\left[t\wedge\tau-\beta_{12,*,t\wedge\tau}\right]_{+}.

For any
s\in[0,\widetilde{t}]
we have

(S2.80)

\eta_{*,s}\lesssim\beta_{12,*,s}\sim\beta_{12,*,t\wedge\tau}+|t\wedge\tau-s|\sim|t\wedge\tau-s|\lesssim\eta_{*,t\wedge\tau}+|t\wedge\tau-s|\sim\eta_{*,s},

i.e.
\beta_{12,*,s}\sim\eta_{*,s}
, where we used the first part of (
S2.39
) and Lemma
S2.3
(4). Therefore, the integral in the lhs. of (
S2.77
) restricted to
[0,\widetilde{t}]
has an upper bound of order

(S2.81)

\int_{0}^{\widetilde{t}}\left(\frac{1}{N\eta_{*,s}^{3}}\right)^{2}\frac{1}{\eta_{*,s}}\left(\frac{\eta_{*,s}}{\widehat{\beta}_{12,t\wedge\tau}}\right)^{4}\left(\frac{\eta_{*,s}}{\eta_{*,t\wedge\tau}}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\left(\frac{1}{N\eta_{*,t\wedge\tau}(\widehat{\beta}_{12,t\wedge\tau})^{2}}\right)^{2}\log N.

Here we used the second part of (
S2.17
) and the bound
\beta_{12,*,t\wedge\tau}\geq\widehat{\beta}_{12,t\wedge\tau}
. In the regime
s\in[\widetilde{t},T]
, from the second step in (
S2.80
) we get that
\beta_{12,*,s}\sim\beta_{12,*,t\wedge\tau}
, so the integral in the lhs. of (
S2.77
) restricted to
[\widetilde{t},T]
is bounded by

(S2.82)

\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\eta_{*,s}}\eta_{*,s}\widehat{\beta}_{12,s}}\right)^{2}\frac{1}{\eta_{*,s}}\left(\frac{\eta_{*,s}}{\eta_{*,t\wedge\tau}}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\eta_{*,t\wedge\tau}}\eta_{*,t\wedge\tau}\widehat{\beta}_{12,t\wedge\tau}}\right)^{2},

where we estimated
\widehat{\beta}_{12,s}\gtrsim\widehat{\beta}_{12,t\wedge\tau}
from (
S2.39
) and used (
S2.17
) for
a=2
. Combining (
S2.79
), (
S2.81
) and (
S2.82
), we finish the proof of (
S2.77
).

Now we have all ingredients to complete the proof of Proposition
S2.5
. Using (
S2.76
) along with (
S2.77
) we get

(S2.83)

\sup_{0\leq s\leq t\wedge\tau}\|\mathcal{Y}^{(3)}_{s}\|^{2}\lesssim N^{2\xi_{3}+3\zeta}\alpha^{2}_{3,t\wedge\tau}\log N\lesssim N^{3\xi_{3}}\alpha^{2}_{3,t\wedge\tau},

provided that
\zeta>0
is chosen to be sufficiently small. Recall the definition of the stopping time
\tau_{3}
from (
S2.70
). From (
S2.83
) we have that either
\tau_{3}>\tau
or
\tau_{3}=T
. Arguing similarly for
\tau_{2}
and
\tau_{4}
and using that
\tau=\min\{\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\tau_{3}\}
, we conclude that
\tau=T
. This finishes the proof of Proposition
S2.5
.
∎

S2.2.3.
Proof of Proposition
S2.10
: bounds on the error terms

For simplicity, we establish the bounds only on the error terms arising from the differentiation of resolvent chains, where the complex conjugates are not involved. The remaining cases are completely analogous and thus are omitted.

Recall from (
S2.68
) that each of the control parameters
\alpha_{k,t}
,
k\in\{2,3,4\}
, is a minimum of two components:
(N\eta_{*,t}^{k})^{-1}
and another term containing
\widehat{\beta}_{12,t}
. The case
k=2
is not an exception as it may seem from the definition of
\alpha_{2,t}
, since
(N\eta_{*,t}\widehat{\beta}_{12,t})^{-1}
is always smaller than
(N\eta_{*,t}^{2})^{-1}
, due to (
4.21
). While the proof of the
(N\eta_{*,t\wedge\tau}^{k})^{-2}
bounds on the lhs. of (
S2.73
) is fairly standard, we first prove the upper bounds involving
\widehat{\beta}_{12,t\wedge\tau}
and later in the end of Section
S2.2.3
explain how to adjust the proof to obtain the bounds purely in terms of
\eta_{*,t\wedge\tau}
.

We start with the estimates on the second term in the lhs. of (
S2.73
), which arises from the martingale term
\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{E}^{(k)}
and show that

(S2.84)

\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\|\mathcal{C}^{(k)}_{s}\|\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\begin{cases}N^{2\xi_{2}}(N\eta_{*,t\wedge\tau}\widehat{\beta}_{12,t\wedge\tau})^{-2},&k=2,\\
N^{2\xi_{3}}(\sqrt{N\eta_{*,t\wedge\tau}}\eta_{*,t\wedge\tau}\widehat{\beta}_{12,t\wedge\tau})^{-2},&k=3,\\
N^{2\xi_{4}}(\eta_{*,t\wedge\tau}(\widehat{\beta}_{12,t\wedge\tau})^{2})^{-2},&k=4.\end{cases}

Recall from the formulation of Proposition
S2.10
that
\mathcal{C}^{(k)}_{s}\mathrm{d}s
is the covariation process of
\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{E}^{(k)}_{s}
. Thus,
\mathcal{C}^{(k)}_{s}
is a matrix of size
2^{k}\times 2^{k}
with entries indexed by
\bm{\sigma},\bm{\omega}\in\{\pm\}^{k}
.
Since the size of
\mathcal{C}^{(k)}_{s}
does not depend on
N
, we have

(S2.85)

\|\mathcal{C}^{(k)}_{s}\|\lesssim\sum_{\bm{\sigma},\bm{\omega}}\Big|\big(\mathcal{C}^{(k)}_{s}\big)_{\bm{\sigma},\bm{\omega}}\Big|,

where the implicit constant in the inequality depends on
k
, but not on
N
. Therefore, it is sufficient to prove (
S2.84
) separately for each of the terms in the rhs. of (
S2.85
). To simplify the presentation, we present the bounds only for diagonal entries, i.e. for
\bm{\sigma}=\bm{\omega}
, while for the off-diagonal entries the proof is identical. We also denote
B_{i}:=E_{\sigma_{i}}
for
i\in[k]
.

Now we consider separately each of the cases
k\in\{2,3,4\}
.

k=2
.
We have from
[
37
, Eq.(5.28),(5.29)]
the following simple estimate on the entries of
\mathcal{C}^{(k)}_{s}
based on the explicit calculation and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality:

(S2.86)

\Big|\big(\mathcal{C}^{(2)}_{s}\big)_{\bm{\sigma},\bm{\sigma}}\Big|\lesssim\frac{1}{N^{2}\eta_{1,s}^{2}}\left\langle\Im G_{1,s}B_{1}G_{2,s}B_{2}\Im G_{1,s}B_{2}^{*}G_{2,s}^{*}B_{1}^{*}\right\rangle+\frac{1}{N^{2}\eta_{2,s}^{2}}\left\langle\Im G_{2,s}B_{2}G_{1,s}B_{1}\Im G_{2,s}B_{1}^{*}G_{1,s}^{*}B_{2}^{*}\right\rangle.

Both terms in the rhs. of (
S2.86
) are the four-resolvent chains exactly of the type which is controlled by the stopping time
\tau_{4}
defined in (
S2.70
). Decomposing each of these chains into the deterministic approximation and the fluctuation around it, we get from (
S2.23
) and (
S2.70
) that the rhs. of (
S2.86
) has an upper bound of order

(S2.87)

\frac{1}{N^{2}\eta_{*,s}^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\eta_{*,s}(\widehat{\beta}_{12,s})^{2}}+\frac{N^{\xi_{4}}}{N\eta_{*,s}^{4}}\wedge\frac{N^{\xi_{4}}}{\eta_{*,s}(\widehat{\beta}_{12,s})^{2}}\right)\lesssim\frac{N^{\xi_{4}}}{N^{2}\eta_{*,s}^{3}(\widehat{\beta}_{12,s})^{2}},\quad\forall\,s\in[0,t\wedge\tau].

Estimating
\widehat{\beta}_{12,s}\gtrsim\widehat{\beta}_{12,t\wedge\tau}
from (
S2.39
), integrating the rhs. of (
S2.87
) in
s\in[0,t\wedge\tau]
by the means of (
S2.17
) applied to
a=3
, and recalling from (
S2.69
) that
\xi_{4}<2\xi_{2}
, we finish the proof of (
S2.84
) for
k=2
.

k=3
.
Similarly to (
S2.86
), we upper bound
|(\mathcal{C}^{(3)}_{s})_{\bm{\sigma},\bm{\sigma}}|
by the sum of three terms, two of which are given by

(S2.88)

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{N^{2}\eta_{1,s}^{2}}\left\langle\Im G_{1,s}B_{1}G_{2,s}B_{2}G_{1,s}B_{3}\Im G_{1,s}B_{3}^{*}G_{1,s}^{*}B_{2}^{*}G_{2,s}^{*}B_{1}^{*}\right\rangle,\\
&\frac{1}{N^{2}\eta_{2,s}^{2}}\left\langle\Im G_{2,s}B_{2}G_{1,s}B_{3}G_{1,s}B_{1}\Im G_{2,s}B_{1}^{*}G_{1,s}^{*}B_{3}^{*}G_{1,s}^{*}B_{2}^{*}\right\rangle,\end{split}

and the third term is analogous to the one in the first line of (
S2.88
). Each of these terms contains six resolvents and thus it is not directly controlled by the stopping time
\tau
, in contrast to the terms in the rhs. of (
S2.86
). This forces us to invoke the so-called
reduction inequalities
(as introduced in
[
32
]
), which bound traces of longer resolvent chains in terms of traces of shorter ones.
To estimate the term in the second line of (
S2.88
), we observe that for all
R,S\in\mathbf{C}^{(2N)\times(2N)}
such that each of the matrices
S,T
is either positive or negative semi-definite, it holds that

(S2.89)

|\langle RS\rangle|\leq 2N|\langle R\rangle\langle S\rangle|.

We apply this elementary reduction inequality to

(S2.90)

R:=G_{1,s}^{*}B_{2}^{*}\Im G_{2,s}B_{2}G_{1,s}\quad\text{and}\quad S:=B_{3}G_{1,s}B_{1}\Im G_{2,s}B_{1}^{*}G_{1,s}^{*}B_{3}^{*},

and note that the trace in the second line of (
S2.88
) equals to
\langle RS\rangle
. Since
\langle R\rangle
and
\langle S\rangle
are controlled by the stopping time
\tau_{3}
, we further estimate each of these traces in the way similar to (
S2.87
). We omit further details concerning integration of the resulting bound in time, since this procedure is similar to the one discussed below (
S2.87
).

Now we estimate the term in the first line of (
S2.88
) and instead of (
S2.89
) use the following reduction bound from
[
23
, Eq.(5.27)]
:

(S2.91)

\left|\left\langle G_{2,s}RG_{2,s}^{*}S\right\rangle\right|\lesssim N\left|\left\langle|G_{2,s}|R\right\rangle\left\langle|G_{2,s}|S\right\rangle\right|,

uniformly in the same set of matrices
R,S
as in (
S2.89
). We take

(S2.92)

R:=B_{2}G_{1,s}B_{3}\Im G_{1,s}B_{3}^{*}G_{1,s}^{*}B_{2}^{*}\quad\text{and}\quad S:=B_{1}^{*}\Im G_{1,s}B_{1}.

However,
\langle|G_{2,s}|R\rangle
and
\langle|G_{2,s}|S\rangle
are not resolvent chains in the usual sense, since they involve the absolute value of a resolvent. To address this issue, we use the following integral representation from
[
32
, Eq.(5.4)]

(S2.93)

|G^{z}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta)|=\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\Im G^{z}(E+\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\eta^{2}+x^{2}})}{\sqrt{\eta^{2}+x^{2}}}\mathrm{d}x,\quad\forall\,z\in\mathbf{C},\,E\in\mathbf{R},\,\eta>0.

We complement (
S2.93
) by the following geometric property of the bulk-restricted domains, essential for the application of (
S2.93
). This property is a special case of
[
23
, Lemma 5.4]
in the sense discussed above Definition
S2.2
.

Lemma S2.11

(Ray property of the bulk-restricted spectral domains)
.

Fix (small)
\kappa,\epsilon,\delta>0
and the final time
T>0
. There exists
t_{*}\in[0,T]
such that
T-t_{*}\sim 1
and we have the following. For any
t\in[t_{*},T]
,
z_{T}\in\mathrm{e}^{-T/2}(1-\delta)\bf{D}
,
w\in\Omega^{z_{t}}_{\kappa,\epsilon,t}
, and
x\geq 0
such that
|\Im w|+x\leq N^{100}
, it holds that

w+\mathrm{sgn}(\Im w)\mathrm{i}x\in\Omega^{z_{t}}_{\kappa,\epsilon,t}.

That is, for
\Im w>0
(
\Im w<0
) the vertical ray which starts at
w
and goes up, leaves the spectral domain only after reaching points with imaginary part greater than
N^{100}
(smaller than
N^{-100}
). Since
T-t_{*}\sim 1
, we may assume that
t_{*}=0
. Otherwise it suffices to redefine
T
, taking the final time equal to
T-t_{*}
.

Applying (
S2.93
) to
|G_{2,s}|
, we get

(S2.94)

\left\langle|G_{2,s}|S\right\rangle=\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\eta_{2,t}^{2}+x^{2}}}\left\langle\Im G^{z_{2,t}}_{t}\left(\Re w_{2,t}+\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\eta_{2,t}^{2}+x^{2}}\right)B_{1}^{*}\Im G_{1,t}B_{1}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}x,

where
\eta_{2,t}:=|\Im w_{2,t}|
. In the regime
\sqrt{\eta_{2,t}^{2}+x^{2}}\geq N^{100}
we trivially estimate the trace in the rhs. of (
S2.94
) by the product of operator norms of the involved resolvents, which integrates to the bound of order
N^{-100}\eta_{1,t}^{-1}
. In the complementary regime
\sqrt{\eta_{2,t}^{2}+x^{2}}<N^{100}
, the spectral parameter
w_{2}^{\prime}:=\Re w_{2,t}+\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\eta_{2,t}^{2}+x^{2}}
lies in
\Omega_{\kappa,\epsilon,t}^{z_{2,t}}
by Lemma
S2.11
, so the trace is controlled by the stopping time
\tau_{2}
and has an upper bound of order
(\widehat{\beta}_{12,t}\left(w_{1,t},w_{2}^{\prime}\right))^{-1}
, where we also employed (
S2.21
). Using additionally the vague monotonicity of
\widehat{\beta}_{12}
in imaginary part from Lemma
S2.8
(4), we conclude that

(S2.95)

\left|\left\langle|G_{2,s}|B_{1}^{*}\Im G_{1,s}B_{1}\right\rangle\right|\lesssim\frac{\log N}{\widehat{\beta}_{12,s}}

with very high probability, for more details see e.g.
[
32
, Eq.(5.29)–(5.31)]
.

Recall the definition of
R
from (
S2.92
). We further bound

(S2.96)

\left|\left\langle|G_{2,s}|R\right\rangle\right|=\left|\left\langle\Im G_{1,s}B_{3}^{*}G_{1,s}^{*}B_{2}^{*}|G_{2,s}|B_{2}G_{1,s}B_{3}\right\rangle\right|\leq\lVert\Im G_{2,s}\rVert\left\langle B_{3}^{*}G_{1,s}^{*}B_{2}^{*}|G_{2,s}|B_{2}G_{1,s}B_{3}\right\rangle.

Finally, we estimate the trace in the rhs. of (
S2.96
) from above by
(\eta_{*,s}\widehat{\beta}_{12,s})^{-1}
similarly to (
S2.94
)–(
S2.95
), bound trivially
\lVert\Im G_{2,s}\rVert\leq\eta_{*,s}^{-1}
, and combine the resulting bound with (
S2.91
) and (
S2.95
). We thus get

\frac{1}{N^{2}\eta_{1,s}^{2}}\left|\left\langle\Im G_{1,s}B_{1}G_{2,s}B_{2}G_{1,s}B_{3}\Im G_{1,s}B_{3}^{*}G_{1,s}^{*}B_{2}^{*}G_{2,s}^{*}B_{1}^{*}\right\rangle\right|\lesssim\frac{(\log N)^{2}}{N\eta_{*,s}^{4}(\widehat{\beta}_{12,s})^{2}}.

Integrating this bound over
s\in[0,t\wedge\tau]
, we finish the proof of (
S2.84
) for
k=3
.

k=4
.
Similarly to (
S2.86
), we upper bound
|(\mathcal{C}^{(4)}_{s})_{\bm{\sigma},\bm{\sigma}}|
by the sum of four terms of the following type:

(S2.97)

\frac{1}{N^{2}\eta_{1,s}^{2}}\left\langle\Im G_{1,s}B_{1}G_{2,s}B_{2}G_{1,s}B_{3}G_{2,s}B_{4}\Im G_{1,s}B_{4}^{*}G_{2,s}^{*}B_{3}^{*}G_{1,s}^{*}B_{2}^{*}G_{2,s}^{*}B_{1}^{*}\right\rangle.

We apply (
S2.91
) with
G_{1,s}
instead of
G_{2,s}
and choose

(S2.98)

R:=B_{3}G_{2,s}B_{4}\Im G_{1,s}B_{4}^{*}G_{2,s}^{*}B_{3}^{*},\quad S:=B_{2}^{*}G_{2,s}^{*}B_{1}^{*}\Im G_{1,s}B_{1}G_{2,s}B_{2}.

This yields that (
S2.97
) is bounded by

(S2.99)

\frac{1}{N\eta_{1,s}^{2}}\left|\left\langle|G_{1,s}|B_{3}G_{2,s}B_{4}\Im G_{1,s}B_{4}^{*}G_{2,s}^{*}B_{3}^{*}\right\rangle\left\langle|G_{1,s}|B_{2}^{*}G_{2,s}^{*}B_{1}^{*}\Im G_{1,s}B_{1}G_{2,s}B_{2}\right\rangle\right|.

Since the traces in the rhs. of (
S2.99
) are analogous, we further focus on the first of them. We use the integral representation (
S2.93
) for
|G_{1,s}|
and denote
\widetilde{G}_{1,s}:=G^{z_{1,s}}_{s}\left(\Re w_{1,s}+\sqrt{\eta_{1,s}^{2}+x^{2}}\right)
, omitting
x
from this notation. We obtain the four-resolvent chain with Hermitization parameters alternating between
z_{1,s}
and
z_{2,s}
, however the spectral parameters of
\widetilde{G}_{1,s}
and
G_{1,s}
differ more than just by a complex conjugation. To remove this discrepancy, we observe that for any
X,Y\in\mathbf{C}^{(2N)\times(2N)}
it holds that

(S2.100)

\left|\left\langle G_{2,s}XG_{2,s}^{*}Y\right\rangle\right|\leq\left\langle|G_{2,s}|X|G_{2,s}|X^{*}\right\rangle^{1/2}\left\langle|G_{2,s}|Y|G_{2,s}|Y^{*}\right\rangle^{1/2}.

We stress that the semi-definiteness of
X
and
Y
is not required in (
S4.11
). This bound immediately follows from the fact that
G_{2,s}
is diagonalizable and from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. Applying (
S4.11
) for
X=B_{4}\Im G_{1,s}B_{4}^{*}
and
Y:=B_{3}^{*}\Im\widetilde{G}_{1,s}B_{3}
, we get

(S2.101)

\begin{split}&\left|\left\langle\Im\widetilde{G}_{1,s}B_{3}G_{2,s}B_{4}\Im G_{1,s}B_{4}^{*}G_{2,s}^{*}B_{3}^{*}\right\rangle\right|\\
&\quad\leq\left\langle|G_{2,s}|B_{4}\Im G_{1,s}B_{4}^{*}|G_{2,s}|B_{4}^{*}\Im G_{1,s}B_{4}\right\rangle^{1/2}\left\langle|G_{2,s}|B_{3}\Im\widetilde{G}_{1,s}B_{3}^{*}|G_{2,s}|B_{3}^{*}\Im\widetilde{G}_{1,s}B_{3}\right\rangle^{1/2}.\end{split}

We further focus again on the first factor in the rhs. of (
S2.101
) and use the integral representation (
S2.93
) for both matrices
|G_{2,s}|
appearing in the product. Denoting the resolvents arising from these representations by
\widetilde{G}_{2,s}
and
\widetilde{G}_{2,s}^{\prime}
, we reduce the first trace in the rhs. of (
S2.101
) to

(S2.102)

\begin{split}&\left|\left\langle\Im\widetilde{G}_{2,s}B_{4}\Im G_{1,s}B_{4}^{*}\Im\widetilde{G}_{2,s}^{\prime}B_{4}^{*}\Im G_{1,s}B_{4}\right\rangle\right|\\
&\quad\leq\left\langle\Im G_{1,s}B_{4}^{*}\Im\widetilde{G}_{2,s}^{\prime}B_{4}^{*}\Im G_{1,s}B_{4}\Im\widetilde{G}_{2,s}^{\prime}B_{4}\right\rangle^{1/2}\left\langle\Im G_{1,s}B_{4}^{*}\Im\widetilde{G}_{2,s}B_{4}^{*}\Im G_{1,s}B_{4}\Im\widetilde{G}_{2,s}B_{4}\right\rangle^{1/2},\end{split}

where we used the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to go from the first to the second line. From the definition of the stopping time
\tau_{4}
(
S2.70
), the fact that
s\leq\tau_{4}
, and (
S2.42
), we get that each of the traces in the rhs. of (
S2.102
) has an upper bound of order
N^{2\xi_{4}}\eta_{*,s}^{-1}(\widehat{\beta}_{12,s})^{-2}
. Finally, combining this bound with (
S2.99
)–(
S2.102
) and performing the integration similarly to (
S2.94
)–(
S2.95
) in the representations (
S2.93
) which we used along the way, we obtain the following bound on the integral of (
S2.97
) in
s\in[0,t\wedge\tau]
:

(S2.103)

\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\frac{1}{N\eta_{*,s}^{2}}\left(\frac{N^{2\xi_{4}}}{\eta_{*,s}\widehat{\beta}_{12,s}}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\frac{N^{4\xi_{4}}}{N\eta_{*,t\wedge\tau}}\left(\frac{1}{\eta_{*,t\wedge\tau}(\widehat{\beta}_{12,t\wedge\tau})^{2}}\right)^{2}.

Here we used the second part of (
S2.39
) and (
S2.17
) for
a=4
. Since
N\eta_{*,t\wedge\tau}\geq N^{\epsilon}
and
\xi_{4}<\epsilon/10
by (
S2.69
), (
S2.103
) finishes the proof of (
S2.84
) for
k=4
.

Next, we prove that

(S2.104)

\int_{0}^{t\wedge\tau}\|\mathcal{F}^{(k)}_{s}\|\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\begin{cases}N^{\xi_{2}}(N\eta_{*,t\wedge\tau}\widehat{\beta}_{12,t\wedge\tau})^{-1},&k=2,\\
N^{\xi_{3}}(\sqrt{N\eta_{*,t\wedge\tau}}\eta_{*,t\wedge\tau}\widehat{\beta}_{12,t\wedge\tau})^{-1},&k=3,\\
N^{\xi_{4}}(\eta_{*,t\wedge\tau}(\widehat{\beta}_{12,t\wedge\tau})^{2})^{-1},&k=4.\end{cases}

Since the proof of (
S2.104
) does not require any additional ideas compared to the proof of (
S2.84
), we only comment on the involved reduction inequalities. The proof of (
S2.104
) for
k=2
does not require any reductions of resolvent chains to shorter ones, and only utilizes the bounds provided by the stopping time
\tau
, single-resolvent local law (
3.34
), and Proposition
S2.4
. For
k=3
reductions are needed only for the terms in the first line of (
S2.56
). There are two different types of such terms, which we present in the lhs. of the following two lines and immediately demonstrate the corresponding reductions performed by the means of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality:

(S2.105)

\begin{split}&\left|\left\langle G_{1,s}E_{\sigma}G_{1,s}B_{1}G_{2,s}B_{2}G_{1,s}B_{3}\right\rangle\right|\leq\frac{1}{\eta_{*,s}^{3/2}}\left|\left\langle\Im G_{1,s}B_{3}^{*}B_{3}\right\rangle\right|^{1/2}\left|\left\langle\Im G_{1,s}B_{1}G_{2,s}B_{2}\Im G_{1,s}B_{2}^{*}G_{2,s}^{*}B_{1}^{*}\right\rangle\right|^{1/2}\\
&\left|\left\langle G_{1,s}B_{1}G_{2,s}E_{\sigma}G_{2,s}B_{2}G_{1,s}B_{3}\right\rangle\right|\leq\frac{1}{\eta_{*,s}^{3/2}}\left|\left\langle\Im G_{1,s}B_{1}\Im G_{2,s}B_{1}^{*}\right\rangle\right|^{1/2}\left|\left\langle G_{1,s}^{*}B_{2}^{*}\Im G_{2,s}B_{2}G_{1,s}B_{3}B_{3}^{*}\right\rangle\right|^{1/2}.\end{split}

Here
\sigma\in\{\pm\}
is fixed, i.e. we do not perform the summation over this index. When the lhs. of both lines of (
S2.105
) appear in the first line of (
S2.56
), they are multiplied by
\left\langle(G_{j,s}-M_{j,s})E_{\sigma}\right\rangle
for
j=1
and
j=2
respectively, which gives an additional small factor
(N\eta_{*,s})^{-1}
. Estimating the rhs. of both lines of (
S2.105
) using that
s\leq\tau
and employing the deterministic bounds from Proposition
S2.4
, we prove (
S2.104
) for
k=3
.

We are now left with (
S2.104
) for
k=4
, where the reduction is needed again only for the terms in the first line of (
S2.56
). All of these terms are of the same form, so we consider only one representative:

(S2.106)

\left\langle(G_{1,s}-M_{1,s})E_{\sigma}\right\rangle\left\langle G_{1,s}E_{\sigma}G_{1,s}B_{1}G_{2,s}B_{2}G_{1,s}B_{3}G_{2,s}B_{4}\right\rangle.

Similarly to (
S4.11
) we estimate the second trace in (
S2.106
) from above by

(S2.107)

\frac{1}{\eta_{*,s}}\left|\left\langle\Im G_{1,s}B_{1}G_{2,s}B_{2}|G_{1,s}|B_{2}^{*}G_{2,s}^{*}B_{1}^{*}\right\rangle\right|^{1/2}\left|\left\langle\Im G_{1,s}B_{4}^{*}G_{2,s}^{*}B_{3}^{*}|G_{1,s}|B_{3}G_{2}B_{4}\right\rangle\right|^{1/2}.

Arguing analogously to (
S2.101
)–(
S2.102
), we get that each of the traces in (
S2.107
) has an upper bound of order
N^{2\xi_{4}}\eta_{*,s}^{-1}(\widehat{\beta}_{12,s})^{-2}
, up to some irrelevant
\log N
factor. Together with the single-resolvent local law (
3.34
), this yields an upper bound of order

\frac{N^{2\xi_{4}}}{N\eta_{*,s}^{3}(\widehat{\beta}_{12,s})^{2}}

on the absolute value of the term in (
S2.106
). Integrating this bound over
s\in[0,t\wedge\tau]
, we finish the proof of (
S2.104
) for
k=4
.

Finally, we explain how to adapt the proof of (
S2.84
) and (
S2.104
) to prove the bounds of order

N^{2\xi_{k}}(N\eta_{*,t\wedge\tau}^{k})^{-2}\quad\text{and}\quad N^{\xi_{k}}(N\eta_{*,t\wedge\tau}^{k})^{-1}

on the lhs. of (
S2.84
) and (
S2.104
), respectively. First, throughout the proof we replace the use of Proposition
S2.4
for estimating deterministic approximations, by Lemma
S2.6
, which provides a weaker control, but purely in terms of
\eta_{*}
. Similarly, for all times
s\leq t\wedge\tau
we use only the bounds involving solely
\eta_{*}
which are provided by (
S2.70
), e.g. for four-resolvent chains we apply

(S2.108)

\left|\left\langle\left(G_{1,s}B_{1}G_{2,s}B_{2}G_{1,s}B_{3}G_{2,s}-M_{1212,s}^{B_{1},B_{2},B_{3}}\right)B_{4}\right\rangle\right|\leq\frac{N^{2\xi_{4}}}{N\eta_{*,s}^{4}}\quad\text{for}\quad 0\leq s\leq t\wedge\tau.

A further modification is required for the estimates where the reduction inequalities (
S2.89
) and (
S2.91
) were used, since these bounds introduce an additional factor
N
, which is not affordable now. These reductions were used only in three instances: for both terms in (
S2.88
) and for the term in (
S2.97
). Now we show how to control these quantities in terms of
\eta_{*}
without incurring factor
N
. We demonstrate this argument only for the first term in (
S2.88
), while for the second term in (
S2.88
) and for (
S2.97
) the estimates are analogous. We have

(S2.109)

\begin{split}&\left|\left\langle\Im G_{1,s}B_{1}G_{2,s}B_{2}G_{1,s}B_{3}\Im G_{1,s}B_{3}^{*}G_{1,s}^{*}B_{2}^{*}G_{2,s}^{*}B_{1}^{*}\right\rangle\right|\\
&\quad\leq\lVert B_{1}^{*}\Im G_{1,s}B_{1}\rVert\left|\left\langle G_{2,s}B_{2}G_{1,s}B_{3}\Im G_{1,s}B_{3}^{*}G_{1,s}^{*}B_{2}^{*}G_{2,s}^{*}\right\rangle\right|\\
&\quad\leq\lVert B_{1}^{*}\Im G_{1,s}B_{1}\rVert\lVert B_{3}\Im G_{1,s}B_{3}^{*}\rVert\left|\left\langle G_{2,s}B_{2}G_{1,s}G_{1,s}^{*}B_{2}^{*}G_{2,s}^{*}\right\rangle\right|\leq\eta_{*,s}^{-4}\left|\left\langle\Im G_{2,s}B_{2}\Im G_{1,s}B_{2}^{*}\right\rangle\right|\lesssim\eta_{*,s}^{-5}.\end{split}

To go from the first to the second line of (
S2.109
) we used that the resolvent chain in the second factor in the second line of (
S2.109
) is either positive or negative semi-definite, depending on the sign of
\Im w_{1,s}
. Similarly, to go from the second to the third line we used that
G_{2,s}B_{2}G_{1,s}G_{1,s}^{*}B_{2}^{*}G_{2,s}^{*}\geq 0
. Additionally, in the first inequality in the third line of (
S2.109
) we estimated
\|\Im G_{1,s}\|\leq\eta_{*,s}^{-1}
and used the Ward identity twice. Finally, it the last estimate (
S2.21
) and the concentration bound provided by
\tau_{2}
were used. This completes the proof of Proposition
S2.10
. ∎

S2.2.4.
Modifications for the case of general
B_{i}\in\mathrm{span}\{E_{\pm},F^{(*)}\}

In Sections
S2.2.1
–
S2.2.3
we proved that the bounds (
S2.24
)–(
S2.26
) propagate in time in the special case when
B_{i}\in\{E_{\pm}\}
,
i\in[4]
. Notably, in this argument we do not assume (
S2.24
)–(
S2.26
) at time
t=0
for the remaining choices of observables. In the current section we treat the general case
B_{i}\in\mathrm{span}\{E_{\pm},F^{(*)}\}
and complete the proof of Proposition
S2.5
. To do so, we use the already established concentration bounds (
S2.24
)–(
S2.26
) for all
t\in[0,T]
in the case when
B_{i}=E_{\pm}
,
i\in[4]
, and slightly adjust the argument presented in Sections
S2.2.1
–
S2.2.3
to cover this more general case.

Throughout this section we consider only four choices for each observable, specifically
B_{i}\in\{E_{\pm},F^{(*)}\}
,
i\in[4]
. This does not decrease the generality of the set-up, since any other observable in Proposition
S2.5
is a linear combination of these four, and the lhs. of (
S2.24
)–(
S2.26
) are linear in each of the observables. In fact, when some of the observables in the resolvent chain equal to
F^{(*)}
, the fluctuation of this chain around its deterministic approximation is expected to be smaller, see e.g.
[
24
, Theorem 4.4]
and
[
37
, Theorem 3.5]
. However, this improvement is not needed for our purposes, and we do not pursue it further. This makes the proof of Proposition
S2.5
for
B_{i}\in\{E_{\pm},F^{(*)}\}
fairly analogous to the proof in the special case
B_{i}\in\{E_{\pm}\}
. These two arguments can be even combined into one, but we keep them separate for clarity of the presentation.

Throughout the proof we freely use the notations introduced in Section
S2.2.1
without mentioning this further. We first consider a general set-up introduced above (
S2.51
), i.e. we follow the time evolution of
k\geq 2
resolvents
G_{j,t}=G^{z_{j,t}}_{t}(w_{j,t})
,
j\in[k]
. The starting point of our analysis is (
S2.55
). We again treat the terms in the third line of (
S2.55
) as error terms and focus on the structure of the so-called linear terms in the second line of (
S2.55
). For any choice of
B_{j}\in\{E_{\pm},F^{(*)}\}
,
j\in[k]
, the number of observables equal to
F^{(*)}
in each of these terms does not exceed the number of
F^{(*)}
observables among
\{B_{j}\}_{j=1}^{k}
. Moreover, the terms in the second line of (
S2.55
) corresponding to the summation index
j
with
B_{j}\in\{F^{(*)}\}
, contain strictly less
F^{(*)}
observables. This suggests the following structural decomposition of the terms in the second line of (
S2.55
) into two groups.
The sum of terms in the first group is given by

(S2.110)

\mathrm{L}_{[k],t}:=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sigma\mathbf{1}\left\{B_{j}\in\{F^{(*)}\}\right\}\left\langle M_{[j,j+1],t}^{B_{j}}E_{\sigma}\right\rangle Y_{[k],t}(B_{1},\ldots,B_{j-1},E_{\sigma},B_{j+1},\ldots,B_{k})\mathrm{d}t,

while the remaining terms contain the same number of
F^{(*)}
observables as the lhs. of (
S2.55
). We treat (
S2.110
) as an error term, while the rest of the terms in the second line of (
S2.55
) contribute to the propagator of the system of equations analogous to (
S2.61
), which we now construct.

Unlike in Section
S2.2.1
, instead of one system for each
k\geq 2
, we construct several systems of stochastic differential equations analogous to (
S2.61
) to accommodate observables equal to
F^{(*)}
. These systems are labeled by the set of indices
\mathcal{J}\subset[k]
, which indicates the indices of observables equal to
F^{(*)}
in
Y_{[k],t}(B_{1},\ldots,B_{k})
, and by the exact choice of
\{B_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\in\{F^{(*)}\}^{|\mathcal{J}|}
. Denote

f:=|\mathcal{J}|,\quad\mathcal{J}:=\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{f}\},\quad[k]\setminus\mathcal{J}:=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k-f}\}.

We fix
B_{j}\in\{F^{(*)}\}
for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
and for the remaining indices
i\in[k]\setminus\mathcal{J}
consider all
2^{k-f}
possible choices
B_{i}\in\{E_{\pm}\}
. Similarly to (
S2.58
) define

(S2.111)

\mathcal{Y}_{[k],\mathcal{J},t}=\mathcal{Y}_{[k],\mathcal{J},t}(B_{j_{1}},\ldots,B_{j_{f}}):=\sum_{\sigma_{i_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{i_{k-f}}\in\{\pm\}}Y_{[k],t}(\cdots)\bm{e}_{\sigma_{i_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{i_{k-f}}}\in(\mathbf{C}^{2})^{\otimes(k-f)},

where the arguments of
Y_{[k],t}
in the rhs. of (
S2.111
) are given by
\{B_{j}^{\prime}\}_{j=1}^{k}
with
B_{j}^{\prime}=B_{j}
for
j\in\mathcal{J}
and
B_{j}=E_{\sigma_{j}}
for
j\notin\mathcal{J}
. Define further the
(\mathbf{C}^{2})^{\otimes(k-f)}
vectors
\mathcal{L}_{[k],\mathcal{J},t}
,
\mathcal{F}_{[k],\mathcal{J},t}
and
\mathrm{d}\mathcal{E}_{[k],\mathcal{J},t}
analogously to (
S2.111
), from (
S2.110
), (
S2.56
) and (
S2.52
), respectively. With these notations we get from (
S2.51
) the following analogue of (
S2.61
):

(S2.112)

\mathrm{d}\mathcal{Y}_{[k],\mathcal{J},t}=\left(\frac{k}{2}+\mathcal{A}_{[k],\mathcal{J},t}\right)\mathcal{Y}_{[k],\mathcal{J},t}\mathrm{d}t+\mathcal{L}_{[k],\mathcal{J},t}\mathrm{d}t+\mathcal{F}_{[k],\mathcal{J},t}\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}\mathcal{E}_{[k],\mathcal{J},t},

where the time-dependent linear operator
\mathcal{A}_{[k],\mathcal{J},t}:(\mathbf{C}^{2})^{\otimes(k-f)}\to(\mathbf{C}^{2})^{\otimes(k-f)}
is given by

(S2.113)

\mathcal{A}_{[k],\mathcal{J},t}:=\sum_{\sigma_{i_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{i_{k-f}}\in\{\pm\}}\bigg(\sum_{l=1}^{k-f}a_{{i_{l}}(i_{l}+1),t}^{\sigma_{i_{l}}}\bigg)P_{\sigma_{i_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{i_{f-k}}}-\sum_{l=1}^{k-f}d_{i_{l}(i_{l}+1),t}S_{l}.

Importantly, no additional analysis of the operator
\mathcal{A}_{[k],\mathcal{J},t}
is required compared to the one performed in Lemma
S2.9
in the special case
f=0
. Analogously to (
S2.64
) we denote

f_{[k],\mathcal{J},r}:=\left[\max\mathrm{spec}(\Re\mathcal{A}_{[k],\mathcal{J},r})\right]_{+},\quad\forall\,r\in[0,T].

Then we have

(S2.114)

\exp\left\{\int_{s}^{t}f_{[k],\mathcal{J},r}\mathrm{d}r\right\}\lesssim\prod_{l=1}^{k-f}\frac{\beta_{i_{l}(i_{l}+1),*,s}}{\beta_{i_{l}(i_{l}+1),*,t}}\lesssim\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\beta_{i(i+1),*,s}}{\beta_{i(i+1),*,t}}.

Here the first bound is derived from (
S2.65
) and (
S2.66
) similarly to (
S2.67
) (see also (
A.27
)), while the second one follows from the first part of (
S2.39
). In principle, (
S2.114
) is an overestimate, and instead of working with the rightmost expression in (
S2.114
) one could perform the refined analysis using the middle expression in (
S2.114
), which would lead to the improvement mentioned in the second paragraph of this section.

By definition,
\mathcal{L}_{[k],\mathcal{J},t}
in the rhs. of (
S2.112
) is constructed from the fluctuations of multi-resolvent chains with strictly less than
f
observables. It is easy to see from (
S2.56
) that the same holds for the forcing term
\mathcal{F}_{[k],\mathcal{J},t}
. However, the quadratic variation of the martingale term
\mathrm{d}\mathcal{E}_{[k],\mathcal{J},t}
typically contains resolvent chains with more than
f
observables equal to
F^{(*)}
. This prevents us from analysing (
S2.112
) inductively, obtaining at first the upper bound on
Y_{[k],t}(B_{1},\ldots,B_{k})
with only one
F^{(*)}
observable, and then concluding the bound in the case of
f
observables
F^{(*)}
, relying on the bounds for all smaller numbers of the off-diagonal observables. The resolution of this issue is to control (
S2.112
) at the same time for all
\mathcal{J}\subset[k]
with
|\mathcal{J}|\geq 1
and for all choices
\{B_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\in\{F^{(*)}\}^{|\mathcal{J}|}
.

Now we restrict the setting to the one introduced in the beginning of Section
S2.2
. In particular, from now on
k\in\{2,3,4\}
,
z_{3,t}=z_{1,t}
and
z_{4,t}=z_{2,t}
. Similarly to (
S2.71
) we denote

(S2.115)

\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}_{\mathcal{J},t}:=\mathcal{Y}_{12,\mathcal{J},t},\quad\mathcal{Y}^{(3)}_{\mathcal{J},t}:=\mathcal{Y}_{121,\mathcal{J},t},\quad\mathcal{Y}^{(4)}_{\mathcal{J},t}:=\mathcal{Y}_{1212,\mathcal{J},t},\quad\forall\mathcal{I}\subset[k],

and use the same notational convention for
\mathcal{A}_{[k],\mathcal{J},t}
,
f_{[k],\mathcal{J},t}
,
\mathcal{L}_{[k],\mathcal{J},t}
,
\mathcal{F}_{[k],\mathcal{J},t}
and
\mathcal{E}_{[k],\mathcal{J},t}
. We fix (small) tolerance exponents
\xi_{k,m}
for
k\in\{2,3,4\}
,
m\in[k]
, such that

(S2.116)

\xi_{k,m}\in(0,\epsilon/10),\quad\xi_{k,1}<\xi_{k,2}<\ldots<\xi_{k,k}\quad\text{and}\quad\xi_{4,4}/2<\xi_{2,1}<\xi_{2,2}<\xi_{4,1},

for all
k\in\{2,3,4\}
and
m\in[k]
, and for the same range of
(k,m)
define the stopping times
\tau_{k,m}
by

(S2.117)

\tau_{k,m}\!:=\inf\left\{\!t\!\in\![0,T]\,:\,\max_{|\mathcal{J}|=m}\,\max_{B_{j}\in\{F^{(*)}\},j\in\mathcal{J}}\,\max_{s\in[0,t]}\max_{|z_{j,T}|\leq\mathrm{e}^{-T/2}(1-\delta)}\max_{w_{j,T}\in\Omega_{\kappa,\epsilon,T}^{z_{j,T}}}\alpha_{k,s}^{-1}\|\mathcal{Y}^{(k)}_{\mathcal{J},s}\|\geq N^{2\xi_{k,m}}\!\right\}\!,

where
\mathcal{J}=\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{m}\}\subset[k]
and
\mathcal{Y}^{(k)}_{\mathcal{J},s}=\mathcal{Y}^{(k)}_{\mathcal{J},s}(B_{j_{1}},\ldots,B_{j_{m}})
. The control parameters
\alpha_{k,s}
for
k\in\{2,3,4\}
are defined in (
S2.68
) and do not depend on
\mathcal{J}
. Finally, we set

(S2.118)

\widetilde{\tau}:=\min_{k\in\{2,3,4\}}\min_{m\in[k]}\tau_{k,m}.

We claim that

(S2.119)

\left(\int_{0}^{t\wedge\widetilde{\tau}}\left(\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{J},s}^{(k)}\|+\|\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{J},s}^{(k)}\|\right)\mathrm{d}s\right)^{2}+\int_{0}^{t\wedge\widetilde{\tau}}\|\mathcal{C}^{(k)}_{\mathcal{J},s}\|\mathrm{d}s\lesssim N^{4\xi_{k,|\mathcal{J}|}-\xi}\alpha_{k,t\wedge\widetilde{\tau}}^{2},

uniformly in
t\in[0,T]
,
k\in\{2,3,4\}
,
\mathcal{J}\subset[k]
with
|\mathcal{J}|\geq 1
for some exponent
\xi>0
which depends only on the exponents introduced in (
S2.116
). In (
S2.119
) we denoted by
\mathcal{C}^{(k)}_{\mathcal{J},s}\mathrm{d}s\in\mathbf{C}^{2^{k-m}\times 2^{k-m}}
,
m:=|\mathcal{J}|
, the covariation process of
\mathrm{d}\mathcal{E}^{(k)}_{\mathcal{J},s}
. We also omitted for brevity the arguments of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{J},s}^{(k)}
,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{J},s}^{(k)}
and
\mathcal{C}^{(k)}_{\mathcal{J},s}
, which are given by
B_{j_{1}},\ldots,B_{j_{m}}\in\{F^{(*)}\}
, where
\mathcal{J}=\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{m}\}
. The bound (
S2.119
) is uniform in these arguments.

The proof of (
S2.119
) is completely analogous to the proof of Proposition
S2.10
, since there we never use that
B_{j}\in\{E_{\pm}\}
for
j\in[k]
. The only difference lies in the additional term
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{J},s}^{(k)}
, which is directly controlled by the stopping time
\widetilde{\tau}
for
s\in[0,\widetilde{\tau}]
. Having (
S2.119
) in hand, we finish the proof of Proposition
S2.5
similarly to the argument presented in Section
S2.2
relying on the stochastic Grönwall inequality and on (
S2.114
).

S2.3.
Proof of Proposition
4.8

To keep the presentation short we only prove this result for matrices with an order one Gaussian component. Then, this additional Gaussian component can be easily removed by a standard GFT argument. Since this proof is similar to the proof of Proposition
S2.5
, and in fact much simpler, we will omit several details and only present the main steps of the proof (we also refer to
[
81
]
for an analogous proof in the special case when all the
w_{i}
’s are on the imaginary axis). For
\eta_{*}\gtrsim 1
, the local laws in (
4.28
)–(
4.29
) follow analogously to the proof of Proposition
S2.1
. We thus now focus on the proof that these local laws can be propagated down to
N\eta_{*}\geq N^{\epsilon}
. For this purpose we consider the flow (
S2.1
), define
G_{t}^{z}(w)
as in (
S2.3
), and use the short-hand notation
G_{i,t}:=G_{t}^{z_{i}}(w_{i})
. Denote (here we suppress the dependence on the
B_{i}
’s from the notation)

(S2.120)

{{G}}_{[{i},{j}],t}:=\begin{cases}G_{i,t}B_{i}\dots B_{j-1}G_{j,t}&\mathrm{if}\quad i<j\\
G_{i,t}&\mathrm{if}\quad i=j\\
G_{i,t}B_{i,t}\dots G_{k,t}B_{k}G_{1,t}B_{1}\dots B_{j-1}G_{j,t}&\mathrm{if}\quad i>j\,,\end{cases}

and by
M_{[i,j],t}
its deterministic approximation (see e.g.
[
24
, Lemma D.1]
for a recursive relation for this deterministic term). For this deterministic approximation we have (this follows by a simple
meta argument
as in the proof of Lemma
S2.6
)

(S2.121)

\lVert M_{[i,j],t}\rVert\lesssim\frac{1}{\eta_{*,t}^{j-i}}.

Additionally, we define
G_{[{i},{j}],t}^{(l)}
exactly as
G_{[{i},{j}],t}
but with the
l
–th factor
G_{l,t}
being
replaced with
G_{l,t}^{2}
. Then, by Itô’s formula and (
S2.53
) we have

(S2.122)

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}\langle(G_{[1,k],t}-M_{{[1,k]},t})B_{k}\rangle&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{a,b=1}^{N}\partial_{ab}\langle G_{[1,k],t}B_{k}\rangle\mathrm{d}B_{ab,t}+\frac{k}{2}\langle(G_{[1,k],t}-M_{{[1,k]},t})B_{k}\rangle\mathrm{d}t\\
&\quad+\sum_{i,j=1\atop i<j}^{k}\sigma\langle G_{[i,j],t}E_{\sigma}\rangle\langle G_{[j,i],t}E_{\sigma}\rangle\mathrm{d}t-\sum_{i,j=1\atop i<j}^{k}\sigma\langle M_{[i,j],t}E_{\sigma}\rangle\langle M_{[j,i],t}E_{\sigma}\rangle\mathrm{d}t\\
&\quad+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\langle G_{i,t}-m_{i,t}\rangle\langle G^{(i)}_{[1,k],t}B_{k}\rangle\mathrm{d}t.\end{split}

For the special choice
B_{k}=N{\bm{x}}^{*}{\bm{y}}
, (
S2.122
) gives the evolution for isotropic quantities. To keep the presentation short, from now on we only focus on proving the averaged local law (
4.28
), the proof for the isotropic local law (
4.29
) being completely analogous.

Define the stopping time

(S2.123)

\tau:=\inf\left\{t\in[0,1]:\big|\langle(G_{[i,j],t}-M_{{[i,j]},t})B\rangle\big|=\frac{N^{2\xi_{j-i+1}}}{N\eta_{*,t}^{j-i+1}}\,\,\forall i<j\leq k\right\},

for small
\xi_{l}\leq\epsilon/10
with
\xi_{l-1}\leq\xi_{l/2}
. From now on, we always assume that
t\leq\tau
. Additionally, throughout this proof we will use (
S2.17
), even if not stated explicitly. We now discuss the estimate in the rhs. of (
S2.122
) one by one. The second term in the rhs. of (
S2.122
) can be easily neglected, as it amounts to a rescaling
e^{-kt/2}
, by a simple change of variables.

For the term in the third line of (
S2.122
) we estimate (assume
i\neq 1
for simplicity of notation)

\big|\langle G_{i,s}-m_{i,s}\rangle\big|\leq\frac{N^{\xi}}{N\eta_{i,s}},\qquad\quad\big|\langle G^{(i)}_{[1,k],s}B_{k}\rangle\big|\leq\langle G_{1}B_{1}B_{1}^{*}G_{1}^{*}\rangle^{1/2}\langle G^{(i)}_{[2,k],s}B_{k}B_{k}^{*}(G^{(i)}_{[2,k],s})^{*}\rangle^{1/2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\eta_{*,s}^{k}},

where we also used the deterministic norm bound
\lVert G_{i,t}\rVert\leq\eta_{i,t}^{-1}
(and that the
B_{i}
’s have bounded norms). We thus obtain

(S2.124)

\left|\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\langle G_{i,s}-m_{i,s}\rangle\langle G^{(i)}_{[1,k],s}B_{k}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s\right|\lesssim N^{\xi}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{N\eta_{i,s}}\frac{1}{\eta_{*,s}^{k}}\,\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\frac{N^{\xi}}{N\eta_{*,t}^{k}}.

For the terms in the second line of (
S2.122
), using (
S2.121
), we estimate (for simplicity of notation we consider
i=1
)

(S2.125)

\begin{split}\left|\sigma\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\big(G_{[1,j],t}-M_{[1,j],t}\big)E_{\sigma}\right\rangle\langle M_{[j,1],t}E_{\sigma}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s\right|&\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\frac{N^{\xi_{j}}}{N\eta_{*,s}^{j}}\frac{1}{\eta_{*,s}^{k+1-j}}\,\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\frac{N^{\xi_{j}}}{N\eta_{*,t}^{k}},\\
\left|\sigma\int_{0}^{t}\langle M_{[1,j],t}E_{\sigma}\rangle\left\langle\big(G_{[j,1],t}-M_{[j,1],t}\big)E_{\sigma}\right\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s\right|&\lesssim\frac{N^{\xi_{k+1-j}}}{N\eta_{*,t}^{k}},\\
\left|\sigma\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\big(G_{[1,j],t}-M_{[1,j],t}\big)E_{\sigma}\right\rangle\left\langle\big(G_{[j,1],t}-M_{[j,1],t}\big)E_{\sigma}\right\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s\right|&\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\frac{N^{\xi_{j}}}{N\eta_{*,s}^{j}}\frac{N^{\xi_{k+1-j}}}{N\eta_{*,s}^{k+2-j}}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\lesssim\frac{N^{\xi_{j}+\xi_{k+1-j}}}{N\eta_{*,t}}\frac{1}{N\eta_{*,t}^{k}}.\end{split}

Finally, we consider the first term in the rhs. of (
S2.122
). Similarly to the computations above, it is easy to see that its quadratic variation process is bounded by
(N^{2}\eta_{*,s})^{-2k-1}
, with very high probability. We thus obtain

(S2.126)

\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\left|\int_{0}^{s}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{a,b=1}^{N}\partial_{ab}\langle G_{[1,k],r}B_{k}\rangle\mathrm{d}B_{ab,r}\right|\lesssim\log N\left(\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{N^{2}\eta_{*,s}^{2k+1}}\,\mathrm{d}\right)^{1/2}\lesssim\frac{\log N}{N\eta_{*,s}^{k}}.

Combining all these estimates we obtain

(S2.127)

\big|\langle(G_{[i,j],t}-M_{{[i,j]},t})B\rangle\big|\lesssim\frac{N^{\xi_{j-i+1}}}{N\eta_{*,t}^{j-i+1}},

with very high probability for any
i<j\leq k
, thus concluding the proof of Proposition
4.8
.
∎

S2.4.
Proof of Proposition
4.2
for general
b\in[0,1]

The statement of Proposition
4.2
for
b=1
immediately follows from the averaged local law in Proposition
4.8
with
k=2
, so we further focus on the case
b\in(0,1)
. Since we proceed by a minor modification of the proof of Proposition
4.2
for
b=0
, we first remind the reader the overall structure of that proof, consisting of two main steps. The first step is the global law from Proposition
S2.1
which establishes the desired bounds on the fluctuations of resolvent chains for spectral parameters at distance of order one from the real axis. In the second step (see Proposition
S2.5
) we propagate these estimates down to the real axis via the characteristic flow. The proof of Proposition
4.2
for
b\in(0,1)
has the same structure up to the following adjustments, which we now describe.

We fix the final time
T:=N^{-b}
, which is consistent with the choice
T\sim 1
made for
b=0
, and keep the definition of the bulk truncated spectral domains
\{\Omega_{\kappa,\epsilon,t}^{z_{T}}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
from Definition
S2.2
. By Lemma
S2.3
(4), for any
|z_{T}|\leq 1-\delta
and
w\in\Omega_{\kappa,\epsilon,0}^{z_{T}}
it holds that
|\Im w|\gtrsim N^{-b}
. In particular, the global law from Proposition
S2.1
cannot be applied in this regime, so we use Proposition
4.8
instead for the lhs. of (
S2.6
)–(
S2.8
). This estimate is then used as the initial condition, which we propagate towards the real axis similarly to Proposition
S2.5
. To adapt this proposition to the current setting, we introduce the new control parameter:

(S2.128)

\widehat{\beta}^{[b]}_{12}(w_{1},w_{2}):=\widehat{\beta}_{12}(w_{1},w_{2})\wedge N^{-b}+|\Im w_{1}|\wedge|\Im w_{2}|\wedge 1,

and define its time-dependent version
\widehat{\beta}^{[b]}_{12,t}
for
t\in[0,T]
similarly to (
S2.20
). Then Proposition
S2.5
holds with
\widehat{\beta}_{12,t}
replaced by
\widehat{\beta}_{12,t}^{[b]}
. To see this we observe that the proof of Proposition
S2.5
does not directly rely on the definition of
\widehat{\beta}_{12}
given in (
4.8
), but it only relies on the properties of this control parameter stated in (
4.9
), (
4.10
), and Lemma
S2.8
. Trivially,
\widehat{\beta}_{12}^{[b]}
satisfies these properties as well.

It remains to observe that
\widehat{\beta}^{[b]}_{12,0}\sim\eta_{*,0}
, so (
S2.24
)–(
S2.26
) folds for
t=0
with
\widehat{\beta}_{12,t}
replaced by
\widehat{\beta}_{12,t}^{[b]}
, by Proposition
4.8
. Propagating these bounds up to the final time
T
, we finish the proof of Proposition
4.2
for
b\in(0,1)
.
∎

S2.5.
Proof of Proposition
4.5

It is known from
[
20
, Theorem 3.4]
that (
4.22
) holds for spectral parameters on the imaginary axis, i.e. for
\Re w_{1}=\Re w_{2}=0
. Thus, we only need to extend this result to the bulk regime
\Re w_{i}\in\mathbf{B}_{\kappa}^{z_{i}}
,
i=1,2
, which we do by a minor modification of the proof of
[
20
, Theorem 3.4]
. The zag step (removal of the Gaussian component) remains unchanged compared in the one in
[
20
, Section 5]
. In the zig step (adding a Gaussian component), we propagate the local law estimate down to the real axis by applying Gronwall inequality to the analogue of (
S2.61
), instead of integrating these equations directly as it was done in
[
20
]
(see e.g. Eq. (4.28) therein). This allows us to avoid extension of the deterministic estimates
[
20
, Eq.(4.27)]
to the bulk regime, and use Lemma
4.3
instead.

We note that
[
20
]
fully covers the regime
|z_{i}|\leq 1+\delta
,
i=1,2
, for a small
\delta>0
, while Proposition
4.5
concerns only
|z_{i}|\leq 1-\delta
. This allows to simplify the proof of
[
20
, Theorem 3.4]
in our set-up by neglecting all
\rho^{z}
factors appearing in the estimates, since they are of order 1.

Appendix S3
The real case

In this section we prove Theorem
2.6
by following a similar proof to Theorem
2.4
. The overall proof structure in the real case remains the same as in the complex case, we thus primarily focus on explaining the main technical differences and new difficulties. We frequently use the convention that a free index
\sigma
appearing in formulas is meant to be summed over
\sigma\in\{\pm\}
if the opposite is not stated. We also use
\xi>0
to denote an
N
-independent exponent which can be taken arbitrarily small and whose exact value may change from line to line.

Denote the self-energy operator introduced in the complex case in (
3.28
) by
\mathcal{S}_{2}
, where the subscript reflects the symmetry type
\beta=2
. In the real case we define

(S3.1)

\mathcal{S}_{1}[R]:=\mathcal{S}_{2}[R]+\frac{\sigma}{N}E_{\sigma}R^{\mathfrak{t}}E_{\sigma},\quad\forall R\in\mathbf{C}^{(2N)\times(2N)}.

Here and everywhere further in this section we denote the transpose of a matrix
R
by
R^{\mathfrak{t}}
. Another quantity closely related to (
S3.1
) which changes compared to the complex case is the bilinear form

(S3.2)

\mathbf{E}\langle WR\rangle\langle WS\rangle=\frac{\sigma}{4N^{2}}\langle RE_{\sigma}SE_{\sigma}\rangle+\frac{\sigma}{4N^{2}}\langle RE_{\sigma}S^{\mathfrak{t}}E_{\sigma}\rangle,

cf. with (
7.10
) in the complex case, where only the first term in the rhs. of (
S3.2
) is present. Though
\mathcal{S}_{1}
differs from
\mathcal{S}_{2}
, we still use the MDE (
3.29
) with
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}_{2}
and the notions of deterministic approximations to resolvent chains introduced in Section
4
, without adapting them to the real case. As our proof shows, this slight conceptual mismatch is still affordable as the new term in (
S3.1
) only amounts to a negligible contribution. The advantage, in particular, is that the two-body stability operator
\mathcal{B}_{12}
defined in (
4.7
) is unchanged, so we do not need to redo the stability analysis from Proposition
4.4
, as well as the rest of the analysis of deterministic approximations along the proof of Theorem
2.4
.

Due to the real symmetry of the model, we often encounter transposes of resolvents in the proof of Theorem
2.6
. These can be written again as resolvents, but with conjugated Hermitization parameter:

(S3.3)

\left(G^{z}(w)\right)^{\mathfrak{t}}=G^{\overline{z}}(w).

Thus, it is convenient to denote the deterministic approximation to
G_{1}B_{1}G_{2}^{\mathfrak{t}}
with
G_{j}=G^{z_{j}}(w_{j})
for
j=1,2
, by
M_{1\overline{2}}^{B_{1}}(w_{1},w_{2})
. In other words, a bar over a subscript
j
means that we are considering the deterministic approximation of the product of two resolvents when
G^{z_{j}}(w_{j})
is replaced by
G^{\overline{z}_{j}}(w_{j})
. We will use this convention also for
\widehat{\beta}_{12}
, which is defined in (
4.8
), and for the deterministic approximations to longer resolvent chains.

This section is structured as follows. First, we state the real case analogues of the technical results listed in Sections
3.2.1
–
3.2.3
. Next, in Section
S3.1
we prove Theorem
2.6
relying on these results. Finally, in Sections
S3.2
–
S3.4
we adapt the proofs of the technical ingredients presented in Sections
6
–
7
to the real case.

We start with stating the analogue of Proposition
3.4
for real i.i.d. matrices, postponing the proof to Section
S3.3
.

Proposition S3.1
.

Let
X
be a real
N\times N
i.i.d. matrix satisfying Assumption
2.1
. Denote
\kappa_{4}:=\mathbf{E}|\chi|^{4}-3
. Fix (small)
\delta,\delta_{r},\epsilon,\kappa,\xi>0
. Uniformly in
z_{l}\in(1-\delta)\mathbf{D}
with
|\Im z_{l}|\geq N^{-1/2+\delta_{r}}
, and
w_{l}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathbf{R}
with
E_{l}:=\Re w_{l}\in\mathbf{B}_{\kappa}^{z_{l}}
and
\eta_{l}\in[N^{-1+\epsilon},1]
,
l=1,2
, it holds that

(S3.4)

\begin{split}\mathrm{Cov}\left(\langle G^{z_{1}}(w_{1})\rangle,\langle G^{z_{2}}(w_{2})\rangle\right)&=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\cdot\frac{\widehat{V}_{12}+\kappa_{4}U_{1}U_{2}}{2}\\
&+\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\frac{1}{N\widehat{\gamma}}+\frac{1}{N|\Im z_{1}|^{2}}+\frac{1}{N|\Im z_{2}|^{2}}+N^{-1/4}\right)\frac{N^{\xi}}{N^{2}\eta_{1}\eta_{2}}\right),\end{split}

where
\widehat{V}_{12}=\widehat{V}_{12}(z_{1},z_{2},w_{1},w_{2})
and
\widehat{\gamma}=\widehat{\gamma}(z_{1},z_{2},w_{1},w_{2})
are defined as

(S3.5)

\begin{split}\widehat{\gamma}(z_{1},z_{2},w_{1},w_{2}):=&\min_{z_{1}^{\prime}\in\{z_{1},\overline{z}_{1}\}}\min_{z_{2}^{\prime}\in\{z_{2},\overline{z}_{2}\}}\gamma(z_{1}^{\prime},z_{2}^{\prime},w_{1},w_{2}),\\
\widehat{V}_{12}(z_{1},z_{2},w_{1},w_{2}):=&V_{12}(z_{1},z_{2},w_{1},w_{2})+V_{12}(z_{1},\overline{z}_{2},w_{1},w_{2}),\end{split}

while
V_{12}
,
U_{l}
for
l=1,2
, and
\gamma
are defined in (
3.36
).

The leading order term in the rhs. of (
S3.4
) was initially computed in
[
29
, Proposition 3.3]
, and our contribution consists in the improvement of the bound on the error term. Compared to (
3.35
), the bound on the error term in the rhs. of (
S3.4
) contains the additional terms
(N|\Im z_{j}|^{2})^{-1}
which blow up as
z_{j}
approaches the real line. In fact, these terms are present only for technical reasons and they can be removed by extending the hierarchy of covariances introduced in the proof of Proposition
3.4
. Specifically, one would need to include into consideration the quantities (
7.28
), where some of the resolvents
G_{1}
are replaced by
G_{1}^{\mathfrak{t}}
. However, in order to keep the adjustments in the real case minimal, we use the same hierarchy of covariances, which leads to the deterioration of the estimate on the error term.

To analyze the sub-microscopic regime (
\eta\ll N^{-1}
) in the Girko’s formula (
3.20
), we establish the following real version of Proposition
3.5
.

Proposition S3.2

(Left tail of the least positive eigenvalue distribution; real case)
.

Let
X
be a real
N\times N
i.i.d. matrix satisfying Assumption
2.1
. Fix (small)
\delta,\delta_{r},\xi>0
and set
\nu_{1}:=1/10
as in Proposition
3.5
. Uniformly in the parameters

z\in(1-\delta)\mathbf{D},\,|\Im z|\geq N^{-1/2+\delta_{r}}\quad\text{and}\quad N^{\xi}\left(N^{-\nu_{1}}+\left(N^{1/2}|\Im z|\right)^{-1/2}\right)\leq x\leq 1,

it holds that

(S3.6)

\mathbf{P}[\lambda_{1}^{z}\leq N^{-1}x]\lesssim\log N\cdot x^{2}.

In other words, Proposition
S3.2
asserts that

(S3.7)

\mathbf{P}[\lambda_{1}^{z}\leq N^{-1}x]\lesssim\log N\cdot x^{2}+N^{\xi}\left(N^{-2\nu_{1}}+\left(N^{1/2}|\Im z|\right)^{-1}\right),

for any fixed
\xi>0
, uniformly in
0<x\leq 1
. This bound immediately follows from (
S3.6
) and the fact that the lhs. of (
S3.6
) monotonically increases in
x
. Note that compared to the complex analogue (
3.41
), the estimate (
S3.7
) contains the additional term
\left(N^{1/2}|\Im z|\right)^{-1}
, which makes (
S3.7
) ineffective for
|\Im z|
just slightly above
N^{-1/2}
.

The real version of Proposition
3.6
required for the analysis of the microscopic regime (
\eta\sim N^{-1}
) in the Girko’s formula (
3.20
) is as follows.

Proposition S3.3
.

Let
X
be a real
N\times N
i.i.d. matrix satisfying Assumption
2.1
, and let
\widetilde{X}
be an
N\times N
GinOE matrix. Let
\widetilde{H}^{z}
and
\widetilde{G}^{z}
be defined as in Proposition
3.6
. Fix (small)
\delta,\delta_{r},\epsilon,\omega_{*}>0
. Then, uniformly in
z\in(1-\delta)\mathbf{D}
with
|\Im z|\geq N^{-1/2+\delta_{r}}
and
\eta\in[N^{-3/2+\epsilon},1]
, it holds that

(S3.8)

\mathbf{E}\langle G^{z}(\mathrm{i}\eta)\rangle=\mathbf{E}\langle\widetilde{G}^{z}(\mathrm{i}\eta)\rangle+\mathcal{O}(N^{\xi}\Phi_{1}^{r}(\eta,z))

for any fixed
\xi>0
, where
\Phi_{1}^{r}=\Phi_{1}^{r}(\eta,z)
is defined by

(S3.9)

\begin{split}\Phi_{1}^{r}:=&\min_{N^{-1+\omega_{*}}\leq t_{2}\leq t_{1}\leq N^{-\omega_{*}}}\bigg(N\mathcal{E}_{0}(t_{2})\left(1+N\eta+\frac{N\mathcal{E}_{0}(t_{2})}{N\eta}\right)\\
&+Nt_{2}\bigg(1+\left(\frac{1}{N\eta}\right)^{3}\bigg)\left(\frac{1}{N|\Im z|^{2}}+\frac{1}{Nt_{1}}\right)+\sqrt{N}t_{1}\left(1+\frac{1}{N\eta}\right)^{4}\bigg),\end{split}

with
\mathcal{E}_{0}
defined in (
3.43
).

The main difference of Proposition
S3.3
from Proposition
3.6
lies in the first term in the second line of (
S3.9
), while the remaining terms in the definition of the control parameter (
S3.9
) already appeared in (
3.43
). This additional term arises from the fact that we do not derive the analogue of the DBM relaxation from
[
14
, Proposition 4.6]
in the real case but instead directly use
[
14
, Proposition 4.6]
. This proof strategy necessitates adding a small complex Gaussian component to the real matrix
X
. Removing this component by GFT leads to the aforementioned deterioration of the bound on the error term. For more details see Section
S3.4
. Alternatively, one could establish Proposition
S3.3
by developing a real analogue of
[
14
, Proposition 4.6]
. However, the proof of this result would not follow by a simple modification of the argument in
[
14
]
and would require a substantial separate analysis, which we avoid here for the sake of brevity.

The second ingredient required for the analysis of the microscopic regime is the real analogue of Proposition
3.7
, which we now state.

Proposition S3.4
.

Let
X
be a real
N\times N
i.i.d. matrix satisfying Assumption
2.1
. Fix (small)
\delta,\delta_{r},\epsilon,\omega_{*}\penalty\ \!>\penalty\ \!0
. Then uniformly in
z_{l}\in(1-\delta)\mathbf{D}
with
|\Im z_{l}|\geq N^{-1/2+\delta_{r}}
and
\eta_{l}\in[N^{-3/2+\epsilon},1]
,
l=1,2
, it holds that

(S3.10)

\left|\mathrm{Cov}(\langle G^{z_{1}}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{1})\rangle,\langle G^{z_{2}}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{2})\rangle)\right|\lesssim N^{\xi}\Phi_{2}^{r}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},z_{1},z_{2}),

for any fixed
\xi>0
, where
\Phi_{2}^{r}=\Phi_{2}^{r}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},z_{1},z_{2})
is defined by

(S3.11)

\begin{split}\Phi_{2}^{r}:=&\min_{N^{-1+\omega_{*}}\leq t_{2}\leq t_{1}\leq N^{-\omega_{*}}}\min_{0\leq R\leq t_{2}|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}}\bigg(N\left(\mathcal{E}_{1}(t_{2},R)+\mathcal{E}_{0}(t_{2})\right)\left(N\eta_{1}+\frac{1}{N\eta_{1}}\right)\left(N\eta_{2}+\frac{1}{N\eta_{2}}\right)\\
&+Nt_{2}\left(\frac{1}{N|\Im z_{1}|^{2}}+\frac{1}{N|\Im z_{2}|^{2}}+\frac{1}{N|z_{1}-\overline{z}_{2}|^{2}}+\frac{1}{Nt_{1}}\right)\left(1+\frac{1}{N\eta_{1}}\right)^{2}\left(1+\frac{1}{N\eta_{2}}\right)^{2}\\
&+\sqrt{N}t_{1}\left(1+\frac{1}{N\eta_{*}}\right)^{3}\frac{1}{N^{2}\eta_{1}\eta_{2}}\bigg),\end{split}

with
\eta_{*}:=\eta_{1}\wedge\eta_{2}
and
\mathcal{E}_{0}
,
\mathcal{E}_{1}
defined in (
3.43
) and (
3.46
), respectively.

The source of the term in the second line of (
S3.11
) is the same as in the discussion below Proposition
S3.3
: we do not prove the real version of Theorem
6.1
, but instead use the complex result directly, which yields an additional loss in the GFT owing to the slight mismatch in the second moments. Eventually, this leads to the additional term in the rhs. of (
2.5
) compared to the (
2.4
) in the complex case. We also remark that due to this term, the control parameter
\Phi_{2}^{r}
becomes ineffective in the regime when
|z_{1}-\overline{z}_{2}|\lesssim N^{-1/2}
, in the sense that the local law (
4.1
) implies a better bound in this regime compared to (
3.45
). Moreover, in the critical case
z_{1}=\overline{z}_{2}
,
\Phi_{2}^{r}
blows up. This is a manifestation of the symmetry of the spectrum of a real matrix
X
with respect to the real axis.

Finally, we formulate the analogue of the local law from Proposition
4.2
in the real setting. While Proposition
4.2
addresses only the two-resolvent chains, along its proof, which is presented in Section
S2
, we also obtained suboptimal local laws for three- and four-resolvent chains as a byproduct. Though these local laws were not needed for the proof of Theorem
2.4
, now they are required as ingredients for the GFT in Propositions
S3.3
and
S3.4
. Additionally, for the same purpose we need to cover the case when
X
is a real i.i.d. matrix with a small complex Gaussian component, however with much less precision: instead of local laws only the size bounds on the two- and four-resolvent chains are required. This is the second statement of the following proposition.

Proposition S3.5
.

(i) Assume the set-up and conditions of Proposition
4.2
modulo the replacement of the complex
X_{0}
and
\widetilde{X}
matrices by real matrices. Recall the definition of
\widehat{\beta}_{12}^{[b]}=\widehat{\beta}^{[b]}_{12}(w_{1},w_{2})
from (
S2.128
). Denote
G_{l}:=G^{z_{l}}(w_{l})
for
l=1,2
. Then, for any fixed
\delta,\epsilon,\kappa>0
, we have

(S3.12)

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\left(G_{1}B_{1}G_{2}-M_{12}^{B_{1}}(w_{1},w_{2})\right)B_{2}\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\prec\frac{1}{N\eta_{*}\widehat{\beta}_{12}^{[b]}},

(S3.13)

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\left(G_{1}B_{1}G_{2}B_{2}G_{1}^{(*)}-M_{121}^{B_{1},B_{2}}\big(w_{1},w_{2},w_{1}^{(*)}\big)\right)B_{3}\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\prec\frac{1}{N\eta_{*}^{3}}\wedge\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\eta_{*}}\eta_{*}\widehat{\beta}_{12}^{[b]}},

(S3.14)

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\left(G_{1}B_{1}G_{2}B_{2}G_{1}^{(*)}B_{3}G_{2}^{(*)}-M_{1212}^{B_{1},B_{2},B_{3}}\big(w_{1},w_{2},w_{1}^{(*)},w_{2}^{(*)}\big)\right)B_{4}\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\prec\frac{1}{N\eta_{*}^{4}}\wedge\frac{1}{\eta_{*}\big(\widehat{\beta}_{12}^{[b]}\big)^{2}},

uniformly in
B_{l}\in\mathrm{span}\{E_{\pm},F^{(*)}\}
,
l\in[4]
,
|z_{i}|\leq 1-\delta
,
\Re w_{i}\in\mathbf{B}_{\kappa}^{z_{i}}
, and
N^{-1+\epsilon}\leq\eta_{i}\leq N^{100}
for
i\in[2]
.

(ii) Fix further
\omega_{*}>0
and consider
\widehat{X}:=\sqrt{1-\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}^{2}}X+\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}\widetilde{X}_{c}
, where
\widetilde{X}_{c}
is an
N\times N
complex Ginibre matrix, independent of
X
,
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}\in[0,1]
, and
X
satisfies the conditions of Proposition
S3.5
(i). Denoting again by
G_{l}=G^{z_{l}}(w_{l})
the resolvent associated to the Hermitization of
\widehat{X}
, we have

(S3.15)

\displaystyle\left|\langle G_{1}B_{1}G_{2}^{\mathfrak{t}}B_{2}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\prec\frac{1}{\widehat{\beta}^{[b]}_{1\overline{2}}},

(S3.16)

\displaystyle\left|\langle G_{1}B_{1}G_{2}^{\mathfrak{t}}B_{2}G_{1}^{(*)}B_{3}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\prec\frac{1}{\eta_{*}\widehat{\beta}^{[b]}_{1\overline{2}}},

(S3.17)

\displaystyle\left|\langle G_{1}B_{1}G_{2}^{\mathfrak{t}}B_{2}G_{1}^{(*)}B_{3}(G_{2}^{\mathfrak{t}})^{(*)}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\prec\frac{1}{\eta_{*}\big(\widehat{\beta}^{[b]}_{1\overline{2}}\big)^{2}},

uniformly in
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}\in[0,N^{-\omega_{*}}]
and in the rest of the parameters as stated below (
S3.14
).

The proof of Proposition
S3.5
is presented in Section
S3.2
. Apart from Proposition
S3.5
, the proof of Theorem
2.6
requires also the real versions of the local laws from Proposition
4.5
and
4.8
. These results hold without any changes in the formulations, and their proofs do not require any additional adjustments apart from the ones discussed in Section
S3.2
. We omit further details.

S3.1.
Proof of Theorem
2.6

In this section we derive the analogues of Propositions
3.1
and
3.2
in the real case from Propositions
S3.1
–
S3.4
and conclude the proof of Theorem
2.6
. We omit most of the calculations as they are analogous to the ones presented in Section
5
.

S3.1.1.
Analysis of the expectation

First, fix
\delta_{r}>0
and assume the set-up and conditions of Proposition
3.1
(modulo the replacement of the complex case by the real one). Denote
\nu_{2}:=1/11
and recall from Proposition
3.1
that
\nu_{0}=1/14
. We claim that

(S3.18)

\mathbf{E}\mathcal{L}_{N}\big(\omega_{a,N}^{(z_{0})}\big)=\frac{N}{\pi}\left(1+\mathcal{O}(N^{-c})\right)+N^{2a+\xi}\mathcal{O}\left(\left(N|\Im z_{0}|^{2}\right)^{-1/3}+N^{-\nu_{2}}\right),

for any
a\in[1/2,1/2+\nu_{0})
, uniformly in
|z|\leq 1-\delta
with
|\Im z|\geq N^{-1/2+\delta_{r}}
. Here
c>0
is some small constant independent of
N
. The main difference of (
S3.18
) from its complex counterpart (
3.5
) is that the error term in (
S3.18
) may be larger than the leading one for sufficiently small
|\Im z_{0}|
, while in (
3.5
) the error term is always smaller.

To prove (
S3.18
), we follow the proof of Proposition
3.1
. Denote

p:=N|\Im z_{0}|^{2}\in[N^{2\delta_{r}},N].

Since the diameter of support of
\omega_{a,N}^{(z_{0})}
is of order
N^{-a}\ll|\Im z_{0}|
, it holds that
N|\Im z|^{2}\sim p
for every
z\in\mathrm{supp}(\omega_{a,N}^{(z_{0})})
. We take
\eta_{c}:=N^{-1+\delta_{1}}
for a small
\delta_{1}>0
, and
\eta_{0}:=N^{-1}p^{-1/6}
to optimize the bounds. The estimates in the mesoscopic regime
\eta\geq\eta_{c}
do not change, while instead of (
5.20
) we have

(S3.19)

|\mathbf{E}J_{T}|+|\mathbf{E}I_{0}^{\eta_{L}}|+|\mathbf{E}I_{\eta_{L}}^{\eta_{0}}|\lesssim((N\eta_{0})^{2}+N^{-2\nu_{1}}+p^{-1/2})N^{2a+\xi}.

Here the additional
p^{-1/2}
term arises from (
S3.7
) employed in the analysis of
|\mathbf{E}I_{\eta_{L}}^{\eta_{0}}|
. In the intermediate regime
\eta\in[\eta_{0},\eta_{c}]
we apply Proposition
S3.3
and optimize (
S3.9
) by choosing

t_{1}:=N^{-3/4}(N\eta)^{3/2}p^{1/3}\quad\text{and}\quad t_{2}:=N^{-1}(N\eta)^{2}p^{2/3}.

For
p\lesssim N^{3/11}
this gives

\Phi_{1}^{r}(\eta)\lesssim(N\eta)^{-1}p^{-1/3}+(N\eta)^{-3}p^{-2/3}+N^{-1/4}(N\eta)^{-5/2}p^{1/3}.

Arguing similarly to the proof of Proposition
3.1
and taking into account the error term from (
S3.19
), we conclude that

\mathbf{E}\mathcal{L}_{N}\big(\omega_{a,N}^{(z_{0})}\big)=\frac{N}{\pi}\left(1+\mathcal{O}(N^{-c})\right)+N^{2a+\xi}\mathcal{O}\left(p^{-1/3}+N^{-2\nu_{1}}+N^{-1/4}p^{7/12}\right),

which immediately implies (
S3.18
) for
p\leq N^{3/11}
. It remains to notice that the bounds in Propositions
S3.2
and
S3.3
improve as
p
increases, so for
p>N^{3/11}
(
S3.18
) holds with
p
replaced by
N^{3/11}
, which gives the error term of order
N^{-1/11}
. This finishes the proof of (
S3.18
).

Recall the definition of
f_{a,N}^{\pm}
from (
3.11
). We show that

(S3.20)

\left|\mathbf{E}\mathcal{L}_{N}\left(f_{a,N}^{+}-f_{a,N}^{-}\right)\right|\lesssim N^{1/2-\alpha+\xi}\left(N^{1/2-a}+N^{a-1/2-\nu_{2}}+N^{a-1/2-2(1/2-\alpha)/3}\right).

To establish (
S3.20
), we argue as in (
3.14
) and use the definition of
\Omega_{N}^{\pm}
from (
3.10
). We get

(S3.21)

\mathbf{E}\mathcal{L}_{N}\left(f_{a,N}^{+}-f_{a,N}^{-}\right)=\int_{\Omega_{N}^{+}\setminus\Omega_{N}^{-}}\mathbf{E}\mathcal{L}_{N}\big(\omega_{a,N}^{(z)}\big)\mathrm{d}z.

For
|\Im z|\geq N^{-1/2+\delta_{r}}
we estimate the integrand in (
S3.21
) by (
S3.18
), while for
|\Im z|<N^{-1/2+\delta_{r}}
we use the rough upper bound of order
N^{2a+\xi}
, which immediately follows from the Girko’s formula (
3.20
) and the single-resolvent local law (
4.1
). However, in the second regime the smallness comes from the volume factor. Indeed, by Assumption
2.5
we have

\left|\left(\Omega_{N}^{+}\setminus\Omega_{N}^{-}\right)\cap\left\{z\,:\,|\Im z|<N^{-1/2+\delta_{r}}\right\}\right|\lesssim N^{-1/2-a+\delta_{r}}.

Combining these inputs and choosing sufficiently small
\delta_{r}>0
, we obtain (
S3.20
) by an elementary calculation.

S3.1.2.
Analysis of the variance

Assume the set-up and conditions of Proposition
3.2
(modulo the replacement of the complex case by the real one). Denote
\nu_{3}:=1/53
and recall that
q_{0}=1/20
. We claim that

(S3.22)

\mathrm{Var}\left[\mathcal{L}_{N}(f_{a,N})\right]\lesssim N^{2(a-\alpha)-2q_{0}(1/2-\alpha)+\xi}+N^{2(a-\alpha)-\nu_{3}+\xi}.

The proof of (
S3.22
) closely follows the one of Proposition
3.2
. First, we upper bound
\mathrm{Var}[I_{\eta_{c}}^{T}]
where
\eta_{c}
is chosen as before. Fix a (small)
\delta_{r}>0
. We represent
\mathrm{Var}[I_{\eta_{c}}^{T}]
in the form (
3.26
) and distinguish between the two regimes of
(z_{1},z_{2})
-integration:
|\Im z_{1}|\wedge|\Im z_{2}|\leq N^{-1/2+\delta_{r}}
and the complementary regime. In the first regime we use the trivial bound

(S3.23)

\left|\mathrm{Cov}\left(\langle G^{z_{1}}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{1})\rangle,\langle G^{z_{2}}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{2})\rangle\right)\right|\lesssim\frac{N^{\xi}}{N^{2}\eta_{1}\eta_{2}},

which immediately follows from (
4.1
). The small volume of the
z_{1},z_{2}
integration regime will compensate
for the crudeness of the bound (
S3.23
). In the regime
|\Im z_{1}|\wedge|\Im z_{2}|\geq N^{-1/2+\delta_{r}}
we employ Proposition
S3.1
and argue as in (
5.38
)–(
5.40
). The only difference is that now the regime
|\Im z_{1}|\wedge|\Im z_{2}|\leq N^{-1/2+\delta_{r}}
is missing in (
5.39
), so it should be added back and taken into account in (
5.40
). This is done by simply estimating
|V_{12}|\lesssim(\eta_{1}\eta_{2})^{-1}
and
|U_{j}|\lesssim 1
,
j=1,2
, as it follows from (
3.36
). Using these inputs, we get that
\mathrm{Var}[I_{\eta_{c}}^{T}]
admits the bound (
5.41
) in the real case, i.e. the upper bound on the mesoscopic regime does not deteriorate compared to the complex case.

Next, we derive an upper bound on

(S3.24)

\mathrm{Var}[I_{\eta_{L}}^{\eta_{c}}]=\left(\frac{N}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\right)^{2}\int_{\mathbf{C}}\int_{\mathbf{C}}\Delta f_{N}(z_{1})\overline{\Delta f_{N}(z_{2})}\int_{\eta_{L}}^{\eta_{c}}\int_{\eta_{L}}^{\eta_{c}}\mathrm{Cov}\left(\langle G^{z_{1}}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{1})\rangle,\langle G^{z_{2}}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{2})\rangle\right)\mathrm{d}\eta_{1}\mathrm{d}\eta_{2}\mathrm{d}^{2}z_{1}\mathrm{d}^{2}z_{2}.

Denote for short

(S3.25)

p=p(z_{1},z_{2}):=\min\left\{N|\Im z_{1}|^{2},N|\Im z_{2}|^{2},N|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2},N|z_{1}-\overline{z}_{2}|^{2}\right\}.

In the regime
p\leq N^{2\delta_{r}}
we again use the bound (
S3.23
) together with the small volume effect. Hence, we can further focus on the complementary regime. For each pair of parameters
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathrm{supp}(f_{N})
with
p>N^{2\delta_{r}}
we take
\eta_{0}=\eta_{0}(z_{1},z_{2})\in(\eta_{L},N^{-1})
. This intermediate scale will be chosen at the end to optimize the bound on the rhs. of (
S3.24
). Note that unlike in the complex case,
\eta_{0}
depends on
z_{1},z_{2}
, so the splitting of the integration regime
[\eta_{L},\eta_{c}]
into
[\eta_{L},\eta_{0}]
and
[\eta_{0},\eta_{c}]
must be done inside the
z_{1},z_{2}
-integration in (
S3.24
).

Fix
z_{1},z_{2}
and consider the cross-regime when
\eta_{1}\in[\eta_{L},\eta_{0}]
and
\eta_{2}\in[\eta_{0},\eta_{c}]
. We have

(S3.26)

\begin{split}\left|\int_{\eta_{L}}^{\eta_{0}}\int_{\eta_{0}}^{\eta_{c}}\mathrm{Cov}\left(\langle G^{z_{1}}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{1})\rangle,\langle G^{z_{2}}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{2})\rangle\right)\mathrm{d}\eta_{1}\mathrm{d}\eta_{2}\right|\lesssim&N^{-1+\xi}\mathbf{E}\left|\int_{\eta_{L}}^{\eta_{0}}\langle G^{z_{1}}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{1})\rangle\mathrm{d}\eta_{1}\right|\\
\lesssim&N^{-2+\xi}\left((N\eta_{0})^{2}+N^{-2\nu_{1}}+\left(N|\Im z_{1}|^{2}\right)^{-1/2}\right).\end{split}

Here in the first line we used (
3.34
) and to go from the first to the second line we combined Proposition
S3.2
with the argument from the proof of Lemma
5.1
. Similarly we get that the same bound holds in the case when
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in[\eta_{L},\eta_{0}]
. In the regime
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in[\eta_{0},\eta_{c}]
we apply Proposition
S3.4
and optimize the error term
\Phi_{2}^{r}
in (
S3.11
) by taking

(S3.27)

R:=p^{4/5},\quad t_{1}:=N^{-3/4}p^{1/20}(N\eta_{*})^{1/2},\quad t_{2}:=N^{-1}p^{1/10}.

For sufficiently small
\eta_{*}
this choice is invalid, since
t_{1}
becomes smaller than
t_{2}
. However, in this situation taking (
S3.27
) in the rhs. of (
S3.11
) would give an upper bound on

\Phi_{2}^{r}
which is much larger than
(N^{2}\eta_{1}\eta_{2})^{-1}
. Since this latter bound holds by the local law (
4.1
), one can make a choice (
S3.27
) for any
\eta_{*}\leq\eta_{c}
.
Integrating the resulting bound for
\Phi_{2}^{r}
over
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in[\eta_{0},\eta_{c}]
and using (
S3.26
), we get

(S3.28)

\begin{split}&N^{2}\left|\int_{\eta_{L}}^{\eta_{c}}\int_{\eta_{L}}^{\eta_{c}}\mathrm{Cov}\left(\langle G^{z_{1}}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{1})\rangle,\langle G^{z_{2}}(\mathrm{i}\eta_{2})\rangle\right)\mathrm{d}\eta_{1}\mathrm{d}\eta_{2}\right|\\
&\quad\lesssim N^{8\delta_{1}+\xi}\left(p^{-1/20}+p^{-9/10}(N\eta_{0})^{-2}+N^{-1/4}p^{1/20}(N\eta_{0})^{-5/2}+(N\eta_{0})^{2}\right).\end{split}

To optimize (
S3.28
) over
\eta_{0}
, we take
\eta_{0}:=N^{-1}p^{-9/40}
.
We use this bound for
p\leq N^{20/53}
, while in the complementary regime employ the same monotonicity idea as above (
S3.20
). It remains to perform the integration over
z_{1},z_{2}
by the means of Lemma
5.2
. This finishes the proof of (
S3.22
).

S3.1.3.
Conclusion of the proof of Theorem
2.6

Combining (
S3.20
) with (
S3.22
) as in Section
3.1
, we get

(S3.29)

\begin{split}\mathrm{Var}\left[\mathcal{L}_{N}(\phi_{N})\right]\lesssim&N^{2(a-\alpha)+\xi}\left(N^{-2q_{0}(1/2-\alpha)}+N^{-\nu_{3}}\right)\\
+&N^{1-2\alpha+\xi}\left(N^{1-2a}+N^{2a-1-2\nu_{2}}+N^{2a-1-4(1/2-\alpha)/3}\right).\end{split}

We optimize the rhs. of (
S3.29
) in
a\in[1/2,1/2+\nu_{0})
, which leads to the choice

(S3.30)

a:=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{4}\min\left\{2q_{0}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\alpha\right),\nu_{3}\right\}.

Substituting (
S3.30
) into (
S3.29
), we finish the proof of Theorem
2.6
.
∎

S3.2.
Proof of Proposition
S3.5

First we prove that (
S3.12
)–(
S3.14
) hold for the Gauss-divisible real matrix
X
satisfying the assumptions of Proposition
S3.5
. We closely follow the proof of Proposition
4.2
presented in Section
S2
and only outline the differences arising in the real set-up compared to the complex one. In particular, we focus on the case
b=0
which corresponds to
\mathfrak{s}\sim 1
, while the general case
b\in[0,1]
follows by a minor adjustment, similarly to Section
S2.4
. Moreover, we consider only observables equal to
E_{\pm}
, for the treatment of off-diagonal observables see Section
S2.2.4
.

We replace the complex-valued Brownian motion in the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process (
S2.1
) with the real-valued one and keep the characteristic flow (
S2.11
) unchanged. This does not affect the estimates in Proposition
S2.5
, and there is only a minor modification in the zig equations in Section
S2.2.1
. Specifically, the generator
\mathcal{A}_{[k],t}
in (
S2.61
) as well as the analysis of the martingale term
\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{E}_{[k],t}
remain unchanged, while the following new terms appear in the forcing term (
S2.56
) due to the real symmetry of the model:

(S3.31)

\frac{\sigma}{N}\sum_{i\leq j}\left\langle G_{1,t}B_{1}\cdots G_{i,t}E_{\sigma}\left(G_{i,t}B_{i}\cdots G_{j,t}\right)^{\mathfrak{t}}E_{\sigma}G_{j,t}B_{j}\cdots G_{k,t}B_{k}\right\rangle.

The emergence of these terms does not change the bounds in Proposition
S2.10
. Moreover, we estimate all terms in (
S3.31
) in the same way by upper bounding the absolute value of the term indexed by
1\leq i\leq j\leq k
in (
S3.31
) (together with the prefactor
N^{-1}
) as follows:

(S3.32)

N^{-1}\left\langle\left|G_{i,t}B_{i}\cdots G_{j,t}\right|^{2}\right\rangle^{1/2}\left\langle\left|G_{j,t}B_{j}\cdots G_{k,t}B_{k}G_{1,t}B_{1}\cdots G_{j,t}\right|^{2}\right\rangle^{1/2},

by the means of Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. This reduces the quantities in (
S3.31
) to the resolvent chains containing only
G_{1,t}^{(*)}
and
G_{2,t}^{(*)}
, which are then further reduced to shorter chains by the reduction inequalities (
S2.89
), (
S2.91
), and estimated by the stopping time (
S2.70
).

Now we prove Proposition
S3.5
(ii). Let
X
be a real matrix satisfying assumptions of Proposition
S3.5
(i). Define
X_{t}
by the flow (
S2.1
) starting from
X
, where
B_{t}
is the complex-valued Brownian motion. Additionally, we evolve
z_{j},w_{j}
for
j=1,2
, along the characteristic flow (
S2.11
). Since (
S3.15
)–(
S3.17
) hold for
t=0
by Propositions
S3.5
(i) and
S2.4
, it suffices to show that these bounds propagate along the flow up to the time
t\leq N^{-\omega_{*}}
in the same sense as in Proposition
S2.5
. We need two inputs to prove this propagation. First, one needs to show that
X_{t}
satisfies the local low (
4.1
). This follows from
[
38
, Lemma B.7]
, which states this result for spectral parameters on the imaginary axis, though its proof immediately extends to the bulk regime. The second input is the two-resolvent local law

(S3.33)

\left|\left\langle\left(G_{1,t}B_{1}G_{1,t}^{(*)}-M_{11,t}^{B_{1}}(w_{1,t},w_{2,t}^{(*)})\right)B_{2}\right\rangle\right|\prec\frac{1}{N\eta_{1,t}^{2}},

for
B_{1},B_{2}\in\mathrm{span}\{E_{\pm},F^{(*)}\}
and
t\leq N^{-\omega_{*}}
. Since the resolvent chain in (
S3.33
) does not involve
G_{1}^{\mathfrak{t}}
, the proof of (
S3.33
) is identical to the zig step in the proof of (
4.28
) for
k=2
.

Let us differentiate the time-dependent analogue of (
S3.15
) along the flow. We get, similarly to (
S2.51
), that

(S3.34)

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}\langle G_{1,t}B_{1}G_{2,t}^{\mathfrak{t}}B_{2}\rangle=&\langle G_{1,t}B_{1}G_{2,t}^{\mathfrak{t}}B_{2}\rangle\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\sigma}{N}\langle G_{1,t}(G_{1,t}B_{1}G_{2,t}^{\mathfrak{t}})^{\mathfrak{t}}G_{2,t}^{\mathfrak{t}}B_{2}\rangle\mathrm{d}t\\
+&\langle G_{1,t}-M_{1,t}\rangle\langle G_{1,t}^{2}B_{1}G_{2,t}^{\mathfrak{t}}B_{2}\rangle\mathrm{d}t+\langle G_{2,t}-M_{2,t}\rangle\langle G_{1,t}B_{1}(G_{2,t}^{\mathfrak{t}})^{2}B_{2}\rangle\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}\mathcal{E}_{[2],t},\end{split}

where
\mathrm{d}\mathcal{E}_{[2],t}
is a martingale term. Unlike in Section
S2.2.1
, we do not subtract the corresponding differential equation for the deterministic approximation from (
S3.34
). Observe that the only linear term in (
S3.34
), in the terminology introduced below (
S2.56
), is the first term in the rhs. of (
S3.34
). This means that for
k=2
there is no analogue of the generator
\mathcal{A}_{[2],t}
from (
S2.61
) in the current setting, so no Duhamel formula or propagators are needed. To estimate the remaining terms in the rhs. of (
S3.34
), one needs only the size bounds of the form (
S3.15
)–(
S2.24
